# TO PAUL III, SUPREME PONTIFF,
# CAMILLO GEROUSCO SENDS GREETINGS.

->**CAMILLO GEROUSCO**<- <note>Camillo Gherusco was a 16th-century scholar and clerk who served in the papal court. Paul III (Alessandro Farnese) was Pope from 1534 to 1549, known for his patronage of the arts and the opening of the Council of Trent.</note>

NOTHING SEEMS to me more conducive to the refinement and ease of acquiring knowledge, nor to a broad experience <term>poly-peiria</term> <note>From the Greek 'poly' (many) and 'peira' (experience); a thorough, multifaceted expertise gained through practice.</note> in affairs and their precise management, than the collection of histories and a ready memory of them. Through these, we grasp the complexities of nature and learn to govern political, public, and private matters. By holding these histories before the mind like a mirror of reality, we are equipped not only to investigate and contemplate ideas through abstract thought, but to see what must be done as if with our very eyes.

I could cite many examples of those who governed with the utmost wisdom and precision, excelling in their learning—not only by their natural gifts but because they became exceptionally shrewd and knowledgeable through the study of history. But since I have the opportunity, O Supreme Pontiff <note>original: "Archieri" (High Priest), a standard Renaissance Greek term for the Pope.</note>, to present you as the most illustrious and steadfast example of all, I shall set aside the examples of others and the ancients who are far removed from our memory. Instead, I will focus my testimony solely upon your sagacity <term>anchinoia</term> <note>A term often used in Greek philosophy for "quickness of mind" or the ability to hit upon the right solution instantly.</note>, your knowledge, and your industriousness in great affairs. 

For I have often observed the great zeal you apply to all branches of learning, especially to the knowledge of history and its ready recall. I have heard you recount histories with ease and accuracy and discourse upon various problems of nature and theology. I am fully convinced that your grasp of these subjects stems from a noble nature combined with the zeal and diligence you apply to every pursuit. You have used your learning intensely, making your memory a storehouse <term>tameion</term> of instructive treasures—surpassing all others in this regard. For I know for certain that no one is a more dedicated seeker of knowledge, having devoted himself to the sciences and philosophy, and having [multi-]

<summary>This page features a formal dedication by Camillo Gerousco to Pope Paul III, praising the utility of history in governance and lauding the Pope’s own legendary memory and intellectual breadth.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Paul III, Camillo Gerousco, historiography, political administration, memory, sagacity, Renaissance papacy</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory preface to Pope Paul III. The previous page introduced the value of history and began praising the Pope’s intellectual sharpness (sagacity). This page specifically focuses on his legendary memory and the intellectual circles of his family, particularly his grandsons, before introducing the author being edited: Aelian.</meta>

...printed, to show clearly that your pursuit of wisdom is not without honor, and that you surpass all those who pride themselves on their memory, for you have preserved yours as firmly as your bodily strength into your current age. Regarding that man of whom they say his exceeding memory was a sign of nobility <note>likely referring to historical figures like Mithridates or Cyrus the Great, famous for their mnemonic feats</note>, I do not only refuse to compare him to you, but I believe he is inferior to you. You have excelled all men in the variety of your education, the power of your knowledge, and your greatest deeds, so that your memory has become a far greater symbol of your nobility, your broad learning <term>polymathia</term> <note>From the Greek 'poly' (much) and 'mathesis' (learning); refers to encyclopedic knowledge across many fields.</note>, and your wide experience <term>poly-peiria</term>.

Driven by your example, the most reverend and illustrious Cardinal Farnese <note>Alessandro Farnese the Younger (1520–1589), the Pope's grandson and a major patron of the arts and letters.</note>, being a Roman and an enthusiast of the Greek dialect, is always immersed in every kind of teaching. He progresses more every day, which is truly marvelous given his management of such great affairs of state. Even while laboring through these duties, he graciously lends his ear to those who meet him, managing matters diligently, prudently, and with great speed. Following in these same footsteps, the most brilliant Cardinal of Santa Fiora <note>Guido Ascanio Sforza di Santa Fiora (1518–1564), another grandson of Paul III, who served as a high-ranking papal official.</note> also pursues all kinds of sciences, wisely transacting necessary business. Just as both of these men are related to you by blood, they similarly pursue the same intellectual interests as you.

Therefore, considering how you take pleasure in the reading of histories, and how you are accustomed to benefit from them and to show favor and love to the studious, I thought it would not be "out of tune" <note>original: "exo melous," a musical metaphor meaning inappropriate or inconsistent.</note> if I were to publish this small book of Aelian’s *Miscellaneous History* <note>original: "Poikile Historia." Claudius Aelian (c. 175 – 235 AD) was a Roman who wrote in Greek; his work is a collection of anecdotes about famous people, nature, and customs.</note> along with certain other manuals. This work provides no small pleasure and implants a great deal of benefit in those who are studious. This is especially true since Aelian, though a Roman, took delight in the Greek dialect when he wrote; and because it is clear that a Roman wrote this, he perhaps has not escaped our notice. Indeed, he wrote so sweetly that he was justly deemed worthy to be called "honey-tongued" and "honey-voiced."

I believe this task of editing should be undertaken all the more because, until now, the work has been read by few or has not been fully understood due to the corruption of the manuscripts. But now...

<summary>In this preface, Camillo Gherusco praises Pope Paul III’s legendary memory and the Greek scholarship of his grandsons, Cardinals Farnese and Sforza, while introducing a new edition of Aelian’s Miscellaneous History.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Paul III, Alessandro Farnese, Cardinal of Santa Fiora, Aelian, Miscellaneous History, memory, Greek language, Renaissance humanism</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory preface to Pope Paul III. The previous page praised the Pope's extraordinary memory and his grandsons, Cardinals Alessandro Farnese and Guido Ascanio Sforza. Here, the author transitions to the specific scholarly works he has edited and is now presenting as a gift.</meta>

Therefore, with much earnestness, I assisted that fellow citizen whom you wished me to help with the work I have corrected. I have shared it so that it might be read by all, having labored so that it would be beneficial and not allowing it to be hidden or neglected. For the way these works serve as a summary to recall many and varied histories <term>historiai</term> <note>original: ἱστοριῶν; in this context, it refers to "inquiries" or "accounts" of natural wonders and human events, specifically those found in the works of Aelian.</note>, and provide knowledge of authors whom none of us currently possess, will be confirmed more by experience than by words. 

Furthermore, I have added the books on Physiognomy <term>physiognomonica</term> <note>The study of a person's character or fortune from their physical features, particularly the face. It was a highly respected field of study in the Renaissance.</note> by Polemon and Adamantius. These are extremely useful for everyone, but especially for one who is about to engage in public affairs, in order to know with whom one should associate, with whom to live, and whom to trust—or whom one ought to shun and guard against. This involves understanding from signs which nature has secretly etched into each person, both in the face and in other parts of the body. 

These matters are written more vividly by Polemon and more clearly by Adamantius than they were by Aristotle <note>The author is comparing his sources to the *Physiomonics* traditionally attributed to Aristotle, claiming these versions are superior.</note>. I have published one of these, which stood in need of no small correction; the other I have included because, although it was printed previously, it was neither accurate nor correct, as will be clearly seen and known by those who compare the two.

Having cleansed all these with great care, and having made them—as far as was in my power—bright and pure from their previous obscurity, and having brought them to a state of clarity, I submit them to your most learned judgment. I return them with the goodwill which you show to all who are diligent, and I dedicate them to your most holy name, so that those who choose to acquire them may benefit all the more. These are gifts which I, owing you the greatest gratitude for your many favors toward me, graciously ask you to receive with kindness. May you consider this favor no less than the others, and I shall preserve all the honors you bestow upon me with an everlasting record of witness. 

Farewell.

<summary>The author presents his newly edited editions of Polemon and Adamantius’s works on physiognomy to Pope Paul III, arguing that these texts are essential tools for judging character in public life and are superior to previous Aristotelian versions.</summary>
<keywords>Polemon, Adamantius, Physiognomy, Aristotle, Pope Paul III, textual criticism, character judgment, gratitude</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the editor's dedication to Pope Paul III into the biographical source material for Claudius Aelianus (Aelian), the author of the "Various Histories" and "On the Nature of Animals." The translator provides the traditional "Life of Aelian" from the Roman-era biographer Philostratus and a brief entry from the Byzantine encyclopedia known as the Suda.</meta>

# THE LIFE OF AELIAN FROM
# Philostratus.

AELIAN was Roman, yet he spoke Attic Greek <term>Atticized</term> <note>original: ἀττίκιζε (attikize); this refers to the Second Sophistic movement's practice of imitating the classical Athenian dialect of the 5th and 4th centuries BCE, which was considered the pinnacle of literary elegance.</note> just like the Athenians from the inland country. This man seems worthy of my praise, first, because he achieved a pure style of speech while living in a city that used another language <note>Referring to Rome, where Latin was the primary language.</note>, and second, because although he was addressed as a "Sophist" <term>sophist</term> <note>original: σοφιστής; in the Roman Empire, this was a prestigious title for professional public speakers and teachers of rhetoric.</note> by those who grant such titles, he did not believe them, nor did he puff up his own ego or become arrogant because of so great a title. Instead, having carefully examined himself and realizing he was not suited for public declamation <term>melete</term> <note>original: μελέτῃ; a prepared practice speech or formal rhetorical performance common among sophists.</note>, he applied himself to writing, and for this he was admired. In general, the character of his style shows a simplicity <term>apheleia</term> <note>original: ἀφέλεια; a technical term in rhetoric for a plain, charming, and unadorned style.</note> that touches upon the grace of Nicostratus; however, in certain ways, he looks toward Dio and his particular intensity. 

Philostratus of Lemnos once met him while Aelian was still holding a book in his hand and reading it with great anger. When Philostratus asked what he was so busy with, Aelian replied, "I have composed a denunciation of the Gynnis." <note>original: γύννιδος (gynnidos); literally "womanish" or "effeminate man." This likely refers to the Emperor Elagabalus, who was despised by traditionalists for his perceived decadence.</note> For that is what I call the tyrant who was recently overthrown, since he shamed the Roman state with every kind of lewdness." Philostratus replied, "I would admire you more if you had denounced him while he was still alive; for to strike at a living tyrant is the mark of a man, but to trample on one who is fallen is the mark of anyone." 

This man used to claim that he had never traveled anywhere outside the borders of Italy, never boarded a ship, and never even knew the sea. Because of this, he was esteemed even more in Rome, as he was seen as a man who honored traditional customs. He was a student of Pausanias, but he admired Herodes <note>Herodes Atticus, the most famous and wealthy sophist of the 2nd century CE.</note> as the most versatile of orators. He lived to be over sixty years old. He died childless, for he declined to father children by never marrying. Whether he was happy or miserable for this is not the time to philosophize.

---

### From the Suda: <note>The *Suda* is a massive Byzantine encyclopedia and dictionary from the 10th century.</note>

AELIAN, from Praeneste <note>Modern Palestrina, near Rome.</note> in Italy, was a high priest and a sophist. He went by the name Claudius and was surnamed "Honey-tongued" <gloss>meliglossos</gloss> or "Honey-voiced" <gloss>meliphthongos</gloss>. He practiced as a sophist in Rome itself during the years following the reign of Hadrian.

<summary>This page provides two biographical sketches of Claudius Aelianus, a 2nd-3rd century Roman author who wrote in high-style Greek. It highlights his literary style, his refusal to travel, and his posthumous critique of a "womanish" tyrant.</summary>
<keywords>Aelian, Philostratus, Suda, Sophist, Attic Greek, Rome, Praeneste, Rhetoric, Biography</keywords>

# TO THE EARNEST READERS ~
## Greetings ~

TOGETHER with these very useful books—Melampus's treatise *On Palpitations* <note>original: περὶ παλμῶν (peri palmōn); a work on "divinatory anatomy" concerning the meaning of involuntary muscle twitches.</note> and the one *On Moles of the Body* <note>original: περὶ Ἐλαιῶν (peri elaion); a guide to physiognomy, predicting a person's character or fate based on birthmarks or moles. The word "elaia" in Greek refers to both an olive and a mole.</note>—this volume is sent for your delight and great entertainment. This is done so that you may know that nothing considered to contribute to your pleasure has been overlooked. Farewell. ~

### An Epigram by Camillo Porzio ###
### on the books of Aelian ~

Take me into your hands, whether you are wise or ignorant of all things,
I shall be of no small benefit to both.
I am accessible to all: a garden full of autumn fruits
of every kind; here you may find Attic honey <term>Attic honey</term> <note>original: ἀττικὸν μέλι (attikon meli); a common metaphor for the refined, elegant literary style of the classical Athenian dialect.</note>.
The greatness of my fatherland obscures my name and my lineage,
For glorious <term>glorious</term> <note>original: κυδιάνειρα (kydianeira); an epic Homeric epithet meaning "bringing glory to men" or "where men win glory."</note> Rome is my home. ~

<keywords>Melampus, divination, physiognomy, Aelian, Camillo Porzio, Attic honey, Rome, epigram</keywords>

# AELIAN’S VARIOUS HISTORY

## BOOK ONE ~

### On the Octopus ~

Octopuses are fierce when it comes to their bellies, and they are unstoppable in eating whatever they can catch. Indeed, they often feed on one another; the smaller one is caught by the larger, and having fallen into those most effective hunting-tools—the so-called "tentacles" <note>original: πλοκάμοις (plokamois); literally "curls" or "braids," used here to describe the octopus's arms.</note> of the fish—it then becomes its dinner. Octopuses also ambush fish in the following manner: they sit beneath the rocks and transform their own color to match them, appearing to be exactly like the rocks they naturally inhabit. Consequently, the fish swim toward the octopus as if toward a rock; then, while they are off their guard, the octopus entwines them in those natural nets, its tentacles. ~

### On Spiders ~

The spiders <note>original: φάλαγγες (phalanges); in antiquity, this term often referred specifically to venomous or hunting spiders, though here it refers to web-weavers.</note> neither know nor wish to know the art of weaving, nor the gifts of the Work-Goddess <term>Work-Goddess</term> <note>original: ἐργάνης δαίμονος (erganēs daimonos); an epithet of the goddess Athena (Athena Ergane), who was the patron of weavers and craftspeople.</note>, at least not in the sense that such a creature would ever make use of a woven garment. Yet, their web is a trap, and serves as a sort of "creel" <note>original: κῦρτος (kyrtos); a conical wicker basket used by fishermen to trap fish.</note> for those who fall into it. The spider <note>original: μελάνουρος (melanouros); literally "black-tailed." While usually a type of fish, Aelian uses it here as a poetic or specific name for a type of spider.</note> remains perfectly still, appearing motionless. Whatever falls in is caught, and the spider holds it fast. It catches only as much as the web is capable of holding and what is sufficient for its meal. ~

### On Egyptian Frogs ~

The race of Egyptian frogs is truly a clever thing, and they far surpass all others in intelligence. If an Egyptian frog encounters a "Nile hydra" <term>Nile hydra</term> <note>original: ὕδρα (hydra); a reference to the common Nile water snake.</note>, it bites onto a piece of reed and carries it crosswise. It grips it tightly and does not let go, holding it with great strength. The snake is then unable to swallow the frog along with the reed, for its mouth cannot stretch wide enough to encompass the length of the reed. Through this wisdom, the frogs overcome the physical strength of the water snakes. ~

### On the Egyptian Dog ~

<meta>The previous page concluded with descriptions of the octopus and common spiders. This page continues Book One of Aelian’s "On the Nature of Animals" (De Natura Animalium), focusing on the clever survival instincts of various creatures, specifically the Egyptian dog, the sea fox, sea turtles, wild boars, and deer.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

<margin>I</margin>
There is also that clever habit of the Egyptian dog: they do not drink from the river <note>Refers to the Nile.</note> all at once, nor greedily, nor freely, stooping over it together. Instead, they lap up water while thirsty, but they are suspicious of the beasts within <note>Crocodiles, which were a constant threat to animals drinking at the Nile’s edge.</note>. They run along the bank and drink by stealth, snatching whatever they can. Then they move on at intervals, and thus they are not destroyed, but succeed in quenching their thirst. ~

->On the Sea-Fox <-

The fox is not only a cunning creature on land, but the sea-fox <note>original: ἀλώπηξ (alōpēx); likely the thresher shark, known in antiquity for its perceived cunning.</note> is also a rogue. It does not suspect the bait, nor does it guard against it due to its lack of restraint. However, the fox utterly despises the hook. For before the fisherman can pull up the rod, the fox leaps up, bites through the fishing line <note>original: ὁρμιάν (hormian); a line made of horsehair or flax.</note>, and swims away again. Often, it has swallowed two or three hooks, yet the fisherman never dines on that creature as it goes wandering through the sea. ~

->On Sea Turtles <-

<margin>19</margin>
Sea turtles give birth on land. First, they bury all their eggs in the earth, then they return back to the waters where they usually swim. They are calculating in this regard, as if they were counting for themselves the forty days in which the hatchlings, having solidified within the eggs, become living creatures. They return again to the place where they hid their offspring, dig up the earth they had piled on top, and lead away the young, which are now moving and able to follow them. ~

->On Wild Boars <-

<margin>3</margin>
It seems that wild boars are not unlearned in a certain kind of medicine and self-treatment. For when they have caught and eaten blackbirds <note>original: κοψίχους (kopsichous); Aelian suggests that eating these birds causes a specific ailment in the boars.</note>, they drag their hind parts along and suffer from sneezing. However, they then gladly make their way to the water, where they gather and eat crabs as a most pleasant seasoning. These crabs become a remedy for their ailment and restore them to health once more. ~

->On the Venomous Spider <-

The venomous spider <term>phalangium</term> <note>original: Φαλάγγιον (phalangion); a term used for various venomous spiders, often the Mediterranean widow spider.</note> is as great an evil to deer as it is to humans, and there is no profit in trying to escape it quickly. However, if the deer taste wild germander <note>original: κηποῦ (kēpou); likely a species of Teucrium or "mountain leek," believed to be an antidote to venom.</note>, the bite causes them no pain. But it is necessary that the germander be of the wild variety. ~

->On the Sick Lion <-

It highlights the "calculating" nature of sea turtles and the use of natural antidotes by boars and deer.</summary>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues Book One of Claudius Aelianus’s "On the Nature of Animals." The previous page discussed the survival instincts of sea creatures and dogs. This page shifts to land animals and birds, specifically focusing on the medicinal knowledge of goats and the prophetic instincts of mice and ants.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

When a lion is sick, nothing else benefits it, but a monkey eaten serves as a medicine for its illness.

### How the wounded goats in Crete are healed
The Cretans are skilled in archery, and they shoot the goats as they graze on the mountain peaks. When these goats are struck, they immediately pluck the dittany <note>original: δικτάμου (diktamou); Origanum dictamnus, a medicinal herb native to Crete believed by the ancients to have the power to eject arrows from wounds.</note> herb; as soon as they taste it, all those arrows fall out of their bodies.

### That mice are prophetic
Mice, it seems, are among the most prophetic of animals. For when a house is growing old and is about to collapse, they are the first to sense it. Leaving behind their mouse-holes and their original haunts, they flee as fast as their feet can carry them and move to a new home.

### About ants
I hear that ants also possess a certain prophetic sense. For whenever a famine is about to occur, they are remarkably industrious in gathering treasures and storing up wheat and other seeds—whatever serves as a meal for ants—to reserve for themselves.

### About Gelon
Gelon of Syracuse <note>A famous tyrant of Syracuse who ruled in the early 5th century BCE, known for his victory over the Carthaginians.</note> dreamt that he was struck by lightning. Because of this, he cried out—not faintly or weakly as one does in a dream, but with a muffled roar, being terribly shocked by the terror of it. The dog that was sleeping beside him was startled by the shout; jumping up, it barked at him very fiercely, threateningly and intensely. Because of this, Gelon was both awakened and released from his fear.

### About the swan
Aristotle <note>Referring to Aristotle’s "History of Animals."</note> says that the swan has beautiful offspring and many of them, yet it also possesses a fierce spirit. Often, indeed, swans have come to anger and battle, even killing one another. The same author says that swans fight against eagles; they defend themselves, but do not start the injustice. That they are lovers of song is already a common legend. I myself have never heard a swan singing, and perhaps no one else has either, yet it is believed that they do sing. They say that the swan’s voice is most melodious and songful at one particular time: when it is near the end of its life. They also cross the open seas and fly over the ocean, and their wings do not grow weary.

α ii <note>A signature mark indicating the second leaf of the first gathering of the manuscript/printed book.</note>

<summary>This page describes the natural instincts and "hidden knowledge" of animals, including the medicinal use of dittany by Cretan goats and the prophetic nature of mice, ants, and swans.</summary>
<keywords>lion, Crete, goats, dittany, mice, ants, Gelon of Syracuse, swan song, Aristotle, animal intelligence</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition in Claudius Aelianus’s "On the Nature of Animals" from land animals to the habits and folklore of birds, specifically pigeons. It then begins a famous historical anecdote regarding the final days of the philosopher Socrates.</meta>

->BOOK<-

### About pigeons ~

It is said that pigeons take turns brooding their eggs. When the chicks are born, the male spits on them to ward off the evil eye <note>original: βασκανθῶσι (baskanthōsi). The belief that spitting could protect against "envy" or magical curses was a common folk remedy in antiquity.</note>, so they might not be bewitched by a bear <note>original: δι' ἄρκτου (di' arktou). This likely refers to a folk superstition that bears could curse the young, or perhaps a reference to the constellation of the Great Bear causing bad luck.</note>. The female lays two eggs; it is said the first is always male and the second is female. Pigeons give birth at every season of the year, so that they go into labor as many as ten times in a single year. An Egyptian account even claims that those in Egypt give birth twelve times.

Aristotle says that the pigeon and the wild dove are different; the pigeon <note>original: περιστερά (peristera)</note> is larger, while the wild dove <note>original: πελειάδα (peleiada)</note> is smaller. He also says that the male does not mount the female until he has first kissed her. Indeed, the females will not endure the company of the males without that preparatory kiss. He adds that females will even mount one another if they are deprived of a male mate, though they achieve nothing with each other except laying "wind eggs" <note>Unfertilized eggs that can never hatch into chicks.</note> from which no young are produced.

If we are to believe Callimachus <note>A famous scholar and poet at the Library of Alexandria.</note>, the wood-pigeon, the *pyrallis* <note>original: πυραλλίδα; a legendary bird often associated with fire.</note>, the common pigeon, and the turtle-dove are all quite different from one another. Indian accounts say that some pigeons in India have an apple-yellow color. Charon of Lampsacus <note>An early Greek historian writing before Herodotus.</note> says that white pigeons appeared at Mount Athos when the Persian triremes were destroyed there during the time of Cambyses <note>Specifically referring to the shipwreck of the Persian fleet at the cliffs of Mt. Athos.</note>.

At Mount Eryx in Sicily, there is a venerable and holy temple of Aphrodite. At a certain time, the people of Eryx celebrate a festival called the *Anagogia* <note>original: ἀναλώγια (analōgia), likely a variation of *Anagogia*, the "Departure" festival.</note>, and they say that Aphrodite is traveling across the sea from Sicily to Libya. At that time, the pigeons vanish from the area, as if they were traveling abroad with the goddess. For the rest of the year, however, it is agreed that a vast multitude of these birds frequents the goddess's temple. Achaean stories tell that Zeus himself changed his form into a pigeon when he fell in love with a woman named Phthia, who lived in the city of Aegium.

### About Socrates when he drank the hemlock ~

When the ship from Delos had arrived <note>The arrival of this sacred ship in Athens signaled that the religious period was over and the execution of Socrates could proceed.</note> and it was necessary for Socrates to die, Apollodorus—a companion of Socrates—came to the prison. He brought with him a costly woolen tunic, an under-tunic, and a cloak of the same kind. He begged Socrates to put on the tunic and throw the cloak... <note>The text ends mid-sentence as the page concludes.</note>

[...garment] then to drink the poison. Apollodorus said to him that it would be a fine thing if he did not lack funeral robes, and if he should die in them. For if he were laid out <note>original: ὑποκάθαι (hypokathai); likely referring to the preparation of the body for burial.</note> in a divine arrangement, the corpse would not be at all unseemly. Apollodorus said these things to Socrates, but he would not tolerate it. Instead, he said to him, and to Crito, and to Simmias, and to Phaedo: "How can Apollodorus think so poorly of us? He seems to believe that after the 'Athenian cup of friendship' <note>original: ἀθηναίων φιλοτησίαν (athēnaiōn philotēsian). This is a grimly ironic euphemism for the hemlock, as a "philotesia" was a cup drunk to seal a bond of friendship.</note> and the drinking of the poison, he will still see Socrates in this way. If he thinks that the body which will shortly be cast at his feet is me, and that I am the one to be buried, it is clear he does not know me."

### Concerning the tiniest four-horse chariots, and an elegy.

These, then, are the much-admired works of Myrmecides of Miletus and Callicrates of Lacedaemon <note>Sparta</note>: their tiny masterpieces. They created four-horse chariots so small that they could be covered by the wing of a fly. And upon a single sesame seed, they inscribed a two-line elegiac poem in letters of gold. In my opinion, a serious person would praise neither of these. For what else are they but a waste of time?

### Concerning luxurious women.

Most women used to "melt away" in their luxury. On their heads they would place high crowns, and on their feet they wore sandals. Long earrings hung from their ears. As for their tunics, they did not sew them together from the shoulders down to the hands, but instead fastened and held them together with brooches <note>original: πόρναις (pornais); likely a transcription error for πόρπαις (porpais), meaning pins or brooches.</note> of gold and silver. And these were the women of very ancient times. As for the luxury of the Athenian women, let Aeschrophanes <note>A comic poet known for satirizing social habits.</note> speak of that.

### Concerning the luxury of the Sybarites, Colophonians, and Corinthians.

<margin>4</margin>
Popular tradition, which has spread to everyone, says that for the Sybarites <note>Residents of Sybaris, a Greek colony in Italy famous for its wealth and decadence.</note> and for the city of Sybaris itself, the cause of their destruction was their great luxury. But I will speak of things that are not known to the many. They say that the Colophonians <note>From a city in Ionia (modern Turkey).</note> were also ruined because of their extreme luxury. For indeed, they grew soft in their extravagance, and through the profligacy of their tables and their excessive consumption, they became insolent. Likewise, the rule of the Bacchiadae <note>A ruling family in Corinth.</note> in Corinth, though it had advanced to great power, was nonetheless dissolved because of their luxury that went beyond all measure.

### Concerning Dionysius plundering the things of the gods.

Dionysius <note>Dionysius I, the tyrant of Syracuse.</note> plundered the wealth from all the temples in Syracuse...

...[temple offerings], and from the statue of Zeus he took away the robe and the ornaments, which they say were of gold, weighing eighty-five talents <note>A talent was a massive unit of weight; eighty-five talents of gold would be an astronomical fortune.</note>. ### How Ismenias bowed before the King of the Persians without shame.

I would not wish to hide the clever and Greek spirit of Ismenias the Theban. While serving as an ambassador for his homeland to the King of the Persians, he arrived and wished to meet with the King personally regarding the purpose of his mission. The Chiliarch <note>term: Chiliarch (χιλίαρχος); literally a "commander of a thousand," but in the Persian court, this official served as the King’s gatekeeper or Grand Vizier.</note>, who brought messages to the King and introduced those seeking an audience, spoke to him. This man, named Tithraustes, said playfully through an interpreter: "O Theban guest, there is a local law for Persians that anyone coming into the King’s sight may not be granted an audience until he has first prostrated himself <note>term: Proskynesis (προσκύνησις); a traditional Persian gesture of deep bowing or prostration which Greeks viewed as a form of worship fit only for gods, and thus humiliating for free men.</note> before him. If you wish to meet with him yourself, you must do what the law requires; otherwise, your business will be conducted through us without you bowing." Ismenias replied, "Lead me in." When he came into the King’s presence and was visible to him, he secretly removed his ring—which he happened to be wearing—and dropped it at his feet. He then quickly bent down as if he were prostrating himself, and picked it up again. Thus, he gave the impression of bowing without actually performing the act. He did nothing to bring shame upon the Greeks, yet he accomplished everything he desired and suffered no ill-will from the Persian.

### Gifts given by the Persian King to ambassadors.

The gifts given by the King to those who come to him as ambassadors, whether they arrived from the Greeks or elsewhere, were as follows: To each, a Babylonian talent of minted silver <note>The Babylonian talent was a specific standard of weight.</note>; also two gold bowls. A Babylonian talent is equal to seventy-two Attic minae <note>Attic minae were a standard Greek unit of weight and currency; the Babylonian version was significantly heavier.</note>. Additionally, they received bracelets, a dagger <note>term: Akinakes (ἀκινάκην); a short, straight sword or dagger characteristic of the Persians.</note>, and a twisted necklace—these together being worth a thousand darics <note>A daric was a high-purity gold coin of the Persian Empire.</note>. Finally, they were given a Median robe, which is called the "gift-bearing" robe <note>original: δωροφορική (dōrophorikē).</note>.

### On Gorgias and Protagoras

Among the Greeks of old, Gorgias of Leontini, Philolaus, and Protagoras the son of Democritus <note>original: "δημοκρίτου" (dēmokritou); while Protagoras is often called a student of Democritus, the author here refers to him as his son.</note> were preeminent in long-lasting fame. Yet in true wisdom, they were as far behind as children are compared to men. For it seems that fame is not at all accurate; it neither sees nor hears clearly. It is often deceived by its senses, and it grants favors to some while telling lies about others.

### On the rivalry between Heracles and Lepreus

Lepreus was the son of Glaucon (son of Poseidon) and Astydameia (daughter of Phorbas). This Lepreus once advised Augeas <note>King Augeas of Elis, who famously refused to pay Heracles for cleaning his stables.</note> to bind Heracles when Heracles demanded the wage promised for his labors. Consequently, Lepreus became an enemy to Heracles because of this advice. Some time later, the son of Zeus [Heracles] arrived among the Cauconians. At the request of Astydameia, Heracles set aside his enmity toward Lepreus. However, a youthful rivalry <note>original: "φιλομαχία" (philomachia); a love of competition or fighting.</note> fell upon them. They competed against each other in the discus throw, in drawing water, and in who could eat a whole bull first. In all these things, Lepreus was defeated. Heracles also challenged him to a drinking contest, and there again Heracles was victorious. Frustrated by his failures, Lepreus took up arms and challenged Heracles to a duel. It was there that he paid the penalty for his actions regarding Augeas, for he was killed while fighting.

### On the magnanimity of Alexander toward Phocion, and Phocion’s toward him

Alexander, the son of Philip—though if anyone thinks him the son of Zeus, it makes no difference to me <note>A reference to the propaganda that Alexander was the divine son of Ammon-Zeus.</note>—is said to have written the greeting "rejoice" <note>original: "τὸ χαίρειν" (to chairein); the standard Greek salutation, which Alexander supposedly reserved only for Phocion as a sign of extreme respect.</note> only to Phocion, the Athenian general. Thus did Phocion captivate the Macedonian. Alexander even sent him one hundred talents of silver and named four cities, telling him to choose one so that he might enjoy the tax revenues from it. These cities were Kios, Elaia, Mylasa, and Patara. Now, Alexander acted with great spirit and magnificence in this. Yet Phocion acted with even greater spirit by accepting neither the money nor the city. However, so that he would not seem to completely despise Alexander’s gift, he honored him in this way: he requested the release of the prisoners held in the citadel at Sardis, specifically Echekratides the...

# On Aglaïs the Glutton

I hear of a woman named Aglaïs, the daughter of Megacles, who was a trumpeter—this was both her profession and her craft. She wore a wig and a crest upon her head. Posidippus <note>Posidippus of Pella, a 3rd-century BCE epigrammatist.</note> confirms these details. Her dinner, it seems, consisted of twelve minas <note>original: "μναῖ" (mnai); a mina was roughly 1 pound or 430 grams.</note> of meat and four choinikes <note>original: "χοίνικες" (choinikes); a choinix was a dry measure approximately equal to 1 quart or 1 liter.</note> of bread, and she drank a chous <note>original: "χόα" (choa); a liquid measure approximately equal to 3 quarts or 3 liters.</note> of wine.

# On the Gluttony of Many People

They say that several men were famous for their gluttony: Pityrras the Phrygian, Cambes the Lydian, Thyus the Paphlagonian, Charidas, Cleonymus, Gisanthus, Charippus, Mithridates of Pontus, Calamodromus of Cyzicus, and Timocreon of Rhodes—who was an athlete and a poet at the same time—as well as Cantibaris the Persian, and Erysichthon the Myrmidon. This last one was also called "Kantharos" <note>original: "κάνθαλος" (kanthalos); likely a nickname referring to a deep drinking cup or a dung beetle, emphasizing an insatiable nature.</note>. It is said that in Sicily there is a temple dedicated to Gluttony, and a statue of Demeter "Sito" <note>original: "σιτοῦς" (sitous); meaning "of the grain" or "of food."</note>. The poet Alcman also admits that he himself was a massive eater. Furthermore, Anaxilas the comic poet says that a certain Ctesias ate a great deal.

# On the Delicacies Mocked by the Rhodians

Indeed, I can relate a Rhodian opinion to you. The Rhodians say that if a man looks at fish with wonder and admiration, being the greatest of gourmands among all others, he is praised by the common people as a gentleman <note>original: "ἐλευθέριον" (eleutherion); meaning someone with a noble or generous spirit.</note>. However, the Rhodians disparage the man who is instead obsessed with meat as vulgar <note>original: "φορτικόν" (phortikon); literally "burdensome" or "low-class."</note> and a slave to his belly. Whether they judge rightly or poorly, let the thoughtful man examine this.

# How a Sheep Bore a Lion

The people of Cos relate that a sheep in the private flock of Nicippus the tyrant gave birth. It did not bear a lamb, but a lion. This was an omen for Nicippus, prophesying his future tyranny while he was still a private citizen.

# That Galestes Was Greatly Loved by Ptolemy No Less for His Intelligence Than for His Beauty

King Ptolemy <note>Likely Ptolemy Philopator or Ptolemy Epiphanes, though the author does not specify.</note> had a favorite named Galestes, who was most beautiful to look upon. Yet the young man’s intellect was even better than his form. Frequently...

...often, at least, Ptolemy met with him and said: "O noble soul <note>original: "ὦ ἀγαθὴ κεφαλὴ" (o agathe kephale); literally 'O good head,' a Greek idiom of endearment similar to 'my dear friend.'</note>, you have never yet been the cause of evil to anyone, but you have done many great favors for many people." On one occasion, the youth was riding with the king, and seeing from a distance some men being led away to their death, he did not remain indifferent. Instead, he said to Ptolemy: "O King, since by some good fortune <note>original: "κατά τινα δαίμονα... ἀγαθὸν" (kata tina daimona agathon); referring to a guiding spirit or divine luck.</note> of those being led away, we happen to be on horseback, come, if it seems right to you, let us spur our horses and, pursuing with greater speed, let us rescue those wretched men and become their saviors—for it is well said that the nature of the gods is to be doers of good to all." The king was exceedingly pleased by the youth's kindness, and loving his compassionate nature even more, he both saved the men and increased his own deep affection for the boy.

### On the law regarding the bringing of gifts to the king

This is a Persian law, most strictly observed among them: whenever the King travels through the territory of Persia, all Persians bring him gifts according to their individual means. Since they are farmers who work the soil and labor with their own hands, they do not offer anything insulting or overly extravagant. Rather, some bring oxen or sheep, while others bring grain or wine. As the King passes by, these items are set out by each man and are formally called "gifts," and they are accepted as such. Those of more humble means offer him milk, dates, or cheese, as well as seasonal fruits <note>original: "τρωκτὰ ὡραῖα" (trokta horaia); literally 'ripe things to be nibbled,' such as nuts or fresh fruit.</note> and other first-fruits of the local countryside.

### On the man who gave water as a gift to the Persian king

This is also a Persian story. They say that a Persian man named Sinaites happened upon Artaxerxes—the one nicknamed "Mnemon" <note>Artaxerxes II (r. 404–358 BCE), called "The Mindful" or "The Remembering" because of his exceptional memory.</note>—far from his own farmstead. Having been left behind by the others, he was thrown into a panic by fear of the law and respect for the king. Not knowing how to handle the situation and unable to bear the shame of being the only Persian not offering a gift—which would make him appear dishonorable—he ran in haste to the nearby river, which is called the Cyrus. He bent down as quickly as his feet could carry him, scooped up some water in both his hands, and said: "King Artaxerxes, because of the haste of your journey, I honor you now with what I have and as I am able, so that you do not go unhonored by me. I pay you honor with..."

...[with] river water. "But when I saw you coming to the waystation <note>original: "σταθμὸν" (stathmon); a regular stopping point or courier station on the Persian Royal Road</note> where you pass by, I felt I should come from home with the very best and most costly things I could manage; thus I would not be less than any of the others who have already welcomed you with gifts." At this, Artaxerxes was pleased and said, "My good man, I accept the gift gladly. Indeed, I value it as much as the most expensive ones—twice as much, I say. First, because water is the best of all things, and second, because it bears the name of Cyrus <note>The river was named the Cyrus (Kuros), sharing the name of the founder of the Persian Empire</note>. See to it that you appear before me at the waystation when I stop to rest." After saying this, he ordered his eunuchs to take the gift from him. They ran up as quickly as possible and received the water from his hands into a golden bowl <note>original: "φιάλην" (phialen); a shallow ceremonial cup or saucer</note>. When the King arrived at the place where he was staying, he sent the man who provided the water a Persian robe, a golden bowl, and a thousand darics <note>A daric was a standard gold coin of the Persian Empire</note>. He commanded the one carrying these items to say to the recipient: "The King commands you to gladden your own soul with this gold, since you gladdened his, not leaving him without a gift or honor, but honoring him as your means allowed. He also wishes you to drink from that water using this bowl."

### On a large pomegranate given to the same King

Omises brought a massive pomegranate <note>original: "Ῥοῖαν" (Rhoian)</note> on a tray to King Artaxerxes while he was traveling through Persia. The King, struck with amazement at its size, asked, "From what garden <note>original: "παραδείσου" (paradeisou); refers to a walled Persian pleasure-garden or orchard</note> do you bring me this gift?" When Omises replied that it was from his own home and his own farming, the King was overjoyed. He sent him royal gifts and declared, "By Mithras! <note>The ancient Persian deity of light and oaths; Artaxerxes' standard oath</note> This man, through such diligence, would be able—in my judgment—to make a large city out of a small one." This story seems to agree that all things—even the products of nature—can become better through diligence, constant care, and unceasing zeal.

The youngest of these was called Kartomes, and for the most part, his actions were not in vain <note>a Greek idiom suggesting the boy was actively and successfully troublesome or wicked</note>. At first, the father tried to educate him and bring him into order through words. But when he would not obey, and when the local judges arrived at the place where the father of the youth lived, [the father went] there...

...having seized the boy and tied his hands behind his back, he led him before the judges and accurately accused him of all the daring crimes he had committed. The father replied: "Most certainly! For in the garden, when I prune and cut away the bitter shoots from the growing lettuce <note>original: "θριδακινῶν" (thridakinon); the ancient Greeks believed pruning certain parts of the plant made the rest more vigorous</note>, the mother lettuce is not at all grieved. Rather, she flourishes all the more, becoming larger and sweeter. In the same way, O King, when I see this one being destroyed—he who was harming my household and the livelihood of his brothers—and see his wickedness against them brought to an end, I myself will increase. I will live in harmony with the rest of my kin, who will no longer have to suffer such things at my hands." Upon hearing this, Artaxerxes praised Rhacoces and appointed him as one of the royal judges, saying to those present:

# AELIAN’S VARIOUS HISTORY

## BOOK TWO

### How Socrates encouraged Alcibiades, so that he would no longer fear the common people more than was proper.

This story concerns Socrates and Alcibiades <note>Alcibiades was a brilliant but volatile Athenian statesman and a student of Socrates</note>. When the latter was in agony and terrified at the thought of coming before the <term>Assembly</term> <note>original: "δῆμον" (demon); the gathering of all male citizens in Athens</note> to speak, Socrates sought to encourage him and rouse his spirits. "Do you not despise that shoemaker?" Socrates asked, naming a specific man. When Alcibiades said he did, Socrates spoke again: "And what of that herald who makes proclamations in the circles of the market? Or that <term>stitcher of old clothes</term> <note>original: "σιλλορράφου" (sillorraphou); likely a craftsman who mends or sews together cheap materials</note>?" When the young son of Cleinias <note>referring to Alcibiades</note> admitted he looked down on them, Socrates replied: "Well then, the Athenian people is simply a gathering of such men. Since you despise them individually, you ought to despise them when they are gathered together." In this way, the son of Sophroniscus and Phaenarete <note>Socrates' parents, a stonemason and a midwife</note> high-mindedly instructed the son of Cleinias and Dinomache.

### Concerning those who praise paintings poorly

Once, when Megabyzus <note>A Persian nobleman and priest of Artemis at Ephesus</note> was praising some cheap and artless paintings while remaining silent before others that were masterfully executed, the young boys of Zeuxis <note>A famous Greek painter known for his realism</note> who were <term>grinding red ochre</term> <note>original: "μηλίδα" (melida); a type of pigment or earth used to make paint</note> began to laugh at him. Zeuxis then said: "O Megabyzus, when you are silent, these children admire you, for they see your fine clothing and the number of attendants around you. But the moment you attempt to say something about art, they will despise you. Therefore, keep yourself to those things for which you are praised; hold your tongue, and do not try to play the expert in things that do not concern you."

### Concerning Alexander, who did not praise a portrait as it deserved

When Alexander <note>Alexander the Great</note> viewed his own portrait in Ephesus, which had been painted by Apelles <note>The most celebrated painter of the ancient world</note>, he did not praise it as much as the quality of the work deserved. However, when a horse was brought in and it neighed at the horse in the painting—treating it as if it were a real animal—Apelles said: "O King, your horse appears to be a much better judge of painting than you, and by **far**." <note>original: "πολὺ" (poly); the catchword for the next page.</note>

<summary>This page opens Book 2 of Aelian's collection, featuring anecdotes where famous figures like Socrates and Apelles use wit to correct the pride or anxieties of their social superiors.</summary>

### Concerning the Love of Chariton and Melanippus, and the Ty-
->rant's compassion toward them ~<-

<margin>Ph.</margin> I wish to tell you of a deed of Phalaris <note>Phalaris was the notorious tyrant of Akragas, Sicily (c. 570–554 BC), famous for his "Brazen Bull" execution device.</note> that is not at all usual for him to perform. This deed reveals an invincible <term>humanity</term> <note>original: "φιλανθρωπίαν" (philanthropian); in this context, it refers to a noble or kindly disposition toward fellow humans.</note>, and for that reason, it seems quite foreign to his character. Chariton was a man of Akragas <note>Ancient Greek city in Sicily, now Agrigento.</note> who was a lover of beauty, and he was extraordinarily devoted to the bloom of youth. He was most passionately in love with Melanippus, also of Akragas, a man of noble soul and striking beauty. Now, Phalaris had caused Melanippus some distress. When Melanippus was involved in a lawsuit against one of Phalaris’s own companions, the tyrant commanded him to drop the charges. When Melanippus would not be persuaded, Phalaris threatened to inflict the ultimate punishments if he did not obey. By the power of the tyrant's necessity, the case was won against justice; the magistrates, at Phalaris's bidding, brought the legal documents to the fire and destroyed them. The young man took this heavily, saying he had been treated with insolence <term>hubris</term> <note>original: "ὑβρίσθαι" (hybristhai); to be treated with humiliating or arrogant violence.</note>. Melanippus confessed his anger to his lover, and he demanded that Chariton become his partner in an attack against the tyrant. He even hurried to recruit other young men whom he knew were particularly distressed by such actions. But Chariton, seeing him so inspired and inflamed by rage, and knowing that none of the citizens would help them due to their fear of the tyrant, told Melanippus that he himself had long desired this. He claimed to be eager above all else to rescue the fatherland from the slavery that had seized it, but he argued that it was not safe to reveal such matters to many people. He begged Melanippus to allow him to consider the matter more carefully and to watch for the right moment; and for a time, as long as it was suitable for the deed, the youth conceded. Chariton then took the entire weight of the daring plot upon himself. He was unwilling to take Melanippus as a partner in the actual deed, so that if he were caught, he alone would pay the penalty and not drag his lover into ruin. When it seemed to be the right time, he took a dagger and rushed toward the tyrant. However, he did not go unnoticed, but was caught by the very great diligence of the bodyguards <note>original: "δορυφόρων" (doryphoron); literally "spear-bearers," the personal guard of a tyrant.</note> who watch over such things. Having been thrown into prison by Phalaris and being tortured on the rack to make him name his fellow conspirators, he endured the pain and...

### Concerning the management of time, and why the Lacedaemonians were not permitted to stroll.

The Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans.</note> were remarkably frugal with their time, managing
every use of it for urgent matters; they did not permit any of the citizens 
to be idle or to be lazy with their time, so that it would not be wasted on 
things outside of <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "ἀρετῆς" (aretes); excellence or moral virtue.</note> and then 
perish in vain. Among other proofs of this, there is also the following:
The <term>Ephors</term> <note>original: "ἔφοροι"; the five senior magistrates of Sparta who oversaw the conduct of citizens.</note> of the Lacedaemonians, having heard that some men after dining 
were taking an evening stroll, sent a message to them: "Do not 
stroll." They viewed this as a sign of men living in luxury <note>original: "τρυφώντων" (tryphonton); living softly or decadently.</note> rather 
than training their bodies. For it was necessary for the Lacedaemonians to 
maintain their health not through strolling, but through 
vigorous exercise.

### <margin>†</margin> An example showing that one should not seek to please the masses.

Hippomachus, the gymnastics trainer, says that when an athlete 
trained by him was wrestling, the whole crowd standing 
around broke into a great shout of applause. Hippomachus struck the athlete 
with his rod and said, "But you have done poorly and not as you 
ought to have done, though you should have known better yourself. 
For these people would not have praised you if they had seen you doing anything 
technical; rather, they cheered because you did nothing correctly." We must 
strive to please not the masses, but those who possess a 
discerning mind regarding what is being done. It seems that Socrates 
also looked down upon the judgment of the many in 
his conversation with Crito, when Crito arrived...

-> ### Concerning the Theban law against the exposure of infants <-

This Theban law is established with both righteousness and a great deal of humanity. It states that it is not permitted for a Theban man to <term>expose</term> <note>original: "ἐκτιθέναι" (ektithenai); the ancient practice of leaving unwanted infants in the wild to die.</note> a child, nor to cast it out into a wilderness, effectively passing a death sentence upon it. Instead, if the father of the child is extremely poor, whether the child is male or female, he must take it to the <term>magistrates</term> <note>original: "ἀρχὰς" (archas); high-ranking city officials.</note> immediately after the mother’s labor, still wrapped in its swaddling clothes. The magistrates, upon receiving the infant, sell it to the person who offers the lowest price. A verbal agreement and a formal contract are made with this buyer, stating that they must truly raise the infant; once the child has grown, the buyer may keep them as a slave, taking their labor as <term>rearing-fees</term> <note>original: "θρεπτήρια" (threptēria); compensation for the cost of food and upbringing.</note>.

-> ### Concerning Xenocles and Euripides competing at Olympia <-

During the ninety-first <note>The Greek text here lists the "first and sixth" Olympiad, but historical records of this event (415 BCE) and the context of the plays suggest it was the 91st Olympiad.</note> Olympiad, in which Exaenetus of Akragas won the <term>stadium-race</term> <note>A sprint of about 200 meters.</note>, Xenocles and Euripides competed against one another. Xenocles came in first—whoever this man actually was—with the plays *Oedipus*, *Lycaon*, *Bacchae*, and the <term>satyr play</term> <note>A lighthearted, ribald play performed after a trilogy of tragedies.</note> *Athamas*. Euripides was second with *Alexander*, *Palamedes*, *The Trojan Women*, and the satyr play *Sisyphus*. Is it not ridiculous that Xenocles did not lose while Euripides was defeated, especially when competing with such plays? Therefore, one of two things must be true: either those who held the power of the vote were foolish and ignorant, far removed from correct judgment, or they were bribed. Both possibilities are disgraceful and entirely unworthy of the Athenians.

-> ### Concerning certain impious decrees of the Athenians <-

What kind of things did the Athenians decree, and that too under a democracy? For the people of Aegina, they decreed to cut off the right thumb of every man, so that they would be unable to carry a spear, yet still be able to pull an oar. They also wished to slaughter the Mitylenaeans; this was decreed at the proposal of Cleon, the son of Cleanetus. Furthermore, they decreed that Samian captives should be branded on the face, specifically with the mark of an owl <note>The owl was the symbol of Athens; this branding was an act of humiliating ownership.</note>. This too was an Attic decree. I wish these things had never happened, nor been ratified, nor spoken of by Athenians—O Athena, Protector of the City, and Zeus, Giver of Freedom, and all the gods of the Greeks!

I hear that Timotheus, the son of Conon, was one of the Athenian <term>generals</term> <note>original: "στρατηγῶν" (stratēgōn); elected military leaders of Athens.</note> during the height of his success, when he was capturing cities with the greatest ease. Yet he did not allow the Athenians to lead him wherever they wished, even when they were swayed by wonder or the praise of the crowds. Instead, it is said he came upon Plato, the son of Ariston, as they happened to meet while Plato was walking outside the city walls with some of his acquaintances. Timotheus saw that Plato was dignified in appearance and had a gentle expression on his face. He was not discussing the collection of <term>taxes</term> <note>original: "εἰσφορᾶς" (eisphoras); special war taxes levied on the wealthy.</note>, nor triremes, nor naval requirements, nor the manning of crews, nor the need for military aid, nor the <term>tribute</term> <note>original: "φόρου" (phorou); payments made by member states to the Athenian empire.</note> from the allies, nor the affairs of the islanders, nor any other such trifles. Instead, they were discussing the things Plato usually spoke of and the matters he took seriously. When Timotheus, the son of Conon, stood there and listened, he exclaimed: "O, what a life! O, true happiness!" From this it is clear that Timotheus did not consider himself truly happy, because his own life was spent not in these philosophical pursuits, but in the reputation and honors he received from the Athenians.

-> ### Concerning the words of Socrates regarding those being slaughtered by the Thirty <-

When Socrates, during the rule of the <term>Thirty</term> <note>The "Thirty Tyrants," a pro-Spartan oligarchy that ruled Athens with extreme violence for eight months in 404–403 BC.</note>, saw the most distinguished citizens being put to death and the wealthiest being plotted against under the tyranny, they say he met Antisthenes and said: "Do not regret that we have become nothing 'great' or 'dignified' in this life, such as those monarchs we see in tragedies—the likes of Atreus, Thyestes, Agamemnon, and Aegisthus. For these men, in the midst of being slaughtered and turned into tragic figures, always find themselves being served and eating wicked meals. But no tragic poet was ever so bold or shameless as to introduce a <term>chorus</term> <note>The group of performers in a Greek play who represented the common people, rather than the "great" kings.</note> being slaughtered in a drama."

-> ### Concerning Themistocles after he ceased his wild living <-

Regarding Themistocles, the son of Neocles, I do not know if this story should be praised. When Themistocles ceased his <term>wild living</term> <note>original: "ἀσωτίας" (asōtias); a lifestyle of excess, wastefulness, or profligacy.</note> after being disowned by his father, he began to practice self-control. He turned away from his mistresses and took up a different passion: the political life of Athens. He applied himself most fervently to holding office and showed himself to be very ambitious. It is said he told his acquaintances: "What would you give to me, who am not yet envied? For he who desires to be envied is surely also in the way of great hopes..."

### Concerning Socrates being satirized in comedy by Aristophanes:

Anytus and his associates were plotting against Socrates because [the people] were being persuaded by him—for reasons and causes that have been stated long ago. However, they were suspicious of the Athenians and fearful of how they might react to a formal accusation against the man. For the name of Socrates was great, both for other reasons and because he exposed the <term>Sophists</term> <note>original: "σοφιστάς" (sophistas); traveling teachers who were often criticized for prioritizing rhetorical skill over absolute truth.</note> as being nothing healthy, nor knowing or saying anything of substance. 

Therefore, from these circumstances, they wished to test the public’s reaction to a slander against him. They did not approve of bringing a direct <term>indictment</term> <note>original: "γραφήν" (graphēn); a public legal action brought against a citizen.</note> against him immediately for the reasons I mentioned before. They feared that his friends, becoming enraged, might prejudice the jurors against them, causing them to suffer some incurable harm, inasmuch as they would be <term>sycophants</term> <note>original: "συκοφαντοῦντες" (sykophantountes); in Athens, this referred to professional accusers who brought malicious or frivolous lawsuits for profit.</note> against a man who was not only innocent of any evil toward the city but, on the contrary, was an ornament to Athens. 

What then do they devise? They persuaded Aristophanes, the writer of comedy—a buffoonish and ridiculous man—taking advantage of his poverty among other things, to satirize Socrates with those very rumors that were so widely circulated: how he was a chatterbox, and how in his speech he made the <term>weaker argument</term> <note>original: "ἥττω λόγον" (hēttō logon); a reference to the Sophistic claim of being able to make the worse cause appear better through clever speech.</note> seem the stronger, and that he introduced strange deities, and did not recognize the traditional gods or sacrifices. He [Aristophanes] taught these same things through his stage characters and tried to convince the audience they were true. 

Aristophanes, taking up a subject that was not very refined, scattering laughter and wily wit from his verses, took the best of the Greeks as his subject. For his play was not against Cleon <note>A powerful and controversial Athenian politician whom Aristophanes frequently attacked.</note>, nor did he satirize the Spartans, or the Thebans, or Pericles himself, but a man who was a friend to the other gods, and especially to Apollo. Since it was a strange thing and a paradoxical drama on the stage—a comedy about Socrates—the play first stunned the Athenians with its unexpectedness. Then, because they were envious by nature and prone to feel <term>malice</term> <note>original: "βασκαίνειν" (baskainein); to look with spite or cast an evil eye upon the successful.</note> toward the best men—not only those in public life and high office, but even more so those famous for good discourse or the dignity of their lives—this play, *The Clouds*, seemed the most pleasant thing to hear. They cheered the poet as never before, shouted for him to win, and commanded the judges from above to award the prize to Aristophanes, but...

<sig>γ</sig>

...to not write clearly. And such were the circumstances regarding the play. As for Socrates, he rarely frequented the theaters, but if ever Euripides, the poet of tragedy, was competing with new tragedies, he would certainly attend. Even when Euripides was competing at the <term>Piraeus</term> <note>original: "Πειραιεῖ" (Peiraiei); the primary port of Athens, which had its own theater and festivals.</note>, Socrates would go down there as well. For he clearly took pleasure in the man, both because of his wisdom and the excellence of his verses.

But there were times when Alcibiades, the son of Cleinias, or Critias, the son of Callaeschrus, would mockingly try to force him to go to the theater to listen to the comic poets. Yet he would not please them; instead, he utterly looked down upon such things, being a man who was temperate, just, and good. Furthermore, he despised their shameful mockery and insults, and the fact that they said nothing <term>healthy</term> <note>original: "ὑγιὲς" (hygies); meaning sound, honest, or of moral substance.</note>. These things deeply annoyed them, and these very circumstances became the seeds of the comedy against him. 

However, it was not only for the reasons agreed upon by Anytus and Meletus; it is also likely that there was a payment of money for these purposes. For since those men wanted—or rather, were striving by every means—to <term>slander</term> <note>original: "συκοφαντῆσαι" (sykophantēsai); to act as a malicious informer or bring false charges.</note> Socrates, and since they were always both poor and cursed, what surprise was it if they took silver for an unsound purpose? Concerning these matters, he himself knows the truth. 

In any case, the drama <note>Aristophanes' play, "The Clouds".</note> was well-received by the audience. For even in the time of <term>Cratinus</term> <note>original: "κρατίνου" (kratinou); a famous comic poet of the time, often a rival to Aristophanes.</note>, more than ever before, the theater was then "ailing in its wits." Since it was the festival of the <term>Great Dionysia</term> <note>original: "Διονυσίων" (Dionysiōn); the major Athenian festival in honor of Dionysus where dramatic competitions were held.</note>, a vast multitude of Greeks had arrived from everywhere to the theater. 

Therefore, when the character of Socrates was being carried about on the stage <note>Refers to the scene in "The Clouds" where Socrates is suspended in a basket.</note> and his name was being called out frequently, I would not be surprised if he was also being seen in the masks of the actors. For it is clear that the stage-designers fashioned the mask to resemble him as closely as possible. But the foreigners among the audience, who did not know the man being satirized, began to murmur among themselves and were asking who this Socrates was. 

Socrates, perceiving this—for he was present in the theater, not secretly nor by chance, but knowing well that they were satirizing him—was sitting in a prominent part of the theater. In order to clear up the ignorance of the foreigners, he stood up and remained standing throughout the entire performance while the actors were competing. Such was the degree to which Socrates rose above the comedy and showed his contempt for the Athenians.

That Xerxes was a ridiculous man, seeing as he had conquered both sea and land...

# SECOND [BOOK]
<sig>γ ii</sig>

...nurtured by the art of Zeus, but for himself he forged new paths and rich land. He became a slave to a plane tree and marveled at the tree. In Lydia, at any rate, they say that having seen a plane tree of great size, he stayed there for that entire day though he had no need to, and he used the wilderness around the plane tree as his camping station. Furthermore, he even fastened costly ornaments to it, such as necklaces and bracelets, honoring its branches. He even left a caretaker for it, as if he were leaving a guard and sentry for a lover. But what beauty did the tree gather from these things? For the acquired ornament, which did not belong to it, hung there nonetheless, and it contributed nothing to its beauty. Since the true beauty of a plant is this: noble branches, thick foliage, a solid trunk, deep roots, the winds blowing through it, the spreading shade beneath it, the seasons that nourish it, and water—some nourishing it through irrigation channels, some flowing down from the heavens. But the cloaks of Xerxes, and the gold of the barbarian, and the other gifts, were of no noble use to the plane tree, nor to any other tree.

->Concerning the thrones of the Ephors being smeared with soot<-

<margin>K</margin> Certain men of Clazomenae <note>original: "Κλαζομενίων" (Klazomeniōn); Clazomenae was a Greek city in Ionia, known for its distinct culture often contrasted with Spartan austerity.</note>, having arrived in Sparta and behaving with insolence and arrogance, went to the thrones of the <term>Ephors</term> <note>original: "ἐφόρων" (ephorōn); the five annual magistrates in Sparta who held supreme power over the state's administration.</note>—where they are accustomed to sit while conducting public business and arranging every civic matter—and smeared these very thrones with soot. When the Ephors found out, they did not grow angry, but instead called the public herald and ordered him to make a public proclamation: "Let this be the marvel: Let it be permitted for the Clazomenians to behave disgracefully." <note>This anecdote highlights the Spartan "laconic" wit; rather than punishing the foreigners, they simply declared that such behavior was expected of non-Spartans, thereby insulting the Clazomenians' entire culture.</note>

->Concerning Phocion<-

<margin>Φ</margin> As for Phocion, the son of Phocus, I for my part never knew him to be wicked. At any rate, when he came before the assembly of the Athenians while it was in session, since he was reproaching them for being ungrateful, he spoke very educatedly and strikingly: "I would rather suffer something at your hands than be wicked myself, or do some evil to you."

->Concerning the wisdom of the Magi among the Persians, and Ochus<-

<margin>H</margin> The wisdom of the <term>Magi</term> <note>original: "Μάγων" (Magōn); the priestly and scholarly caste of ancient Persia, specializing in ritual, astronomy, and interpretation of omens.</note> among the Persians knows, among other things, whatever is lawful for them to know: both how to sacrifice and how to practice divination. They were the first to predict the cruelty of <term>Ochus</term> <note>original: "Ὦχου" (Ōchou); refers to Artaxerxes III Ochus, a king known for his ruthlessness in reclaiming Egyptian territories.</note> toward his subjects, and his murderous nature.

<page-num>91</page-num>

...they know this, having judged by certain secret symbols. For when his father Artaxerxes died, and Ochus <note>Artaxerxes III, known as Ochus, ruled the Persian Empire from 358–338 BCE.</note> succeeded to the kingdom of the Persians, the <term>Magi</term> <note>original: "μάγοι" (magoi); a priestly caste of ancient Persia specializing in astrology and divination.</note> commanded one of the eunuchs standing nearby to watch Ochus—once the table was set—to see which of the items laid out he would reach for first. The eunuch stood there observing this. Ochus, stretching out his hands, took a small knife from the items laid out with his right hand, and with the other hand he pulled toward himself the largest of the loaves of bread. Having placed the meat upon it and then cutting it, he ate greedily. When the Magi heard this, they prophesied two things: abundance from the seasons and a harvest that would last throughout the time of his rule, but also many killings. And they were not proven wrong.

---

### # On Sumptuous Dinners

Timotheus, the son of Conon and a general of the Athenians, once became weary of sumptuous dinners and those military banquets. Being invited by Plato to a symposium <note>A "symposium" was a Greek drinking party characterized by intellectual conversation.</note> in the Academy, and being treated with both simplicity and refinement, he said to his family upon returning: "Those who dine with Plato also spend the following day feeling well." Because of this, Timotheus disparaged luxurious dinners as vulgar, since they certainly do not bring joy on the following day. There is also a story that Cleander, his brother, told a similar tale, though not saying the same thing: that on the next day Timotheus met Plato and said, "You, Plato, dine better for the next day than for the present one."

---

### # On Alexander wishing to be called a god

When Alexander defeated Darius and conquered the empire of the Persians, he became very proud of himself. Being elevated to a divine status by the good fortune that surrounded him then, he sent word to the Greeks that they should vote him a god. This was ridiculous; for what he did not possess from nature, he sought to gain from those men. Therefore, while others voted various things, the Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans.</note> voted this: "Since Alexander wishes to be a god, let him be a god." Thus, in their laconic <note>original: "λακωνικῶς" (lakōnikōs); the Spartans were famous for their "laconic" or pithy and blunt style of speech.</note> way and according to their local custom, the Lacedaemonians rebuked the madness of Alexander.

---

### # On the gentleness of King Antigonus

They say that Antigonus <note>Likely Antigonus I Monophthalmus, one of the successor kings after Alexander.</note> the king was a man of the people and gentle. And...

# BOOK TWO
<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>SECOND BOOK 11</header>

For anyone who has the leisure to know these things and to examine each point for themselves—matters which will be discussed elsewhere—what I am about to say will be told in a very gentle and humble manner. This **Antigonus** <note>Likely Antigonus II Gonatas (c. 319–239 BCE), King of Macedonia, known for his philosophical leanings.</note>, seeing that his son was treating his subjects with excessive violence and boldness, said: "Do you not know, my son, that our kingship is a **glorious servitude**?" <note>original: "ἔνδοξον εἶναι δουλείαν" (endoxon einai douleian). This famous phrase suggests that a king is a servant to the law and the welfare of his people.</note> These words of Antigonus to his son are expressed with great mildness and humanity. To whoever does not see it this way, such a person does not seem to me to understand a kingly or political man, but rather one who would prefer to live with a tyrant.

->Concerning Pausanias, the beloved of the poet Agathon<-

<gloss>Truly</gloss> <note>The gloss "ἦ" (ē) confirms a point of emphasis: "Indeed."</note> Pausanias of the Kerameikos <note>A district in Athens famous for its pottery and cemetery.</note> was the lover of the poet **Agathon**. This fact is widely discussed. However, what has not reached everyone’s ears—but what I shall recount—is how they once arrived at the court of **Archelaus** <note>Archelaus I, King of Macedon (r. 413–399 BCE), was a famous patron who invited many Athenian artists to his court.</note> while they were still in their relationship as lover and beloved. Now, Archelaus was a man of passion, no less than he was a lover of the arts. When he saw Pausanias and Agathon frequently quarreling with one another, he assumed the lover was being slighted by his young favorite. Archelaus therefore asked Agathon why he was so frequently hostile and offensive to the man who loved him most of all. Agathon replied, "I will tell you, O King. I am neither ill-tempered toward him, nor is my life shaped by any lack of refinement. But if I understand anything about life or the art of poetry, I find it the sweetest thing for lovers and their beloveds to **reconcile after a disagreement**. Indeed, I am convinced that nothing brings them such delight. I frequently grant him this pleasure, hoping to draw closer to him even more often; for he rejoices when our strife is continually resolved. If I were always consistent and mild, there would be no such variety of feeling." Archelaus praised these words, so the story goes. They say that the poet Euelpides <note>The OCR reads "Euelpides," but historical sources suggest this refers to the famous playwright **Euripides**, who was also at Archelaus's court.</note> was also a lover of this same Agathon, and it is said that he took great care in writing the play *Chrysippus* <note>A lost play by Euripides dealing with the myth of Chrysippus and Laius.</note> to please him. Whether this is certain, I do not know, but I know it is among the most widely told stories.

->That the Mantineans have the best laws<-

I hear that the people of **Mantinea** <note>An ancient city in Arcadia, Greece.</note> achieved the state of being most excellently governed <term>eunomia</term> <note>original: "εὐνομία"; a state of good order and well-balanced laws.</note>, no less so than the Locrians, the Cretans, or the Spartans themselves, or even the Athenians. For the work of **Solon** <note>The famous lawmaker of Athens (c. 638–558 BCE).</note> was a solemn achievement, even if afterwards the Athenians...
