# BOOK TWO
<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>BOOK II</header>

...little by little, they corrupted some of the laws that those among them had written for them.

### That Nicodorus, who was a boxer, also became a lawgiver.

**Nicodorus** <note>A famous athlete from Mantinea in Arcadia, active in the 5th century BCE.</note> the boxer was among the most reputable of the Mantineans. However, late in life, after his athletic career, he also became a lawgiver for them. In this, he served his fatherland far better as a statesman than he ever did through the proclamations of his victories in the stadiums. They say that **Diagoras of Melos** <note>Known as "the Atheist," Diagoras was a poet and philosopher. Melos was his home island.</note> composed the laws with him, having become a lover of those laws. If there is anything further to say regarding Nicodorus—lest I seem to include praise within the account of Diagoras—let the story reach its end here. For Diagoras was "hated by the gods," <note>original: "θεοῖς ἐχθροῖς" (theois echthrois). This is a pun on Diagoras’s reputation for atheism; Aelian is expressing a common ancient prejudice against him.</note> and it is not pleasant for me to remember him at length.

### That Milo, though strong in body, was not courageous in soul.

There were some who dismissed the legendary strength of **Milo of Croton** <note>The most famous wrestler of antiquity, a six-time Olympic victor known for incredible feats of strength.</note>, saying such things about him: no opponent was able to pry a pomegranate <note>A classic test of Milo's strength was holding a pomegranate so tightly that no one could take it, yet so delicately that he did not bruise the fruit.</note> from his hand. Yet his mistress could very easily take it from him, often competing against him in sport. From this, one may perceive that Milo was strong in his body, but he was not brave in his soul.

### That the sixth of Thargelion is a favorable day for the Greeks.

They say that the sixth day of the month of **Thargelion** <note>A month in the Athenian calendar roughly corresponding to May or June.</note> has been the cause of many good things, not only for the Athenians but for many others. For instance, **Socrates** was born on this day. The **Persians** were also defeated on this day <note>This refers to the Battle of Marathon in 490 BCE, though modern calculations of the date vary.</note>, and the Athenians still sacrifice three hundred goats to the "Goddess of the Wild" <note>Artemis Agrotera. This sacrifice was a "votive offering" promised by the general Miltiades before the battle.</note> in fulfillment of the vow made by **Miltiades**. 

On the sixth of the same month, they say the **Battle of Plataea** occurred, where the Greeks were victorious. As for the earlier defeat I mentioned <note>The Battle of Artemisium, a naval engagement during the Persian invasion.</note>, it happened then as well. At **Mycale** too, the victory of the Greeks is agreed to have been a gift of no other day than this—if indeed the victories at both Plataea and Mycale happened on the same day. 

Furthermore, they say it was on the sixth of the month that **Alexander the Macedonian**, the son of Philip, destroyed the many thousands of the Barbarians <note>A common Greek term for non-Greeks, specifically the Persians in this context.</note>. This was when Alexander overthrew **Darius**. They agree that all these things happened in the same month, and even that Alexander himself was both born and departed from this life on that day.

# BOOK TWO
<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>SECOND [BOOK]</header>

...was believed to have happened on the same day.

## On Hyperborean Apollo, and on some of the wonders concerning Pythagoras.

**Aristotle** <note>The famous philosopher and polymath (384–322 BCE).</note> says that the people of **Croton** <note>A Greek city in southern Italy, famous for its athletes and for being the home of Pythagoras's school.</note> addressed **Pythagoras** as the "Hyperborean Apollo" <note>The Hyperboreans were a mythical people living in a land of eternal spring in the far north; to be called a "Hyperborean Apollo" suggests Pythagoras was seen as a semi-divine incarnation of the god.</note>. **Aristotle**, the son of Nicomachus, also adds the following: that once, on the same day and at the same hour, Pythagoras was seen by many people in both **Metapontum** and at the games in **Croton** as he was standing up. Pythagoras was also seen revealing that one of his thighs was made of gold. The same author also says that while he was crossing the river **Cosas**, the river addressed him. He says that many people heard this greeting.

### That Anniceris was a skilled horseman, and that one who strives too much over small things neglects the great.

**Anniceris of Cyrene** <note>Cyrene was a Greek city in North Africa famous for its horse-breeding.</note> took great pride in his horsemanship and his chariot driving. On one occasion, he even wished to display his skill to **Plato**. Having yoked his chariot, he drove many laps around the **Academy** <note>The gymnasium and garden in Athens where Plato taught.</note>, keeping so precisely to the track that he never strayed from the wheel-ruts, but always stayed exactly within them. While everyone else was, as might be expected, struck with amazement, Plato criticized his excessive diligence. He said it is impossible for someone who devotes such immense care to small and worthless things to ever strive for truly great matters; for since his entire mind is tied up in those trifles, he must inevitably neglect things that are truly and justly worthy of admiration.

## How the cock-fight competition began.

After the victory over the **Persians**, the **Athenians** established a law that cocks should fight publicly in the theater for one day every year. I will explain where this law originated. When **Themistocles** <note>The Athenian general and statesman who led the Greeks against the Persian King Xerxes.</note> was leading the citizen-force against the barbarians <note>original: "βαρβάρους" (barbarous); to the Greeks, this meant anyone who did not speak Greek, specifically the Persians in this context.</note>, he saw two cocks fighting. He did not look at them idly; he halted his phalanx and said to his men: "These birds do not suffer for their fatherland, nor for their ancestral gods, nor for the heroes of their fathers, nor for glory, nor for freedom, nor for their children. They fight only so that neither might be defeated, and that one should not yield to the other." By saying this, he encouraged the Athenians. Therefore, what happened...

# BOOK 14
<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>BOOK 14</header>

...to them it was a signal for virtue at that time, and he wished to preserve it as a reminder for similar deeds.

->How Pittacus represented Fortune.<-

**Pittacus** <note>Pittacus of Mitylene (c. 640–568 BCE) was one of the Seven Sages of Greece.</note> dedicated ladders in the temples of **Mitylene**. These served no practical use, but were intended solely as a votive offering. By this, he was alluding to the sudden shifts of **Fortune**, moving up and down; in a sense, those who are fortunate are ascending, while those who are unfortunate are descending.

->Concerning Plato.<-

**Plato**, who always held the first rank, began with a poetic impulse and wrote erotic verses. Later, he burned them, looking down on them after comparing them to the works of **Homer** and seeing how much they fell short. He also composed two tragedies and even worked on a tetralogy <note>A group of four plays—three tragedies and one satyr play—submitted for competition at Athenian festivals.</note>. He was just about to compete, having already given the scripts to the actors. But before the festival of **Dionysus** <note>The Great Dionysia, the primary Athenian festival where dramatic competitions were held.</note>, he happened upon **Socrates**. Once captured by that man's Sirenic charm <note>A reference to the Sirens of mythology, whose song was irresistible; Socrates was often described as having a similarly hypnotic intellectual pull.</note>, he not only withdrew from the competition then and there, but utterly cast aside the writing of tragedies and stripped himself for the pursuit of philosophy.

->That none of the barbarians are atheists.<-

And who would not praise the wisdom of the barbarians? For none of them have fallen into atheism, nor do they harbor doubts about the gods—whether they exist or not, or whether they care for us or not. No one among them ever conceived an idea like those of **Euhemerus of Messene**, or **Diogenes the Phrygian**, or **Hippon**, or **Diagoras**, or **Sosias**, or **Epicurus** <note>A list of Greek philosophers and thinkers who were frequently accused of atheism or skepticism regarding divine providence.</note>—neither the **Indian**, nor the **Celt**, nor the **Egyptian**. 

These aforementioned barbarians say that there are gods, and that they exercise providence over us. They claim that the gods signal the future through birds, through omens, and through entrails, and through various other teachings and disciplines. These things are a lesson to mankind, stemming from the gods' providence toward them. They also say that many things are revealed through dreams and through the stars. Because they hold a strong faith in these matters, they sacrifice with purity, and live in holy abstinence, and perform initiations, and observe the laws of the secret rites, and practice all other religious duties. From these actions, it is acknowledged that they powerfully revere and honor the gods.

<summary>This page features anecdotes on Pittacus's symbolic use of ladders to represent the fickleness of fortune and Plato’s transition from poetry to philosophy after meeting Socrates. It concludes by contrasting the inherent piety of non-Greek "barbarian" nations with the skepticism of several Greek philosophers.</summary>

<keywords>Pittacus, Plato, Socrates, Fortune, Atheism, Barbarian Wisdom, Divine Providence, Greek Philosophers, Religious Rites</keywords>

# BOOK TWO
<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>SECOND BOOK</header>

### Concerning the change of Heracles’ name, and Apollo’s oracle for him :~

Certain Pythian <note>Pythian refers to the traditions of the Oracle at Delphi, dedicated to the god Apollo.</note> accounts say that **Heracles**, the son of **Zeus** and **Alcmene**, was called "Heracleides" <note>original: "Ἡρακλείδην" — a name meaning 'descendant of Heracles,' though other traditions say his birth name was Alcaeus.</note> by birth. Later, he came to **Delphi** for a particular reason to seek an oracle, and he did not fail to receive that for which he came. The god [Apollo] addressed these specific words to him:

> "**Phoebus** <note>A common title for Apollo, meaning 'the Radiant One.'</note> names you **Heracles** as your namesake,
> For by bringing glory to **Hera** <note>original: "Ἥρα" — The name Heracles (Ἡρακλῆς) literally means "Glory of Hera." It is a divine irony, as the goddess Hera was famously his enemy and the source of his many trials.</note> among men, you shall possess undying fame." :~

### Concerning the statues of rivers :~

We can see the physical nature of rivers and the flow of their streams. However, those who honor them and craft their statues depict them in various ways: some prefer to represent them in human form, while others give them the shape of bulls. Those of **Stymphalus** <note>A city in Arcadia.</note> liken the river **Erasinus** to a bull, depicted on its forehead. The **Lacedaemonians** [Spartans] do the same for the **Eurotas**, the **Sicyonians** and **Phliasians** for the **Asopus**, and the **Argives** for the **Cephisus**. 

Others, however, depict them as men: the people of **Psophis** represent the **Erymanthus** this way. The **Alpheius** is shown as a man by the people of **Heraea**, as is the same river by the people of the **Cnidian Chersonese**. The **Athenians** represent the **Cephisus** as a man in high honor, but with small horns subtly showing. In **Sicily**, the **Syracusans** liken the **Anapus** to a man of dignity, while they honor the spring **Cyane** with the image of a woman. The people of **Segesta** honor the **Porpax**, the **Crimisus**, and the **Telmassus** in the form of men. The people of **Akragas** <note>Modern Agrigento, Sicily.</note> sacrifice to their namesake river [the Akragas], likening it to a beautiful boy; they even dedicated a finely carved ivory statue of it at Delphi, which bears the name of the river and is the figure of a boy. :~

### Concerning old age :~

They say that **Epicharmus** <note>A Greek comic playwright and philosopher (c. 524–435 BCE) who lived in Sicily.</note>, when he was very old, was sitting in a lounge with some men of his own age. Each of those present spoke: one said, "Five more years of life are enough for me." Another said, "For me, three," and a third said, "For me, four." 

Interrupting them, Epicharmus said, "My good men, why do you quarrel and disagree over a few days? By some divine fate, we are all at the sunset of our lives. Therefore, it is time for us all to set sail as quickly as possible, before the 'dog' <note>A likely reference to the "Dog Days" of summer (the heat of Sirius) or a metaphor for a biting, harsh end.</note> and the unwept misery of old age arrive." :~

> For **Sleep** is the brother of **Death**, and very much a 'Gorgian' end. <note>original: "πολὺ γοργίου τελευτῆς" — This refers to the famous rhetorician Gorgias of Leontini, who on his deathbed reportedly said he was finally handing over "Sleep to his brother Death."</note> :~

# BOOK
<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>BOOK TWO</header>

**Gorgias of Leontini** <note>A famous Greek sophist and rhetorician from Sicily, renowned for living to the age of 108.</note>, having already reached the end of his life and being very far advanced in age, was overtaken by a certain weakness and lay slipping gradually into sleep. When one of his close friends came to visit him and asked how he was doing, **Gorgias** said, "Sleep is already beginning to hand me over to his brother." <note>original: "τῷ ἀδελφῷ" — In Greek mythology, Sleep (Hypnos) and Death (Thanatos) were twin brothers.</note>

### Concerning Socrates growing old and falling ill

**Socrates** himself, when he was in very deep old age, fell into a sickness. When someone asked him how he was, he replied that it was well for both outcomes: "For if I live, I shall have more who emulate me; but if I die, I shall have more who praise me."

### Concerning the law forbidding those who are ill from drinking wine

Among the many other excellent and necessary laws of **Zaleucus the Locrian** <note>The legendary lawgiver of the Locrians in southern Italy, said to have created the first written Greek law code.</note>, this one is not the least. If any of the **Epizephyrian Locrians** <note>Greek colonists in the "toe" of Italy.</note>, while sick, drank unmixed wine without the order of his physician—even if he recovered—his penalty was death, because he drank what had not been prescribed for him.

### The law of the Romans and certain others forbidding wine, neither entirely, nor to every age

There is also this law of **Massalia** <note>Modern-day Marseille, France, then a Greek colony.</note>: that women should not be acquainted with wine, but that every age of women should drink water. **Theophrastus** <note>A Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle.</note> says that this law still prevails among the **Milesians** as well, and that the Ionian women—specifically those of **Miletus**—obey it. And why should I not also mention the **Roman** law? For I would surely be rightly accused of irrationality if I committed the customs of the **Locrians**, the **Massalians**, and the **Milesians** to memory, yet neglected those of my own fatherland. <note>The author, Claudius Aelianus, was a Roman citizen who lived in Italy but wrote in Greek.</note> Therefore, there was a very strong law among the **Romans** that neither a free woman nor a female servant drank wine; nor indeed did the well-born men from their youth until the age of thirty-five.

### The Cretan law concerning lessons

The **Cretans** commanded that children of free birth learn the laws with a certain melody, so that they might be charmed by the music and receive them more easily into their memory; and furthermore, so that if they did anything forbidden, they could not offer the excuse that they had acted out of ignorance. As a second lesson, they ordained that the children learn the hymns of the gods; and thirdly, the praises of good men.

**Vocabulary:** <note>original: "Γοργίας, Σωκράτης, Ζάλευκος, Μασσαλία, Ρωμαίων νόμος, Κρῆτες, οἶνος, νόμοι"</note> **Gorgias**, **Socrates**, **Zaleucus**, **Massalia**, **Roman law**, **Cretans**, **wine**, **laws**.

# BOOK TWO
<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>SECOND BOOK</header>

### How animals are affected by wine in various ways, and those that get completely drunk

Every irrational creature is naturally affected by wine in its own way, especially those animals that become intoxicated after gorging themselves on grapes or grape-seeds. Even ravens become drunk when they eat the herb known as *sinousa* <note>original: "σινοῦσαν" — An unidentified plant, possibly a type of hemlock or a plant with narcotic properties.</note>. Dogs, too, can be driven into a Bacchic frenzy <note>The author uses the verb "βακχεύονται," referring to the wild, ecstatic behavior of the followers of Dionysus, the god of wine.</note>. As for the monkey and the elephant, if they drink wine, the elephant forgets his strength and the monkey forgets his cunning; in this state, they become very easy to capture because of their weakness.

### On certain lovers of drink and heavy drinkers

The following are said to have been legendary lovers of drink: **Dionysius**, the tyrant of Sicily; **Nisaeus**, also a tyrant; **Apollocrates**, the son of the tyrant **Dionysius**; and **Hipparinus**, who was a nephew of the same **Dionysius**. Others include **Timolaus** the Theban, **Charidemus** of Oreus, **Arcadon**, **Erasineus**, **Alcetas** the Macedonian, and **Diotimus** the Athenian. This **Diotimus**, in fact, was nicknamed "The Funnel" <note>original: "χώνη"</note> because he would place a funnel in his mouth and allow the wine to be poured in incessantly. 

It is said that **Cleomenes** the Lacedaemonian <note>A King of Sparta known for his erratic behavior and eventual madness.</note> was not only a heavy drinker but also fell into the "Scythian vice" <note>original: "σκυθικὸν κακόν" — The Greeks usually diluted their wine with water; drinking it "Scythian style" meant drinking it undiluted, which was considered barbaric and dangerous.</note>, becoming a habitual drinker of unmixed wine. **Ion of Chios**, the poet, is also said to have been unrestrained when it came to wine. 

When **Alexander the Macedonian** held the funeral for **Calanus** <note>A gymnosophist or "naked philosopher" from India who accompanied Alexander and later chose to die by self-immolation on a pyre.</note>, the Indian philosopher, he organized musical, equestrian, and athletic competitions. To please the Indians and to honor their local customs, he added a drinking contest to the aforementioned prizes in honor of **Calanus**. The first-place prize was a talent <note>A massive unit of currency, roughly 6,000 drachmae.</note>, the second was thirty minae, and the third was ten minae. The man who won the victory was **Promachus**.

Furthermore, at the festival of the *Choes* <note>The "Feast of Jugs," part of the Anthesteria festival dedicated to Dionysus in Athens.</note>, a golden crown was offered as a prize to the one who could drink the most. **Xenocrates** of Chalcedon <note>A student of Plato and head of the Academy, known for his sternness and gravity.</note> won this prize. After the banquet, when he returned home, he took the crown and placed it upon the statue of **Hermes** standing before his door, just as he had done on previous days with his crowns of flowers, myrtle, garden greens, and laurel. He left it there and went to rest. It is also said that **Anacharsis** <note>A famous Scythian philosopher who traveled to Athens.</note> drank a vast amount while visiting the house of **Periander**.

# BOOK TWO
<page-num>51</page-num>
<header>BOOK TWO</header>

It was possible for him to bring his own provisions from home; however, it is a particular trait of the Scythians to drink wine unmixed <note>original: "ἄκρατον" (akraton) — Ancient Greeks traditionally diluted wine with water; drinking it neat was considered a mark of "barbarian" excess or lack of self-control.</note>. The philosophers Lacydes and Timon are also said to have been very heavy drinkers. Then there is the Egyptian Mycerinus: when an oracle reached him from Buto <note>An ancient city in the Nile Delta famous for its temple and oracle of the goddess Wadjet.</note> foretelling that his life would be short, he wished to outsmart the prophecy by doubling his time. By adding the nights to his days, he spent his time awake, drinking all the while. Nor did the Egyptian Amasis lack this same passion, as Herodotus provides sufficient evidence to prove.

We should not fail to mention Niketeles of Corinth, nor Skopas the son of Kreon. They say that King Antiochus, too, was a "wine-bibber" <note>original: "οἰνόφλυξιν" (oinophlyxin) — A term for someone habitually drunk or overflowing with wine.</note>. Indeed, because of this, his kingdom was managed for him by Aristaios and Themison of Cyprus, while he himself was clearly marked by his heavy drinking during his reign. Antiochus, the one called "The Illustrious" <note>Antiochus IV Epiphanes, a Seleucid king known for his eccentric and often erratic public behavior.</note> who was given to the Romans as a hostage, also had an unquenchable thirst for unmixed wine. His namesake, Antiochus, who fought the Medes against Arsaces, was likewise a slave to drinking. Even the one called Antiochus "the Great" was known to toy with *zythos* <note>original: "ζύτοις" — A type of beer or barley wine common in Egypt and the Near East, usually viewed as an inferior or "foreign" drink by the Greeks.</note>. 

Furthermore, an "unjust impulse" toward wine killed Agron, the King of the Illyrians, as it brought on a fatal case of pleurisy. Another Illyrian king was also quite noble at drinking unmixed wine for himself. As for Orophernes, the King of the Cappadocians, we shall mention how he, too, became a thirsty drinker. 

If we must also mention women, it would be strange not to mention a woman who loved the city—and loved drinking even more. Let us speak of them: they say that a woman named Cleo, entering into drinking contests not only with other women but with the men who were her fellow guests, was the most formidable of all and defeated them every time. To my mind, it is a most shameful thing to wear a victory crown for such a thing.

---

### On the Reputation of Plato, and on Equality

The reputation of Plato and the account of his virtue reached even the Arcadians and the Thebans. They sent ambassadors to him, pleading with the utmost urgency that he come to them. This was not merely to oversee the education of the youth, nor just to converse with them on the topics of philosophy; rather, for something even greater than these: they called upon him to be their legislator <note>original: "νομοθέτην" (nomotheten) — A prestigious role in Greek society where a wise man was invited to draft a city's entire legal and constitutional framework.</note>. He was certainly pleased to be honored with such an invitation...

<header>SECOND BOOK</header>

...and they were going to obey. He asked those who had come to him, however, how they felt about everyone having an equal share. When he learned from them that they were quite opposed to this, and that your "Equality Before the Law" <note>original: "Ἰσονομίαν" (Isonomian) — A fundamental concept in Greek democracy meaning equal rights for all citizens.</note> would not persuade them, he declined the visit to their country.

### Some of the Best Greeks were the Poorest:

The best of the Greeks were also the poorest. These included Aristides the son of Nicomachus, Phocion the son of Phocus, Epaminondas the son of Polymnis <note>original: "Πολυμάπιδος" — This appears to be a transcription variant for Polymnis, the father of the famous Theban general Epaminondas.</note>, Pelopidas the Theban, Lamachus the Athenian, Socrates the son of Sophroniscus, and Ephialtes the son of Sophonides and those of their circle.

### Description of a Painting by Theon the Artist:

The craftsmanship of Theon the artist is acknowledged as excellent in many other works, but especially in this painting. It depicts a hoplite <note>A heavily armed infantry soldier, the backbone of ancient Greek armies.</note> rushing to the rescue as enemies have suddenly invaded, pillaging and ravaging the land at the same time. The young man seems vividly and quite spiritedly rushing into the fight. You would say he was possessed, as if driven mad by Ares <note>The Greek god of war.</note>. His eyes look fierce, and having snatched up his weapons, he seems to be running at full speed to charge the enemy. He holds his shield out in front, shakes his bare sword—looking like he is intent on slaughter—and his gaze is fixed on the kill. He threatens through his entire posture that he will spare no one.

Theon did not add any unnecessary details: no fellow soldier, no captain, no company of men, no horseman, nor an archer. Instead, this single hoplite was enough to fulfill the requirements of the image. Furthermore, the artist did not uncover the painting or show it to those who had gathered for the viewing until he had placed a trumpeter beside it. He ordered the trumpeter to blow the "charge" <note>original: "προορμητικὸν ἐμπνεύσαι μέλος" — A specific military signal used to incite troops to rush the enemy.</note>, a melody that was piercing and as loud as possible, the kind used to wake men for battle. As soon as the harsh and terrifying melody was heard—resembling a trumpet signaling a hoplite’s sudden sortie—the painting was revealed. The soldier was seen, and the music made the vision of him rushing to the rescue appear even more vivid and striking.

# BOOK THREE

## A Description of the Thessalian Tempe

Come now, let us also describe in words and recreate the so-called Thessalian **Tempe**. For it is agreed that speech, if it possesses descriptive power, is no less capable of showing whatever it wishes than those who are skilled in the manual arts of painting or sculpture.

It is a place situated between Mount **Olympus** and Mount **Ossa**. These are exceedingly high mountains, which seem to have been split apart as if by some divine providence, and they receive a space in the middle. The length of this space extends for forty *stadia* <note>original: "σταδίους" — a stadium was roughly 600 feet; 40 stadia is about 4.5 miles</note>. As for its width, in some places it is a *plethron* <note>original: "πλέθρου" — about 100 feet</note> and in others slightly more. 

Flowing through the middle of it is the river called the **Peneus**, into which other rivers flow, sharing their waters with it and making the Peneus great. This place has various and diverse haunts—not the work of human hands, but the spontaneous creations of nature, which took pride in its beauty when the place was first formed. 

For much ivy, very thick and lush, flourishes and blooms there; in the manner of noble vines, it creeps up the high trees and grows together with them. Plenty of *smilax* <term>smilax: a climbing, flowering vine often associated with Dionysian rituals</term> runs up the cliff itself and shades the rock. The rock itself is hidden, and all that is seen is the green foliage, a feast for the eyes. 

In the level and open areas, there are various groves and continuous sheltered spots—most pleasant retreats for travelers to take refuge in during the summer season, which offer a welcome cooling. Numerous springs flow through, and streams of cold and most sweet water pour forth. It is said that these waters are beneficial for those who bathe in them and contribute to their health. 

Birds of various kinds, scattered here and there, sing—especially the songbirds—providing a wonderful feast for the ears. They lull one to sleep effortlessly and with pleasure, banishing the fatigue of passersby through their melody. At each... <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "παρ᾽ ἑκά-" (at each...), to be continued on the next page.</note>

<summary>Aelian begins Book 3 with a vivid description of the Vale of Tempe, emphasizing its natural beauty, the Peneus river, and its role as a refreshing sanctuary for travelers.</summary>
<keywords>Thessaly, Vale of Tempe, Mount Olympus, Mount Ossa, Peneus River, nature description, ekphrasis, Aelian</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Vale of Tempe from Aelian’s "Various History," Book 3, before transitioning into a biographical anecdote about the philosopher Anaxagoras.</meta>

->BOOK THREE<-

Along the river are the retreats mentioned before, and the resting places. Through the middle of the Vale of Tempe, the **Peneus** river moves leisurely and gently, smooth as oil. The shade from the trees growing along its banks and their overhanging branches is so abundant that for most of the day, it acts as a roof to keep out the sun’s rays, providing those who sail upon it a journey in the cool air. All the neighboring people gather there, coming together to offer sacrifices, hold social gatherings, and drink together. Since many people are constantly sacrificing and making offerings, it is only natural that those walking or sailing there are accompanied by the sweetest scents. Thus, the lovely Tempe is truly a place inspired by a higher power. 

In this very place, the children of the **Thessalians** say that **Apollo** of **Pytho** purified himself at the command of **Zeus**, after he had shot and killed the **Python** <note>original: "Πύθωνα" — the monstrous serpent that guarded the oracle of Delphi</note>, who then still guarded the oracle of the earth at **Delphi**. Having crowned himself with this very laurel—the laurel of Tempe—and taking a branch in his right hand, he came to Delphi and took over the oracle, as the son of Zeus and **Leto**. 

There is an altar in the very spot where he crowned himself and took up the branch. Even now, the people of Delphi still follow the custom of sending noble youths, choosing a chief envoy <term>architheoros: the leader of a sacred delegation sent by a city to a religious festival or oracle</term> from among themselves. These youths arrive and offer magnificent sacrifices in Tempe; they then depart again after weaving crowns from the same laurel with which the god once crowned himself when he was in love <note>Aelian likely refers here to Apollo's pursuit of Daphne, who turned into a laurel tree</note>. They travel along that road called the **Pythian Way**, which leads through **Thessaly**, **Perrhaebia**, the region of **Oeta**, and the lands of the **Aenianians**, **Malians**, **Dorians**, and the **Western Locrians**. These peoples escort the envoys with no less reverence and honor than those who receive the sacred offerings from the **Hyperboreans** <note>A mythical people of the far north who were legendary worshippers of Apollo</note> for the same god. Furthermore, at the **Pythian Games**, they give the crown made from this laurel to the victors. 

Let this much be said by me regarding the Thessalian Tempe.

***

### On Anaxagoras bravely bearing the death of his children

**A**naxagoras of **Clazomenae** <note>A prominent Pre-Socratic philosopher (c. 500–428 BC)</note> was once engaged in serious study when a man approached him to say that his two sons had died—the only children Anaxagoras had. He, not being disturbed at all, said: "I knew that I had fathered mortals."

# BOOK III

### Concerning Xenophon, who bore the death of his son with courage:~

While Xenophon <note>Xenophon (c. 430–354 BCE) was a famous Greek historian, soldier, and student of Socrates.</note> was performing a sacrifice, a messenger arrived from Mantinea <note>A city in the Peloponnese; the Battle of Mantinea took place in 362 BCE.</note> reporting that his son, Gryllus, had died. At this news, Xenophon removed his **sacrificial wreath**, yet he continued with the ritual. However, when the messenger added that his son had died while victorious, Xenophon placed the wreath back upon his head. This story is well-known and has been shared by many:~

### That Dion was not shaken by the death of his son:~

Dion, the son of Hipparinus and a student of Plato, happened to be transacting public business and community affairs when his son fell from the roof into the courtyard and died. Dion was not altered by this tragedy at all; instead, he remained focused on what he had been doing from the start and continued with his work as if nothing had happened:~

### Anaxagoras showed no distress upon seeing his dead son:~

They say that when certain people brought the body of Anaxagoras’s <note>Anaxagoras (c. 500–428 BCE) was a Pre-Socratic philosopher famous for his scientific views on the cosmos.</note> son back from the battle line, he looked upon him without the color of his face changing and without shedding a tear. He simply praised him as a **brave soldier** and gave orders for his burial:~

### Concerning the greatness of spirit of Crates:~

Crates the Theban <note>Crates of Thebes (c. 365–285 BCE) was a famous Cynic philosopher who gave away his fortune to live in poverty.</note> was observed to be high-minded in all matters and was contemptuous of the things admired by the masses, especially wealth and his native land. It is well known to everyone that he surrendered his entire estate to the people of Thebes. However, another story about him is not so familiar: when he was leaving Thebes after it had been rebuilt, he said, "I have no need for a city that Alexander <note>Alexander the Great, who famously destroyed Thebes in 335 BCE.</note>—or anyone else—might one day raze to the ground again":~

### Concerning the verbal abuse of the masses:~

Demochares, the nephew of Demosthenes, wished to demonstrate that he looked down upon the slander of the masses. Seeing some wicked men sitting in an atrium who were desperate to speak ill of others in every possible way, he asked them, "What are you 'buying so poorly' today?" Through this name <note>The original Greek uses a word meaning 'to haggle for a bad reputation' or 'to deal in slander.'</note> he exposed the true nature of their character:~

### That Phrynichus was elected general because of a poem:~

The Athenians elected Phrynichus <note>Phrynichus was a pioneer of Greek tragedy; the Athenians were famously moved by his plays.</note> to the rank of <term>strategos</term> <note>A military general or commander.</note> neither because of political pressure nor his family's status, nor even because he was wealthy. Often in Athens, men were admired and put in charge of the state's affairs for those reasons, but in this case, it was for his poetry.

<summary>A collection of short biographical anecdotes illustrating the Stoic and Cynic virtues of emotional control, indifference to tragedy, and the unconventional ways the Athenians rewarded talent.</summary>

->[THE] THIRD<-

...[distributed his property]. But since he had mastered melodies suitable for the pyrrhic dances <term>pyrrhic: a traditional dance performed in armor that mimicked combat movements</term> and tragedy, and had also labored over war-like songs, he so dominated the theater and won over those present that they immediately elected him general <term>strategos: a high-ranking military commander</term>. They believed that he would lead military affairs well and as necessary, since the melodies and poems he produced for the army were not out of harmony with men in arms.

### Concerning Love

What man in love, with battle pressing on and war closing in, would not join his beloved in the fray? But the unloving man flees and runs away from the lover, being profane and uninitiated <note>original: "ἀτέλεστος" — meaning one who has not been initiated into the sacred mysteries of a god</note> in the rites of the god Love. Such a man [the lover] becomes courageous to the full extent of his soul’s capacity and his body’s strength. The enemy fears him as one possessed by a god, just as they fear those possessed by Dionysus <note>Dionysus: the god of wine and ritual ecstasy; his followers were often thought to possess supernatural strength or frenzy</note>. By Zeus, there is no commonality between the madness of Ares <note>Ares: the god of war</note> and the madness of Love. For those seized by the other god [Dionysus]—of whom Homer says they seem to be mad while alive—those covered by the spirit of wine do not fight as well as those inspired by Dionysus himself once. But the "devotees of Love" <note>original: "Ἔρωτος Βάκχοι" — literally "Bacchants of Love," comparing lovers in battle to the ecstatic worshippers of Dionysus</note> who go to war, driven both by the impulse of Ares and the burning of Love, endure a double service—unwillingly, according to the Cretan goodwill—and they achieve double success. Therefore, no one would blame a soldier if he could not endure to face an opponent bloodthirsty from both Ares and Love; for the soldier is stationed under one god, but his opponent is under the power of two.

### Concerning the lovers and beloveds in Lacedaemon

Regarding the Ephors <term>ephors: a council of five senior leaders in Sparta (Lacedaemon) who oversaw the kings and public conduct</term> in Lacedaemon, I have many other fine things to report, but I have chosen to speak of these for now. When one of the beautiful youths among them chose a rich lover instead of a good man who was poor, the Ephors imposed a fine on the youth, punishing his greed <note>original: "φιλοχρηματίαν" — literally "love of money"</note>, it seems, with the loss of money. It is also clear that they fined a certain noble and good man because he did not love any of those youths who were naturally gifted; they penalized him because, being good himself, he loved no one. For it was evident that he would have made his beloved like himself. Indeed, it was clear that the goodwill of lovers toward their boys produced virtues <note>original: "ἀρετὰς" — excellence or moral virtue</note> whenever the lovers themselves were honorable. For this too is a Spartan law: when a youth errs through simplicity of character or because of his young age, the authorities forgive him. But they punish the lover... <margin>They hold him responsible for the youth's behavior</margin>

# On the Soul: ~

<margin>o</margin> The Peripatetics <term>Peripatetics: followers of Aristotle’s school of philosophy, so named because they often walked while debating</term> say that during the day, the soul is mortal and entangled with the body, unable to clearly contemplate the truth. At night, however, it is released from its service to the body; it becomes spherical and gathers in the area around the chest <note>the ancients often believed the heart or chest was the seat of the intellect</note>, where it becomes more prophetic, which is the source of dreams. ~

# On Love among the Spartans: ~

The beautiful youths of the Spartans are not pampered or boastful toward their lovers. On the contrary, one can learn from them the opposite of what is seen in other attractive youths. Indeed, they themselves ask their lovers to "breathe into" them <note>original: "εἰσπνεῖν" — a technical term in Spartan culture for the inspiration or mentorship a lover provided to a youth</note>. This is a Spartan expression meaning "to love." 
<margin>i</margin> A Spartan and love know no shame. For if a youth dared to endure an insult, or a lover dared to commit an outrage, it did not profit either of them to remain in the city in such a state of excess. Either they were banished from their homeland or, even more severely, they were removed from life itself. ~

# On the Drunkenness of the Tapyri: ~

The nation of the Tapyri <note>Tapyri: an ancient tribe from the southern coast of the Caspian Sea</note> is so fond of wine that they essentially live in it. They consume the majority of their lives in its company. They do not only use it as a drink, but wine also serves as their ointment, just as oils do for other people. ~

# On the Drunkenness of the Byzantines: ~

The Byzantines <note>Byzantines: inhabitants of the ancient Greek city of Byzantium, long before it became Constantinople</note> are such terrible drunkards that the word is they live in taverns. They have moved out of their own homes and bodies, so to speak, and they lease their houses to foreigners visiting the city. They not only abandon their homes but also their wives. As a result, the Byzantines are blamed for a double vice: both Drunkenness and Procuring <term>procuring: acting as a pimp or facilitator for illicit relationships</term>. Since they are dissolving into drunkenness and wine, they rejoice when they hear a flute—indeed, their only "work" is to be played to—but they cannot endure the sound of a trumpet even at the start of a battle. From this, one can perceive that the Byzantines are completely alienated from weapons and wars. For this reason, Leonidas <note>not the famous Spartan king, but a later commander of Byzantium</note>, their general during a siege, observed that when the enemy attacked the walls, the men abandoned their guard posts and spent their days in their usual haunts...

### BOOK THREE ~

...[Leonidas] <note>Leonidas of Byzantium, a general during a siege of the city</note> ordered the taverns to be set up for the soldiers right upon the city walls. This clever trick finally persuaded them—though slowly—not to abandon their posts, since the very excuse they sought for leaving was now provided for them right at hand. It is said that he did this because of their inner spirits. Menander <note>A famous Athenian playwright of the New Comedy (c. 342–290 BCE)</note> seems to agree with this characterization when he says: "Byzantium makes everyone drunk; it was drinking the whole night through." ~

->**On the Drunkenness of the Argives, Corinthians, Thracians, and Illyrians** ~<-

The people of Argos and Corinth are also known to be quite "refined" <note>likely used ironically here</note> in their excessive approach to wine. As for the Thracians, it has already been confirmed and is widely rumored that they are the heaviest of drinkers. Nor is there any disagreement regarding the Illyrians; they have even added a particular custom that involves the guests present at a banquet: any man may toast the women if he wishes, even if the woman is not related to him. ~

->**A Comparison of the Generals Demetrius and Timotheus** ~<-

Next, one might ask who was the better general: Demetrius the Besieger <term>Demetrius Poliorcetes: (337–283 BCE) a Macedonian king famous for his massive siege engines</term> or Timotheus the Athenian? I will speak using one of two perspectives. Just as some of you prefer one over the other, Demetrius took cities through force, greed, and by causing the greatest suffering; he acted unjustly, bringing up siege engines, shaking down the walls, and undermining them. Timotheus, however, won by persuading and teaching through reason, showing that it was more profitable to listen to the Athenians. ~

->**That Philosophy is Not Un-political, and Which Philosophers Governed** ~<-

Now, while some in the field of philosophy lived in quiet because they were good only in their intellectual contemplation, others actually corrected public affairs. Zaleucus did this for the Locrians, and Charondas for those in Catana—and also in Rhegium when he fled from Catana. Archytas was a good leader for those living under tyrants, and Solon for the Athenians. Bias and Thales greatly benefited Ionia, Chilon the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note>, Pittacus the Mitylenaeans, and Cleobulus the Rhodians. Anaximander even led the colony that arrived from Miletus [to Apollonia]. Furthermore, Xenophon was a good soldier and an even better general when he went up with Cyrus; and when Cyrus and his officers died, and necessity called for someone to save the fleeing Greeks and lead them back to their homes, he was that very man.

# BOOK <note>original: "ΒΙΒΛΙΟΝ"</note>

Plato, the son of Ariston, brought Dion back to Sicily, and through the advice and teachings he provided, he overthrew the tyranny of Dionysius. Socrates, however, was not pleased with the Athenian constitution; he saw the democracy of the Athenians as being essentially tyrannical and monarchical in nature. Because of this, he did not vote for the death sentence of the ten generals <note>The generals who were controversially executed after the Battle of Arginusae (406 BCE) for failing to rescue shipwrecked sailors</note>. Furthermore, he refused to take part in the impieties of the Thirty <term>The Thirty Tyrants: a pro-Spartan oligarchy installed in Athens after the Peloponnesian War</term>. Yet, when it was necessary to fight for the fatherland, he was a soldier who served without making excuses. Thus, he campaigned at Delium <note>The transcription says "Delos," but historically refers to the Battle of Delium in 424 BCE</note>, at Amphipolis, and at Potidaea.

Aristotle likewise restored his own homeland <note>Stagira</note>, not only after it had fallen to its knees, but by providing it with laws. Demetrius of Phalerum also governed most illustriously at Athens, until the envy common to the Athenians drove him out. Even while living in Egypt with Ptolemy, he took the lead in establishing legislation. And who would deny that Pericles, the son of Xanthippus, was a philosopher? Or Epaminondas the son of Polymnis, Phocion the son of Phocus, Aristides the son of Lysimachus, Ephialtes the son of Sophonides, and—further down in time—Carneades and Critolaus?

These men <note>Carneades, Critolaus, and Diogenes the Stoic</note> even arrived in Rome as ambassadors sent by the Athenians, appearing like a salvation sent from heaven. They so thoroughly swayed the Senate that people said the Athenians had sent ambassadors who were not merely persuading us, but were compelling us to do whatever they wished. I, for my part, find the politics of the "worthless" philosophers to be quite different from the works of Persaeus, who educated Antigonus, or the works of Aristotle, for it is clear he was practicing philosophy while with Alexander, the son of Philip, when he was a youth. Lysis, the well-known pupil of Pythagoras, also personally educated Epaminondas. If anyone, therefore, rejects the philosophers from public life, I certainly would not agree with him. Such are these matters. For I would not choose a life of total inactivity <note>original: "apragmosyne"</note> or a mere love of quietude over their examples, nor would I snatch away the influence they exerted. ~

### BOOK THREE

->**THREE**<-

...[Europe, Asia, and] Libya are islands <note>original: "βύην νῆσον", likely part of "Λιβύην" (Libya). Silenus argues that the known world is merely a group of islands surrounded by the ocean.</note> which the Ocean flows around in a circle. He explained that the only true continent is that which lies outside this world of ours. He described its size as infinite. It nourishes various large animals, and the men there are twice our size; their lifespan is not like ours, but is also twice as long. He said there are many great cities with unique ways of life, governed by laws that are the polar opposite of those we follow.

He spoke of two cities that are the largest of all, which are in no way like one another. One is named **Warlike** <note>original: "μάχιμον" (Machimos)</note>, and the other **Pious** <note>original: "εὐσεβῆ" (Eusebes)</note>. The Pious people live in peace and deep wealth, harvesting the fruits of the earth without the need for plows or oxen. Farming and sowing are not necessary labors for them. They remain healthy and free from disease, ending their lives in great laughter and enjoyment. They are so truly just that even the god <note>referring to a deity or divine presence</note> often does not disdain to visit them.

On the other hand, the inhabitants of the Warlike city are themselves extremely bellicose; they are born with weapons, are constantly at war, and subdue their neighbors. This single city holds power over many nations. Its inhabitants number no fewer than two hundred thousand <term>twenty myriads</term>. They rarely die of sickness; instead, they usually perish in battle, struck by stones or wood, for they are invulnerable to iron. They possess such an abundance of gold and silver that gold is held in less honor among them than iron is among us.

He said that they once even attempted to cross over to these islands of ours. Having sailed across the ocean with ten million men, they reached the land of the Hyperboreans <note>A mythical people described in Greek legend as living in a land of perpetual sunshine in the far north</note>. But when they learned that these were considered the most blessed people among us, they looked down on them as wretched and living meanly, and for this reason, they disdained to advance any further.

He added something even more wonderful: a people called the **Meropes** <term>literally "Articulate ones," a name used in mythology for early humans</term> inhabit many large cities there. At the furthest edge of their country, he said, is a place called **No-Return** <note>original: "ἄνοστον" (Anostos)</note>. It resembles a chasm, and is covered neither by darkness nor light, but is shrouded in a misty, reddish air. Two rivers flow around this place; one is called the River of Pleasure, and the other the River of Grief. Beside each of these...

# BOOK THREE

->**THREE**<-

...said to resemble a tree the size of a large plane tree <note>The plane tree (*Platanus orientalis*) was famous in antiquity for its massive size and shade.</note>. It bears fruits of two kinds. First, those growing by the River of Sorrow have such a nature that if someone tastes them, they shed such a flood of tears that they consume their entire remaining life in lamentation and thus reach their end. But the other trees, which grow all along the River of Pleasure, bear an opposite fruit. For whoever tastes of these ceases from their former desires; not only that, but if they loved someone, they even forget that love, and they forget themselves. Gradually, they become younger: they retrace the ages they have already passed through and return to their earlier years. One casts off old age and returns to his prime; then he retreats to the age of a youth, then becomes a child, then an infant, and in this state, he is finally dissolved <note>original: "ἐξαναλύθη" — implying a return to nothingness or a dissolution of the physical form</note>. If anyone finds the man from Chios <note>A reference to the historian Theopompus of Chios, the original source of this myth.</note> trustworthy in saying these things, let them believe him. To me, however, he seems a tedious teller of myths, both in these matters and in others.

### On the disagreement of Aristotle with
### Plato.

It is said that the rift between Aristotle and Plato first arose from the following circumstances. Plato was not pleased with Aristotle’s way of life, nor with the way he presented his physical person. For Aristotle used elaborate clothing and footwear, and he cut his hair in a style that was unusual to Plato. He also used to beautify himself by wearing many rings. Furthermore, there was a certain mocking expression <note>original: "φαιδρά" can mean bright or cheerful, but in this context suggests a self-satisfied or mocking air.</note> upon his face. His unrestrained talkativeness also bore witness against his character. It is clear that all these things are alien to a philosopher. Therefore, seeing these traits, Plato did not admit the man to his inner circle, preferring instead Xenocrates, Speusippus, Amyclas, and others. He welcomed these men with honor and included them in his philosophical discussions. 

Once, when Xenocrates had gone on a journey to his homeland, Aristotle was observed for a time surrounding himself with a circle of his own followers, among whom was Mrasones of Phocis and others of that kind. At that time, Speusippus was ill and was therefore unable to walk with Plato. Plato was now eighty years old, and due to his age, his memory was beginning to fail him. Therefore, attacking him and plotting against him, Aristotle, with great ambition...

...making his inquiries and, in a certain manner, acting in an adversarial way, he was clearly being both unjust and ungrateful. Because of these things, Plato left the outer <term>walkway</term><note>original: "peripatos" — a covered walk or colonnade in a gymnasium where philosophers held discussions.</note> and walked inside with his companions. After three months had passed, <term>Xenocrates</term><note>A devoted student of Plato who eventually became the third head of the Academy.</note> arrived from the <term>Academy</term><note>Plato's famous school in Athens.</note> and found Aristotle walking there, but he did not find Plato. Seeing him with his acquaintances, not returning to Plato from the walk but going off by himself toward the city, Xenocrates asked one of those in the walkway where Plato might be, for he suspected that he was feeling unwell. The man replied, "He is not ill. However, Aristotle has harassed him and forced him to withdraw from the walkway; having retreated, he now practices philosophy in his own garden." When Xenocrates heard this, he immediately went to Plato and found him conversing with those who were with him; they were quite numerous, men of great repute, and those who were considered the most distinguished of the youths. When he had finished his discourse, he greeted Xenocrates with kindness, as was fitting, and Xenocrates greeted him in return. When the gathering was dismissed, Xenocrates said nothing to Plato nor heard anything from him regarding the matter, but instead gathered the companions and very strongly rebuked <term>Speusippus</term><note>Plato’s nephew, who took over the Academy after Plato's death.</note> for yielding the walkway to Aristotle. He then set upon the young man <note>referring to Aristotle</note> with great vigor and reached such a point of contention that he drove him out and restored the accustomed place to Plato.

### On Lysander, and the gifts brought
### to him.

When Lysander the Spartan arrived in Ionia, his foreign hosts there sent him many things, and among them were a bull and a <term>cake</term><note>original: "plakous" — a rich, flat cake made with honey and cheese, viewed as a luxury by the austere Spartans.</note>. Looking at the cake, he asked what that pastry was supposed to be. The one bringing it replied that it was prepared from honey, cheese, and various other ingredients. Lysander said, "Well, give this to the <term>Helots</term><note>The state-owned serfs or slaves of the Spartans.</note>, for it is not a food for a free man," but he ordered the bull to be slaughtered according to ancestral customs and dined with pleasure.

## On the magnanimity of Themistocles:~

Once, while still a boy, <term>Themistocles</term><note>A famous Athenian politician and general who later led the Greeks to victory against the Persians at the Battle of Salamis.</note> was returning from school. When <term>Peisistratus</term><note>The tyrant who ruled Athens in the 6th century BC. He was known for his populist policies but nonetheless held absolute power.</note> approached, the boy’s tutor told him to step a little out of the road since the tyrant was coming near. But Themistocles replied with a very free spirit: "Is this road not wide enough for him?" Thus, even from that early age, a certain nobility and high-mindedness were already apparent in Themistocles.

## On the piety of Aeneas, and the mercy of the Greeks toward the Phrygians :~

When <term>Ilium</term><note>Another name for the city of Troy.</note> was captured, the <term>Achaeans</term><note>A common term used by ancient authors for the Greeks.</note> took pity on the fate of those who had been taken prisoner. In a very noble Greek fashion, they made a proclamation: each free person could take whatever one of their personal belongings they wished and carry it away. <term>Aeneas</term><note>The Trojan hero who, according to legend, survived the fall of Troy and traveled to Italy to become the ancestor of the Romans.</note> took up and carried his ancestral gods, looking past everything else he owned. The Greeks were so pleased by the man's <term>piety</term><note>original: "eusebeia" — a core Greek virtue involving proper respect for gods, parents, and tradition.</note> that they allowed him to take a second possession. This time, he took his father, who was very heavily aged, placed him on his shoulders, and carried him out. Overwhelmed with wonder at this act as well, the Greeks restored all of his personal property to him. They acknowledged that even those who are natural enemies become gentle toward men of piety—those who treat the gods and their parents with such reverence.

## On Alexander:~

Fine indeed were <term>Alexander’s</term><note>Alexander the Great, King of Macedon.</note> achievements at the <term>Granicus</term><note>A river in modern-day Turkey, site of Alexander's first major victory against the Persians.</note>, and those at <term>Issus</term>, and the battle at <term>Arbela</term>, and the capture of <term>Darius</term><note>Darius III, the King of Persia.</note>, and the many peoples who were made subjects of the Macedonians. Fine too was his conquest of all the rest of Asia, and the Indians who submitted to Alexander. Fine was his success at Tyre, his actions among the <term>Oxydracae</term><note>A Punjabi tribe encountered by Alexander during his Indian campaign.</note>, and his other deeds. Why is it necessary now, in this brief account, to encompass such great military excellence in a man? Let it be granted that Fortune favored Alexander in most things, giving him whatever she could. Alexander was noble for not being defeated by his own good fortune, nor giving up in the face of the favor she showed him. Yet, there were other things that were no longer fine in Alexander. Because of these, as they say, on the fifth day he would drink with <term>Medius</term><note>A friend and commander of Alexander known for hosting excessive banquets.</note>, then on the sixth he would sleep off the drinking bout. He lived only as much of that day as was needed to rise and give orders to his commanders about the next day’s march, saying it would begin at dawn. On the seventh, he feasted with <term>Perdiccas</term><note>One of Alexander's primary generals and later a regent of the empire.</note> and drank again, and on the eighth he...

### On the love of beauty in Xenophon : ~

<term>Xenophon</term><note>A student of Socrates and a famous general who led the "Ten Thousand" Greek mercenaries out of Persia.</note> used to take great care of his weapons, and he said that it was right to have beautiful arms. For he argued that to a victor, the most beautiful attire is fitting, and for one dying in battle, it is honorable to lie in a beautiful suit of <term>panoply</term><note>original: "panoplia" — the full suit of armor worn by a hoplite soldier.</note>. For to a noble man, these are the ornaments that truly adorn him. It is said that the son of Gryllus <note>i.e., Xenophon</note> had an Argolic shield, an Attic breastplate, a Boeotian helmet, and an Epidaurian horse. I would say that such things belong to a man who loves beauty and who considers himself worthy of fine things: ~

### On Leonidas, and the three hundred who died voluntarily for Greece : ~

<term>Leonidas</term><note>The Spartan king who famously led the defense against the Persians at Thermopylae in 480 BC.</note> the Lacedaemonian, and the three hundred with him, chose the death that had been prophesied for them at <term>the Gates</term><note>original: "pylais" — referring to Thermopylae, which literally means "The Hot Gates."</note>. Having fought well and nobly for the sake of Greece, they met a glorious end. They left behind for themselves immortal glory and a good reputation throughout the ages: ~

### On Pindarus the tyrant : ~

<term>Pindarus</term><note>Not the famous poet, but a local ruler of Ephesus in the 6th century BC.</note>, the son of Melas and grandson of Alyattes the Lydian, succeeded to the tyranny over the Ephesians. Regarding punishments, he was harsh and relentless; however, in other matters, he seemed to be a lover of his city and prudent. He took great care that his fatherland should not be enslaved by the <term>barbarians</term><note>A term used by Greeks to describe any non-Greek speakers, in this case, the Lydians.</note>. He proved this to be the case by the following: when <term>Croesus</term><note>The legendary wealthy king of Lydia.</note>, who was his uncle on his mother's side, was subduing <term>Ionia</term><note>The central coastal region of ancient Anatolia (modern Turkey), where Ephesus was located.</note>, he sent an embassy to Pindarus demanding that the Ephesians submit to him. When Pindarus would not comply, Croesus besieged the city. But when one of the towers was overturned—the one which was later called "the Traitor"—and the danger was visible to all eyes,

->**BOOK THREE**<-

**Pindar**<note>This refers to Pindar the tyrant of Ephesus, the nephew of Croesus, not the famous lyric poet of the same name.</note> advised the **Ephesians**, when they were being besieged, to tie ropes from the city gates and the walls and fasten them to the columns of the **Temple of Artemis**, effectively leaving the city as a dedicated offering to Artemis. By doing this, he intended to secure a sacred sanctuary for **Ephesus**. He also advised that the people should go and petition the **Lydian** king. When the Ephesians offered their plea, it is said that **Croesus**<note>The King of Lydia (reigned c. 560–546 BC), legendary for his immense wealth.</note> laughed. Accepting what had been done with a mild spirit, he granted the Ephesians their departure with freedom, but he ordered Pindar to leave the city. Pindar did not object. Taking those of his friends who wished to depart with him, and entrusting his son and the majority of his property to the city, he chose **Pasicles** from among the living to be the guardian of both his child and his money. He then departed for the **Peloponnese**, choosing a self-imposed exile from a life of power rather than making his homeland subject to the Lydians. ~

### On Plato’s poverty, and how he took up philosophy. ~

This story was also famous, though whether it is true I do not know; however, it has been recorded that **Plato**, the son of Ariston, was supposedly driven by poverty and a lowly state to the point that he was about to be enrolled for military service. But being intercepted by **Socrates**, Plato "named his weapons"<note>original: "ὠνόμασε τὰ ὅπλα" — a phrase suggesting he laid down his arms or identified his true calling; Socrates convinced him to fight with arguments rather than shields.</note>; his impulse was checked after Socrates spoke to him as was fitting, so that he would instead desire a love for philosophy. ~

### How Socrates checked the vanity of Alcibiades. ~

Seeing that **Alcibiades** was puffed up with <term>vanity</term><note>original: "typhos" — a term often used by philosophers to describe the "fog" or "smoke" of worldly pride and delusion.</note> because of his wealth and was thinking greatly of himself because of his landholdings, **Socrates** led him to a place where a small tablet was displayed containing a map of the earth. He ordered him to look for **Attica** there. When he found it, Socrates ordered him to pick out his own fields. When Alcibiades said, "But they are not written anywhere," Socrates replied, "Are you then so proud of things that are not even a part of the earth?"

### On the poverty and vanity of Diogenes. ~

**Diogenes of Sinope**<note>The famous Cynic philosopher who lived in a large ceramic jar and advocated for a life according to nature, stripped of all social pretension.</note> used to say constantly of himself that he fulfilled the curses found in tragedy and endured them. For he was "a wanderer, homeless, deprived of a fatherland, a beggar, poorly clad, possessing only a life that lasted a day." And yet, despite these things, he was no less proud than **Alexander** was of his rule over the civilized world, even when Alexander had conquered the **Indians** and returned to **Babylon**. ~

### On the poverty of the Wise. ~

->**BOOK THREE 22**<-

**Amoebeas** the <term>citharode</term><note>A professional singer who performed while playing the kithara, a type of ancient lyre.</note> was said to be extremely self-controlled; though he had a very beautiful wife, he did not have intercourse with her. So too was **Diogenes**, the tragic actor. **Clitomachus** the <term>pankratiast</term><note>A competitor in the pankration, a grueling ancient Greek sport combining boxing and wrestling.</note>, if he ever saw dogs mating, would turn away. And at a banquet, if any talk of sexual matters slipped into the conversation, he would stand up and depart.

### On the diligence of Nicias regarding his own art.

**Nicias** the painter had such great diligence regarding his painting that he often forgot to take food, being so consumed by his craft.

### On Alexander and Hercules learning to play the lyre.

**Alexander**, the son of Philip, while still a boy and not yet an adolescent, was learning to play the lyre. When his teacher ordered him to strike a certain string according to the melody, and specifically the one required by the music, Alexander asked, "What difference will it make if I strike this one instead?" pointing to another. The teacher replied, "It makes no difference to one who is going to be a king; but it does to one who is going to be a lyre-player." For he feared, it seems, that the boy might be uneducated, like [the student of] **Linus** again. For Linus was teaching **Hercules**, while he was still a child, to play the lyre. When Hercules touched the instrument rather unmusically, Linus became angry with him; Hercules, in a fit of rage, struck Linus with the plectrum and killed him.

### On Satyrus the flute-player.

**Satyrus** the flute-player was a frequent listener of the philosopher, and being charmed by what was said, he would remark:
> ->"If only I did not set these shining bows aside with fire."<- <note>original: "Εἰ μὴ ἐγὼ τάδε τόξα φαεινὰ σὺν πυρεὶ θείην" — Satyrus is likely paraphrasing a poetic line to compare his flutes to weapons he would burn to dedicate himself to philosophy.</note>
He was hinting at his flutes with this, and in a way disparaging his own art by comparison to philosophy.

### A law common to the Spartans and the Romans.

There was a law among the **Spartans** and the **Romans** that no one was permitted to buy food however they wished, nor as much as they wished. They commanded this so that the citizens would clearly practice self-restraint, especially regarding the matters of the table.

### That it was not permitted to laugh in the Academy.

There was also this Attic saying, which says that formerly in the **Academy**<note>The school founded by Plato in Athens, known for its solemnity.</note> it was not even permitted to laugh. For they endeavored to keep the place free from insolence and idle behavior.

### Why Aristotle left Athens.

When **Aristotle** left Athens because of a legal judgment, and someone asked him what kind of city Athens was, he said, "All-beautiful..."

It connects his departure to the earlier execution of Socrates, framing it as a move to protect the city from "sinning twice" against philosophy.</meta>

# BOOK III

...but for her, "pear upon pear grows old, and fig upon fig," <note>original: "ὄγχνη ἐπ' ὄγχνη γηράσκει, σύκον δ' ἐπὶ σύκῳ". This is a quote from Homer’s *Odyssey* (7.120), describing the eternal harvest in the gardens of King Alcinous.</note> speaking of the <term>sycophants</term><note>A "sycophant" (literally "fig-shower") in ancient Athens was a professional informer or a person who brought malicious lawsuits. Aristotle is using a pun here: the Homeric quote mentions "figs," which reminds him of the "sycophants" infestation in Athens.</note>. And to the man who asked why he had left Athens, he replied that he did not wish for the Athenians to sin twice against philosophy—alluding to the tragedy of **Socrates** and the danger he himself was facing.

### A law of the Ceans regarding the elderly.

There is a law among the people of **Keos** <note>An island in the Cyclades.</note>: those who have reached a very advanced age, as if inviting themselves to a guest-dinner or some festive sacrifice, gather together, crown themselves with wreaths, and drink <term>hemlock</term><note>A poison made from the plant *Conium maculatum*, famously used for the execution of Socrates.</note>. They do this when they are conscious that they are no longer useful for the labors that benefit their homeland, their minds already beginning to fail due to the passage of time.

### Certain things first discovered in Athens.

They say that in **Athens** the olive tree and the fig tree were first discovered, which were the first fruits the earth gave up. The Athenians were also the first to establish the practice of giving and receiving justice through legal trials. Furthermore, they were the first to devise athletic competitions involving the body, and they were the first to strip naked and rub themselves with oil for exercise. **Erichthonius** <note>A legendary early king of Athens.</note> was the first to yoke horses to a chariot.

### What various people ate in ancient times.

The **Arcadians** ate acorns, the **Argives** ate pears, the **Athenians** ate figs, and the **Tirynthians** ate wild pears for their dinner. The **Indians** ate reeds <note>Likely a reference to sugar cane.</note>, the **Carmanians** ate dates, the **Maeotae** and **Sauromatae** ate millet, and the **Persians** ate *permindon* <note>original: πέρμινδον. An unidentified fruit, possibly a type of berry or processed date cake.</note> and cress.

### On Satyrs, Tityri, and Sileni.

The fellow dancers of **Dionysus** <note>The god of wine and theater.</note> were the **Satyrs**, whom some call **Tityri**. They took this name from the "chirping" sounds <note>original: τερετισμάτων. Imitative sounds like those of a cicada or a pipe.</note> in which Satyrs delight. The name Satyrs comes from "grinning" <note>original: σεσηρέναι. Refers to a wide, teeth-baring grin or a sneer.</note>, and **Sileni** from "mockery" <note>original: σιλαίνειν. Connected to *sillos*, a type of satirical poetry or lampoon.</note>, for they say *sillos* is a form of insult mixed with unpleasant jesting. The clothing of the Sileni consisted of shaggy tunics. This costume alludes to the cultivation provided by Dionysus: the thick growth of the vineyards and the density of the vine branches.

### Many epithets for Dionysus.

Because of the abundance of fruit, the ancients used the word *phlyein* <note>To bubble over or gush.</note>. From this, they called Dionysus **Phleon** <note>The Abundant One.</note>, as well as **Protruges** <note>The Pre-vintager.</note>, **Staphylites** <note>Of the Grape Cluster.</note>, **Omphakites** <note>Of the Unripe Grape.</note>, and by various other names.

### On certain frenzied women...

# BOOK III

It is said that the daughters of **Proetus** were also "darkened" <note>original: "κελαινὰ" (kelaina), likely referring to their wretched or gloomy state of mind.</note>, or rather, it was the queen of **Cyprus** <note>Aphrodite, who was often called the Cyprian queen because her cult was centered on the island of Cyprus.</note> who brought this upon them. Over a part of the **Peloponnese**, they wandered naked and in a frenzy, and they even ventured into other lands of **Greece**, wandering while afflicted by this disease. I hear that a certain Bacchic sting <note>The "sting" or *oistros* refers to the gadfly-like madness sent by a god.</note> also fell upon the women of the **Lacedaemonians** <note>The Spartans.</note>, as well as those of the **Chians**, and the women of the **Boeotians** were driven to the height of madness. The tragedy <note>Likely a reference to Euripides' play *The Bacchae*.</note> cries this out as well.

But they say that only the daughters of **Minyas**—**Leucippe**, **Arsippe**, and **Alcithoe**—refused to join this service to **Dionysus**. The reason was that they longed for their husbands, and because of this, they did not dance as **Maenads** <note>Female followers of Dionysus who danced in a state of divine possession.</note> for the god. He became angry. While they were occupied with their looms and laboring over their handiwork—though not with much enthusiasm—suddenly ivy and vines began to creep around the looms. Snakes nested in their wool-baskets. From the ceilings, wine and drops of milk began to drip. 

Even these wonders did not persuade them to enter the service of the <term>daemon</term> <note>original: "δαίμονοσ". In this context, it refers to a divinity or supernatural power (Dionysus), not a "demon" in the modern sense.</note>. Therefore, they committed a terrible deed outside of **Cithaeron** <note>Mount Cithaeron, the traditional site of Bacchic madness near Thebes.</note>, not just the deeds usually done on the mountain. For they tore apart the son of **Leucippe**, who was still tender and young, just as the Maenads tear apart a fawn when seized by madness. Then, fleeing from there because of the pollution <note>original: "ἄγος" (agos), a term for a religious curse or blood-guilt resulting from a heinous crime.</note>, these women who were originally the daughters of Minyas were pursued. Following this, they were transformed into birds. One became a crow, another a bat, and the third an owl.

### Concerning the cithara-player murdered by the Sybarites

In **Sybaris** <note>A Greek city in southern Italy known for its extreme luxury and wealth.</note>, a cithara-player <note>A professional singer who accompanied himself on the cithara, a type of lyre.</note> was singing during a competition which they were holding in honor of **Hera**. When the Sybarites fell into a riot over him and took up arms against one another, the cithara-player, frightened and still in his professional robes, fled for refuge to the altar of **Hera**. But even there, they did not spare the singer. 

A little later, blood seemed to bubble up in the temple of Hera, just like a perennial spring. The Sybarites sent messengers to **Delphi** to consult the oracle. The **Pythia** <note>The high priestess of the Temple of Apollo at Delphi who delivered oracles.</note> replied:

> Depart from my tripods! Blood is still upon your hands,
> A great deal of it, dripping from the stone threshold, keeps you away.
> I will not give you oracles, for you slew a servant of the Muses
> Before the altars of Hera, not fearing the vengeance of the gods.
> For those who have done evil, the end brought by justice is not delayed,
> Nor can it be turned aside, not even if they were the offspring of Zeus.

<summary>This page describes the consequences of resisting or insulting the gods, specifically Dionysus's punishment of the Minyades and the divine vengeance following the murder of a musician in a temple.</summary>

<margin>T</margin>hey pay the price with their own heads and their own children, and calamity follows upon calamity in their houses. <note>original: "Αὐτῇσιν κεφαλῇσι καὶ οἱ σφετέροισι τέκεσσιν..." This appears to be a poetic quotation, likely from Hesiod, regarding the divine punishment of the wicked.</note> Justice was not slow. At any rate, when the people of **Croton** <note>A Greek city in Southern Italy, famous for its athletes and as a rival to the luxurious city of Sybaris.</note> took their revenge, the people of **Sybaris** were uprooted by them, and their city vanished.

### Concerning the one who is unable to help his companions, the one who refuses to help, and another who helps but fails:

<margin>T</margin>hree young men from the same city arrived at **Delphi** <note>The most famous religious site in Ancient Greece, home to the Oracle of Apollo.</note> as sacred observers. They happened to encounter bandits. One of them fled, while the second drew a sword to attack the criminals as they set upon the third man. He struck at the bandit, but by the thrust of his sword, he accidentally hit his friend instead. To the one who fled, the **Pythia** <note>The high priestess of the Temple of Apollo at Delphi, who delivered oracles in a trance state.</note> gave this response:

> You were present nearby when your friend was dying and did not help;
> I give you no oracle. You are defiled. Depart from the temple.

And to the other man, when he questioned the Pythia, she gave this response:

> You killed your companion while defending him; blood has not defiled you,
> and your hands are cleaner than they were before.

### An oracle given to Philip

<margin>T</margin>hey say that an oracle was delivered to **Philip** <note>Philip II of Macedon, the father of Alexander the Great.</note> from the shrine of **Trophonius** <note>A hero or deity with a famous subterranean oracle in Boeotia known for its terrifying initiation rites.</note> in **Boeotia**, warning him that he must "beware the chariot." The story goes that because of this oracle, he never again stepped onto a chariot. However, there is a remarkable double meaning in this story. Some say that the sword of **Pausanias** <note>The bodyguard who assassinated Philip in 336 BCE during a wedding procession.</note>, with which he killed Philip, had a chariot carved in ivory upon its handle. Others say he was killed near the place called the "Chariot" <note>original: "ἅρμα" (Harma), a specific location in Boeotia.</note> near the **Pteleon** lake <note>original: "πτελεθῶντα λίμνην". This refers to the geography surrounding the fulfillment of the prophecy.</note>. The first story is common, but the second has not reached everyone’s ears.

---

### A law of the Stagirites:

<margin>T</margin>his is a law of the people of **Stagira** <note>The birthplace of the philosopher Aristotle.</note> and is entirely Greek in spirit: "Do not take what you did not set down." <note>A proverb regarding honesty and property, emphasizing that one should not claim something they did not personally place or earn.</note>
