# AELIAN’S HISTORICAL MISCELLANY

## BOOK FOUR

### Diverse Customs of Nations

<margin>A</margin>
There is a law of the **Lucanians** <note>An ancient Italic tribe living in what is now Southern Italy.</note>: if a stranger arrives at sunset and wishes to enter someone's home, and the owner does not receive the man, the owner is to be fined and must face legal punishment for "bad hospitality." It seems to me that this law is pleasing both to the common people and to **Zeus Xenios** <note>The aspect of the king of the gods specifically concerned with the protection of guests and strangers.</note>.

I hear that the **Dardanians** <note>An Illyrian tribe residing in the Balkans, often described by Greek writers as having primitive or unusual habits.</note> who live under the Illyrian rule wash only three times in their entire lives: after birth, at their weddings, and when they die.

**Indians** neither lend money nor do they know how to borrow. Moreover, it is not lawful for an Indian man to either commit an injustice or to be the victim of one. Therefore, they do not make written contracts or keep deposits <note>They operated on a system of mutual trust rather than legal paperwork.</note>.

There is a law of the **Sardinians** <note>Inhabitants of the island of Sardinia.</note> that when fathers grew old, the sons would kill them by beating them with clubs and then bury them. They considered it shameful for one who was excessively old to continue living, as the body, having suffered through old age, committed many errors. Among these same people, there is another such law: their trials were just, and while living, one had to be judged and give an account <term>euthynas</term> <note>original: "εὐθύνας" - a formal public audit or accounting of one's life and finances to prove they were a productive citizen.</note> demonstrating by what means he lived.

The **Assyrians** gather the maidens who are of marriageable age in a certain city and proclaim a market for them; then each man, having bought one, takes away his bride.

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues Book Four of Aelian's Historical Miscellany, moving from the hospitality laws of the Lucanians to the customs of other nations and anecdotes regarding famous Greek artists and heroes.</meta>

->BOOK [FOUR]<-

Among the **Byblians** <note>A people likely associated with the city of Byblos in Phoenicia, or a similar group known in antiquity for strict honesty.</note>, if a man comes across anything on the road, he does not pick up what he did not put down; for he does not consider such a find to be a "discovery," but rather an act of injustice.

The **Borbyccae** <note>An obscure tribe, possibly located in the northern regions or the East, often cited in ancient "paradoxography" (writings on wonders and strange customs).</note> kill those who have lived over seventy years, sacrificing the men and hanging the women.

The **Colchians** <note>Inhabitants of Colchis, an ancient region on the eastern coast of the Black Sea (modern-day Georgia).</note> bury their dead in animal hides; after sewing them up, they hang them from trees.

Among the **Lydians** <note>An Iron Age kingdom of western Asia Minor (modern Turkey) known for its wealth and early use of coinage.</note>, it was the custom for women to work as courtesans before they lived with their husbands. Once they were married, however, they were expected to be chaste. It was impossible for a woman who committed a transgression with another man to obtain forgiveness.

## On the dispute of Nicostratus and Laodocus
## against Laodocus the singer:

A certain story says that **Nicostratus** the *kithara* player <note>original: "κιθαριστὴν" - A musician who played the kithara, a professional version of the lyre.</note>, while disagreeing with **Laodocus** the *kitharode* <note>original: "κιθαρῳδῷ" - A performer who both sang and played the kithara simultaneously.</note> over matters of music, remarked that Laodocus was "a small man in a great art," while he himself was "a great man in a small art." It is not only a noble thing to increase the status of one’s house and wealth, but also to advance one’s art—if indeed one ought to pay attention to Nicostratus, who spoke these words with such conviction.

## Concerning the painters Polygnotus and Dionysius:

**Polygnotus of Thasos** and **Dionysius of Colophon** were both painters. Polygnotus painted on a grand scale, depicting great deeds and contests in massive proportions. The work of Dionysius, however, imitated the art of Polygnotus in every way except for the size—specifically in its precision, the expression of emotion (*pathos*), the depiction of character (*ethos*), the use of poses, the delicate rendering of garments, and all other such details.

## Theban Law concerning craftsmen and painters.

I hear that a law exists in **Thebes** <note>A major city-state in central Greece.</note> which commands craftsmen—specifically painters and sculptors—to imitate their subjects "toward the better" <note>This implies an idealistic style, making the subject look more beautiful or noble than in reality.</note>. The law threatens those who ever mold or paint subjects "toward the worse" <note>A realistic or unflattering portrayal, sometimes called "peintographia."</note> with a fine as punishment.

## Who remembered favors:

Certain men remembered past benefits and returned the favor. **Theseus** did so for **Hercules**. When **Aidoneus**, the king of the **Molossians** <note>A people of ancient Epirus; here Aelian uses a legendary name for their king, sometimes confused with the god of the underworld.</note>, had imprisoned Theseus after he arrived with **Pirithous** to kidnap the king's wife—Theseus was not seeking the marriage for himself, but did this to favor Pirithous—Hercules came to the Molossian...

### [BOOK] FOUR ~

Having arrived, [Heracles] rescued **Theseus**, and for this reason, Theseus raised an altar to him. Furthermore, the **Seven who fell at Thebes** <note>The "Seven Against Thebes," a group of heroes who died in a famous war against the city of those who came before them.</note> also showed their gratitude. For through those who arrived to bury the slain, they established the games in their honor, which many people believe were originally established for **Archemorus** <note>A child whose death was traditionally said to have inspired the Nemean Games; Aelian is citing an alternative tradition involving the Athenian intervention.</note>. **Heracles** also showed gratitude to **Nestor**; for when **Neleus** refused to purify Heracles <note>original: "καθῆραι" – to ritually cleanse someone of blood-guilt after a killing.</note>, the other sons agreed with Neleus, but Nestor did not. Because of this, when Heracles captured the city, he killed Neleus and his other sons, but not only did he spare Nestor, he also gave him his father’s kingdom to rule. The **Athenians**, as a whole people, also showed gratitude to the descendants of Heracles; since Heracles, their forefather, had been a benefactor to Theseus, the Athenians for this reason restored them to the **Peloponnese** <note>This refers to the "Return of the Heraclidae," a legendary migration/conquest of the Peloponnese by the descendants of Heracles.</note>. Heracles also showed gratitude to the three hundred and sixty men from **Cleonae**; for because they had joined him in his campaign against the **Molionidae** and died honorably in battle, he surrendered to them the honors at **Nemea** <note>The Nemean Games.</note>, which he had received from the people of Nemea when he overcame the lion that lived among them and was destroying their lands. **Menestheus**, having been persuaded concerning the **Tyndaridae** <note>Castor and Pollux, the twin brothers of Helen of Troy.</note>, was also not ungrateful; for after they expelled the son of Theseus and took Theseus’s mother **Aethra** captive, they gave the kingdom to Menestheus. For this reason, Menestheus was the first to call them both "Lords" <note>original: "ἄνακτάς" (Anaktas) – a title used for the Dioscuri in Athens.</note> and "Saviors." And **Darius**, the son of **Hystaspes**, having received a cloak from **Syloson** while he was still a private citizen, when he gained control of the kingdom, gave Syloson the rule of his fatherland, **Samos**. One might say this was a trade of "golden things for bronze" <note>original: "χρυσέα χαλκείων" – a proverbial expression from Homer's Iliad referring to an unequal but favorable exchange.</note>. ~

**W**hen the **Lacedaemonians** <note>The Spartans.</note> wished to destroy the city of the **Athenians**, they questioned the god, and he answered: "Do not move the common hearth of **Greece**." ~

### BOOK III ~

The Spartans, at any rate, when **Pausanias** <note>A Spartan general and regent who won the Battle of Plataea but was later accused of treason.</note> was found to be <term>medizing</term> <note>original: "μηδίζοντα" — siding with the Medes (Persians), which was considered the ultimate betrayal of the Greek city-states.</note>, not only killed him by starvation, but also cast his corpse outside of their borders, as **Epimedes** says ~

### On the Change of Fortune ~

Is there anyone who does not know that the shifts of fortune are swift and sharp-turning? The **Lacedaemonians** <note>The Spartans.</note>, for instance, once held dominion over the **Thebans**, but then they themselves were so thoroughly subdued by them that the Thebans not only reached the **Peloponnese** but even crossed the **Eurotas** <note>The primary river flowing through the city of Sparta; for an enemy to cross it was a historical humiliation for the Spartans.</note>. They laid waste to the entire Lacedaemonian territory and were very close to capturing the city itself, had not **Epaminondas** <note>The brilliant Theban general and statesman.</note> feared that all the Peloponnesians might unite in a single spirit and break away from the influence of **Sparta** ~

When **Dionysius the Tyrant** <note>Dionysius I of Syracuse, a famous and powerful ruler.</note> was placed under siege by the **Carthaginians** and saw no hope of safety appearing, he became despondent and felt ready to yield. However, one of his companions named **Ellopides** approached him and said, "**O Dionysius, tyranny is a beautiful shroud.**" <note>original: "καλὸν ἐντάφιον ἡ τυραννίς" — a famous ancient proverb suggesting it is better to die as a king than to live as a commoner in exile.</note> Ashamed by these words, he regained his resolve and, with only a few men, defeated many tens of thousands, and even increased the size of his empire ~

And **Amyntas the Macedonian**, having been driven out by the neighboring barbarians and having lost his rule, intended to abandon the country entirely; for he was content if he could only save himself. But when someone spoke to him, echoing the words of **Ellopides**, he seized a small stronghold, gathered a few soldiers, and recovered his kingdom ~

Because of the weakness of the animal, the **Egyptians** in their local language used to call **Ochus** <note>Artaxerxes III Ochus, the Persian King who reconquered Egypt.</note> "the Ass," mocking his character. In retaliation for this, he forcibly sacrificed the **Apis** <note>The sacred bull of Memphis, a living god to the Egyptians.</note> to an ass ~

**Dion**, the son of Hipparinus, while he was an exile because of **Dionysius**, defeated him later with only two hundred soldiers; thus, the man who was formerly the ruler himself became the exile ~

The **Syracusans**, with only nine <term>triremes</term> <note>A standard ancient warship with three banks of oars.</note>, fought against all one hundred and twenty ships of the **Carthaginians** and were overwhelmingly victorious ~

### On the Humility of Plato and the Ingratitude of Aristotle ~

**Plato**, the son of Ariston, shared a tent at **Olympia** with men who did not know who he was...

# BOOK FOUR

...[to those men], while he himself remained unknown to them. He so won them over and befriended them through their shared time together—eating with them simply and joining in all their activities—that it seemed to his companions that they had encountered a man of great <term>paideia</term> <note>original: "παιδείᾳ" — a word encompassing education, culture, and character development.</note>. Yet he mentioned neither the **Academy** <note>The famous school founded by Plato in Athens.</note> nor **Socrates**. He revealed only that his name was **Plato**. 

When they later came to **Athens**, he received them with great kindness. The strangers said, "O Plato, show us your namesake, the famous student of Socrates; take us to see his school, and introduce us to the man so that we might gain something from him." 

He, smiling gently as was his habit, said, "I am that very man." 

They were struck with amazement that they had been with such a man for so long and had not known it, as he had associated with them without vanity <note>original: "ἀτύφως" — literally "without smoke," meaning without the "smokescreen" of pride or pretension.</note> and without affectation, demonstrating that he was able to delight his companions even without his philosophical discourses.

***

Plato used to call **Aristotle** a "colt." What he intended by that name is clearly understood: a colt, once it has had its fill of its mother's milk, kicks its mother. Plato was thus hinting at a certain ingratitude in Aristotle. Indeed, Aristotle had received the greatest seeds and provisions for philosophy from Plato; then, having been filled with the best things and becoming unruly, he built a rival school <note>original: "ἀντιπερίβολον" — a rival enclosure or wall.</note> against him. He led his own followers in the **Peripatos** <note>The "Walkway" or school of Aristotle, so named because he often taught while walking.</note>, keeping his own companions and students, and strove to be a rival to Plato.

### How Pericles conducted himself toward the Athenian people

Was not **Pericles**, the son of Xanthippus, also a servant to the **People of Athens**? It seems so to me. At any rate, whenever he was about to come before the **Assembly**, he prayed that no word might escape him that would provoke the people to anger, as he sought to be persuasive and avoid an unwanted reputation.

### Concerning the "luxury" of Socrates

**Diogenes** <note>Diogenes the Cynic, known for his extreme poverty and criticism of others' lifestyles.</note> used to say that even Socrates himself lived in luxury. He claimed this because Socrates took care of his little house, his small couch, and even the sandals which Socrates occasionally wore.

### Concerning the image of Helen painted by Zeuxis

<note>Zeuxis was a celebrated Greek painter of the 5th century BCE, famous for his realism. His painting of Helen of Troy was said to be a composite of the five most beautiful virgins of Croton.</note>

# BOOK ONE

**Zeuxis of Heraclea** <note>A famous Greek painter of the late 5th century BCE, known for his realism.</note>, when he painted the portrait of **Helen** <note>The legendary Helen of Troy.</note>, made a great deal of money from that work. For he did not simply allow just anyone who wished to behold her freely and at random; rather, it was necessary to first pay a sum of silver and only then to view the painting. Because the man from Heraclea received a "fee" <note>original: "μίσθωμα" — a word often used for wages or the hire of a person.</note> for the painting, the Greeks of that time used to call that particular Helen "The Courtesan" <note>original: "ἑταίραν" (hetaira) — literally "companion," referring to a high-class prostitute.</note>.

->The opinion of Epicurus, and on happiness.<-

**Epicurus** the **Gargettian** <note>Referring to his home district, Gargettus, in Athens.</note> used to say that to the man for whom a little is not enough, nothing is enough. The same man said he was ready to compete even with **Zeus** for the prize of happiness, provided he had a barley cake and water. Since Epicurus praised these things, yet claimed to praise "pleasure," we shall eventually see what he truly meant <note>The author likely hints at the common misunderstanding that Epicureanism was about hedonism, whereas Epicurus focused on simple, stable pleasures.</note>.

->On the thrift and guarding of money.<-

Oftentimes, money that has been gathered one coin at a time with great labor flows away—according to **Archilochus** <note>An early Greek lyric poet.</note>—into the filthy lap of a prostitute. For just as a sea urchin <note>original: "ἐχῖνον" (echinos).</note> is easy to catch but difficult to hold onto <note>A reference to the urchin's spikes making it painful to grip firmly.</note>, so it is with money. And **Anaxagoras** <note>A Pre-Socratic philosopher.</note> says in his work *On Kingship* that it is difficult to gather money, but even more difficult to set a guard around it.

->On certain people who became musical or learned other things while in illness, and grew strong.<-

They say that **Hiero**, the tyrant of **Sicily** <note>Specifically Hiero I of Syracuse.</note>, was at first a private citizen and the most uncultured <term>amousos</term> <note>original: "ἀμουσότατον" — literally "without the Muses," meaning uneducated in the arts or lacking refinement.</note> of men, and that his rustic nature did not differ in the slightest from that of his brother, **Gelon**. But when it happened that he fell ill, he became the most cultured of men, for he devoted the leisure time afforded by his sickness to listening to learned discourses. Having thus become a lover of the arts, Hiero associated with **Simonides of Ceos**, **Pindar of Thebes**, and **Bacchylides of Iulis** <note>Three of the most famous lyric poets of the era.</note>; but his brother Gelon remained an uncultured man.

They say that **Ptolemy II** <note>Ptolemy II Philadelphus, the King of Egypt who founded the Library of Alexandria.</note> also became highly cultured after falling ill. And **Plato** says that **Theages** <note>A character in a Socratic dialogue often attributed to Plato.</note> took up philosophy for no other reason than his "sickliness" <note>original: "νοσοτροφίαν" — the state of being an invalid or being "nursed" by a chronic illness.</note>. For that illness, by barring him from a career in politics, drove him into the love of wisdom <term>philosophia</term>. And what man of sense would not also connect the illnesses of **Alcibiades**, **Critias**, **Pausanias** the **Lacedaemonian** <note>A Spartan general.</note>, and others? Alcibiades and Critias, so that they might not become listeners of **Socrates** without also being insolent—sometimes favoring the Spartans, and sometimes acting in the Boeotian manner, and...

<summary>This page contains a collection of anecdotes concerning the painter Zeuxis, the philosopher Epicurus, and the management of wealth, concluding with a discussion on how illness can lead individuals toward culture and philosophy.</summary>

### FOURTH [BOOK]. 27

...you are again playing the Thessalian <note>original: "θετταλίζῃ" — to "Thessalize" meant to be politically wavering or to favor the Persians, as the inhabitants of Thessaly were often accused of doing.</note>, and pleasing the Medes and Persians, yet not becoming a friend of **Pharnabazus** <note>A famous Persian satrap (governor) who dealt frequently with Greek city-states during the late 5th century BCE.</note>. The man from **Keos** <note>An island in the Cyclades.</note>, having become most tyrannical and murderous, caused his homeland much grief; and being himself hated, he ended his life.

**Straton**, son of **Corrhagus**, seems to have fallen ill at a very opportune time. For because of his long-standing inheritance and wealth, he was not accustomed to exercise. But having suffered a pain in his neck and requiring the treatment found in gymnasiums, he at first used them only for his health; however, advancing further into the skill and putting it into practice, he won at the **Olympics** in a single day—in both wrestling and the *pancration* <note>term: pancration — an ancient Greek "all-powers" martial art that combined elements of boxing, wrestling, and kicking.</note>—and he did so again at the next Olympiad, as well as at the **Nemean**, **Pythian**, and **Isthmian** games <note>These were the four "Crown Games" of ancient Greece, forming a grand slam of athletic achievement.</note>.

**Democrates** the Wrestler, having developed a disease in his feet, when he entered the competition and stood in the stadium, drew a circle around himself. He ordered his opponents to pull him outside of that line. They were defeated, being unable to move him; but he, never having shifted from his stance, departed having been firmly crowned as the victor.

->Characteristics of certain ancient men<-

If you pay attention to **Callias** <note>A wealthy Athenian socialite famous for hosting parties.</note>, Callias will turn you into a lover of drink; if to **Ismenias** the flute-player, a boaster; if to **Alcibiades** <note>An Athenian general known for his brilliant but extravagant and luxurious lifestyle.</note>, a gourmet; if to **Crobylus** <note>A nickname for the orator Hegesippus, who wore his hair in a "top-knot" (crobylus).</note>, a formidable speaker; if to **Demosthenes**, a rhetorician; if to **Epaminondas**, high-minded; if to **Agesilaus**, also virtuous; if to **Phocion**, also just; and if to **Aristides** and **Socrates**, wise.

->Wonders and opinions of Pythagoras<-

**Pythagoras** <note>The 6th-century BCE philosopher and mathematician.</note> taught men that he was born of higher origins than are found in the nature of mortals. For on the same day and at the same hour, he was seen in both **Metapontum** and **Croton** <note>Greek colonies in Southern Italy.</note>; and at **Olympia**, he displayed that one of his thighs was made of gold. He also reminded **Myllias** of Croton that he was actually **Midas**, son of **Gordias**, the Phrygian <note>A reference to Pythagoras’s doctrine of "metempsychosis" or the reincarnation of the soul.</note>, and he stroked a white eagle which had waited for him. Furthermore, while crossing the river **Cosa**, he was addressed by the river itself, which said to him, "Greetings, Pythagoras!"

He used to say that the leaf of the mallow is most sacred. He said that number is the wisest of all things; and second is the one who gave names to things. He explained that an earthquake was nothing other than a gathering of the dead. He claimed that the rainbow is the radiance of the sun...

[Text begins mid-sentence] ...to be heard, and the sound frequently falling on the ears was a voice that grew strong. It was not possible to question him about anything, or to ask further about what was said, but those present treated his words like a divine oracle <note>original: "χρησμῷ θείῳ"</note>. As he moved through the cities, a report spread that **Pythagoras** had arrived not to teach, but to heal. This same Pythagoras commanded his followers to abstain from eating hearts, and from white roosters, and above all from anything that died of itself <note>term: carrion — meat from animals that died of natural causes or disease rather than being slaughtered.</note>, and not to use public baths, nor to walk on the main highways; for it was unclear if those places were pure.

### Concerning the respect and honor shown by Dionysius toward Plato.

<margin>Book 4</margin>
When **Plato** arrived in Sicily by invitation, after **Dionysius** had sent many successive letters, the younger Dionysius himself brought him in upon a chariot. Dionysius acted as the driver, while he had **Aristippus** <note>A philosopher from Cyrene and a pupil of Socrates, known for his presence at the Syracusan court despite the tyrant's volatility.</note> standing beside him. At that time, they say a witty man of Syracuse, who was well-read in **Homer**, upon seeing this sight, recited those famous lines from the *Iliad*, altering them slightly:

> The beechen axle groaned aloud
> Beneath the weight; for it bore a terrible god, and a most excellent man.
<note>original: "Μέγα δ' ἔβραχε φήγινος ἄξων / Βριθοσύνῃ, δεινὸν γὰρ ἄγε βροτὸν, ἄνδρα τ' ἄριστον." This is a parody of Iliad 5.838-839. In the original, the axle groans because it carries the goddess Athena and the hero Diomedes. Here, the "terrible god" refers to the tyrant Dionysius and the "excellent man" to Plato.</note>

Dionysius, who was usually suspicious of everyone, nevertheless felt such respect for Plato that he allowed only him to speak his mind, even though he had pushed the famous companions of **Dion** to the utmost extremes.

### That Philip honored education, and concerning Aristotle.

**Philip of Macedon** was said to be not only skilled in warfare and a powerful speaker, but he also honored education most graciously. At any rate, by providing **Aristotle** with unstinting wealth, he became the cause of much varied experience, and especially of the knowledge concerning animals. The son of **Nicomachus** <note>Aristotle's father was Nicomachus, a physician.</note> reaped the fruits of his research into their history because of Philip's abundance. Philip also honored Plato and **Theophrastus**.

### Concerning Democritus, and the reputation concerning him, Theophrastus, Hippocrates, and others.

A story tells that **Democritus of Abdera** was wise in all other matters and particularly desired to remain hidden <note>This refers to the Epicurean/Democritean ideal of "living unnoticed" (lathe biōsas).</note>, making this his great aim. For this reason, he traveled through much of the earth. He came even to the **Chaldaeans**, and to **Babylon**, and to the **Magi**, and the wise men <note>original: "σοφιστὰς" — sages or teachers of wisdom.</note> of the **Indians**. He divided the inheritance from his father **Damasippus** into three parts...

[Text begins mid-sentence] ...among the three brothers by themselves. Taking only the silver as travel-money <note>original: "ἐφόδιον"</note> for his journey, he left the rest of the estate to his brothers. For this reason, **Theophrastus** praised him, saying that he gathered the best "provisions" — better even than **Menelaus** or **Odysseus**. For those men wandered precisely like Phoenician merchants, no different at all, collecting mere wealth; their travels and their means of acquisition had only this as their pretext.

### Why the Abderites called Democritus "Philosophy," and on his public speeches.

**D**emocritus used to laugh at everyone and say they were mad, which is why his fellow citizens called him "Gelasinos" <note>term: The Laugher — from the Greek word "gelao," to laugh.</note>. The same people say that **Hippocrates**, at his first meeting regarding **Democritus**, initially got the impression that he was insane; but as their association progressed, he came to admire the man exceedingly.

**T**hey say that although **Hippocrates** was a Dorian <note>The Dorians were one of the major ethnic groups of ancient Greece, typically speaking a distinct dialect.</note>, for the sake of **Democritus**, he wrote his works in the Ionic dialect <note>original: "ἰάδι φωνῇ" — Ionic was the dialect of philosophy and science in this period, associated with the region of Ionia.</note>.

### The favorites of Socrates and Plato.

**I**t is said that the favorite of **Socrates** was **Alcibiades**, and of **Plato**, **Dion**. Likewise, Dion indeed benefited from his lover <note>note: The term "favorite" (paidika) refers to the mentor-student relationships common in Classical Athens, which often carried romantic or pedagogical connotations.</note>.

### Concerning the luxury of the Athenians.

**T**he Athenians of old used to wear purple-dyed <note>original: "ἁλουργῆ"</note> garments and put on embroidered tunics. They would tie up the hair on their heads in buns, fastening them with golden cicadas <note>term: Golden Cicadas — a "tettix," an ancient Athenian hair ornament that symbolized their claim to be "autochthonous" or native to the soil, just like the cicada.</note>, and they went out wearing other dangling golden ornaments. Their servants carried folding chairs <note>original: "ὀκλαδίας... δίφρους"</note> behind them, so they would not have to sit down just anywhere by chance. It is clear that their tables and the rest of their lifestyle were also quite luxurious. Yet, being men of this sort, they won the Battle of Marathon <note>note: The author is highlighting the paradox that the Athenians were both highly refined and formidable soldiers.</note>.

### Concerning certain spendthrifts.

**I**t is said that **Pericles**, **Callias** (the son of Hipponikos), and **Nicias**, having squandered their wealth on profligacy and a life of pleasure, reached a point of extreme recklessness. When their money finally failed them, the three of them toasted one another with a final drink of hemlock <note>term: Hemlock — a poisonous plant used in ancient Athens for executions and suicides.</note>, departing this life as if they were leaving a banquet.

### How friendships are most often dissolved.

**L**eoprepes the Cean, the father of **Simonides**, happened to be sitting in a wrestling school one day, when he saw some youths who were on very friendly terms with one another...

# BOOK I

[Text begins mid-sentence] ...disposed, he asked the man how their friendship might best endure. He said, "If you each yield to one another’s anger, and do not advance together in passion, but instead approach one another in turn."

### Concerning the strange madness of Thrasyllus

**Thrasyllus** of Aexone <note>Aexone was a coastal district, or deme, of ancient Attica, south of Athens.</note> suffered a strange and novel madness. Leaving the city and going down to the Piraeus <note>The primary port city of Athens.</note>, and living there, he believed that all the ships coming into harbor were his own. He would register them and then send them out again. He took immense joy in those coming to port and entering the harbor. He spent a long time living in this illness. But when his brother returned from Sicily, he turned him over to a doctor to be cured, and so he was freed from the disease. He often recalled his time spent in madness and said he was never so happy as when he rejoiced in the safe arrival of those ships which did not belong to him at all.

### Concerning Electra

Xanthus the lyric poet—who was older than **Stesichorus of Himera**—says that **Electra**, the daughter of **Agamemnon**, did not have this name at first, but was called **Laodice**. However, after **Agamemnon** was murdered and **Aegisthus** married **Clytemnestra** and became king, the Argives called her **Electra** because she remained "unwed" <note>original: "ἄλεκτρον" (alektron)</note> and was growing old as a virgin, for she had no share in a husband and had not experienced the marriage bed <note>term: *lektros* — the marriage bed. The name is presented here as a pun: *A-lektra* meaning "the bed-less one."</note>.

### Concerning the gift of Pamphaes and about Diotimus

That **Pamphaes** the Prienean <note>original: "Γεινηνὸς" — likely a corruption of "Πριηνεὺς" (Prienean). Pamphaes of Priene was a known benefactor of Croesus.</note> gave thirty minae <note>term: *mina* — a unit of currency equal to 100 drachmae; thirty minae was a significant sum, enough to support a family for years.</note> to **Croesus the Lydian** while his father was still alive. When **Croesus** took up the throne, he sent back to him a wagon half-full of silver.

That **Diogenes**, receiving a small coin from **Diotimus of Carystus**, said: "*May the gods give you as much as you desire in your heart, O man, and a home.*" <note>Diogenes is quoting Homer's *Odyssey* here, but with a cynical edge.</note> It seemed, however, that **Diotimus** was somewhat effeminate <note>original: "μαλθακώτερος" — literally 'softer'.</note>.

### That Pherecydes suffered from the lice-disease because of his atheism

**Pherecydes of Syros** <note>A 6th-century BCE thinker often credited with writing the first prose book in Greek.</note> ended his life in the most painful way of all men, his entire body being consumed by lice. When his appearance became shameful, he avoided the company of the living. Whenever someone approached and asked how he was doing, he would stick his finger through the hole in the door—stripped of flesh—and...

...marks on the flesh, he claimed that his whole body was in such a state. The children of the inhabitants of Delos say that the god <note>Referring to Apollo, the patron deity of the island of Delos.</note> smiled upon him in Delos whenever he performed his hymns there with his disciples. They say he spoke many other things concerning his own wisdom—which they even used to sing—claiming that he sacrificed to none of the gods, and yet lived no less pleasantly and free from pain than those who sacrifice hecatombs <note>A hecatomb was a grand public sacrifice, originally consisting of one hundred oxen.</note>. ### That Alexander ridiculously believed 
### there were infinite worlds

I cannot convince myself not to laugh at **Alexander**, the son of Philip. It is said that upon hearing from the writings of **Democritus** <note>Democritus was a philosopher known for the atomic theory and the idea of "innumerable worlds." He was also called the "Laughing Philosopher."</note> that there were infinite worlds, he was distressed because he was not yet master of even this single one. As for why **Democritus** laughed at him—a man who made a habit of laughing at human folly—what need is there to say?

# AELIAN’S HISTORICAL MISCELLANY
## BOOK FIVE

### That Tachos died because of a more luxurious diet

**Tachos the Egyptian** <note>Also known as Pharaoh Teos of the 30th Dynasty (r. 362–360 BCE).</note>, as long as he followed his native diet and lived simply, spent his time as the healthiest of men. But when he arrived among the Persians and fell into their luxurious way of life, he could not bear the unaccustomed richness of their food. He ended his life due to dysentery, having exchanged luxury for death.

### How Pherecydes died

**Pherecydes**, the teacher of **Pythagoras**, fell into a sickness. First, he broke out in a hot, sticky sweat, similar to mucus; then it became savage, and later he suffered from a louse infestation <note>original: "ἐφθειρίασε" (ephtheiriase). This "disease of lice" was a common trope in ancient biography for the deaths of notable figures.</note>. As his flesh dissolved into lice, a wasting away spread over him, and in this manner, he departed this life.

### Concerning the Pillars of Heracles

**Aristotle** says that the landmarks now called the **Pillars of Heracles** <note>The Strait of Gibraltar, marking the limit of the known world to the ancient Greeks.</note>, before they were named—

<meta>This page continues the "Historical Miscellany" by Aelian. # BOOK ELEVEN

...we know it is said that they were once called the Pillars of Briareus <note>original: "Βριαρέω καλεῖσθαι." Briareus was one of the mythological "Hundred-Handed" giants (Hekatoncheires).</note>. But after **Heracles** cleansed the land and the sea and became an undisputed benefactor of mankind, the people, in his honor, disregarded the memory of Briareus and renamed them the **Pillars of Heracles**.

->Concerning the plants blossoming in Delos.<-

There is a story among the people of **Delos** that certain plants—an olive tree and a palm—blossomed on the island. They say that **Leto** <note>The mother of the twin gods Apollo and Artemis.</note> grasped these plants and immediately gave birth. How could anyone fail to tell of this?

->Concerning the poverty and greatness of mind of Epaminondas.<-

<margin>5</margin>**Epaminondas** <note>A famous Theban general and statesman known for his integrity and for leading Thebes to dominance over Sparta.</note> owned only one cheap cloak, and it was always dirty. Whenever he sent it to be cleaned, he was forced to stay at home because he lacked another garment to wear. Despite living in such poverty, when the King of the **Persians** sent him a great sum of gold, he did not accept it. In my view, the one who did not take the gift possessed a greater mind than the one who offered it.

->Concerning the voluntary death of Calanus.<-

<margin>1</margin>It is also worth praising the end of **Calanus the Indian**. It is said that his departure from life was truly admirable. Calanus, an Indian sage <term>sophist</term> <note>original: "σοφιστὴς." Here, it refers to the "Gymnosophists" or "naked philosophers" of India whom Alexander encountered.</note>, bid a long farewell to **Alexander**, the **Macedonians**, and to life itself. When he wished to release himself from the bonds of the body, a pyre was prepared in the Pasian suburb of **Babylon**. The wood was dry and selected for its fragrance, consisting of portions of cedar, reed, cypress, myrtle, and laurel. After performing his usual exercises—for he was a runner—he ascended to the middle of the pyre and stood there, crowned with a wreath of reed. The Sun shone down upon him, and he worshipped it. This was the signal for the Macedonians to light the pyre. Once it was ignited, he was surrounded by the flames but stood perfectly still, and did not move until he was consumed. They say that even **Alexander** was struck with amazement and remarked that Calanus had defeated greater opponents than he had; for Alexander had struggled against **Porus**, **Taxiles**, and **Darius**, but Calanus had struggled against pain and death.

->Concerning Anacharsis.<-

<margin>70</margin>While the **Scythians** wander extensively within their own land, **Anacharsis**, being a wise man, took his wandering even further. He came to **Greece**, and **Solon** <note>An Athenian statesman and one of the Seven Sages of Greece.</note> held him in great admiration.

# BOOK FIVE

<meta>catchword: θ ιι</meta>

->How some bore mockery:<-

**M**ockery and insults, it seems to me, have no power if they encounter a steadfast mind—they are simply dissolved. However, if they strike a base and lowly spirit, they gain strength. In such cases, they have not only caused frequent grief but have even led to death. Let these men be proof of this: **Socrates**, on the one hand, laughed when he was being mocked in the comedies <note>original: "κωμῳδούμενος." This refers to the satirical plays of Aristophanes, such as *The Clouds*, which ridiculed Socrates.</note>, but **Poliagrus** <note>A man otherwise little-known to history, mentioned here as a contrast to Socrates' stoicism.</note> hanged himself.

---

->Concerning Aristotle:<-

**A**ristotle, having squandered his father’s wealth, rushed into military service. When he fared poorly in that profession, he appeared next as a seller of medicines <term>pharmakopōlēs</term> <note>original: "φαρμακοπώλης." In antiquity, this often implied a street-vendor of herbal remedies, a trade much lower in status than that of a philosopher.</note>. But after drifting into the **Peripatos** <note>The "Walking Place" or Lyceum, the school of philosophy in Athens.</note> and listening to the lectures, he found he was naturally superior to many. From then on, he changed his habits and acquired the character he had previously lacked.

---

->The number of ships and the count of hoplites which the<-
->Athenians lost:<-

**T**he **Athenians** always worked with great diligence to build their fleet. Over time, they experienced both great successes and heavy failures. They lost two hundred triremes <note>original: "τριήρας." A trireme was the standard warship of the era, powered by three banks of oars.</note> in **Egypt** along with their crews; one hundred and fifty around **Cyprus**; two hundred and forty in **Sicily**; and two hundred at the **Hellespont**. As for their heavy infantry <term>hoplites</term>, forty thousand were lost in **Sicily**, and one thousand perished at **Chaeronea** <note>The Battle of Chaeronea in 338 BCE, where Philip II of Macedon defeated the Greek city-states.</note>.

---

->Concerning the cruelty of the King of the Thracians<-
->toward his own sons:<-

**T**he King of the **Thracians**, whose name is said to be **Teres**, fled to **Mount Rhodope** when **Xerxes** <note>The Persian King who invaded Greece in 480 BCE.</note> marched against **Greece**. He advised his six sons not to join the campaign against Greece, for it was clear the man was a friend to the Greeks. However, they did not obey him. Upon their return, he had all of them blinded, for they had not acted like Greeks <note>original: "μὴ ποιήσας ἑλληνικά." In this context, it implies they failed to respect the Hellenic values of freedom and resistance to Persian tyranny.</note>.

---

->How Demades was fined for voting<-
->Alexander to be a god:<-

**I** cannot help but love the **Athenians** for this. When the assembly was being held, **Demades** <note>An Athenian politician and orator known for his pragmatism and cooperation with Macedon.</note> came forward and proposed a decree to recognize **Alexander** as the thirteenth god. The people, unable to bear the extreme nature of this impiety <term>asebeia</term>, imposed a fine on Demades of one hundred talents. This was because he had enrolled Alexander, who was but a mortal, among the **Olympians**.

->BOOK<-

### That the Athenians are quick to embrace political change.

<margin>H</margin>The Athenians, as it turns out, were remarkably fickle <note>original: "εὐτράπελοι" — meaning versatile or prone to turning easily.</note> in their forms of government and more inclined toward revolutions than any other people. For a time, they bore the rule of Kings moderately under Cecrops, Erechtheus, Theseus, and the descendants of Codrus. They then experienced Tyranny <term>tyrannis</term> <note>original: "τυραννίδος." In the Greek sense, this refers to a ruler who seized power unconstitutionally, not necessarily a "tyrant" in the modern sense.</note> under the Pisistratids. They made use of an Aristocracy until the time of the Four Hundred. Later, ten citizens were chosen each year to rule the city. Finally, they fell into a worse state of anarchy during the establishment of the Thirty <note>The "Thirty Tyrants," a pro-Spartan oligarchy installed after the Peloponnesian War.</note>. Whether one ought to praise this ever-shifting way of life, I myself do not know.

### Attic Law concerning the burial of bodies and the slaughter of oxen.

<margin>N</margin>This also was an Athenian law: that whoever should happen upon the unburied body of a human being must, by all means, cast earth upon it, and they must bury it facing the west. This custom was strictly observed among them. Furthermore, they held that a plow-ox that had labored under the yoke with the plow or the wagon should not be slaughtered, because such an animal is effectively a farmer <note>original: "γεωργός"</note> and a partner in the labors of mankind.

### The Courts in Athens concerning homicide.

<margin>O</margin>There were specific Athenian courts for various crimes: for those who killed with premeditation, the court was at the **Areopagus** <note>The "Hill of Ares," the most prestigious and ancient court in Athens.</note>. For those who killed involuntarily, the court was at the **Palladium**. For those who admitted to killing but argued that it was done justly, the trials were held at the **Delphinium**.

### That a child was condemned to death for sacrilege.

<margin>O</margin>When a golden leaf fell from the crown of the statue of **Artemis**, a young child picked it up. Some people overlooked the act, but the judges placed toys, knucklebones, and the golden leaf before the child. When he reached for the gold again, they executed him as a temple-robber <term>hierosylos</term> <note>original: "ἱεροσύλην." Sacrilege or temple-robbing was a capital offense in Athens.</note>. They did not grant him mercy because of his age, but punished him for the nature of the deed.

### Concerning the religious scrupulousness of the Athenians.

<margin>O</margin>The Athenians were so possessed by religious scrupulousness <note>original: "δεισιδαιμονίας" — often translated as "superstition," but here implying an extreme, fearful devotion to religious law.</note> that if someone cut a small wooden tablet from a hero’s shrine, they would put him to death. Indeed, when **Atarbius** struck and killed the sacred sparrow of **Asclepius** <note>The god of healing; his temples often housed sacred animals.</note>, the Athenians did not overlook the matter with impunity, but executed Atarbius, and it did not seem—

### BOOK FIVE

[...they accepted] neither excuse nor pardon, nor madness, placing the things of God <note>original: "τὰ τοῦ θεοῦ" — referring to divine law or religious purity</note> before both of these. For though the act was said to be involuntary, they <note>the judges</note> still saw fit to find fault and pass judgment.

->**Concerning a pregnant woman condemned to death.**<-

The Council of the Areopagus <note>The high court of Athens, which met on the "Hill of Ares" and handled cases of homicide and religious matters.</note>, having arrested a certain poisoner <note>original: "φαρμακίδα" — can refer to a sorceress, a poisoner, or a dealer in drugs.</note> whom they were about to put to death, did not execute her until she had given birth. Because she was pregnant when she was seized, they spared the innocent <note>original: "ἀναίτιον"</note> infant from the sentence, judging that the cause for death belonged to the mother alone.

->**How Aeschylus was saved when judged for impiety.**<-

When Aeschylus the tragic poet was being judged for impiety <note>original: "ἀσεβείας." Aeschylus was famously accused of revealing the secrets of the Eleusinian Mysteries in one of his plays.</note> regarding a certain drama, and the Athenians were ready to stone him to death, his youngest brother Amynias stepped forward. Drawing back his cloak, Amynias revealed his arm, which was missing its hand. It happened that Amynias had distinguished himself for valor at the Battle of Salamis, where he lost his hand and was the first of the Athenians to receive the prize for bravery. When the judges saw the man’s suffering, they were reminded of his great deeds and acquitted Aeschylus.

->**Concerning the fast of the Tarentines and its origin.**<-

When the people of Tarentum were besieged by the Romans and were on the verge of being taken by hunger, the people of Rhegium passed a decree to fast one day out of every ten, and to give that portion of food to the Tarentines. Having been saved by this relief, the Tarentines still remember that suffering and celebrate a festival which they call "The Fast."

->**That Medea did not kill her own children.**<-

There is a certain account claiming that the rumor against Medea is false, and that she did not kill her children, but rather the Corinthians did. They say this story is a myth told in favor of the woman from Colchis <note>Medea.</note>, and that the playwright Euripides composed his drama <note>The play *Medea*.</note> this way because the Corinthians requested it. Thus, the lie prevailed over the truth because of the poet’s skill. They say that because of the daring crime committed against the children, the Corinthians—even to this day—offer sacrifices to the dead <note>original: "ἐναγίζουσι"</note> children, as if paying a tribute to them.

# AELIAN’S HISTORICAL MISCELLANY

## BOOK SIX

->On the anger, inhumanity, insolence, injustice, and arrogance of some toward others.<-

---

<margin>1</margin>
When the Athenians conquered the Chalcidians, they divided the land known as the "horse-rearing" territory <note>original: "ἱππόβοτον" — a fertile region of Euboea famous for pasturing the horses of the aristocracy</note> into forty allotments. They dedicated sacred precincts to Athena in the place called Lelantos, while the rest of the land was leased out. They erected pillars <note>original: "στήλας"</note> near the Royal Stoa <note>the *Stoa Basileios*, an important public building in the Athenian marketplace where laws were displayed</note> which recorded the lease agreements. They also bound their captives in chains, yet even this did not extinguish their wrath against the Chalcidians.

<margin>2</margin>
When the Lacedaemonians <note>the Spartans</note> conquered the Messenians, they took for themselves half of everything produced in Messenia. They forced free women to march in funeral processions and weep for the dead, even for those who were strangers and not related to them at all. Some of the men they left behind to work as farmers <note>original: "γεωργεῖν" — referring to the helot system of forced agricultural labor</note>, while others they sold into slavery or put to death.
The Athenians, too, acted with insolence <note>original: "ὕβριζον" — from *hubris*, meaning outrageous or insulting behavior</note> when they met with good fortune, failing to bear their success with moderation. For instance, they forced the daughters of the resident aliens <note>original: "μετοίκων" — *metics*, foreigners living in Athens who had no citizen rights</note> to carry sunshades <note>original: "σκιαδηφορεῖν"</note> for the Athenian girls during processions; they forced the alien women to serve the Athenian women, and the alien men to carry trays <note>original: "σκαφηφορεῖν" — a ritualized form of humiliation to show the aliens' subordinate status</note>.

<margin>3</margin>
When the Sicyonians captured Pellene, they placed the wives and daughters of the Pellenians in brothels <note>original: "ἐπ᾿ οἰκήματος" — literally "in a building," a standard euphemism for a house of prostitution</note>. By the gods of the Greeks! These acts are most savage and, in my memory, are not considered honorable even among the barbarians.

<margin>5</margin>
When Philip <note>Philip II of Macedon, father of Alexander the Great</note> won the Battle of Chaeronea <note>The decisive battle in 338 BCE where Macedonia established hegemony over the Greek city-states</note>, both he and all the Macedonians were uplifted by the result, while the Greeks were terribly dismayed. The various cities surrendered to him, bringing gifts of submission. This was done by the Thebans, Megarians, Corinthians, Achaeans, Eleans, and all the Euboeans living on the coast. However, Philip did not keep the agreements he made with them...

...but he enslaved everyone, having performed unjust and lawless acts.

->On the bravery of the son of Armatidas<-

The son of Armatidas, coming as an ally to the Athenians along with other citizens, fought excellently at first. However, when his weapons were overwhelmed <note>original: "κατακαλυφθέντων" — likely meaning his shield or armor was rendered useless or stripped away during the heat of the fight</note>, he continued to struggle, fighting with his bare hands, and ended his life without glory <note>original: "ἀκλεῶς" — meaning he did not receive the formal recognition a soldier with proper equipment would have</note>. Therefore, people later spoke of the young man, praising him in the Homeric style; yet his actual name was not carefully recorded, and so it remains unknown.

->On the boy Isadas<-

When Isadas was still a boy, and the law had not yet called him to take up arms, he leaped out from the gymnasium <note>the place where youths trained and exercised naked</note> and entered into danger. The Lacedaemonians <note>the Spartans</note> crowned him with a wreath for his courage, but they also fined him because he rushed at the enemy before the required age and without the customary armor of his country.

->On those who were betrothed to the daughter of Lysander<-

When Lysander <note>a famous Spartan admiral and general</note> died, it was discovered that he was poor. A man who had been betrothed to Lysander’s daughter while he was still alive drew back from the engagement once the girl was left an orphan. Because it was revealed that Lysander had been poor at the end of his life, the suitor claimed he would no longer take her as his wife. For this, the Ephors <note>the five high-ranking Spartan magistrates who oversaw the kings and the law</note> fined the man. They judged that he did not think like a Spartan, nor like a Greek at all, for he forgot his dead friend and valued the greed for wealth above loyalty.

->On the Athenian ambassadors<-

The Athenians sent ambassadors to Arcadia. When these men took a different road than the one they had been commanded to take, the Athenians put them to death—even though they had been successful in their mission <note>the Athenian democracy was notoriously strict, sometimes executing successful generals or officials for technical violations of their orders</note>.

->Spartan laws<-

Was this not also a Spartan law? That among the Spartans, any man who fathered three sons had an exemption from guard duty, and if he fathered five, he was released from all public services <note>original: "λειτουργιῶν" — *liturgies*, which were expensive public duties or taxes imposed on wealthy citizens</note>. It was also the custom of the country to sleep on the ground. Furthermore, it was not permitted for a Spartan man to know a menial trade <note>term: *banausou* — manual labor or craftsmanship, which the Spartans considered beneath the dignity of a full citizen, leaving such work to the *perioikoi* (dwellers-around)</note>. It was mandatory to wear crimson cloaks during battles. This was done to give their appearance a certain dignity, and also so that the color of any blood flowing from their wounds would not be easily seen by their opponents, making the sight more intense and especially terrifying.

<summary>This page contains anecdotes demonstrating the strictness of ancient Greek life, including the Spartan balance between rewarding bravery and punishing law-breaking, and the Athenian insistence on absolute obedience to state orders.</summary>

# BOOK SIX

<meta>catchword: pericles</meta>

It was not permitted for a Spartan man even to strip the enemy of their spoils. Those who fought well and excelled in bravery were crowned with olive wreaths or other branches and were honored with praises. But those who excelled to the highest degree were buried with great honor, with purple cloaks <note>original: "φοινικίδες" — the distinctive crimson military cloaks of the Spartans</note> draped over them.

### Concerning the earthquake that fell upon Sparta
When the Lacedaemonians <note>the Spartans</note> forcibly removed all the servants who were suppliants at Tainaron <note>a sanctuary of Poseidon at Cape Matapan, where it was a sacrilege to harm those seeking refuge</note> and slaughtered them—these were servants of the Helots <term>Helots: the state-owned serfs of Sparta who often rebelled against their harsh masters</term>—Poseidon became enraged. An earthquake fell upon Sparta and shook the city so violently that only five houses were left standing in the entire city.

### Concerning the assassination of Artaxerxes
They say that when Bagoas the eunuch, who was an Egyptian, plotted against Artaxerxes—the one nicknamed Ochus <note>Artaxerxes III, known for his cruelty</note>—the king was assassinated, and his body was chopped into pieces and thrown to the cats. Someone else was buried in the royal tombs in his place to maintain appearances. Many acts of sacrilege <note>original: "θεοσυλίαι" — literally "temple-robbing" or insults to the gods</note> are attributed to Ochus, especially regarding Egyptian customs. Bagoas was not satisfied with merely killing Ochus; he even made knife handles from the king's thigh bones, demonstrating his murderous hatred through these objects. He hated him because, while in Egypt, Ochus had killed the Apis <note>the sacred bull of Memphis, a central deity in Egyptian religion</note>, behaving with the same character as Cambyses <note>a previous Persian king who also allegedly insulted the Egyptian gods</note> had done before.

### Concerning the treasure sought by the Delphians at Pytho
Since the fame of the sanctuary reached the Delphians through the poems of Homer, claiming that the shrine of Apollo possessed ancient wealth—specifically in those verses:
> Nor all the wealth that the stone threshold of the Archer <note>original: "ἀφήτορος" — an epithet of Apollo meaning the one who delivers oracles or the archer</note>
> Phoebus Apollo holds within, in rocky Pytho <note>the ancient name for Delphi</note>,
The Delphians attempted to dig. The plan was based more on suspicion than fact, yet they set to work. However, when earthquakes occurred around the oracle, it served as a sign that they should stop their search.

### The law concerning citizens established by Pericles
While serving as general for the Athenians, Pericles proposed a law that if a person was not born from two citizen parents, they should have no share in the state <note>this restricted Athenian citizenship to those of pure Athenian descent on both sides</note>. Yet, a divine retribution <term>Nemesis: the personification of divine resentment and the punisher of hubris</term> followed him because of this law. For his two legitimate sons, Paralus and Xanthippus, died during the public distress of the plague, and there was left only the...

# BOOK SIX

...honorably according to the laws; however, those who did not share in the rights of the state according to the ancestral law <note>Aelian refers here to Pericles' law of 451/450 BC, which restricted Athenian citizenship to those born of two Athenian parents. This resulted in many being sold into slavery or losing their status.</note>.

### Concerning Gelon's desire to surrender his authority

Gelon, after defeating the Carthaginians <note>referring to the Battle of Himera in 480 BC</note>, brought all of Sicily under his control. Later, coming into the marketplace unarmed <note>original: "γυμνὸς" — literally "naked," but in this context meaning without his royal robes, armor, or bodyguard</note>, he declared that he would return the sovereign power to the citizens. They, however, did not wish it—clearly because they had experienced his rule and found him more inclined toward the people than was typical for the absolute power of monarchs. For this reason, a statue of him stands in the temple of the Sicilian Hera, showing him unarmed, and the inscription of Gelon confirmed the deed.

### Concerning the prosperity of Dionysius, and what kind of end he met

Dionysius the Second <note>The tyrant of Syracuse (r. 367–344 BC), known for his spectacular fall from power</note> held an authority that was very well fortified in the following manner: first, he possessed no fewer than four hundred ships—both <term>hexeres and penteres: massive warships with six and five banks of rowers respectively</term>—and an infantry force of one hundred thousand men, along with nine thousand cavalry. The city of the Syracusans was adorned with the greatest harbors and surrounded by a very high wall. He had equipment ready for five hundred ships. <margin>50</margin> There was also stored for him grain amounting to one million <term>medimnoi: a Greek unit of dry measure, roughly 55 liters</term>, and an armory filled with equipment for a hundred ships, as well as swords, spears, many greaves, breastplates, and <term>catapults: ancient engines for hurling stones or bolts</term>. Indeed, the catapult was an invention of Dionysius himself. He also had a vast number of allies. Relying on these things, Dionysius thought he possessed a rule bound with adamant <note>original: "ἀδάμαντι δεδεμένην" — a famous expression suggesting his power was as unbreakable as the hardest mythical metal</note>.

But this man first killed his brothers, then saw his sons violently slaughtered and his daughters violated and then murdered while naked. Not one of his descendants received a proper burial; some were burned alive, while others were chopped into pieces and cast into the sea. This happened after he was ousted by Dion <note>his former advisor and brother-in-law</note> and when Hipparinus <note>misspelled "Isparinus" in the transcription</note> attacked the government. He himself ended his life as an old man living in extreme poverty.

Theopompus <note>a Greek historian of the 4th century BC</note> says that from his excessive drinking of unmixed wine, his eyesight was so ruined that he could barely see. He spent his time in barbershops, acting as a jester, and continued to live disgracefully in the very heart of Greece <note>referring to his exile in Corinth</note>, enduring a most painful life. He served as no ordinary example to mankind regarding the need for self-control and the orderly arrangement of fortune, or...

# BOOK TWELVE

...by the change of Dionysius from such great heights to such humble circumstances. <note>This concludes the previous chapter regarding Dionysius II, who fell from being a powerful tyrant to living as a schoolteacher in Corinth.</note>

### Concerning tyrannies that did not last until the third generation in Greece

It is said that the Divine Power <note>original: "τὸ Δαιμόνιον" — referring to fate or a guiding divine agency</note> does not allow tyrannies to produce many generations. Instead, it either wipes them out immediately, in the manner of a pine tree <note>In the ancient world, it was believed that if a pine tree was cut down, it would never sprout again from the stump, making it a symbol of total extinction</note>, or it destroys the children and descendants. However, the Greeks remember only these few as having lasted until the third generation: the tyranny of Gelon in Sicily, that of the Leuconids around the Bosporus <note>The Cimmerian Bosporus, near the modern-day Crimea</note>, and that of the Cypselids in Corinth.

### Concerning the plot against Darius

I hear of this most gentle deed of Darius, the son of Hystaspes <note>Darius I, known as Darius the Great, who ruled the Persian Empire at its height</note>. Aribazes the Hyrcanian <note>Hyrcania was a satrapy of the Persian Empire located on the southeastern coast of the Caspian Sea</note> plotted against him, along with other prominent men among the Persians. The plot was intended to take place during a hunt. Having learned of this beforehand, Darius did not falter, but commanded them to take up their arms; then, while they were on horseback, he ordered them to prepare their weapons for use. Seeing a demonstration of their intent, he said: "Why then do you not do that which you set out to do?" They, seeing the steady gaze of the man, abandoned their impulse. Fear seized them to such an extent that they dropped their spears, dismounted their horses, and bowed down to Darius, surrendering themselves to
->whatever he might wish<-

AELIAN'S HISTORICAL MISCELLANY
B ii

## BOOK SEVEN

### Concerning Semiramis, and how she seized the empire of the Assyrians

<margin>S</margin>
Semiramis the Assyrian is celebrated in various ways by different authors. She was the most beautiful of all women, even if she used her beauty quite simply. When she was summoned to the King of the Assyrians because of the fame of her beauty, he was struck by the woman and fell deeply in love with her. She then requested from the King that she might receive the royal robes as a gift and rule over Asia for five days, doing whatever she pleased during that time. She did not fail in her request. Once the King had seated her upon the throne and she realized she held everything in her hands and by her will, she commanded the bodyguards to kill the King. In this way, she seized the empire of the Assyrians. Deimon <note>A little-known historian, likely of the 4th century BCE, who wrote on Persian and Middle Eastern history</note> is the one who relates these things.

### Concerning Straton, and the luxury of Nicocles

<margin>S</margin>
Straton of Sidon <note>A king of the Phoenician city-state of Sidon in the 4th century BCE</note> is said to have surpassed all men in luxury and his manner of living. Theopompus the Chian <note>A prominent Greek historian known for his biting critiques and descriptions of royal decadence</note> says that he modeled his entire life after the lifestyle of the Phaeacians <note>In Homer’s *Odyssey*, the Phaeacians were a mythical people famous for their effortless, banquet-filled lives</note>, which Homer, in his great-minded way, portrayed so dramatically in his songs. For Straton, there was not just one singer to perform during his dinner or play the flute for him; rather, there were many female musicians, flute-players, and courtesans of striking beauty, along with dancers. He also competed fiercely in these matters with Nicocles the Cypriot <note>Nicocles was the King of Salamis in Cyprus and a contemporary of Straton; both were famous for their rivalry in extravagance</note>. For each of them, hearing from travelers about the arrangements at the other’s court, would strive to outdo his rival in royal expenditure. However, they did not continue in these pursuits to the end; for both of them met their end by a violent death.

### A speech of Aristippus to restrain grief

<margin>O</margin>
When certain of his companions were mourning most heavily, Aristippus <note>A pupil of Socrates and the founder of the Cyrenaic school of philosophy, which focused on the mastery of pleasure and the avoidance of pain</note> said many other things to them to restrain their grief, including this in his opening remarks: "I have come to you, not as one who will grieve along with you, but so that I might stop you from grieving."

<summary>This page contains the opening of Book 7, featuring the legend of how Semiramis usurped the Assyrian throne, a description of the decadent rivalry between King Straton and King Nicocles, and a philosophical anecdote from Aristippus on grief.</summary>
<keywords>Semiramis, Assyria, Straton of Sidon, Nicocles of Cyprus, Theopompus, luxury, Aristippus, Cyrenaics, grief, ancient history</keywords>

->BOOK<-

### In Praise of the Mill

Pittacus <note>Pittacus of Mytilene (c. 640–568 BCE), one of the Seven Sages of Greece</note> used to praise the Mill very highly, including this commendation: that in a small space, it provides various forms of exercise. There was even a certain song called "the mill-song" <note>original: "epimylios," a traditional folk song sung by workers while grinding grain</note>.

### Concerning the Manual Labor of Odysseus and Achilles

Even Laertes <note>The father of Odysseus</note> was discovered by his son performing manual labor, pruning a plant, despite his very advanced age. Odysseus himself also admits to knowing many tasks and being a craftsman in them; he claimed that no other mortal could compete with him in woodcraft <note>original: "drymosyne"</note>, in tending a fire, or in splitting a quantity of wood. Furthermore, when he built his raft, he did not require shipwrights but constructed it very quickly by himself. Achilles, too, though he was the third generation from Zeus <note>Achilles was the son of Peleus, who was the son of Aeacus, the son of Zeus</note>, personally butchered the meat, hastening to prepare a meal for the ambassadors <note>The leaders of the Greek army—Ajax, Odysseus, and Phoenix—who came to Achilles' tent to beg him to return to the Trojan War</note> who had arrived from the Achaean camp.

### A Certain Scythian’s Reply Concerning the Cold

Once, while snow was falling, the King of the Scythians asked a certain man if he was cold, for the man was enduring the weather while naked. The man asked the King in return if his forehead was cold. When the King replied that it was not, the man said: "Then neither am I, for I am all forehead." <note>The Scythians were nomadic people of the Eurasian steppes, famous in Greek literature for their hardiness and immunity to the elements</note>

### Concerning the Sleeplessness of Demosthenes

Pytheas used to mock Demosthenes <note>The most famous Athenian orator, known for his rigorous self-discipline</note>, remarking that his arguments "smelled of the lamp-wicks." He said this because Demosthenes would stay awake through the entire night, thinking and practicing the things he intended to say when he went before the Athenian assembly.

### Concerning Alexander’s Mourning for the Death of Hephaestion

When Hephaestion died, Alexander threw weapons into the funeral pyre and poured gold and silver out with the corpse; he also included clothing of great value to the Persians. He sheared the manes of the warhorses and the pack animals, adopting a Homeric style of mourning <note>Referring to how Achilles mourned Patroclus in the *Iliad*</note>. Indeed, imitating Achilles—though he acted more violently and passionately than his model—Alexander even "shaved" the acropolis of Ecbatana by tearing down its battlements. Up until he cut his own hair, his actions seemed Greek to me; but when he laid hands on the walls, Alexander was already mourning in a barbarian <note>original: "barbarikos," meaning foreign or non-Greek, often implying excess</note> fashion, and he even changed his own royal attire.

<summary>A collection of brief anecdotes highlighting the dignity of manual labor among Homeric heroes, the stoicism of the Scythians, and the intense behaviors of historical figures like Demosthenes and Alexander the Great.</summary>

# BOOK SEVEN

...to his anger, and surrendered everything to passion and tears.

### The Death of Hephaestion
Hephaestion died in Ecbatana <note>A major city in ancient Media, modern-day Iran</note>. A story circulates that Alexander did not place a monument over the corpse immediately; rather, it is said that he intended to seize various treasures for the purpose, but the funeral fire did not reach the youth after he had died, being overtaken instead by Alexander’s own death <note>The author suggests the lavish funeral preparations for Alexander's favorite general were interrupted by the king's own sudden demise in 323 BCE</note>.

### Concerning a Modest Woman
Is great modesty not a virtue? It certainly seems so to me, especially in the case of Phocion’s wife <note>Phocion (c. 402–318 BCE) was an Athenian statesman known for his extreme frugality and honesty</note>. She wore Phocion’s own cloak and felt no need for a saffron-colored robe <note>original: "crokotos," a luxury garment associated with wealth and festivals</note>, or a Tarentine gown <note>original: "Tarantinos," a fine, semi-transparent fabric from Tarentum</note>, or a fancy wrap, or an embroidered mantle, or a hairnet, or a veil, or dyed tunics. She was clothed first in her modesty, and secondly in whatever garments were at hand.

### Concerning the Wife of Socrates
Socrates said to Xanthippe <note>Socrates' wife, often portrayed in later literature as difficult or sharp-tongued</note>, when she did not want to put on his cloak to go and see the festival procession: "Do you see? You are walking out not to see the spectacle, but rather to be a spectacle yourself."

### Footwear of Roman Women
The majority of Roman women are accustomed to wearing the same style of footwear as the men.

### A Saying of Lysander or Philip Concerning Perjury
One should deceive children with knuckle-bones <note>original: "astragalos," used like dice in games</note>, but men with oaths. Some say this was a saying of Lysander <note>A Spartan admiral</note>, while others attribute it to Philip of Macedon <note>The father of Alexander the Great</note>. Whichever of the two it was, the statement is incorrect in my opinion. Perhaps it is no surprise that I do not agree with Lysander; for he was a tyrant, and my own character—as is clear from what I have written—does not find such views pleasing.

### Concerning the Endurance of Agesilaus
Agesilaus the Spartan <note>Agesilaus II, King of Sparta (r. 398–360 BCE)</note>, even when he was already an old man, often went barefoot and without an under-tunic, wrapping himself only in a coarse cloak <note>term: "tribon" — the characteristic simple, rough cloak worn by Spartans and later by Cynic philosophers</note>, even early in the morning during the winter season. When someone criticized him, saying he was attempting feats more suited to a young man than his age, he replied: "But the young men of the city look to me, just as foals look to a full-grown horse."

### Concerning Philosophers as Soldiers and Statesmen
And were philosophers not also good at the affairs of war? It seems so to me, seeing as the people of Tarentum chose Archytas <note>A Pythagorean philosopher and mathematician</note> as their general six times. Melissus served as an admiral, and Socrates served in the army three times <note>Socrates fought with distinction at Potidaea, Delium, and Amphipolis</note>. Plato...

### How the allies of the Mytileneans were punished for revolting. ~

When the Mytileneans held command of the sea, they imposed a specific punishment upon those allies who attempted to revolt: they decreed that the children of the rebels were not to learn their letters, nor were they to be taught music. They considered this to be the most heavy and bitter of all punishments—to spend one's life in ignorance and without the culture of the Muses <note>original: "amathia kai amousia," referring to a life lacking both basic literacy and the refinement of the arts</note>. ~

### Concerning Rome, Romus, Romulus, and Servia. ~

Rome was founded by Romus and Romulus, who were the sons of Ares and Servia; she was one of the daughters of the Latin rulers. ~

### Concerning Eudoxus when he arrived in Sicily. ~

When Eudoxus <note>Eudoxus of Cnidus, a renowned astronomer and mathematician</note> arrived in Sicily, Dionysius <note>Dionysius II, the tyrant of Syracuse</note> was very grateful for his arrival. However, Eudoxus was not moved at all by flattery or the ruler's advances. Upon his arrival, he spoke as if he were staying at a good inn where Plato was also a guest; he confessed that he had not come for the sake of the tyrant, but because of Plato. ~

### How the Egyptians are resilient under torture, and concerning Indian women. ~

They say that Egyptians are remarkably resilient when enduring torture, and that an Egyptian man would sooner die while being racked <note>original: "strebloumenos," referring to being stretched or twisted on a torture device</note> than confess the truth. Among the Indians, the women endure the fire along with their deceased husbands; the wives actually compete for this privilege, and she who is chosen by lot is burned together with him <note>An ancient Greek observation of the practice of Sati in India</note>. ~

Solon acted as general in the battle for the island of Salamis. Having captured two Megarian ships, he transferred Athenian soldiers into them...

Having changed his anchorage through trickery, he cut down many of the Megarians while they were unarmed. He also prevailed in the legal arguments over the island <note>The island of Salamis, which was contested between Athens and Megara.</note>, not by cleverness of speech, but by the weight of the evidence itself. For having opened ancient graves, he demonstrated that all the bodies were lying toward the west according to his own ancestral custom, whereas the Megarians were buried at random and haphazardly. The Lacedaemonians <term>Lacedaemonians: the Spartans, who acted as arbitrators in the dispute</term> judged the case. ~

->Concerning an old man of Ceos who dyed his hair.<-

A man from Ceos <note>An island in the Cyclades known for its strict laws and respect for the elderly.</note> arrived in Sparta, being already an old man. In other respects he was a braggart, but he was ashamed of his age, and for this reason he tried to hide his gray hair with dye. When he came before the Spartans and appeared with his head looking such a way, he spoke on the matters for which he had come. But Archidamus <note>Archidamus III, a King of Sparta.</note>, the king of the Spartans, stood up and said: "What healthy <note>original: "hygies," meaning both physically healthy and intellectually sound or honest.</note> thing could this man say, who carries a lie not only in his soul but even on his head?" He dismissed the arguments presented by him, discrediting the Cean because he saw he was acting out of fear <note>Presumably fear of being judged for his age, which led to his deception.</note>. ~

->Concerning Caesar and Pompey’s diligence in learning<-
->what was proper for ruling well.<-

Julius Caesar did not disdain to frequent the doors of teachers, and Pompey sought out those who possessed mastery <note>original: "kratēseis," referring to those who had reached the height of their discipline or skill.</note>. For they did not, simply because they held great power, look down on those who were able to benefit them in the greatest matters; rather, they sought them out, even though they themselves held such high status. For it seems they did not desire the mere use of power, but to rule well
