# AELIAN'S HISTORICAL MISCELLANY

## EIGHTH BOOK <note>original: "ΒΙΒΛΙΟΝ ΟΓΔΟΟΝ"</note>

### Concerning the divine sign of Socrates

Socrates used to say—presumably to Theages, Demodocus, and many others—concerning the <term>daimonion: a "divine sign" or internal voice that Socrates claimed warned him against certain actions</term> that lived with him, that a sacred voice was often assigned to him. He says that whenever it occurs, it signals him to turn away from what he is about to do, but it never encourages him to act. And again, he says that if one of his friends shares a plan with him and that voice intervenes, it warns them against it. "And so," he says, "it advises me, and I in turn advise the one consulting me, and I do not allow them to act in a way that ignores the divine warning." 

He used to bring forward Charmides, the son of Glaucon, as a witness; for Charmides consulted him about whether he should train for the Nemean games <note>The Nemean Games were one of the four great athletic festivals of Ancient Greece, held in honor of Zeus.</note>. As soon as Charmides began to speak, the voice intervened, and Socrates tried to prevent Charmides from continuing with his plans. But Charmides was not persuaded, and his efforts did not result in the success he sought.

### Concerning the wisdom of Hipparchus, his zeal for the educated, and the Homeric epics

Hipparchus, the son of Peisistratus <note>Peisistratus was a famous "tyrant" of Athens, though the term then referred to a ruler who took power unconstitutionally rather than a cruel one.</note>, was the eldest of Peisistratus's sons and the wisest of the Athenians. He was the first to bring the poems of Homer to Athens and compelled the <term>rhapsodes: professional performers who recited epic poetry from memory</term> to recite them at the Panathenaic festival <note>The Panathenaia was the most important religious and cultural festival in Athens, dedicated to the goddess Athena.</note>. He even sent a <term>pentekontor: a Greek galley ship with fifty oars</term> for Anacreon of Teos, in order to bring him to his court. 

He also held Simonides of Ceos in high regard and always kept him by his side, persuading him with great gifts and payments, as was expected. For Simonides was indeed a lover of money—no one would deny it. Hipparchus’s true life's work was his zeal for educated men. He wished to educate the Athenians under his own guidance, striving to rule over a better class of people; for he did not think he should begrudge anyone wisdom, being a man of noble character. Plato says these things, if indeed the *Hipparchus* <note>A dialogue titled "Hipparchus" was attributed to the philosopher Plato in antiquity, though modern scholars often debate its authenticity.</note> is truly a work of Plato.

## Concerning the ritual slaughter of the ox among the Athenians, and the festivals of Dipolieia and Bouphonia <note>original: "Περὶ βοὸς παρ' Ἀθηναίοις σφαγῆς, καὶ Διϊπολίων, καὶ βουφονίων ἑορτῶν"</note> :~

This is an Athenian custom: whenever an ox is slaughtered, they hold a trial for all those involved, judging each person in turn for the murder. They eventually acquit the people but find the knife guilty, declaring that it was the knife that killed the animal. The day on which they perform these rites is called the festival of the <term>Dipolieia: a festival dedicated to Zeus Polieus ("Zeus of the City")</term>, and also the <term>Bouphonia: literally "the ox-slaying"</term>.~

## Concerning the extravagance of Poliarchus :~

They say that Poliarchus, the Athenian, reached such a height of luxury that when the dogs and roosters he was fond of died, he would hold public funerals for them. He would invite his friends to the burial procession, bury the animals at great expense, erect monuments over them, and even have epitaphs carved upon them.~

## Concerning Neleus, Medon, and the twelve cities of Ionia ~

Neleus, the son of Codrus, having failed to obtain the kingship, left Athens. This happened because the <term>Pythia: the priestess of the Oracle at Delphi</term> had pre-judged the right to rule in favor of Medon, despite Medon being <note>original: "σεσολοικισμένῳ" - this refers to Medon being lame or having a physical defect, which was often viewed as a disqualification for kingship.</note> physically impaired. Neleus put into port at Naxos, not by choice, but because he was forced there by a storm. When he wished to set sail again, contrary winds prevented him. While he was at a loss because of these circumstances, the diviners declared that the camp needed to be purified, as many of those sailing with him did not have "clean hands" <note>This likely refers to "blood guilt" or having committed a crime that required ritual cleansing.</note>, and Neleus himself might have killed someone somewhere and required purification. He withdrew, and <insert>then</insert> he heard this and was persuaded <insert>the</insert> counselors; however, the knowledge of those whom he later left behind did not come from themselves, and they instead settled in Naxos. 

Neleus eventually arrived in Ionia and first settled Miletus, having driven out the Carians, the Mygdones, the Leleges, and other barbarians. From these efforts, the twelve cities were called Ionia. They are as follows: Miletus, Ephesus, Erythrae, Clazomenae, Priene, Lebedos, Teos, Colophon, Myus, Phocaea, Samos, and Chios. Later, he also settled many other cities on the mainland.~

## Concerning the illiteracy and lack of education of the barbarians :~

They say that none of the ancient Thracians knew how to read or write. Indeed, all the barbarians living in Europe considered it a great disgrace to use letters. However, the report is that those living in Asia did make use of them.

-># BOOK 8, CHAPTER 10<-

...to them even more, they actually think and dare to say that Orpheus was never a wise man, despite being a Thracian, but rather that the myths surrounding him are falsely attributed. Androtion says this—if he is to be believed—to provide evidence of the illiteracy and lack of education among the Thracians.

### Concerning the weddings celebrated by Alexander after he defeated Darius : ~

When Alexander captured Darius, he hosted wedding feasts for himself and his friends; there were ninety bridegrooms in total. An equal number of bridal chambers were prepared for them. The hall that received and entertained them held a hundred couches. Each couch had feet made of silver, while Alexander’s own couch had feet of gold. The entire room was adorned with purple fabrics and multicolored garments of costly <term>Barbarian weaving: at this time, "Barbarian" referred specifically to the intricate and luxurious Persian styles</term>. He also included his personal guest-friends in the banquet, seating them opposite himself. In the courtyard, the rest of the forces—the infantry, the navy, the cavalry, as well as the visiting embassies and the Greeks residing there—were feasted. The meals were announced by trumpets; a <term>summons melody: original "synkletikon melos"</term> was played when it was time to enter for food, and a <term>recall melody: original "anakletikon"</term> when the signal was given to depart. He performed the wedding sacrifices for five consecutive days. Many musicians and actors arrived, both for comedies and tragedies. There were also distinguished "wonder-workers" from India <note>These were likely street performers, magicians, or jugglers from the recently contacted Indian territories</note>, who were thought to surpass all others in their grand displays of showmanship.

### Concerning the art of painting : ~

They say that Conon of Cleonae perfected the art of painting. Before him, it was still in its early stages and was executed unskillfully and without expertise by his predecessors; it was, in a sense, still in its "swaddling clothes" and "living on milk" <note>This is a metaphor for the infancy of the medium before the development of perspective or anatomy</note>. For this reason, Conon was the first to receive a more substantial fee for his craftsmanship.

### Concerning a tyrant murdered by his own favorite : ~

Archelaus, the tyrant of Macedonia—for that is what Plato calls him, rather than "king"—was killed by his "favorite," Crateuas. Crateuas was so in love with the idea of being a tyrant that he desired it no less than he had been desired by the king. Crateuas killed his lover, Archelaus, believing that he himself would then become a tyrant and a happy man. However, the youth held the tyranny for only three or four days before he was, in turn, plotted against and killed by others.

<summary>This page describes Alexander the Great's mass wedding at Susa, the technical "infancy" of painting before Conon of Cleonae, and the brief, bloody usurpation of the Macedonian throne by Crateuas.</summary>

This page concludes a narrative about the Macedonian King Archelaus and shifts into a series of historical and philosophical observations regarding Athenian lawgivers, the physical decay of the natural world, and the unexpected failures of famous orators.</meta>

# EIGHTH BOOK

...he died. It would be most fitting to add to this Macedonian drama that poetic line:
> "As chance shines upon us, it brings evil to the heart." <note>This likely refers to the sudden reversal of fortune for Archelaus, who was killed by his favorite, Craterus.</note>

It is said that Archelaus lied to him, promising to give him one of his daughters in marriage, but when he instead married the girl to another, Craterus, in a fit of extreme indignation, murdered Archelaus.

## Concerning Solon, his associates, Draco, and the laws.

<margin>S</margin> The Athenians chose Solon as an <term>elected leader: original "archon," a high-ranking magistrate</term> to rule them, for they did not select him by the usual lottery. Once he was chosen, he organized the city and wrote the laws which they still observe today. At that time, the Athenians stopped using the laws of Draco. Those earlier laws were nicknamed "the bonds" <note>original: "desmoi," referring to their extreme severity</note> because of their harshness. They preserved only his law regarding homicide.

## Concerning the diminishing or destruction of things, and the world itself.

<margin>Th</margin> We should not be surprised if the nature of man, being mortal and fleeting, is forced to undergo decay. After all, we see even rivers failing. We hear that even the highest mountains are diminishing. Why else do sailors say the mountains they see appear smaller than they were seen previously? This same process of falling and suffering affects even Mount Olympus, which was once thought to be divine. Those who have more deeply studied the nature of the universe even say that the cosmos itself is perishing.

## Concerning Demosthenes, Aeschines, Theophrastus, and Demochares.

<margin>P</margin> It is a paradox, though nonetheless true, that Demosthenes failed <note>original: "ekpesontos," meaning he lost his composure or "fell out" of his speech</note> when he went to Macedonia. On the other hand, Aeschines—the son of Atrometus from the <term>deme: a local administrative district</term> of Kothokidai—was highly esteemed by the Macedonians. There was a great "fire" or spirit in the embassies because of his confidence. The reason for this was Aeschines' friendship with Philip <note>Philip II of Macedon, father of Alexander the Great</note>, the gifts he received from him, and the fact that Philip listened to him mildly and with pleasure, looking upon him with a gentle gaze and showing his goodwill. These factors were all like a tow-rope for Aeschines' boldness and the fluency of his words. 

Demosthenes was not the only one to suffer this in Macedonia, despite being the most formidable of speakers. Even Theophrastus of Eresus <note>a famous philosopher and successor to Aristotle</note> experienced this. He failed while speaking before the Council of the <term>Areopagus: the high court of Athens</term>. He offered this excuse for his speechlessness: that he was struck with awe by the dignity of that assembly. To this, Demochares replied most sharply and readily...

Democharis gave him the floor, saying: "O Theophrastus, we were Athenians, but not the Twelve Gods who act as judges." <note>original: "δώδεκα θεοὶ οἱ δικάζοντες." This appears to be a pointed remark by Democharis—an orator known for his bluntness—suggesting that the philosophers should not expect the same reverence as the Olympian council of judges.</note>

### Those who did not laugh

<margin>A</margin> They say that Anaxagoras of Clazomenae <note>An influential Pre-Socratic philosopher (c. 500–428 BC) who brought philosophy to Athens.</note> was never seen to laugh, nor even to smile at all. They also say that Aeschylus <note>The father of Greek tragedy.</note> was a mighty enemy to laughter. And then there was Heraclitus, because he wept for everything in life. <note>Heraclitus is traditionally contrasted with the "Laughing Philosopher" Democritus; he viewed the world with such melancholy that he earned the nickname "The Weeping Philosopher."</note>

### Concerning the death of Diogenes

When Diogenes of Sinope <note>The famous Cynic philosopher who advocated for a life according to nature and rejected social conventions.</note> was finally ill and near death, he carried himself to a place by a bridge near a gymnasium and threw himself down. He gave instructions to the <term>gymnasium-guard: original "palaistrophylax," the official responsible for the wrestling grounds</term> that as soon as he realized he had breathed his last, he should throw his body into the Ilissus <note>A river in Athens.</note>. Diogenes cared so little for death or for his own burial.

### Concerning Philip’s self-control in victory, and the things he wished to be reminded of.

<margin>B</margin> After defeating the Athenians at Chaeronea <note>The decisive battle in 338 BC where Philip II of Macedon secured his grip on Greece.</note>, Philip was uplifted by his success, yet he remained master of his reason and did not act with <term>arrogance: original "hybris," the dangerous pride that leads to a fall</term>. Because of this, he believed he ought to be reminded by one of his servants from within his quarters that he was only a human being. He assigned this task to the boy, and they say he would neither go out himself nor allow anyone seeking an audience to enter until the boy had shouted this three times every day. He would say to him: "Philip, you are a man." <note>original: "Φίλιππε ἄνθρωπος εἶ." This served as a "memento mori," ensuring the king did not forget his mortality amidst his triumphs.</note>

### Concerning Solon and Pisistratus

<margin>C</margin> When Solon was already growing old, he suspected that Pisistratus was attempting to establish a tyranny. This occurred when they came before the Athenian assembly and Pisistratus requested a bodyguard. Seeing that some of the Athenians listened to the request with total indifference, while others paid close attention to Pisistratus, Solon said that he was wiser than some and braver than others. He was wiser than those who did not realize that if Pisistratus received a bodyguard for his person, he would become a tyrant; and he was braver than those who knew it but remained silent. Once Pisistratus seized power and became tyrant, Solon sat in front of his house, setting out his shield and his spear. He said, "I have armed myself and will help the fatherland as best I can." Although he was no longer a general due to his age, he remained loyal in his conviction. Nevertheless, Pisistratus—whether out of respect for...

# BOOK EIGHT

<margin>S.</margin> [Solon] did no wrong to the man, nor to his wisdom, even though in their youth, it is said, he became his favorite. At any rate, he did nothing harmful to Solon. Shortly thereafter, Solon ended his life at a great age, leaving behind a great reputation for wisdom and courage. They erected a bronze statue of him in the <term>Agora: the central public marketplace and meeting space of a Greek city</term>, and they also buried him at public expense near the gates by the wall, on the right as one enters; and a tomb was built for him.

### Concerning Scythes, son of Oinykinos, monarch of the Zancleans:
<margin>Th.</margin> It is said that Scythes, the son of Oinykinos and monarch of the people of Zancle <note>Zancle was the ancient name for Messina in Sicily.</note>, went up to Asia to visit King Darius. The King considered him to be the most righteous of all the men who had come up to him from Greece.

<margin>O.</margin> For although he had fallen out with the King, he reached Sicily and then returned from Sicily back to the King again. Democedes of Croton <note>A famous physician who served at the Persian court but escaped back to Greece.</note> did not do this, and for this reason, Darius spoke poorly of him, calling him a deceiver and a most wicked man. Because of his loyalty, Scythes lived in great prosperity among the Persians until he ended his life.

### Concerning Euthymus, and the hero in Temesa, and a proverb:
<margin>E.</margin> Euthymus of Locri, a boxer among those in Italy, was a good man, and it is believed he was most marvelous in the strength of his body. Indeed, the Locrians show a stone of immense size which he carried and placed before his doors. He also put an end to a <term>hero: in this context, a "hero" refers to a powerful and often vengeful spirit of a deceased person</term> in Temesa who was exacting tribute from the neighbors. For having arrived at the hero's temple—which was <term>forbidden: original "abaton," a sacred space not to be trodden by the uninitiated</term> to most—he fought against him and forced him to return more than he had plundered. From this event, a proverb began to circulate, said of those who gain something unexpectedly: "The hero in Temesa will come to them." <note>This proverb likely implies that an unexpected gain might be followed by a confrontation with a powerful force, or refers to the hero's forced restitution.</note>

<margin>L.</margin> It is also said that this same Euthymus, upon going down to the Caecinus river, which is located before the city of the Locrians, vanished.

### Epitaph of Anaxagoras, and an altar to him:
<margin>E.</margin> > Here lies Anaxagoras, who journeyed furthest 
> to the very limit of truth regarding the heavenly order.

An altar also stands in his honor, one inscribed to "Mind" <note>original: "νοῦ," *Nous*—Anaxagoras was famous for positing that Mind was the ordering force of the universe.</note> and the other to "Truth."

# AELIAN’S VARIOUS HISTORY

## BOOK NINE.

->That Hiero both loved education and was a benefactor,<-
->and lived in love with his brothers.<-

<margin>⊙ 1</margin>
They say that Hiero of Syracuse was a friend to all Greeks <note>original: "philellene"</note>, and that he honored education with great courage. They also say he was most ready to perform acts of kindness; indeed, they claim he was more eager to give than those asking were to receive. He was also most brave in spirit, and he lived with his brothers without any strife, even though they were his rivals for power, loving them very much and being loved by them in return. In this part of his life, they say, he also lived in the company of the poets Simonides and Pindar. Simonides did not hesitate to visit him, even though he was weighed down by old age. For while the man from Ceos <note>Simonides of Ceos was a famous lyric poet</note> was by nature fond of money, they say the generosity of Hiero encouraged him even further.

### Concerning the victory of Taurosthenes.

<margin>⊙ 2</margin>
It is said that the victory of Taurosthenes at the Olympic Games was announced to his father on the island of Aegina on the very same day by a phantom. Others, however, say that Taurosthenes took a pigeon with him that was still nursing its young, which were still unfeathered and unable to fly. After he won, he released the dove after tying a purple ribbon to it. The bird, hurrying back to its chicks, returned from <term>Pisa: the region in the Peloponnese where the Olympic Games were held</term> to Aegina in a single day.

### Concerning the luxury and arrogance of certain men
### in Alexander’s circle.

<margin>⊙ 3</margin>
Alexander spoiled his companions by showering them with luxury. Hagnon, for instance, wore gold nails in his boots. Whenever Cleitus was about to conduct business, he walked upon purple robes to meet those who had requests. Perdiccas and Craterus, who were fond of exercise, were followed by leather skins <note>Large sheets of leather or awnings used to create portable enclosures</note> a <term>stadium: an ancient measurement of about 600 feet</term> in size. By using these to enclose a massive space during their military encampments, they exer... <note>The text breaks off here; the full word likely meant "they exercised."</note>

<meta>This page continues the description of the legendary extravagance and courtly protocol of Alexander the Great’s inner circle, which began on the previous page with an account of his companions' luxurious habits. ### NINTH.

...they exercised. A great deal of dust followed them, carried by pack animals, as it was considered useful for their gymnasiums.

Leonnatus and Menelaus, who were fond of hunting, were followed by linen nets sufficient in number to enclose a great distance. As for Alexander himself, his tent contained one hundred couches, and fifty golden columns supported it, holding up the roof. The roof itself was overlaid with gold and was elaborately worked with costly embroideries. First, five hundred Persians, those called the <term>Apple-bearers: an elite guard so named because of the golden apples serving as counterweights on their spear-butts</term>, stood inside around the tent, dressed in robes of purple and quince-yellow. After them stood a thousand archers dressed in flame-colored and hyacinth-dyed <note>original: "hyacinth-dyed," referring to a deep blue or purple color</note> garments; and in front of these stood one hundred of the Macedonian <term>Silver-shields: an elite infantry unit known as the Argyraspides</term>. In the middle of the tent was placed a golden throne, upon which Alexander sat to give audience, while his bodyguards stood all around him. The tent was surrounded by an outer enclosure where one thousand Macedonians and ten thousand Persians were stationed; no one dared to approach him easily, for there was a great fear of him, as he ruled with both high-minded pride and a fortune that had risen to absolute power.

### Concerning Polycrates' devotion to Anacreon, 
### and his jealousy.

Polycrates of Samos was a lover of the arts, and he honored Anacreon of Teos <note>Anacreon was a famous lyric poet known for his songs of wine and love</note>, treating him with great devotion and taking delight in him and his songs; he did not merely enjoy his company for the sake of luxury.

Anacreon praised Smerdies <note>a youth in Polycrates' court</note> too warmly, as he was the favorite of Polycrates. Then, the boy was pleased by the praise and greeted Anacreon with dignity—the poet loving the boy's soul and not just his body. Let no one, by the gods, slander the poet from Teos to us, nor call him unrestrained! But Polycrates became jealous because Anacreon had honored Smerdies, and he saw that the poet was loved in return by the boy. So Polycrates shaved the boy’s hair, intending to shame him and thinking he would cause Anacreon grief. However, Anacreon did not stoop to accusing Polycrates, showing self-restraint and mastery over his emotions. Instead, he shifted the blame in a song to the boy, as if accusing him of madness and of arming "ignorance" against his own walls <note>This is a metaphor for the boy's lost hair</note>. Let Anacreon himself sing the song about the tragedy of the hair, for he will sing it better than I can.

<meta>This page continues from the previous accounts of excess and royal conduct, shifting from the luxury of Alexander the Great and Polycrates to a series of moral anecdotes about famous Greek figures—Themistocles, Pericles, Socrates, and the downfall of the tyrant Dionysius II.</meta>

BOOK

### Concerning Hiero and Themistocles ~

<margin>9</margin> When Hiero <note>Hiero I, the tyrant of Syracuse in Sicily</note> arrived at the Olympic games as they were beginning, intending to lead a team of horses in the competition, Themistocles spoke out. He declared that a man who had not shared in the greatest of dangers <note>Themistocles refers to the Persian Wars, which Hiero did not assist with</note> ought not to share in the great public festivals; and for this, Themistocles was highly praised.

### Concerning Pericles, and his sons who died of the plague ~

<margin>70</margin> Pericles, though he lost his children to the plague <note>the Great Plague of Athens, 430–426 BCE</note>, bore their deaths with the greatest courage. He even persuaded the rest of the Athenians to bear the deaths of their own loved ones with more cheer.

### Concerning the cheerfulness of Socrates in all circumstances ~

<margin>5</margin> Xanthippe <note>the wife of Socrates, often portrayed in literature as difficult</note> used to say that although ten thousand changes had gripped the city, Socrates’ face remained the same through them all; she observed him always with the same expression whether he was leaving the house or returning to it. For he was fittingly composed for every situation, his mind was always cheerful, and he was above all grief and superior to every fear.

### That Dionysius was licentious regarding women ~

<margin>70</margin> When the younger Dionysius <note>Dionysius II, the tyrant of Syracuse</note> visited the city of the Locrians—his mother Doris being a Locrian woman—he took over the most magnificent houses in the city. Strewing the floors with roses, wild thyme, and other flowers, he would send for the daughters of the Locrians and consort with them in the most licentious manner. For this, he eventually paid a heavy price. When his tyranny was overthrown by Dion <note>Dion of Syracuse, a former associate turned liberator</note>, the Locrians took Dionysius's wife and daughters and forced them into prostitution. Everyone joined in treating them with unbridled insolence, especially those relatives of the young women who had been corrupted by Dionysius. When they were finally satisfied with their outrages, they killed the women by piercing them under their fingernails with needles. They then ground their bones in mortars, and after stripping the flesh from the bones, they placed a curse upon anyone who would not taste of it. Any remains that were left over, they cast into the sea. Dionysius himself, living in Corinth and experiencing many varied changes of fortune, ended his life in extreme poverty as a <term>begging priest: a metragyrtes, or wandering priest of the mother-goddess Cybele</term>, beating drums and followed by the sound of flutes.

### That Demetrius was also licentious ~

<margin>4</margin> Demetrius the Besieger <note>Demetrius I Poliorcetes, a king and successor to Alexander known for his military engines</note> would capture cities and then, in his own lux—

->NINTH [BOOK]<-

...he continued to misuse his power, acquiring for himself an annual income of one thousand two hundred talents <note>A "talent" was a massive unit of currency; 1,200 talents was a staggering sum, equivalent to millions of dollars today</note>. Of this, he spent very little on his army, wasting the rest on his own licentiousness. Perfumes were sprinkled upon his floors, and at every hour the most seasonal of flowers were strewn beneath him so that he might walk upon them. He was also unrestrained regarding women and pursued youthful loves. He was deeply concerned with being beautiful; he plucked his hair, dyed it blond, and anointed his face with ointments. He also practiced induced sweating <note>likely referring to steam baths for skin care</note> and used various other oils, taking pride in his "cheerfulness" <note>Original: εὐθυμίαι (euthymia), a term usually meaning "peace of mind," here used ironically to describe his dedication to luxury</note>.

### Concerning Plato's disregard for his own life ~

Plato was told that the location of his school, the Academy, was unhealthy. When physicians advised him to move to the Lyceum <note>another garden and gymnasium in Athens, later associated with Aristotle</note>, he refused, saying: "I would not move even to the peaks of Mount Athos <note>a mountain in northern Greece famous for its height and clear air</note> for the sake of living a longer life."

### Concerning the painter Parrhasius ~

Others testify—as do the inscriptions on his own works—that the painter Parrhasius used to wear a purple robe and a golden crown. He once competed in a contest at Samos. He encountered an opponent who was not much less skilled than himself, and Parrhasius was defeated. His entry was a painting of Ajax <note>the Greek hero known for his strength and tragic temper</note> competing against Odysseus for the armor of Achilles. After losing, Parrhasius replied quite wittily to a friend who was grieving with him; he said that while he himself cared little for the defeat, he felt for the son of Telamon <note>Ajax</note>, who had now been defeated a second time for the same prize <note>referring to the mythological story where Ajax lost the armor to Odysseus and committed suicide</note>. Parrhasius also carried a staff wrapped with golden spirals and wore sandals fastened with golden laces. They say that he did not work on his art reluctantly or with strain, but very cheerfully and easily; for he would even sing, and by humming in this way, he tried to lighten the toil of his craft. Theophrastus <note>a student of Aristotle and a famous botanist and character-writer</note> is the source for these stories.

### Concerning the Epicureans expelled by the Romans and Messenians ~

...happened to the young men, and the Messenians treated the son of Eupicrates with insolence. ~

### Concerning the Gluttony and Corpulence of Dionysius ~

I hear that Dionysius of Heraclea <note>Heraclea Pontica was a Greek city on the coast of the Black Sea</note>, the son of the tyrant Clearchus, through his daily gluttony <note>original: adēphagia, meaning compulsive overeating</note> and luxury, became excessively fleshy and swollen without even realizing it. Because of the size of his body and the mass of his flesh, he suffered from difficulty breathing <note>original: dyspnoia</note>. His physicians, they say, prescribed a remedy for this condition: they fashioned very long, thin needles, which they would push through his sides and stomach whenever he fell into a deep sleep. It was the duty of others to watch this carefully; the needle would pass through the "fated" and, in a sense, alien flesh until it reached a point that was no different from stone. But if the needle reached a part where his body was still healthy and "his own"—rather than that which was alien due to excessive fat—then he would feel it and wake from his sleep. When conducting business, he would dine with those who wished to approach him, placing a "chest" <note>original: kibōton</note> in front of his body; some say it was not a chest but a small tower <note>original: pyrgiskon, or a turret-like structure</note>, so that the rest of his body was hidden and only his face, protruding from the top, would speak. By the gods, that man wore a wicked covering, more like the cage of a wild beast than the clothing of a human being! ~

### Concerning the Thinness of Philitas’s Body ~

They say that Philitas of Cos <note>a famous poet and scholar of the 4th century BCE</note> had an extremely thin body. Since he was easily knocked over by any cause, they say he had soles made of lead in his shoes so that he would not be overturned by the winds whenever a stiff breeze blew. If he was so weak that he could not withstand a breeze, how was he able to carry such a heavy burden? This story does not convince me, but I have recorded what has been said about the man. ~

### Concerning Homer ~

Because Homer was the leader of all poetry, people gave him the highest honors and ranked all others second to him. When performing a sacrifice, they would invite Apollo and Homer to the guest-feast. In addition to this, it is said that being at a loss for how to provide a dowry for his daughter, he gave her the epic poem *The Cypria* <note>an ancient Greek epic, now lost, that narrated the events leading up to the Trojan War</note> to have as her dowry; Pindar <note>the famous lyric poet of Thebes</note> agrees with this story. ~

<meta>This page continues the collection of historical anecdotes, moving from the physical ailments and habits of tyrants and poets to legendary origins and character studies of famous statesmen. # NINTH <note>original: ENATON; likely referring to the ninth book of the collection, such as Aelian's *Varia Historia*</note>

->Concerning Italy, and Marus the horse-parted man:<-

The first inhabitants of Italy were the original children of the soil <note>original: autochthones, meaning people believed to have sprung directly from the earth</note>. Among the oldest generation was a certain man called Marus. They say that his front parts were like those of a human, but his rear parts were those of a horse. They say that his name, translated into Greek, means "horse-parted" <note>original: hippomerēs</note>. However, it seems to me that he was simply the first to mount a horse and fit it with a bridle; because of this, he was believed to have a dual nature. They tell myths about him, saying that he lived for one hundred and twenty-three years, and that he lived even after dying three times—though these things do not seem credible to me.

They say that very many nations inhabited Italy, more so than any other land. The reason for this was the mildness of the seasons, the excellence of the countryside, the abundance of water, and its fruitfulness. It is excellent for grazing and is watered by rivers. Furthermore, a fine sea lies alongside it, punctuated everywhere by harbors, which are abundant and easy to sail into. Moreover, the gentle and mild nature of the inhabitants encouraged many to migrate there. In ancient times, they say that one thousand one hundred and ninety-seven cities <note>original: hepta kai ennekonta pros tais chiliais</note> were inhabited in Italy.

->Concerning the vanity of Demosthenes:<-

This account seems to accuse Demosthenes <note>The famous 4th-century BCE Athenian orator</note> of light-headedness. It says that he was completely filled with vanity <note>original: typhos, meaning smoke, delusion, or extreme pride</note>. Even the water-carriers, if they ever happened to be drunk near him as he passed by, would cheer him so much that he was uplifted by them. He was clearly fond of such praise, as he was inexperienced with the actual applause <note>original: synkrotēmatōn, referring to the rhythmic clapping or noise</note> found in the public assemblies.

->Concerning Themistocles:<-

Themistocles <note>The Athenian general and statesman who led the Greeks against Persia</note>, the son of Neocles, used to compare himself to plane trees <note>the Greek text says "sphisi" but the context of "sheltering under branches" refers to the "the plane trees" (*platanous*), a famous comparison attributed to him</note>. He said that men treat them with great importance when they have need of them—such as when they must take shelter under their branches during a storm—but as soon as fair weather returns, those same people pluck at them and break off their twigs. The same man used to say to me <note>the authorial "me" suggests a recorded saying</note> that if someone were to show him two paths, one leading to Hades <note>the underworld</note> and the other to the speaker's platform <note>original: bēma, the rostrum in the Athenian assembly</note>, he would have more gladly taken the one leading to Hades.

->That Demosthenes, when called by Diogenes, did not want to enter a tavern:<-

Diogenes <note>Diogenes of Sinope, the Cynic philosopher known for his public rebukes of social norms</note> once saw Demosthenes in a tavern <note>original: kapēleion, a place for drinking and trade, often considered disreputable for a high-status politician</note>. Seeing him drinking, Diogenes called out to him. When Demosthenes did not answer, Demosthenes later said he was ashamed to be seen going into a tavern. But Diogenes replied: "And yet, your master enters here every single day." By "master," he meant the common citizens <note>original: dēmotas</note>. This was to demonstrate that the demagogues and the orators are actually the slaves of the masses.

# BOOK

### Concerning Aristippus :~

While Aristippus <note>a student of Socrates and founder of the Cyrenaic school of philosophy, which emphasized pleasure</note> was sailing, a great storm arose and he became very deeply disturbed. One of his fellow passengers said, "Aristippus, are you also afraid like the common folk?" To this he replied, "And with very good reason! For your concern is over a miserable life <note>original: kakodaimonos biou</note>, and that is your present danger; but my concern is over a life of true happiness <note>original: eudaimonias, referring to the philosophical state of flourishing</note>."

### Concerning Theramenes :~

It happened that Theramenes <note>an Athenian statesman during the Peloponnesian War</note> was spending time in a certain house, and as soon as he stepped out of it, the building immediately collapsed. All the Athenians congratulated him on his unexpected survival, but he, contrary to everyone’s expectation, cried out, "O Zeus, for what occasion are you saving me?" And not long after, he was put to death by the Thirty <note>the 'Thirty Tyrants,' a pro-Spartan oligarchy that ruled Athens</note>, being forced to drink hemlock.

### Which men were diligent regarding medicine :~

Alcmaeon the Pythagorean <note>an early medical theorist</note> was very deeply diligent regarding the art of medicine. Plato also took the greatest care in it, as did Aristotle the son of Nicomachus, and many others.

### Concerning Aristotle when he was ill :~

Aristotle was once ill, and the physician gave him a certain instruction. But Aristotle said to him, "Do not treat me like a cowherd or a digger <note>meaning: do not give me orders without explanation, as if I were an uneducated laborer</note>, but teach me the reason <note>original: aitian</note> first; then you will find me ready to obey." By saying this, he taught that one should offer nothing without a rational cause.

### Concerning the luxury of Smindyrides :~

Smindyrides the Sybarite <note>a citizen of Sybaris, a city famous for its extreme wealth and decadence</note> reached such a point of luxury <term>tryphe: a lifestyle of extreme softness and indulgence</term>—for indeed, luxury was the main pursuit of all Sybarites, and their lives dissolved into it—that after resting upon a bed of rose leaves and sleeping on them, he stood up saying he had blisters from his bed. For he had reclined upon a cloak spread on the ground <note>original: chameune</note>, rather than a mattress or any soft bed, such as a bull’s hide. Such a bed was fitting for a hard and noble soldier like Diomedes <note>a hero of the Trojan War</note>, beneath whom was spread "the hide of an ox that lives in the fields" <note>a quote from Homer's Iliad</note>.

### How Pisistratus treated his own citizens :~

When Pisistratus <note>the tyrant of Athens in the 6th century BCE</note> became master of the government, he used to send for those who were idling in the marketplaces and ask them what the reason was for their being unemployed. He would say, "If your pair of oxen has died..."

<summary>This page presents a series of anecdotes about famous Greek figures, illustrating philosophical wit in the face of death, the importance of rational medicine, and the legendary decadence of the Sybarites.</summary>

# BOOK NINE

"...taking it from me, go and perform your work. But if you are unskilled in your seeds <note>meaning the medicinal ingredients or their proportions</note>, let my treatment be by my own hand, fearing lest the bitterness <note>original: chole, literally 'bile' or 'gall'</note> of these things produce some ill-effect." ~

### Concerning Zeno and Antigonus :~

**A**ntigonus the King <note>likely Antigonus II Gonatas, King of Macedon</note> held Zeno of Citium <note>the founder of the Stoic school of philosophy</note> in great respect and high regard. Once, having drunk too much wine, the King came upon Zeno in a drunken revelry, kissing him and embracing him as one does when intoxicated. He urged Zeno to command him to do something, swearing a youthful oath that he would not refuse the request. Zeno said to him, "Go and vomit." By this, he both somberly and magnanimously rebuked the King's drunkenness, while also showing care for him, lest he should suffer a rupture from over-fullness. ~

### Simplicity of character :~

**A** certain man rebuked a Laconian <note>a Spartan</note> named Chosippus while he was mourning very intensely and passionately. He replied simply, "What can I do? For I am not the cause of this feeling, but my nature is." ~

### Concerning Diogenes :~

**A** Spartan was reciting the verse of Hesiod <note>an early Greek poet known for 'Works and Days'</note> which says:
> Not even an ox would perish, if there were not a bad neighbor,
while Diogenes <note>the Cynic philosopher, famous for his biting wit</note> was listening. Diogenes said, "And yet the Messenians and their oxen have perished, and you Spartans are their neighbors!" <note>A sharp political jab; the Spartans had famously conquered and enslaved their neighbors, the Messenians</note> ~

### That Socrates was fearless, and despised gifts :~

**L**ate one night, Socrates was returning from a dinner. Some rowdy young men, having learned of his route, lay in wait for him as he returned, holding lit torches and wearing masks. It was their habit to play such pranks on others during their leisure time, usually for the worse. When Socrates saw them, he was not disturbed at all; instead, he stopped and asked them the same sorts of questions he usually asked others in the Lyceum or the Academy <note>two of the famous gymnasia and schools of Athens where philosophers taught</note>. ~

**W**hen Alcibiades <note>a wealthy and ambitious Athenian general</note> made a show of sending many gifts to Socrates, Xanthippe <note>Socrates' wife</note> was astonished by the items sent and asked to keep them. But Socrates said, "Let us instead compete with the ambition of Alcibiades, showing our own ambition by not accepting the gifts." When someone said to him that it is a great thing to obtain what one desires, he replied, "But it is a greater thing not to desire anything to begin with." ~

### Concerning the foresight of Anaxarchus :~

**W**hen Anaxarchus <note>a philosopher who accompanied Alexander the Great</note> was on campaign with Alexander, a storm arose...

# BOOK NINE

...learning that Alexander was not going to think it worthy to make camp in that place, he burned whatever gear he had at the station, and placed wood upon the baggage-animals. But when they arrived at the station and there was a need for wood, Alexander's own beds were burned so that he might be able to warm himself. When someone reported that there was a fire with Anaxarchus <note>a philosopher who accompanied Alexander on his campaigns</note>, he went to him and anointed himself in the tent of Anaxarchus. Having learned of his foresight, he praised him exceedingly. And for what he had thrown away, he gave him double, including gear and garments, because of the <note>likely "parousis", meaning "present"</note> present need.

->Concerning an athlete who excelled but died before receiving his crown:<-

**A**n athlete from Croton <note>a Greek city in Italy famous for its Olympians</note>, an Olympic victor, while going toward the judges <note>original: "hellanodikai", the officials of the Ancient Games</note> to receive his crown, was seized by a fit and died, brought down by a single fall.

->Concerning Phryne the courtesan, and the statues of Kimon's horses:<-

**T**he Greeks set up a statue of Phryne the courtesan <note>a famous and extremely wealthy woman known for her beauty</note> at Delphi upon a very high column. I will not say "the Greeks" in a general sense, lest I should seem to bring blame upon everyone for the things they love most of all; rather, it was the more unrestrained among the Greeks. The statue was made of gold. And the horses of Kimon <note>Kimon was a famous Athenian statesman whose horses won three Olympic victories</note> were of bronze, and these were made in the likeness of those very horses of Kimon which they most resembled at Athens.

->The answer of a youth when asked by his father what he had learned:<-

**A** youth from Eretria <note>a city on the island of Euboea</note> attended the school of Zeno <note>Zeno of Citium, the founder of Stoicism</note> for a long time. When he returned, his father asked, "What have you learned?" The boy said he would show him. When his father became angry and struck him with many blows, the boy remained silent and endured it. He then said, "This is what I have learned: to bear the anger of a father."

->Concerning those who dress sumptuously:<-

**D**iogenes <note>the Cynic philosopher, famous for his poverty</note> came to Olympia, and observing certain young men from Rhodes at the festival dressed in very expensive clothing, he laughed and said, "This is vanity <note>original: "typhos", a term meaning pride, smoke, or social pretension</note>." Then, coming upon some Spartans in wretched and dirty sleeveless tunics <note>original: "exomis", a simple garment favored by laborers and philosophers</note>, he said, "This is another kind of vanity."

->Concerning the high-mindedness of Antisthenes regarding his torn garment:<-

**W**hen Socrates saw Antisthenes <note>a pupil of Socrates and a foundational figure for the Cynics</note> always making visible a torn part of his cloak, he said, "Will you not stop showing off your vanity to us?"

# THE NINTH BOOK

# Concerning Anaxarchus and a Musician

A singer <term>psaltes: a musician who plays a stringed instrument, like a harp or lyre, with their fingers</term> was performing for Autoponus <note>This name is unusual and may be a transcription error for a more common name like Antigonus</note>. The man repeatedly criticized him, saying he was tightening the strings in vain and then using the middle string incorrectly. The musician, becoming indignant, said: “Do not cast the blame so poorly, O King; it is as if I ought to know the precision of these things better than you do.”

### How Anaxarchus Mocked Alexander for Attributing Divinity to Himself

Anaxarchus, who was nicknamed "The Fortunate" <note>original: "Eudaemonikos"</note>, used to mock Alexander for believing himself to be born of a god. Once, when Alexander fell ill, his physician ordered a broth <note>original: "rhophema," a medicinal soup or gruel</note> to be prepared for him. Seeing this, Anaxarchus laughed and said, “So, the hopes of our 'god' now lie in a spoonful of soup!”

# Concerning Alexander and the Lyre of Paris

When Alexander went to Ilium <note>the ancient city of Troy</note>, he spent his time observing the sights with great diligence. Someone approached him and offered to show him the lyre of Alexander <note>original: "Alexandrou," referring to Paris, who was also called Alexander</note>. But the king replied that he would much rather see the lyre of Achilles. For with Achilles’ instrument, he longed for the possession of a good soldier—the one with which he used to sing of the "glories of brave men" <note>a reference to the Iliad, where Achilles sings of the "klea andron"</note>. He said the lyre of Paris, however, played only adulterous melodies, the kind used to seduce and charm women.

# Concerning Ridiculous and Paradoxical Passions

Who would not call these particular passions both ridiculous and strange? There was Xerxes, for instance, when he fell in love with a plane tree. Then there was a certain bold young man of noble birth who fell passionately in love with the statue of Good Fortune <note>original: "Agathe Tyche"</note> that stood by the Prytaneion <note>the seat of government and town hall in a Greek city</note>. He would embrace and kiss the statue. Eventually, driven mad and goaded by his desire, he went before the Council and offered a massive sum of money to purchase the image. When he could not persuade them, he decorated the statue with many ribbons, crowned it with garlands, offered sacrifices, and dressed it in expensive finery. Finally, after weeping a thousand times, he took his own life.

Glaucus likewise fell in love with a cithara-player. Some say a dog fell in love with him, and others say a goose. <note>Large flourish in manuscript</note> In Soloi of Cilicia, a dog fell in love with a boy named Xenophon. Furthermore, it was not only handsome youths who inspired such things; in Sparta, a mackerel <note>original: "kolios," a species of fish</note> reportedly fell in love with a young man.

# Concerning the Pilots of the Carthaginians

The Carthaginians used to keep two pilots on their ships. They said it was absurd for a vessel to have two rudders, yet for the person most vital to the passengers—the one who holds command over the ship—to be alone and isolated, without a second opinion or a partner.

<vocab>Anaxarchus, Alexander, Paris, Ilium, Achilles, Xerxes, Carthaginians, broth, lyre, Prytaneion, statue, mackerel</vocab>

BOOK NINE

->Concerning Pausanias and Simonides<-

They say that at a banquet, Simonides of Ceos <note>A famous Greek lyric poet known for his wisdom and epitaphs</note> and Pausanias the Lacedaemonian <note>The Spartan general who led the Greeks to victory at Plataea but later fell into disgrace</note> were present. Pausanias commanded Simonides to say something wise. Simonides laughed and said, "Remember that you are only human." Pausanias dismissed this remark as trivial; at the time, his arrogance left him in no need of such advice, for he was already consumed by his desire to side with the Persians <note>original: "medizein," the act of favoring the Medes or Persians, considered a betrayal of Greece</note> and was prideful because of his guest-friendship with the Persian King—and perhaps he was also carried away by wine. But later, when he was at the Bronze House <term>Chalkioikos: "The Dweller in the Bronze House," the title of Athena at her bronze-plated temple in Sparta, where Pausanias eventually sought sanctuary</term> and was struggling against starvation, about to die the most painful of deaths, he then remembered the words of Simonides. He cried out three times: "O guest from Ceos, how great a thing your word truly was! But in my folly, I thought it was nothing."

->Concerning Artaxerxes and Darius<-

After Artaxerxes <note>Artaxerxes II, King of Persia</note> had killed his eldest son Darius for plotting against him, his second son, reflecting on his father's deeds, drew his short-sword <note>original: "akinakes," a traditional Persian dagger or short-sword</note> and killed himself in front of the palace.

# AELIAN’S HISTORICAL MISCELLANY
## BOOK TEN

->Concerning Pherenice Watching the Olympic Games<-

<margin>P</margin>
Pherenice brought her son to the Olympic Games to compete. When the judges of the Greeks <term>Hellanodikai: The official organizers and judges of the ancient Olympic Games</term> forbade her from watching the contest <note>Women were traditionally barred from attending the Olympics under penalty of death</note>, she stepped forward and argued her case. She stated that her father was an Olympic victor, as were her three brothers, and now she herself had brought a son to compete in the Olympics. She won over the people and the law that barred women; thus, she watched the Olympic Games.

->Concerning the Self-Control of Eubatas<-

<margin>E</margin>
When Lais <note>A famous and beautiful courtesan of ancient Greece</note> saw Eubatas of Cyrene, she fell intensely in love with him and proposed marriage to him. Eubatas, fearing her schemes against him, promised that he would do so, but he did not actually associate with her.

<summary>This page recounts the tragic realization of the Spartan general Pausanias regarding his own mortality and the beginning of Book Ten, featuring the rule-breaking attendance of Pherenice at the Olympics.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the themes of Book Ten, specifically focusing on the virtue of self-control (sophrosyne) through the story of Eubatas of Cyrene. It then transitions into a series of short natural history observations and historical anecdotes typical of Aelian’s "Varia Historia," including a list of famously thin individuals from Athenian history.</meta>

# BOOK TEN 45

Eubatas <note>Eubatas of Cyrene was a famous Olympic victor in the stadium race (408 BCE)</note>, having lived a temperate life, found himself bound by a promise he had made after his victory. In order to avoid appearing to break his agreements with the woman <note>Referring to the famous courtesan Lais of Corinth, who had asked him to marry her if he won</note>, he had a portrait of Lais painted and brought it back to Cyrene. He claimed that he was "taking Lais with him" and thus had not violated their contract. In return for his self-control <term>sophrosyne: a Greek ideal of excellence of character and soundness of mind, involving temperance and moderation</term>, his lawful wife erected a massive statue of him in Cyrene, rewarding his virtue. ~

### Concerning the peculiar nature of certain animals. ~

As soon as the chicks of partridges poke their feet out of the shell <note>original: "lemmatos," referring to the husk or eggshell</note>, they are immediately ready to run. Similarly, when the ducklings see the light, they swim directly from the labor of birth. It is also said that lion cubs, having scratched at their mothers' wombs with their claws, hurry toward the light. ~

### Concerning the swiftness of Alexander’s deeds. ~

Alexander, the son of Philip, once marched forty-three stadia <note>roughly 8 kilometers or 5 miles</note> in full armor in a single stretch. He then engaged the enemy and defeated them before even allowing his camp to rest. ~

### Concerning tyrants, from the fables of Aesop. ~

This story is told: In a fable of Aesop the Phrygian <note>The legendary storyteller and former slave</note>, when someone asked why a pig cries out so loudly—as if it truly knew what was coming—since it has no oil or other products to give, and immediately dreams of death, it is clear how this benefits those who handle them. Tyrants are similar to this night-owl of Aesop’s; they are suspicious and fear everything, for they know that everyone's eyes and ears are fixed upon their very soul. ~

### Concerning thin men. ~

The comic poets used to mock several men for their extreme thinness: Sannyrion the comic poet, Meletus the tragic poet <note>famous as one of the accusers of Socrates</note>, Cinesias the leader of circular choruses, and Philetas the poet of hexameters. It is said that Archestratus the diviner, when he was captured by enemies and placed upon a scale, was found to weigh only as much as an obol <note>A tiny Greek coin; a humorous exaggeration of his weight</note>. Phanauros was also extremely thin and wore a golden neck-ring <term>maniakēn: a Persian-style torc or necklace</term>. They say that the poet Hipponax was not only small and ugly in body, but also thin. Furthermore, Philippides, against whom there is a speech by Hyperides, was so lean that when a body becomes completely wasted away, they say it has been "Philippid-ized" <note>original: "pephilippidōsthai"</note>, as the poet Alexis bears witness. ~

### Concerning certain Astrologers, and the Great Year:

<meta>This page continues the miscellaneous anecdotes of Book Ten, moving from the physical feats of Alexander and the fables of Aesop to scientific records, moral philosophy, and humorous sketches of ancient life. It maintains the style of "Varia Historia," a collection of brief, educational stories intended to illustrate human nature and history.</meta>

# BOOK TEN

Oenopides of Chios, the astronomer <note>In the 5th century BCE, "astrologos" referred to what we now distinguish as an astronomer—someone who studies the movements of stars and planets.</note>, dedicated a bronze tablet at Olympia. He inscribed upon it the astronomical cycle of fifty or sixty years <note>Historical sources suggest Oenopides calculated a "Great Year" of 59 years to align the solar year with the lunar months.</note>, declaring this period to be the "Great Year" <term>Great Year: a period after which the positions of the sun and moon return to the same relative places in the sky.</term>.

Themetor the Laconian, also an astronomer, erected pillars and recorded the solstices of the sun. He also defined the Great Year as he saw it, stating that it lasted nineteen years <note>original: "twenty years minus one." This is the Metonic cycle, used to synchronize solar and lunar calendars.</note>.

->Concerning Beneficence<-

Aristotle of Cyrene <note>A philosopher and student of Socrates who founded the Cyrenaic school, focusing on ethics.</note> used to say that one should not offer favors to everyone. He argued that either the person receiving the favor will be troubled by trying to repay it, or, if they fail to repay it, they will appear ungrateful.

->That Philipsios was a glutton<-

Philipsios was a glutton and a slave to his stomach. Once, while a stew-pot was simmering in a tavern, he was delighted for a time, and the hearth hissed with the aroma. But as his appetite intensified and he was overcome by his nature—which was, O gods, in a poor state—unable to endure the sight of it, he ordered his servant to buy the pot. When the servant said the seller was charging a high price for a profit, Philipsios replied, "It will be all the more pleasant if I buy it for more." We should remember such stories, not to emulate these men, but so that we might avoid such behavior.

->Concerning the ancient painters<-

When the art of painting was just beginning and was, in a sense, "in its milk and swaddling clothes" <note>A metaphorical way of describing the infancy or primitive beginnings of the craft.</note>, the artists depicted animals so unskillfully that the painters had to write labels on them: "this is an ox," "that is a horse," "this is a tree."

->Concerning Diogenes suffering from a sore shoulder<-

Diogenes <note>Diogenes of Sinope, the famous Cynic philosopher known for his extreme poverty and sharp wit.</note> had a pain in his shoulder, caused, I believe, by a bruise or some other injury. Since he seemed to be in great pain, one of those who disliked him mocked him, saying, "Why don't you just die, Diogenes, and rid yourself of these evils?" But he replied, "Be gone! It is proper for those who know what they must do and say in life to live—the very group you admit I belong to. If," he continued, "you do not know what should be said or done, it is a fine thing for you to die; but for me, who is knowledgeable in these things, it is right to live."

->Archytas’s maxim concerning men<-

Archytas <note>Archytas of Tarentum, a mathematician and Pythagorean philosopher who was a friend of Plato.</note> used to say that just as it is a difficult task to find a fish that has no bones, so it is to find a man who does not possess something murky or "thorny" <note>Meaning a prickly, difficult, or flawed aspect of their character.</note>.

<summary>This page presents a collection of anecdotes covering the origins of Greek astronomy, the early development of painting, and moral reflections from philosophers like Diogenes and Archytas. It contrasts the pursuit of scientific precision with the humorous failings of human appetite and character.</summary>
<keywords>Oenopides, Themetor, Great Year, astronomy, Aristotle of Cyrene, gluttony, Diogenes the Cynic, Archytas, history of art, ancient Greek philosophy</keywords>

# TEN :~

### That Archilochus accuses himself :~

Critias blames Archilochus <note>Archilochus was a famous 7th-century BCE lyric poet known for his biting wit and for being the first to write extensively about his own life.</note> because he spoke so poorly of himself. For if Archilochus had not released such a reputation about himself to the Greeks, we would never have learned that he was the son of a slave woman and a wine-maker. Nor would we know that he left his home in Paros because of poverty and lack of resources to go to Thasos, nor that when he arrived there, he became an enemy to the local people. We would not even know that he spoke ill of friends and enemies alike. Furthermore, we would not have known that he was an adulterer, nor that he was lewd and insolent, had we not learned it from him. And the most shameful thing of all: that he threw away his shield <note>original: "τὴν ἀσπίδα ἀπέβαλεν." In Greek culture, a "shield-thrower" (rhipsaspis) was the ultimate coward, yet Archilochus famously wrote a poem about abandoning his shield to save his life.</note>. Therefore, Archilochus was not a good witness for himself, leaving behind such a name and such a report. These are not my accusations against Archilochus, but those of Critias <note>Critias was one of the "Thirty Tyrants" of Athens and a writer; he often criticized the moral failings of poets.</note>. :~

### On Laziness :~

Socrates used to say that laziness <term>laziness: "argia," which can also mean leisure or a refusal to engage in trade or manual labor.</term> is the sister of freedom. As evidence, he pointed to the Indians and the Persians as being the most free of men; he noted that both groups are very "lazy" when it comes to making money. On the other hand, he said the Phrygians and the Lydians were very industrious, yet they lived in a state of slavery. :~

### Concerning the suitors of the daughters of Aristides and Lysander :~

While Aristides <note>Aristides "the Just" was an Athenian statesman renowned for his integrity and his willing poverty.</note> was still alive, those Greeks who were considered most prominent sought his daughters in marriage. They were not, as it turned out, looking at the life of Aristides, nor did they truly admire his justice. For if they were truly admirers of such virtue, they would have remained committed to the betrothal afterward. Instead, once he died, they decided they wanted nothing to do with the girls. For after his death, it became known that the son of Lysimachus <note>This refers to Aristides himself, who was the son of Lysimachus.</note> was poor. This poverty frightened away those who had previously seemed like suitable—and, in my judgment, most noble—matches. A similar thing happened regarding Lysander <note>A famous Spartan general who, despite his power, died poor.</note>. For when the suitors learned that he had died in poverty, they turned away from the marriage. :~

### Concerning Antisthenes and Diogenes :~

Because Antisthenes <note>The founder of the Cynic school of philosophy.</note> urged many toward philosophy, but no one paid him any attention, he eventually became frustrated and refused to admit anyone. He even tried to drive Diogenes <note>Diogenes of Sinope, the most famous of the Cynics, who lived in a ceramic jar.</note> away from his company. But because Diogenes was more persistent than those who had come before, Antisthenes threatened to strike him with his staff. He even raised it over Diogenes' head. Yet Diogenes did not shrink away; instead, he behaved even more like a philosopher, offering him his...

# BOOK 10 :~

<margin>A</margin>
He <note>Referring to Diogenes the Cynic, who was determined to learn from Antisthenes.</note> said, "Strike as you wish, but I will offer my head, and you will not find a staff so hard that it could drive me away from your school, nor will you ever strike me so hard in your anger as to make me leave."

### On those who became wealthy from public affairs :~

<margin>L</margin>
Cleitarchus <note>A historian known for his accounts of Alexander the Great.</note> says that Themistocles, the son of Neocles, was poor when he first entered politics, having inherited an estate of only three talents <term>talent: a massive unit of weight and currency; three talents was a comfortable but modest sum for an aristocrat.</term>. However, after he oversaw public affairs and was later exiled, his property was confiscated; it was then discovered that he possessed a fortune of more than eighty talents. Similarly, it is said of Cleon that before he came forward to manage public affairs, he had no private means at all, yet afterwards, he left behind an estate of fifty talents.

### On Daphnis of Syracuse, and bucolic songs :~

<margin>D</margin>
Some say Daphnis the cowherd was the beloved of Hermes, while others say he was his son, and that he received his name from a particular event. He was born of a nymph and, once delivered, was exposed in a laurel grove <note>original: "δάφνῃ" (daphne), from which he takes his name.</note>. They say the cows he tended were sisters to the Cattle of the Sun, which Homer mentions in the *Odyssey*. While Daphnis was herding in Sicily, a certain nymph fell in love with him. She consorted with him while he was beautiful and young, just at the age of his "first beard"—that time when the downy hair is the most graceful bloom of beautiful youths, as Homer also says somewhere. They made a pact that he should not approach any other woman, and she threatened that it was his destiny to lose his sight if he transgressed. They held this covenant <term>covenant: "rhetra," a formal or even divinely sanctioned agreement.</term> with one another. Some time later, however, a king’s daughter fell in love with him, and while he was intoxicated, he broke the agreement and slept with the girl. From this event, pastoral songs <note>original: "βουκολικὰ μέλη" (boukolika mele), the origins of "bucolic" or "pastoral" poetry.</note> were first sung, and the theme of these songs was his suffering and the loss of his eyes. It is said that Stesichorus of Himera was the first to begin this type of songwriting.

### On the man who swallowed his own teeth :~

<margin>E</margin>
Eurydamas of Cyrene won a victory in boxing, even though his teeth were knocked out by his opponent. He swallowed them so that his adversary would not realize what had happened <note>In ancient boxing, showing signs of injury was seen as a sign of weakness that would encourage the opponent.</note>.

### On Agesilaus :~

<margin>O</margin>
When the Persian King wrote to Agesilaus <note>Agesilaus II, the famous King of Sparta.</note> asking to be his private friend, Agesilaus wrote back saying that he could not be a private friend to him. However, if the Persian were a friend to the Spartans as a community, it was clear that he would be Agesilaus' friend as well; for Agesilaus considered himself to be counted among the whole of his people.

<summary>This page recounts the persistence of Diogenes the Cynic, examines the suspicious growth of wealth in Athenian politicians, explains the mythological origins of pastoral poetry through the tragedy of Daphnis, and highlights Spartan communal loyalty through King Agesilaus.</summary>
<keywords>Diogenes, Antisthenes, Themistocles, Cleon, corruption, Daphnis, pastoral poetry, Stesichorus, boxing, Eurydamas, Agesilaus, Sparta</keywords>

# BOOK TEN.

### On Plato.~

When Plato was still being carried in the arms of his mother, Perictione, his father Ariston was performing a sacrifice on Mt. Hymettus to the Muses or the Nymphs. While the others were occupied with the sacred rites, she laid Plato down in a nearby thicket of dense, lush myrtle bushes. As he slept, a swarm of bees from Mt. Hymettus settled on his lips and hummed softly, prophesying through this sign the future eloquence of Plato. <note>Bees were often associated with "honeyed" speech in Greek mythology, a sign that the child would become a great orator or poet.</note>

### On Dioxippus.~

In the presence of Alexander and the Macedonians, Dioxippus <note>A famous Olympic champion in the pancratium (a mix of wrestling and boxing) who traveled with Alexander the Great’s court.</note> fought a duel against Corragus, a Macedonian heavy infantryman. Dioxippus, armed only with a club, knocked away Corragus’s spear, then seized the man despite his full suit of armor. Stepping upon his opponent's neck as he lay on the ground, Dioxippus snatched away the sword the soldier wore at his side and killed the infantryman. However, Dioxippus became hated by Alexander for this feat; being a man of sharp mind <note>original: "ἀγχονόος" (agchonoos)</note>, he grew despondent at Alexander's obvious dislike and took his own life.

## AELIAN’S VARIOUS HISTORY

### BOOK ELEVEN.~

### On Oecadmus and the *Palaia*.~

In ancient times, Oecadmus became a lawgiver, having devised on his own the Sicilian custom known as the *Palaia* <term>Palaia: likely referring to an ancient form of wrestling or a specific legal code governing physical discipline in Sicily.</term>.

### On the epics of Oroebantius, Dares, and Melisander.~

The traditions of Troezen claim that the epic poems of Oroebantius of Troezen existed before the time of Homer. They say the same of Dares the Phrygian, whose *Phrygian Iliad* I know is still preserved even today. Melisander of Miletus also wrote about the battle between the Lapiths and the Centaurs <note>A legendary conflict in Greek mythology representing the struggle between civilization and barbarism.</note>.

### On Iccus and wrestling.~

Iccus of Tarentum <note>A renowned athlete and physician of the 5th century BCE.</note> took up wrestling and lived with great self-control during his time of training, maintaining a very disciplined and restricted diet throughout his life.

<summary>This page concludes Book 10 with the omens of Plato's eloquence and the tragic fate of the athlete Dioxippus, then begins Book 11 by highlighting ancient lawgivers, pre-Homeric poets, and the disciplined lifestyle of Iccus of Tarentum.</summary>

...and he died ignorant of the works of Aphrodite. <note>original: "ἀφροδίτης ἀμαθὴς" (aphrodites amathes). In this context, "Aphrodite" is a common euphemism for sexual love or physical desire, suggesting the individual remained chaste until death.</note> ~

### On the Baldness of Agathocles ~

They say that Agathocles, the tyrant of Sicily, was most ridiculously ashamed of the state of his head. As it became bare, with his hair gradually falling out, he felt such shame that he fashioned a covering for his hair out of a myrtle wreath. This was his disguise for his baldness. The people of Syracuse certainly knew about his baldness and were not unaware of this hair-piece he wore; however, they remained silent because of his insane record of daring deeds and impieties. <note>Agathocles (r. 317–289 BC) was a powerful but notoriously cruel ruler of Syracuse.</note> ~

### On Certain People Unjustly Condemned for Sacrilege ~

There was a man named Euthyon at Delphi. Plotting against him, the Delphians secretly placed some of the sacred treasures into his baskets, where he was carrying incense and sacrificial cakes. Having caught him and accused him of being a temple-robber, they led him away to the cliff and threw him off, following the Delphic law. <note>Delphi had a specific legal tradition where those guilty of sacrilege (theosulia) were executed by being cast from the Hyampeia cliff on Mount Parnassus.</note> ~

### On an Adulterer ~

It happened that an adulterer was caught in Thespiae. He was being led through the marketplace in bonds when his companions rescued him. A riot then broke out, resulting in many deaths. <note>Thespiae was an ancient Greek city in Boeotia.</note> ~

### On Lysander and Alcibiades ~

Epecles the Spartan used to say that Sparta could not have endured two Lysanders, and Archestratus the Athenian said that the city of Athens could not have endured two Alcibiadeses. So intolerable, it seems, were the characters of these two men. <note>Lysander was the Spartan general who won the Peloponnesian War; Alcibiades was a brilliant but volatile Athenian statesman. Both were known for their overwhelming ambition and "larger-than-life" personalities that threatened the stability of their respective cities.</note> ~

### On the Death of Hipparchus ~

Hipparchus was killed by Harmodius and Aristogeiton. The cause was that during the Panathenaic festival, he did not allow the sister of Harmodius to carry a basket <note>original: "κανοῦν" (kanoun). Carrying the sacred basket in a procession was a high honor for young noblewomen.</note> to the Goddess according to the local custom, claiming she was not worthy. <note>Hipparchus was one of the tyrants of Athens. This insult to Harmodius's sister sparked the famous assassination in 514 BC, leading to Harmodius and Aristogeiton being celebrated as "Tyrannicides" and founders of Athenian democracy.</note> ~

### On Certain Excellent Men who were Poor and Refused Gifts ~

The best of the Greeks lived in poverty throughout their entire lives. While some may praise wealth, let us look at the best of the Greeks, whose lot in life was poverty. Among these was Aristides, the son of Lysimachus, a man who accomplished many great deeds in war and established fair taxes for the Greeks. <note>Aristides "the Just" was a celebrated Athenian statesman known for his integrity; he was so honest that even after managing the massive treasury of the Delian League, he died too poor to pay for his own funeral.</note> Yet this man, despite his character, was neither...

### BOOK ELEVEN ~

...he left behind enough for his own family, yet Phocion remained poor. When Alexander the Great sent him a hundred talents, Phocion asked for what reason the King was giving him such a gift. When the messengers replied that Alexander considered him alone among the Athenians to be a "noble and good" man, Phocion replied: "Then let him allow me to remain so." <note>original: "καλὸν καὶ ἀγαθὸν" (kalon kai agathon). This was the Greek ideal of a "gentleman," possessing both physical beauty and moral excellence. Phocion (c. 402–318 BC) was an Athenian general known for his extreme frugality and refusal of bribes.</note> ~

**A**nd Epaminondas, the son of Polymnis, was also poor. When Jason sent him fifty pieces of gold, Epaminondas remarked that Jason was "beginning to use his hands for injustice" <note>By attempting to bribe or corrupt him.</note>. Having later borrowed fifty drachmas from one of his fellow citizens, he used them as travel money to the Peloponnese. When he discovered that his shield-bearer had taken money from a prisoner of war, he said to him: "Give me back my shield, and go buy yourself a tavern in which to settle down; for now that you have become rich, you will no longer be willing to face danger." <note>Epaminondas was a Theban general whose victory at Leuctra ended Spartan hegemony. He was famous for dying as poor as he lived.</note> ~

**W**hen Pelopidas’s friends rebuked him for neglecting his finances—a thing they claimed was "profitable for life"—he replied that money was indeed profitable, but pointed to a man named Nicomedes who was lame and crippled, saying money was profitable for *him*. <note>Pelopidas (d. 364 BC) was a Theban statesman. His point was that a healthy, capable man has no need for excess wealth; only the infirm require it for survival.</note> ~

**I**t is said that Scipio, having lived for fifty-four years, never bought nor sold anything; so little, it seems, did he require. When someone showed him a shield that was beautifully decorated, he remarked: "It is proper for a Roman man to place his hopes in his right hand, not in his left." <note>Scipio Aemilianus (185–129 BC). The "right hand" holds the sword (offense/action), while the "left hand" holds the shield (defense/display).</note> ~

**E**phialtes, the son of Sophonides, was extremely poor. When his companions offered him ten talents, he refused them, saying: "This money would force me, out of shame before you, to grant favors that are not just; and if I did not grant those favors, I would appear ungrateful." <note>Ephialtes (d. 461 BC) was an Athenian politician who diminished the power of the Areopagus and was a mentor to Pericles.</note> ~

->About Zoilus :~<-

**Z**oilus of Amphipolis—the man who wrote critiques against Homer, Plato, and others—was a student of Polycrates (the same Polycrates who wrote the accusation against Socrates). This Zoilus was nicknamed the "rhetorical dog." His appearance was as follows: his beard hung long, his head was shaved to the skin, and his cloak reached only above his knees. He was unpleasantly filthy and spent his leisure time making himself hated by many. He was a truly wretched and malicious man. When one of the learned men asked him why he spoke ill of everyone, he replied: "Because I wish to do evil, but I lack the power to do it." <note>Zoilus (c. 400–320 BC) was so famous for his harsh criticisms of Homer that his name became a byword for a spiteful, nitpicking critic.</note> ~

<summary>This page presents moral anecdotes emphasizing the integrity and voluntary poverty of leaders like Phocion and Epaminondas, contrasting their virtue with the spiteful character of the critic Zoilus.</summary>
