### BOOK ELEVEN

->**Concerning Dionysius the Sicilian** :~<-

**D**ionysius the Sicilian <note>Dionysius I (c. 432–367 BC), the tyrant of Syracuse, was a complex figure known for his military power, patronage of the arts, and personal eccentricities.</note> greatly honored the art of medicine. He even practiced it himself, performing surgeries, cauterizing wounds, and carrying out all other medical duties :~

->**Concerning the honey-cake sent to Socrates by Alcibiades** :~<-

**A**lcibiades <note>A brilliant and wealthy Athenian politician and student of Socrates.</note> sent a large and beautifully prepared <term>honey-cake</term> <note>original: "πλακοῦντα" (plakounta), a flat, layered cake typically made with flour, cheese, and honey.</note> to Socrates, intended as a gift from a beloved youth to his teacher. However, Xanthippe <note>Socrates’s wife, who is frequently portrayed in ancient anecdotes as having a legendary temper.</note>, becoming exasperated in her usual manner, kicked the cake, threw it out of the basket, and trampled it underfoot. Socrates simply laughed and said, "Well then, you won't have a share of it either." If anyone thinks I am recording trivialities by telling these stories, they do not realize that the character of a serious man is tested even in such small matters; he shows his virtue by looking down on those things which the masses call the "glory of the table" and "offerings of the feast" :~

->**Concerning the sharp-sighted man in Sicily** :~<-

**T**hey say that a certain Sicilian man’s eyesight became so sharp that from Lilybaeum <note>A city on the western tip of Sicily, modern-day Marsala.</note> he could look across the sea toward Carthage <note>The great North African city in modern Tunisia. The distance across the open sea is roughly 140 miles (225 km), making this a popular ancient "marvel" or tall tale.</note>. His vision was never deceived, and they say he would even count the number of ships sailing out from the harbor of Carthage, and he never once made a mistake :~

# AELIAN’S VARIOUS HISTORY
## BOOK TWELVE
### Concerning Aspasia

<margin>1.</margin> Aspasia, the daughter of Hermotimus, a woman of Phocaea <note>Phocaea was an ancient Ionian Greek city on the western coast of Anatolia.</note>, was raised as an orphan after her mother died in the pangs of childbirth. Because of this, Aspasia was brought up in poverty, yet she lived modestly <note>original: "σωφρονως" (sōphronōs); implying a sense of self-control and moral decency expected of well-bred women.</note> and with great resilience. A dream visited her frequently and prophesied good things for her, hinting at the fortune that awaited her: that she would be joined in marriage to a man of noble and good character.

While she was still a child, a growth <note>original: "φυμα" (phyma); a tumor or swelling.</note> appeared on her face, right under her chin. It was a miserable sight to see, and it caused great distress to both the father and the girl. Her father eventually showed her to a doctor <note>original: "ἰατρός" (iatros)</note>, but the man promised to heal her only if he received three <term>staters</term> <note>A stater was a high-value coin; the demand highlights the family's extreme poverty.</note>. The father said he did not have the money, and the doctor replied that he, likewise, had no medicine to provide. Aspasia was naturally pained by this. She went away and wept, holding a mirror on her knees; seeing her reflection in it, she suffered greatly. 

As she fell asleep from her sorrow, a very timely dream came to her. In her sleep, a dove arrived. It transformed into a woman and said, "Take heart." The woman told her to bid a long farewell to those doctors and their medicines, and instructed her to take the rose petals from the crowns of <term>Aphrodite</term> <note>The goddess of love and beauty.</note>—whichever ones were already dried—grind them up, and sprinkle them on the growth. After the girl heard this and followed the instructions, the growth vanished. 

Aspasia became the most beautiful of her peers once again, as if she had received her youthful bloom from the most beautiful of goddesses. She possessed an abundance of charms such as no other woman of that time. Her hair was golden and slightly curly. She had very large eyes, her nose was slightly hooked <note>original: "ἐπίγρυπος" (epigrupos); an aquiline nose was often considered a mark of royalty or distinction in the ancient world.</note>, and her ears were rather small. Her skin was soft, and her complexion was like that of roses. Because of this, the Phocaeans called her "Milto" <note>original: "Μιλτώ"; meaning "Vermilion" or "Rosy," referring to her natural blush.</note> while she was still a child. Her lips were a deep red, and her teeth were whiter than snow. She also had graceful ankles, and was such as <term>Homer</term> <note>The legendary author of the Iliad and Odyssey, often cited by Aelian as the standard for poetic description.</note> describes the most beautiful women to be, etc.

<summary>This page describes the impoverished childhood of Aspasia of Phocaea and her miraculous healing from a facial disfigurement via a dream sent by Aphrodite. It concludes with a detailed description of her celebrated physical beauty, which earned her the nickname "Milto."</summary>
<keywords>Aspasia of Phocaea, Hermotimus, Aphrodite, miraculous healing, ancient medicine, Milto, Greek beauty standards, Homer</keywords>

# BOOK TWELVE
### (Continued)

...[referring to] her own voice, calling her "beautiful-ankled" <note>The text begins mid-sentence. The author is likely detailing nicknames or epithets given to Aspasia in her youth.</note>. Her voice was sweet and soft, so much so that one might say they were listening to a Siren <note>In Greek mythology, Sirens were creatures whose beautiful songs were famously irresistible.</note> when she spoke. Furthermore, she was entirely free from the typical meddlesomeness and vanity often associated with women. 

For while wealth tends to provide such extravagances, she was poor and raised by a father who was himself impoverished; therefore, she sought nothing elaborate or excessive to adorn her appearance. Eventually, Aspasia was brought to Cyrus, the son of Darius <note>Cyrus the Younger, a Persian prince who attempted to seize the throne from his brother Artaxerxes II.</note>, from Gargettis. She came along with the brother of Artaxerxes, though not of her own will, nor did her father send her willingly; rather, she was taken by force. This was a common fate for women, who were often seized when cities were captured, or through the violence of tyrants and <term>satraps</term> <note>A satrap was a provincial governor in the ancient Persian Empire.</note>. 

One of these officials led her away to Cyrus along with other young maidens. Very quickly, she was preferred over all the other concubines <note>original: "παλλακίδων" (pallakidōn)</note> because of her simple character, her modest manner, and the fact that her beauty was natural and unadorned. What also contributed to her being so greatly loved was her intelligence. Indeed, Cyrus often consulted her as an advisor even on urgent matters, and once he followed her counsel, he never regretted it. 

When Aspasia first came before Cyrus, he happened to have finished his dinner and was about to begin drinking in the Persian fashion. For the Persians do not consider it wise to merely fill themselves with wine; they devote themselves to the drinking and the accompanying courses with great intensity, as if they were competing against a rival in a contest. While the drinking bout was underway, four Greek maidens were led in to Cyrus, and among them was the most beautiful Aspasia. 

They were all elegantly prepared; three of them had been attended to by their own women who had accompanied them. Their hair was already braided, and their faces were decorated with pigments and cosmetics <note>original: "φαρμάκοις" (pharmakois); in this context, it refers to face paints or ointments rather than medicine.</note>. They had also been instructed by their attendants on how to please Cyrus: how they should flatter him, how they should not turn away when he approached, how they should not recoil if he touched them, and how they should submit if he kissed them. These were indeed the lessons and practices of courtesans <note>original: "ἑταιρικὰ" (hetairika); referring to the *hetairai*, sophisticated companions or high-status courtesans in ancient Greece.</note>, the work of women who use their beauty to dazzle. Each of these three, therefore, strove to surpass the others in allure. 

But Aspasia would not agree to put on a costly <term>tunic</term> <note>A chiton, the standard draped garment of the Greeks.</note>, nor would she deign to wrap herself in a multi-colored cloak; she would not even submit to being bathed. Instead, calling upon all the gods, she cried out in Greek, shouting her own name as a free-born woman and the name of her father, and she called down curses upon herself for her predicament.

<summary>This page describes how Aspasia of Phocaea was captured and brought to the court of Cyrus the Younger. Unlike the other captives who used cosmetics and flattery to win the prince's favor, Aspasia maintained her modest, natural appearance and protested her loss of freedom.</summary>

<keywords>Aspasia of Phocaea, Cyrus the Younger, Persians, cosmetics, courtesans, Greek maidens, satrap, modesty, intelligence</keywords>

# BOOK TWELVE

### TWELFTH BOOK

...and of the children, believing that she was submitting to a clear and admitted state of slavery, she initially resisted the expensive and **purple** <note>original: "πορφύραν" (porphyran) — a dye so expensive in the ancient world it was reserved for royalty</note> clothing that was unusual for her body. However, having been struck by necessity, she put them on, yielding to the commands despite her pain. She did so not with a gentle spirit, but with a heavy heart, forced into what was happening. Now, the other women who had come before her looked directly at Cyrus, smiled at him, and feigned a cheerful expression. But Aspasia looked downward, her face filled with a deep, crimson blush, and her eyes were full of tears; it was clear to all that she was ashamed of what was being done.

When Cyrus ordered the women to sit near him, the others obeyed without the slightest hesitation. But the woman from Phocaea <note>original: "ἡ φωκαΐς" (hē phokaïs) — referring to Aspasia's hometown, Phocaea in Ionia</note> paid no attention to the command until **Sakapas**, the man who had brought her, forced her to sit down. As Cyrus looked them over, examining their eyes, their cheeks, and their fingers, the other women allowed it, but she would not endure it. Indeed, when Cyrus touched her with only the tip of his finger, she cried out and said he would "howl with pain" <note>original: "οἰμώξεσθαι" (oimōzesthai) — a common Greek idiom meaning "you'll regret it" or "you'll pay for it"</note> for doing such things.

Cyrus was highly delighted by this, especially when she stood up and tried to run away. When he touched her hem <note>The Greek text "μοιρῶν" is debated; it likely refers to a part of her garment or hair</note>, the son of Darius <note>Cyrus the Younger</note> was utterly amazed by her noble spirit. This was not typical of a Persian, but turning to the man who had purchased her, he said, "This is the only woman you have brought who is truly free and incorruptible. The others are like merchants' wares, both in their appearance and even more so in their character." 

Because of this, Cyrus loved her more than any woman he had ever met. In time, Cyrus won her over, and he was loved by her in return. Their friendship <note>original: "φιλία" (philia) — meaning deep affection or love</note> reached such a point that they were nearly equal in honor, and their relationship was not at all different from the harmony and moderation of a **Greek marriage**. 

Consequently, the fame of his love for Aspasia reached Ionia and all of Greece; even the Peloponnese was filled with stories about Cyrus and her. The report even reached the **Great King** <note>Artaxerxes II, the King of Persia and Cyrus’s brother</note>. It was believed that Cyrus did not care to be with any other woman after her. Because of these events, throughout Asia, people remembered Aspasia through her "golden visions" <note>original: "χρυσίων φασμάτων" — a reference to the divine omens she received</note>, and they spoke of her as more precious than gold. She herself believed from the beginning that the goddess had been her protector, and she offered sacrifices of initiation and thanksgiving to **Aphrodite**. First, she commissioned a golden statue <term>eidolon</term> <note>A cult image or representation of a deity</note> of great size to be made for the goddess.

# BOOK XII

->BOOK XII.<-

...she was considered to be a living image of **Aphrodite** <note>The Greek goddess of love and beauty</note>, and Cyrus even placed a stone-set dove <note>original: "πελειάδα" (peleiada) — the dove was a bird sacred to Aphrodite</note> beside her. Every day he sought her favor with sacrifices and words of praise. He also sent many beautiful gifts to her father, **Hermotimus**, and made him appear to be a wealthy man. She lived with great modesty <note>original: "σωφροσύνῃ" (sophrosynē) — a core Greek virtue implying self-control and moderation</note>, as both the Greek and Persian women testify.

Once, a necklace was brought to Cyrus from Thessaly, sent by **Scopas the Younger** <note>A member of the ruling Skopadae family in Thessaly, known for their wealth</note>. The gift was an ornament from Sicily belonging to Scopas. This necklace was thought to possess marvelous craftsmanship and was wrought with great intricacy. Everyone to whom Cyrus showed it was filled with wonder. Yielding to the midday heat, he went to Aspasia’s quarters in the middle of the day; finding her sleeping on her bedding, he lay down quietly beside her and remained still. He stayed awake while she slept. When she finally woke and saw Cyrus, she embraced him and showed him affection in her usual manner.

Taking the necklace out of its small chest, he showed it to her, remarking that it was worthy of either a king’s daughter or a queen mother. When she agreed, he said, "Behold, I give it to you then; let me see your neck adorned with such luxury." But she, not unaccustomed to gifts but speaking most wisely and with great cultivation, replied: "And how could I dare to put on a gift worthy of **Parysatis** <note>The powerful mother of Cyrus the Younger and King Artaxerxes II</note>, the woman who gave you birth? Instead, Cyrus, send this to her; I will provide you with a beautiful neck even without such ornaments." 

Aspasia expressed her thoughts with a high-mindedness and a royal spirit that surpassed what is usually seen in women. For women are often terribly fond of finery. Cyrus, delighted by her answer, kissed Aspasia and wrote down every detail of what had been done and said in a letter, which he sent to his mother along with the necklace. When Parysatis received the gift, she was no less pleased by the words sent to her than by the gold itself. In return for this, she rewarded Aspasia with great and royal gifts. She was particularly gladdened by the fact that, although Aspasia was held in the highest esteem by her son, she did not wish to be seen as a rival to the woman who bore him. 

Aspasia praised the gifts but said she did not need them, as a great deal of money had already come to her through other presents. She sent them back to Cyrus, saying: "Since you have need of many men <note>Cyrus was at this time secretly recruiting a mercenary army, the "Ten Thousand," to seize the Persian throne from his brother</note>, these [funds] could be..."

<summary>This page recounts Aspasia's rise to influence through her modesty and strategic refusal of a luxury necklace, which she suggested be given to Cyrus's mother, Parysatis. This act of deference secured her the favor of the Queen Mother and cemented her status at court.</summary>

...profitable. To me, you and he are beloved and modest, and for these reasons she emulated Cyrus as was fitting. Undeniably, this woman was admired, both for the beauty of her body and even more for the nobility of her soul. 

But when Cyrus was killed in the battle against his brother <note>The Battle of Cunaxa (401 BC), a failed rebellion by Cyrus the Younger against Artaxerxes II</note>, and Cyrus’s camp was captured, she was taken along with the other spoils of war. She did not fall into enemy hands by mere chance or accident; rather, the King—**Artaxerxes**—searched for her with great care. He already knew of her fame and her virtue <note>original: "ἀρετὴν" (aretēn) — a term implying both moral excellence and reputation</note>. 

When they brought her to him bound in chains, he was indignant. He threw the guards who were still holding her into prison and ordered that she be given costly ornaments. But she, upon hearing this, wailed and wept; she was forced with great difficulty to put on the royal robe provided by the King, for she was mourning Cyrus intensely. Once dressed, she appeared the most beautiful of women. Immediately, **Artaxerxes** was inflamed with passion; he was struck by her beauty and placed her first among all his women, honoring her to the highest degree. 

Through these actions he sought to please her, hoping to persuade her to forget Cyrus and teaching her to love him no less than she had his brother. He achieved this hope only late and slowly. Her deep-seated devotion to Cyrus had melted into **Aspasia’s** heart, acting like a most stubborn love-charm <note>original: "φίλτρον" (philtron) — a potion or spell; here used metaphorically for her unbreakable affection</note>.

Some time later, **Tiridates the Eunuch** died. He was the most beautiful person in Asia and had reached the prime of his youth. He ended his life while still quite a young man, just emerging from childhood. It was said that the King loved him most shamefully <note>The author, Aelian, often injects moral judgments regarding the private lives of the Persian kings</note>. Because of this, the King mourned most heavily and suffered terribly. Public mourning was declared throughout all Asia, as everyone sought to please the King. However, no one dared to approach him or offer comfort, for they believed his grief over the loss was incurable. 

After three days had passed, **Aspasia** put on mourning clothes and, while the King was away at his bath, she stood there weeping, her eyes fixed on the ground. When he saw her, he was astonished and asked why she had come. She replied: "O King, seeing you in such grief and pain, I have come to comfort you, if you are willing; but if you are angry, I shall go back." The Persian <note>King Artaxerxes II</note> was delighted by her care and ordered her to go up to his chamber and wait for him. She did as she was told, and when he returned...

# BOOK TWELVE

->BOOK TWELVE<-

...and how the eunuch’s grey adornment <note>original: "πολιὰν" (polian) — likely referring to a wig or an elaborate hair-styling provided by the King's eunuch</note> with the black clasp on **Aspasia** suited her, and how it became the youth <note>referring to the eunuch servant</note>; and her beauty shone even more brightly for her lover. Once he had been captured by these charms, he esteemed her until her prime faded. She was so composed that he actually obeyed her. By pleasing her, he was guided by her; she was the only person in all Asia—not only among the women, but they say even among the King’s sons and relatives—who could comfort **Artaxerxes**. She healed the suffering of his grief, as the King yielded to her care and was restored to life by her consolation.

### ~ On the Muses ~

**N**o sculptor or painter has ever represented the daughters of Zeus <note>The Muses, the goddesses of the arts and sciences</note> as armed figures. This confirms the belief that the life dedicated to the **Muses** must be both peaceful and gentle.

### ~ On Epaminondas, Daiphantus, and Iolaidas ~

**W**hen **Epaminondas** <note>The famous Theban general and statesman</note> was mortally wounded at Mantinea <note>The Battle of Mantinea (362 BC), where Thebes fought Sparta</note>, he was carried to his tent still breathing. He called for **Daiphantus**, intending to appoint him as his successor to lead the army, but the men told him that Daiphantus was dead. Then he called for **Iolaidas**; when he was told that he too had died, he advised the Thebans to make peace with the enemy and reach an agreement, as no capable general was left in Thebes to command them.

### ~ On Sesostris ~

**T**he Egyptians say that **Sesostris** <note>A legendary King of ancient Egypt, often identified with Ramses II</note> was educated in his wisdom by **Hermes** <note>The Greek name for the Egyptian god Thoth, the patron of writing and intellect</note> before all others.

### ~ On Lais ~

**L**ais the courtesan, as **Aristophanes of Byzantium** tells us, was also called "The Axe" <term>Axine: a nickname implying she was sharp, predatory, or "chopped" through her lovers' fortunes</term>. This epithet was evidence of the harshness of her character.

### ~ On Marius and Cato, the Fathers ~

**I**t is right to laugh at those who think highly of themselves because of their ancestors. Among the Romans, people do not even know the father of **Marius**, yet we admire Marius himself for his great deeds. As for **Cato the Elder**, one would also have to go searching to find who his father was.

### ~ On Alexander and Hephaestion ~

**A**lexander crowned the tomb of **Achilles**, and **Hephaestion**

# BOOK TWELVE

...in the case of Patroclus, hinting that he [Hephaestion] was also the beloved of Alexander, just as Patroclus was of Achilles.

->On the plot of Cleomenes against Archonides<-

**Cleomenes** the Spartan took **Archonides** as one of his companions and made him a partner in his affairs. He swore that if he gained power, he would do everything "with his [Archonides'] head" <note>original: "σὺν τῇ αὐτοῦ κεφαλῇ" — This is a grim play on words: in Greek, "with one's head" can mean "together with that person" (sharing his life/thoughts) or, literally, with the physical head.</note>. Once he held the rule, he killed his companion, cut off his head, and placed it in a jar of honey. Whenever he was about to do anything, he would lean over the vessel and tell the head what he was doing, claiming that he was not breaking his agreement or his oath, but was indeed deliberating "with the head of Archonides."

->How Timesias willingly left his homeland<-

**Timesias** of **Clazomenae** <note>A Greek city on the coast of Ionia, in modern-day Turkey</note> acted as a virtuous leader for the Clazomenians, but he was followed by that envy that so often pursues good men. This envy began to overpower Timesias. At first, he cared little for being envied, but they say this specific event drove him from his fatherland: He was passing by a schoolhouse when the boys, dismissed by their teacher, were playing. A quarrel broke out between two boys over a boundary line in their game. One of them swore, "I wish I could smash Timesias' brain like that!" When Timesias heard this, he perceived it as a sign of unrestrained envy and felt he was so hated by the citizens that even the children despised him, let alone the men. Thus, he departed his homeland of his own accord.

->That the Aeginetans were the first to strike coinage<-

The people of **Aegina** <note>An island in the Saronic Gulf, once a major rival to Athens</note> were once the most powerful among the Greeks. Having attained prosperity over time—and for good reason—they possessed a great naval force and were preeminent. Furthermore, they proved themselves brave in the **Persian Wars**, and for this reason they were awarded the prize for excellence. They were also the first to strike coinage, and from them the currency was called "Aeginetan."

->On the Palatine Hill, the Temple of Fire, and the Altar<-

The **Romans** established an altar and a temple of Fire <note>original: "πυρσοῦ" (pyrsou) — likely referring to the Temple of Vesta, where the sacred flame of Rome was kept</note> beneath the **Palatine Hill** <note>original: "Παλλαντίῳ" (Pallantiō) — the most central of the seven hills of Rome</note>.

->On the adulterer caught in Crete<-

In **Gortyn**, **Crete**, an adulterer caught in the act was brought before the authorities. Once convicted, he was crowned with wool. This "crown" was a public accusation against him, signifying that he was unmanly, effeminate, "good for women," and a man of vanity <note>original: "ἐπιπλάσητο" (epiplasēto) — suggesting one who is artificial, overly made-up, or fraudulent</note>.

<summary>A collection of historical anecdotes from Aelian, covering the treachery of Cleomenes of Sparta, the self-imposed exile of Timesias, the naval and economic power of Aegina, and Roman and Cretan social customs.</summary>

# BOOK TWELVE

...publicly as one of the fifty saviors; yet he lived in extreme poverty <note>original: ἀλιμώτατος (alimotatos) — literally 'most hungry,' implying he died without wealth despite his honors</note>, and took no further part in public life.

->How the courtesan Gnathaena silenced a chatterbox<-

**Gnathaena** <note>A famous Athenian courtesan known for her sharp wit</note> was visited by a man from the **Hellespont** <note>The narrow strait in northwestern Turkey linking the Aegean to the Sea of Marmara</note>. He was her lover and boasted of his own fame. While they were drinking, he talked excessively and seemed quite vulgar. **Gnathaena** interrupted him: "So you say you have come from the Hellespont?" When he agreed, she said: "And how is it you did not know the most important of the cities there?" When he asked which one that might be, she replied: "**Sigeum**" <note>A clever pun: Sigeion was a real city in the Troad, but the name is nearly identical to the Greek word for 'silence' (σιγή). She was telling him to be quiet.</note>, and through this melodic play on the name, she effectively silenced him.

->On those of beautiful appearance<-

They say that **Alcibiades** was the most charming and beautiful of the Greeks, and **Scipio** <note>Likely Scipio Africanus the Elder or Younger, both noted for their handsome features</note> of the Romans. They say that **Demetrius Poliorcetes** <note>Demetrius "the Besieger," a king of Macedonia famous for his striking looks</note> also laid claim to such beauty. **Alexander**, the son of Philip, is said to have been effortlessly beautiful. His hair was swept back and blond, and they say there was something formidable <note>original: φοβερόν (phoberon) — 'striking' or 'awe-inspiring'</note> in **Alexander's** appearance. When **Homer** wishes to demonstrate beauty through nature, he compares it to trees, saying: "He shot up like a sapling" <note>A quote from the Iliad, 18.56, describing Achilles</note>.

->On certain men who played with children<-

They say that **Hercules** rested from the exhaustion of his labors by engaging in games; the son of **Zeus** and **Alcmene** <note>Hercules' parents</note> played very vigorously with children. Our own **Euripides** <note>The famous Greek playwright</note> alludes to this when he portrays the god [Hercules] saying: "I play, for I always love a change from my labors." He says this while holding a child. **Socrates** too was once caught by **Alcibiades** playing with **Lamprocles** <note>Socrates' eldest son</note> while he was still an infant.

**Agesilaus** <note>A famous King of Sparta</note> once mounted a reed like a horse and "rode" with his son when the boy was small. To a man who laughed at him, he said: "Be silent for now; but when you yourself become a father, then you may speak your mind to other fathers." Even **Archytas of Tarentum** <note>A renowned Pythagorean philosopher and statesman</note>, though he was a great politician and philosopher and had many servants, took great delight in his children and played with the children born to his household slaves <note>original: οἰκοτρίβων (oikotribon) — slaves born and raised within the household</note>; he especially loved to join them in their games and playthings.

<meta>This page continues Book Twelve of Claudius Aelianus's "Historical Miscellany." # BOOK TWELVE

### Why Alexander felt threatened by the excellence of his men : ~

Alexander felt a sense of threat from **Perdiccas** <note>One of Alexander's primary generals who became regent after his death</note> because he was so warlike; from **Lysimachus** because he was an excellent strategist; and from **Seleucus** <note>Founder of the Seleucid Empire</note> because of his great courage. The ambition of **Antigonus** troubled him, while the leadership qualities of **Attalus** put him on his guard. Finally, he was wary of **Ptolemy** <note>The general who later became Pharaoh of Egypt</note> because of his cleverness and skill.

### On Demetrius visiting the house of a courtesan : ~

It is told that **Demetrius** <note>Demetrius Poliorcetes, "the Besieger," known for his flamboyant lifestyle</note>, despite ruling over so many nations, used to visit the house of the courtesan **Lamia** <note>A famous flute-player and mistress of Demetrius</note> in full armor and wearing his royal diadem. It would have been shameful enough for him to simply summon the woman to his own home, yet he went to her house with great affection. However, it is said that Lamia preferred **Theodorus** the flute-player over Demetrius; for when Lamia sent for Theodorus, he actually ignored her invitation <note>This highlights the irony that a common musician held more power over the courtesan's heart than a powerful king</note>.

### That Phaon was beautiful : ~

Because **Phaon** was the most beautiful of men, **Aphrodite** <note>The goddess of love</note> hid him among **lettuces** <note>original: θριδακίναις (thridakinais) — ancient Greeks associated lettuce with impotence and the gardens of Adonis</note>. Another account says that he was a ferryman and followed that trade. Aphrodite once arrived wishing to be ferried across; he received her gladly, though he did not know who she was, and with great care he took her wherever she wished to go. In return, the goddess gave him an <term>alabastron</term> <note>A small pottery or glass vessel used for holding oil or perfume</note> containing an ointment. By rubbing himself with this, Phaon became the most beautiful of men, and the women of **Mytilene** <note>The capital of the island of Lesbos</note> fell desperately in love with him. In the end, however, he was caught in the act of adultery and put to death.

### Concerning Sappho : ~

As for the poetess **Sappho**, the daughter of **Scamandronymus**, even **Plato**, the son of Ariston, records that she was "wise" <note>Plato famously referred to Sappho as the "Tenth Muse"</note>. I also learn that there was another Sappho in **Lesbos**, who was a courtesan and not a poetess.

### On the nightingale and the swallow : ~

**Hesiod** says that the nightingale is the only bird that never sleeps, but remains awake through everything. The swallow, he says, does not remain entirely awake, but has lost half of its sleep. They pay this penalty because of the tragedy that occurred in **Thrace** and the crimes committed during that lawless meal <note>Refers to the myth of Tereus, who raped his sister-in-law Philomela. In revenge, his wife Procne killed their son Itys and served him as a meal to Tereus. The gods turned them all into birds: a nightingale, a swallow, and a hoopoe</note>.

### On Spartan women : ~

Those Spartan mothers who learned that their sons had fallen in battle would go themselves to inspect the wounds...

...they inspected their bodies, both the front and the back. And if the wounds were mostly on the front <note>Wounds on the front indicated the soldier died facing the enemy</note>, the mothers, being proud and maintaining a solemn expression, looked sternly upon their children as they carried them to their **ancestral tombs**. But if the wounds were otherwise <note>i.e., on the back, suggesting they were struck while fleeing</note>, the mothers, feeling ashamed and wailing, and hurrying to hide themselves as much as possible, departed. They left the corpses to be buried in the **common grave** <note>original: πολυανδρίῳ — a mass grave for common soldiers or those without honor</note>, or they carried them secretly to their family burial plots. ~

### On the strength of Titormus and Milo, and a certain proverb ~

They say that **Milo of Croton** <note>A legendary six-time Olympic wrestling champion</note>, who was very proud of his physical strength, happened upon the herdsman **Titormus**. Seeing that Titormus was of massive stature, Milo wished to test his strength. Titormus claimed he possessed no great power. However, going down to the river **Euenus** and stripping off his cloak, he took up a massive stone. First, he pulled it toward himself, then pushed it away; he did this two or three times. After this, he lifted it as high as his knees. Finally, lifting it upon his shoulders, he carried it for eight **fathoms** <note>original: ὀργυιὰς — a measurement of roughly 6 feet, the span of outstretched arms</note> and threw it. **Milo of Croton** could barely even roll the stone. 

Then, for a second feat, Titormus went to his herd and, standing in their midst, seized the largest bull—which was a wild creature—by the foot. The bull struggled to run away but could not. As another bull passed by, Titormus caught it by the foot with his other hand, holding both similarly. Witnessing this, Milo stretched his hands toward the heavens and cried out, "O Zeus, have you sired a second **Heracles** <note>The Greek hero Hercules, known for his twelve labors and divine strength</note> for us?" For this reason, they say the proverb originated there: "**This one, this one is a Heracles.**" <note>original: οὗτος οὗτος Ἡρακλῆς</note> ~

### On the bravery of the Celts <note>The printed text reads "ἀτολμίας" (cowardice), but this is almost certainly a scribe's error for "εὐτολμίας" (bravery), as the text describes their extreme courage.</note> ~

I hear that the **Celts** are the most danger-loving of men. The themes of all the songs they compose are about the men who have died beautifully in war. They go into battle wearing crowns, and they even raise **trophies** <note>Monuments built on the battlefield to commemorate victory</note>. In this, they both take pride in their deeds and leave behind reminders of their excellence in the **Greek fashion**. They consider it so shameful to flee that they will not even run out of their houses when they are collapsing or falling down upon them. Many are even caught by fire and consumed rather than abandon their homes. Many also stay their ground against the flooding sea...

# BOOK TWELVE

...against the tides. There are even some who take up their arms and rush into the waves, meeting their force with naked swords and brandishing spears, as if they were capable of frightening or wounding the sea itself.

### On the Luxurious Living and Gluttony of Smindyrides

They say that **Smindyrides the Sybarite** <note>Sybaris was a Greek city in southern Italy famous for its extreme wealth and hedonism</note> reached such a level of luxury <note>original: τρυφῆς — meaning soft living or extravagance</note> that when he went to **Sicyon** as a suitor for **Agariste**, the daughter of **Cleisthenes**, he brought with him a thousand cooks, a thousand fowlers, and a thousand fishermen.

### Many Have Benefited and Derived Profit from Certain Great Men

Thus, **Odysseus** benefited from **Alcinous**, **Achilles** from **Chiron** <note>The wise Centaur who tutored many heroes</note>, **Patroclus** from **Achilles**, **Agamemnon** from **Nestor**, **Telemachus** from **Menelaus**, and **Hector** from **Polydamas**. Those among the Trojans who listened to him benefited from **Antenor**. The followers of **Pythagoras** benefited from Pythagoras himself. Those who associated with **Democritus** <note>The "laughing philosopher" who proposed the atomic theory</note> gained much from the relationship. 

If the **Athenians** had paid attention to **Socrates**, they would have been happy in all things, provided they had practiced philosophy. **Hiero**, the son of Dinomenes <note>Tyrant of Syracuse</note>, benefited from **Simonides of Ceos** <note>One of the great lyric poets of Greece</note>; **Polycrates** <note>Tyrant of Samos</note> from **Anacreon**; **Xenophon** son of Gryllus <note>The soldier and historian</note> from his teachers; and **Antigonus** from **Zeno** <note>Zeno of Citium, the founder of Stoicism</note>. 

And lest I forget those related to my own history, just as I have mentioned the Greeks—for it matters to me, as I am of the same heritage <note>original: ἀγχερμαῖός εἰμι — literally "a neighbor to Hermes," indicating a shared cultural or intellectual lineage</note>—**Lucius Anicius** benefited from the man of **Ascalon**. **Maecenas** <note>The famous patron of poets under Augustus</note> benefited from **Areius**, **Cicero** from **Apollonius**, and **Augustus** from **Athenodorus**. **Plato**, who is a savior to me <note>Aelian views Plato as a spiritual or intellectual guide</note>, says that even **Zeus** had a counselor; who it was and in what manner they advised him, we learn from his writings.

### On Certain Drunkards

The heaviest drinkers among men, so it is said, were **Xenagoras of Rhodes**, whom they nicknamed "The Jar" <note>original: Ἀμφορέα — an amphora, a large storage vessel</note>, and **Heracleides the Boxer**, and **Proteas**, the son of Lanike and childhood companion of **King Alexander**. It is said that Alexander himself drank more than any other man.

### That Hercules was Merciful to His Enemies

They say that **Hercules** behaved with great mercy toward his enemies. For he was the first since the beginning of time to return the bodies of the dead under a truce <note>original: ὑποσπόνδους — meaning "under a libation," a formal treaty allowing the recovery of the fallen</note> so that they might be buried. Before that, men disregarded the slain, leaving them to be a feast for dogs—as **Homer** also describes...

# BOOK TWELVE

...to endure five dangers <note>The Greek text here is slightly corrupt; it likely refers to enduring trials</note>, and to become the subjects of songs.

->On the Leokoreion in Athens.<-

**T**he **Leokoreion** in **Athens** was the name given to the sanctuary <note>original: τέμενοϛ — a piece of land dedicated to a god or hero</note> of the daughters of **Leos**: **Praxithea**, **Theope**, and **Eubule**. The story goes that these women were put to death for the sake of the city of Athena, after Leos offered them up in accordance with an oracle from **Delphi**. For the oracle stated that the city could not be saved in any other way unless those women were sacrificed.

->What Plato said regarding the extravagance<-
->of the people of Akragas.<-

**P**lato, the son of Ariston, having seen the people of **Akragas** <note>A wealthy Greek city in Sicily, modern-day Agrigento</note> both building expensively and dining in a similar fashion, remarked that the people of Akragas build as if they are going to live forever, but dine as if they are going to die tomorrow. **Timaeus** <note>A Greek historian from Sicily</note> also says that they used silver oil-flasks and scrapers <note>original: στλεγγίσιν — "strigils," used by athletes to scrape oil and sweat from the skin</note>, and that they even had couches made entirely of ivory.

->On the drunkenness of the Tarentines and the luxury of the Cyrenians.<-

**I**t was the custom of the **Tarentines** <note>Inhabitants of Taras, a Greek colony in Southern Italy</note> to begin drinking from early morning and to be drunk by the time the market was full <note>original: πλήθουσαν ἀγοράν — usually mid-morning, when business was most active</note>. The people of **Cyrene** <note>A Greek city in modern Libya</note> drifted into such a state of luxury that they invited **Plato** to be their lawgiver. They say he refused them because of their long-standing laziness <note>original: ῥᾳθυμίαν — a lack of discipline or "easiness of spirit"</note>. **Eupolis** <note>A famous Athenian playwright of Old Comedy</note> also confirms this in his play *The Marikas*, noting that even the most frugal among them possessed a signet ring worth ten minas <note>A mina was a high value of currency; ten minas was an enormous sum for a single ring</note>. Everyone marveled at the exquisite engraving of the ring.

->On various Greek wines.<-

**C**ome now, and I shall list for you the names of Greek wines that were highly esteemed by the ancients. They called one **Pamnian**, which was sacred to **Demeter**; another was **Chian** wine from the island of Chios; others were **Thasian**, **Lesbian**, and in addition to these, a certain sweet wine called **Propon** <note>original: πρόπων — literally "the forerunner," perhaps referring to its early harvest or its character</note>, named for its taste. There was also a **Cretan** wine, and the **Syracusans** had one called **Polion**, named after a local king. They also drank **Koan** wine, calling it by that name, and **Rhodian** wine, naming it in the same manner.

**O**r <note>Probably a variant or error for Ποῖ (Whither) or Ἢ (Or)</note> were those things not proofs of luxury among the Greeks? For they even mixed their wines with perfume <note>original: μύρῳ — scented oil</note> and drank it so, which necessitated such a mixture; this wine was called **myrrh-wine** <note>original: μυῤῥινίτης — myrrhinites</note>. **Philippus**, the comic poet, mentions it.

<sig>P</sig>

# BOOK TWELVE

<meta>catchword: twenty.</meta>

### On the clothing and footwear of Pythagoras, Empedocles, Hippias, and Gorgias.

**Pythagoras of Samos** chose to wear white clothing, and he wore a golden crown and trousers <note>original: ἀναξυρίδας (anaxyrides) — long, patterned trousers typically associated by the Greeks with Persians and other Eastern peoples</note>. **Empedocles of Akragas** used sea-purple <note>original: ἁλουργεῖ — a costly dye derived from sea snails, signifying high status</note> garments and bronze sandals. **Lyris** <note>Possibly a reference to a painter or a lost author</note> depicts **Hippias** and **Gorgias** in purple clothing as well.

### That the Romans did not accept the plot of Pyrrhus's physician.

**Cineas**, the physician of **Pyrrhus** <note>Pyrrhus was the King of Epirus who fought the Romans in the 3rd century BCE; historians more commonly name the diplomat Cineas and the physician Nicias, but Aelian here identifies the physician as Cineas</note>, they say, wrote secretly to the **Roman Senate**. He requested money and promised to kill Pyrrhus with poisons, but the Romans did not accept the offer. For the Romans know how to be noble through virtue, not to overcome their enemies through craft, villainy, or treachery. Indeed, they even revealed Cineas's intention to Pyrrhus himself.

### On the loves of Pausanias and Apelles.

Many loves of the ancients have been committed to our memory, and this one is not the least among them. **Pausanias** <note>Likely referring to the 5th-century BCE Spartan general</note> was in love with his own wife, while **Apelles** <note>The most famous painter of ancient Greece</note> fell in love with the concubine of **Alexander**, whose name was **Pankaste**. She was from **Larissa** by birth, and they say she was the first woman Alexander ever lived with.

### On Periander, Miltiades, the Sibyls, and the Bakides.

Note that there were two men named **Periander**: one was the Wise Man, and the other was the tyrant. There were also three men named **Miltiades**: the one who settled the **Chersonese**, the son of **Cypselus**, and the son of **Cimon** <note>The famous general who fought at the Battle of Marathon</note>. There are four **Sibyls** <note>term: Sibyl — a woman in antiquity believed to possess powers of prophecy</note>: the Erythraean, the Samian, the Egyptian, and the Sardian. Others say there are six more, making ten in all, including the Cumaean and the Jewish Sibyls. There are three men named **Bakis** <note>term: Bakis — a generic name for inspired male prophets, similar to the female Sibyls</note>: the Greek, the Athenian, and the Arcadian.

### On the number of Niobe’s children.

The ancients do not seem to agree with one another regarding the number of **Niobe's** children <note>Niobe famously boasted of her many children, leading the goddess Leto to send Apollo and Artemis to kill them</note>. **Homer** says there were six sons and as many daughters. **Lasus** says twice seven (fourteen), while **Hesiod** says nineteen—unless those verses are not actually by Hesiod, but are falsely attributed to him, as many others are. **Alcman** says ten, **Mimnermus** says twenty, and **Pindar**

<summary>Aelian describes the distinctive dress of famous philosophers and sophists, the honorable conduct of Romans toward King Pyrrhus, and clarifies historical figures with shared names or uncertain mythological details.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Empedocles, Romans, Pyrrhus, Apelles, Alexander the Great, Periander, Miltiades, Sibyls, Bakis, Niobe, ancient poets</keywords>

# BOOK 4

### On Alexander when he lacked provisions, and how they avoided hunger because of the smoke of a deer. <note>The Greek title mentions "smoke of a deer" (καπνὸν ἐλάφου), but the text itself discusses silphium and the lifestyle of the Bactrians; this may be a scribal error in the original manuscript or a lost reference.</note>

**Alexander**, when he was pursuing **Bessus** <note>Bessus was the Persian satrap who assassinated Darius III and was pursued by Alexander through the mountains of central Asia</note> and found himself and his men in want of food, and with their pack-animals failing, they began to eat raw meat. However, **Silphium** <note>original: Σίλφιον (silphion) — a plant highly prized in antiquity for medicine and seasoning, now believed to be extinct</note>, which grew there in abundance, helped them by making the meat tender and edible.

In **Bactria**, the soldiers occupied the villages themselves, where the inhabitants lived amidst the smoke <note>Likely referring to the use of smoke for heat or preserving food in the harsh climate</note> and were seen clearing the snow away from their doors.

### On the horses of the Sacae and some of their customs.

The horses of the **Sacae** <note>An ancient nomadic Scythian tribe from the Eurasian Steppe</note> are trained so that if the rider is thrown, the horse stands still for him to remount. If a man wishes to marry a maiden, he must engage in single combat with the girl; if she wins, she takes him captive and she rules and commands him. If, however, she is defeated, she is ruled by him. They fight this duel until victory is achieved, not unto death. When the **Sacae** are in mourning, they hide themselves away in subterranean and shaded houses.

### On the courage of Perdiccas, and the Lioness.

**Perdiccas** the **Macedonian**, who campaigned alongside **Alexander**, was so courageous that he once entered a cave alone where a lioness had her lair. Although he did not find the lioness there, he came out carrying her cubs. Because of this, **Perdiccas** was greatly admired. It is believed not only among the **Greeks** that the **Lioness** is the most valiant and difficult beast to fight, but also among the barbarians. They say, for instance, that **Semiramis** of **Assyria** did not pride herself if she captured a lion or killed a leopard or any other such creature, but she felt great pride only if she managed to overpower a **Lioness**.

### On the provisions that followed Xerxes.

Among the other provisions that followed **Xerxes** <note>The King of Persia who invaded Greece in 480 BCE</note>—which were full of luxury and arrogance—was water from the **Choaspes** <note>The kings of Persia famously drank only the water of the River Choaspes, which was boiled and carried in silver vessels</note>. When he became thirsty in a certain desert place, and his supply-train had not yet arrived, a proclamation was made through the camp asking if anyone had water from the **Choaspes** for the king to drink. A man was found who had a small amount of it, and it was foul-smelling; nevertheless, **Xerxes** drank it and considered the man who gave it to him a benefactor, because...

### About the painter Protogenes:

<margin>G</margin> They say that **Protogenes** <note>A celebrated Greek painter of the 4th century BCE</note>, when he was painting his *Ialysus* <note>The eponymous hero of the Rhodian city of Ialysus; the painting was Protogenes' most famous work</note>, spent seven years completing it. When **Apelles** <note>The most famous painter of antiquity and contemporary of Protogenes</note> saw it, he first stood speechless, struck with awe at the intensity of the execution. Then, looking away, he said: "The labor is great, and so is the craftsman, yet the work lacks that certain grace of the hand; if the man had achieved that, his spirit would touch the very heavens."

---

### About certain men raised by wild beasts:

<margin>K</margin> They say a dog <note>original: κύων (kyon) — legend says Cyrus was suckled by a bitch named Spako</note> nursed **Cyrus** <note>Cyrus the Great, founder of the Persian Empire</note> the son of Mandane. A deer nursed **Telephus**, the son of Auge and Hercules. A horse nursed **Pelias**, the son of Poseidon and Tyro. Furthermore, they say **Alexander** <note>Also known as Paris, the prince of Troy</note>, the son of Priam, was raised by a bear, and **Aegisthus**, the son of Thyestes and Pelopia, was raised by a goat.

---

### Those who became illustrious from obscure origins:

<margin>D</margin> I hear that **Darius** <note>Darius I "the Great"</note>, the son of Hystaspes, was a quiver-bearer for Cyrus. The last **Darius** <note>Darius III, the king defeated by Alexander the Great</note>, who was conquered by Alexander, was the son of a slave woman. **Archelaus**, the King of the Macedonians, was the son of Simiche, a slave. **Menelaus**, the grandfather of Philip, was counted among the illegitimate, and his son **Amyntas** was believed to be an attendant and slave of Aeropus. **Perseus**, whom the Roman **Paulus** <note>Lucius Aemilius Paullus, who defeated the Macedonians at Pydna</note> overthrew, was Argive by descent, but the son of an undistinguished man. **Eumenes** <note>One of Alexander's generals</note> is believed to have been the son of a wagon-maker and a funeral-flute player. **Antigonus** <note>Antigonus the "One-Eyed"</note>, the son of Philip—who was called the Cyclops for this reason—was a manual laborer for Polyperchon and even a thief. <margin>D</margin> **Themistocles**, who defeated the barbarians at sea and alone understood the voices of the gods in the oracles, was the son of a Thracian woman, and his mother was called Abrotonon. **Phocion**, who was nicknamed "The Good," was the son of a man who made pestles <note>A manual laborer who carved tools for grinding</note>. They say that **Demetrius of Phalerum** was a domestic servant from the household of Timotheus and Conon. As for **Hyperbolus**, **Cleophon**, and **Demades**, although they became leaders of the Athenian people, no one could easily name their fathers. The Spartans **Callicratidas**, **Gylippus**, and **Lysander** were called *mothakes* <term>mothakes: a social class in Sparta consisting of the sons of non-citizens or poor citizens who were permitted to undergo the Spartan education (agoge) alongside the elite</term>. This was the name given to those whose fathers sent them to compete in the gymnasiums alongside the wealthy; **Lycurgus** <note>The legendary lawgiver of Sparta</note> granted this status to those who remained steadfast in the training of the youths.

### About those who spent a long time in the stone quarries of Sicily:

<margin>P</margin> **Regarding those who lived for a long time in the stone quarries of Sicily:**

<margin>A</margin> In Sicily, there were **stone quarries** <term>lithotomies (deep pits or caves created by excavating stone for building)</term> that extended for a great distance, reaching a length of one **stadium** <note>roughly 600 feet</note> and a width of two **plethra** <note>roughly 200 feet</note>. Men lived in these places for such a long time that they even married and fathered children there. Some of those children had never once seen a city; when they eventually came to **Syracuse** and saw horses harnessed to chariots, they fled away screaming, so utterly were they struck with terror. The most beautiful of the caves there was named after the poet **Philoxenus** <note>Philoxenus of Cythera, a famous dithyrambic poet</note>. It is said that while he was living there, he composed "The Cyclops," the finest of his poems, viewing the punishment and sentence imposed by **Dionysius** <note>The tyrant Dionysius I of Syracuse, who reportedly imprisoned the poet for criticizing his own amateur poetry</note> as nothing, for even in the midst of his misfortune, Philoxenus continued to serve the Muses.

---

### About Midas, Plato, and Pindar as infants:

<margin>Ph</margin> **Regarding the infancy of Midas, Plato, and Pindar:**

The Phrygian tales also sing of these things: when **Midas** the Phrygian was still a sleeping infant, ants crawled into his mouth and, with great industry and affection, deposited grains of wheat. In the case of **Plato**, bees settled on his lips and built a honeycomb while he slept. And when **Pindar** <note>The celebrated lyric poet of Thebes</note> was left exposed outside his father's house, bees served as his nurses, placing honey upon his lips instead of milk.

---

### About a sign revealing monarchy to Dionysius:

**Regarding a sign that revealed the coming monarchy to Dionysius:**

<margin>D</margin> They say that **Dionysius**, the son of Hermocrates, was once crossing a river. His horse slipped in the mud and became stuck; Dionysius leaped off, reached the bank, and walked away, leaving the horse behind as if it were lost. However, the horse followed him, and when Dionysius heard it neighing, he turned back. As he took hold of the horse’s mane to mount it, a swarm of bees settled upon his hand. When Dionysius consulted the **Galeotae** <note>A family of seers or diviners in Sicily</note> about this event, they told him it was a sign signifying that he would become a monarch.

---

### About Aristomache, the wife of Dion:

**Regarding Aristomache, the wife of Dion:**

<margin>D</margin> Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Younger</note> drove **Dion** <note>The brother-in-law and later rival of Dionysius I, and a friend of Plato</note> out of Sicily, but he kept Dion's wife, **Aristomache**, and their child under guard. Later, he gave the woman—against her will—to one of his guards who was his most devoted servant, **Polycrates** <meta>Catchword: Polykratei (to Polycrates)</meta>

# BOOK TWELVE

...gave the woman to **Polycrates** <note>A friend of the tyrant Dionysius the Younger</note>, and he was at enmity with the **Syracusans**. But **Dion**, having taken control of **Syracuse**, took [her] back from **Dionysius** to the **Locrians**. There, **Arete**, the sister of **Dion**, addressed him, but **Aristomache** <note>Dion’s wife</note> went away veiling herself out of shame; she did not dare to address him as a husband since she had been forced and had not preserved the law of the marriage bed toward him. However, when **Arete** spoke in her defense, recounting the compulsion she suffered from **Dionysius**, **Dion** brought his wife and son to him and sent them to his house.

### Regarding the poems of Homer

That the **Indians**, having translated the works of **Homer** into their own native tongue, sing them; and not only they, but also the kings of the **Persians**, if one must believe those who record histories concerning these matters.

### That Phocion did not hold a grudge

**Phocion**, the son of **Phocus**, having many times served as general, was condemned to death. While he was in prison and about to drink the **hemlock** <note>A poisonous plant used for executions in Athens</note>, the executioner offered him the cup. His relatives asked if he had any message for his son. He charged him to hold no grudge against the **Athenians** for the "loving-cup" <term>philotesia (literally a cup shared in friendship, here used by Phocion as a bittersweet metaphor for his execution)</term> which I now drink. Whoever does not praise and marvel at this man, it seems to me that such a person is incapable of any great thought.

### Regarding the Lacedaemonians not being diligent about education

The **Lacedaemonians** <note>The Spartans</note> were inexperienced in **music** <note>In the Greek sense, "music" (mousike) encompassed all the arts of the Muses, including literature and education</note>. For their concern was for gymnastics and weapons. If ever they required the help of the **Muses**, whether because they were falling ill, losing their minds, or suffering some other such public calamity, they would send for foreign men as if they were doctors. According to the **Pythian oracle**, they sent for **Terpander**, **Thales**, **Tyrtaeus**, **Nymphaeus of Cydonia**, and **Alcman**, for he was a singer to the flute. Even **Thucydides** <note>The famous historian of the Peloponnesian War</note> agrees that they were not diligent about education in what he says regarding **Brasidas** <note>A prominent Spartan general</note>. He says, at least, that he was unable to speak like a **Lacedaemonian**, but rather like a commoner.

### Regarding the vanity of Menecrates, and how Philip laughed at them

**Menecrates** the physician reached such a height of **vanity** <term>typhos (delusional pride or arrogance)</term> that he called himself **Zeus**. He once sent a letter to **Philip of Macedon**...

->BOOK<-

...to the king, as a private citizen, Menecrates the Zeus sends greetings <note>original: "εὖ πράττειν" (eu prattein) - a standard greeting meaning 'do well'</note>. 
But Philip wrote back: “Philip to Menecrates, I wish you health” <note>original: "ὑγιαίνειν" (hygiainein) - Philip uses a word meaning both 'to be healthy' and 'to be sane,' a pointed jab at the doctor’s delusions</note>. I advise you to take yourself to the regions around **Anticyra** <note>Anticyra was famous for producing hellebore, a plant used in antiquity to treat madness</note>. By this, he was hinting that the man was out of his mind.

<margin>E</margin> Once, Philip hosted a magnificent feast and invited this man to dinner. He ordered a separate couch to be prepared for him alone. When Menecrates sat down, Philip placed an incense burner before him and burned incense for him, while the others feasted; the meal was indeed magnificent. At first, Menecrates held out and rejoiced in the honor, but when hunger slowly crept up on him and proved that he was indeed a human being—and a powerless one at that—he stood up and went away. He said that Philip had mocked him quite tunefully <note>or 'fittingly'</note> by exposing his folly.

## To what Isocrates compared Athens

<margin>I</margin> **Isocrates** the orator used to say regarding the city of the **Athenians** that it was similar to courtesans <term>hetairai (high-status female companions/prostitutes)</term>. For just as those who are captivated by the beauty of such women wish to associate with them, yet no one is so excessively out of their mind as to endure living with <note>or 'marrying'</note> one of them; so too, visiting the city of the **Athenians** is most pleasant, and in this regard it surpasses all others in **Greece**, but to live there permanently is no longer safe. By this, he was hinting at the local <term>sycophants (professional legal accusers and informers)</term> and the plots arising from the demagogues.

## Regarding the pretexts of the greatest wars

<margin>E</margin> It has not escaped my notice that the origins of the greatest wars seem somehow to have been trivial. For they say the **Persian War** took its beginning from the dispute of **Maeandrius** of **Samos** against the **Athenians**; the **Peloponnesian War**, however, was due to the **Megarian** tablet <note>The 'Megarian Decree' which excluded Megara from trade with the Athenian Empire</note>. The so-called **Sacred War** arose from the collection of fines by the **Amphictyons** <note>A council of Greek states that managed the Sanctuary of Apollo at Delphi</note>. The conflict at **Chaeronea** arose because the **Athenians** were rivals with **Philip** and were unwilling to yield.

## How Aristotle tried to stop Alexander's anger

<margin>A</margin> **Aristotle**, wishing to stop **Alexander** from being angry and to cease his many grievances, wrote these things to him: "Wrath and anger occur not toward equals, but toward those who are superior. To you, however, no one is equal."

# TWELFTH [BOOK]

Aristotle, by giving necessary advice to Alexander, proved helpful to many; through his influence, he even rebuilt his own fatherland after it had been razed to the ground by Philip <note>original: "κατεσκαμμένην" (kateskammenēn). This refers to the city of Stagira, which Aristotle’s student, Alexander the Great, rebuilt at his teacher's request.</note>.

### Concerning those among the Libyans killed by elephants, either in hunts or in battles.

The Libyans bury those who have been killed by elephants—whether in hunts or in battles—with great distinction, and they sing certain hymns over them. The theme of these hymns is that the deceased was a "good man" <note>or "brave man"</note> to have stood as an opponent against such a beast. They even say that such a death is not truly dying at all, for the tomb of the fallen serves as a lasting monument.

### What Diogenes said about the Megarians.

Diogenes of Sinope <note>the famous Cynic philosopher known for his extreme poverty and biting wit</note> said many things to criticize the ignorance and lack of education of the Megarians. He famously said he would rather be a Megarian’s ram than his son. By this, he was hinting that the Megarians take great care of their livestock, but none at all for their children's upbringing.

### Concerning the portents shown to the Thebans when Alexander led his army against them.

When Alexander, the son of Philip, led his forces against **Thebes**, the gods sent signs and portents to the citizens, foretelling the disasters that were very nearly upon them. However, the Thebans, believing the rumors that Alexander had died among the **Illyrians**, hurled many insults and blasphemies against him. Consequently, the lake at **Onchestos** gave out a terrifying and continuous roar, and its waters were seen surging violently. Within the city, by the **Ismenus** <note>a river near Thebes</note>, the spring called **Dirce**—which had flowed with pure and sweet water throughout all previous time—suddenly and unexpectedly was filled with blood. While the Macedonians took heart, the Thebans were being warned by the divine powers.

In the city's temple of **Demeter**, a spider wove its web <note>literally "its own art"</note> across the face of the goddess's statue, working in the very place it usually avoids. Furthermore, the statue of **Athena**, known as **Alalkomeneis**, spontaneously caught fire without any flame being brought near it; many other such signs occurred.

### Concerning Dioxippus.

**Dioxippus**, the Athenian athlete and Olympic victor, was making his formal entry into **Athens** according to the custom of champion athletes. The crowds flocked together in great numbers...

When Dioxippus <note>a famous Athenian Olympic champion in the pankration, a brutal combat sport</note> saw her, he was immediately conquered by her beauty. He spent the rest of the time gazing at her, turning his head back and changing color many times in his face. Because of this, his distraction became obvious to many, as he was no longer looking at his opponent with focus. He was particularly condemned for this lapse in self-control. Diogenes of Sinope <note>the Cynic philosopher mentioned on the previous page</note> was present, and looking toward those nearby, he said: "See your great athlete, his neck being twisted back by a young girl."

### Concerning Truth and Beneficence.

Pythagoras <note>the famous mathematician and philosopher</note> used to say that these two things were the finest gifts given by the gods to humanity: to speak the truth and to do good to others. He further stated that each of these actions resembles the works of the gods themselves.

### Concerning Dionysius and Philip.

A meeting once took place between Dionysius the Younger <note>the deposed tyrant of Syracuse</note> and Philip, the son of Amyntas <note>Philip II of Macedon, father of Alexander the Great</note>. As one might expect, many topics were discussed, and among them was this: Philip asked Dionysius how it was that, having inherited such a great empire from his father, he had failed to preserve it. Dionysius replied quite aptly: "My father left me everything else, but the Good Fortune <term>Tyche</term> <note>original: "τύχην" (tychēn), referring to the personified goddess of luck and success</note> by which he acquired and guarded those things—that, he did not leave me..."

### Concerning the honor given to the North Wind.

Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Elder of Syracuse</note> sailed against the people of Thurii <note>a Greek colony in southern Italy</note>, leading three hundred ships filled with heavy infantry. But the North Wind <note>Boreas</note> blew against them, shattered the vessels, and annihilated his naval power. Because of this, the Thurians sacrificed to the North Wind. They even passed a decree to make the wind a citizen, assigned him a house and a plot of land, and performed annual rites for him. Thus, the Athenians were not the only ones to consider the wind a kinsman <note>The Athenians called Boreas their "son-in-law" because he supposedly carried off the Athenian princess Orithyia</note>; the Thurians also officially recorded him as their benefactor. Pausanias says that the people of Megalopolis also...

### A Persian Law concerning those who advise the King.

There is also this Persian law: if someone is about to give advice to the King on secret or disputed matters, he stands upon a golden brick. If he is judged to have offered necessary and sound advice, he departs taking the brick with him as a reward for his counsel. Nevertheless, he is still whipped, because he dared to contradict the King. In my judgment, this is an unpleasant situation for a free man, not...

# BOOK TWELVE

### Concerning the Love of Archedice

A certain young man fell in love with Archedice, a courtesan <note>Greek: *hetaira*, a high-class companion and entertainer</note> from Naucratis <note>a Greek trading colony in Egypt</note>. She was exceedingly arrogant and terribly coarse in her demands, requiring an unusual fee; after receiving it, she would associate with the giver for only a short time and then dismiss him. The young man, being deeply in love with her but unable to obtain her favors because he was not at all wealthy, eventually dreamed that he was with her. Immediately after this dream, his desire for her ceased.

### Concerning the Corpse of Alexander

When Alexander, the son of Philip and Olympias, ended his life in Babylon, he who claimed to be the son of Zeus lay there as a corpse. While those around him were quarreling over the kingship, he remained without a share of the burial that even the very poor receive—nature's common law requiring that the one who is no longer living be hidden away. Instead, he was left neglected for thirty days, until Aristander of Telmessus <note>Alexander’s favorite seer and omen-reader</note>, either becoming possessed by a god or seized by some other divine coincidence, came into the midst of the Macedonians. He declared to them that Alexander had been the most fortunate of all kings throughout the ages, both while living and now that he was dead. For he said the gods had told him that the land which received the body in which his soul first dwelt would be most radiant and remain unconquered throughout time. 

Upon learning this, a great rivalry arose among them, each man desiring to bring the body to his own kingdom, so that he might possess it as a sacred relic <note>original: *agos*, a term for something both holy and potentially cursed/powerful</note>, hoping to have it as a treasure and an unshakable stronghold <note>the OCR reads *Homeron* (Homer), but this is likely a transcription error for *hormeterion* (a base or stronghold)</note> for a secure kingdom. Ptolemy, acting as a merchant of sorts, secretly removed the body and with great haste carried it to the city of Alexander <note>Alexandria</note> in Egypt. While the other Macedonians remained quiet, Perdiccas <note>one of Alexander's leading generals and the designated regent</note> attempted to pursue him. He was not so much concerned with honoring Alexander or performing the sacred rites for the dead, but rather he was inflamed and ignited by the prophecies spoken by Aristander. 

When he caught up with Ptolemy, a very fierce battle took place over the corpse. In a way, it was sister to the battle over the phantom at Troy, which Homer sings of, saying that Apollo cast an image of Aeneas <note>referring to a scene in the *Iliad* where Apollo creates a decoy to save the real hero</note> into the midst of the heroes. Ptolemy checked the momentum of Perdiccas by making a decoy <note>Greek: *eidolon*, a phantom or image</note> resembling Alexander, and prepared...

# AELIAN’S VARIOUS HISTORY
## BOOK THIRTEEN.

->Concerning Atalanta.<-

This is an Arcadian story concerning Atalanta, the daughter of Iasion. When she was born, her father "exposed" her <note>abandoned her in the wilderness to die</note>, for he said he had need of male children, not daughters. The man who took her to expose her did not kill her; instead, he went to Mount Parthenion <note>a mountain in the Peloponnese, famous as the site where Pan appeared to the runner Philippides</note> and placed her near a spring. There was a hollowed-out rock there, shaded by a dense thicket of trees. Death was the sentence passed upon the infant. Yet she was not overlooked by Fortune, for a little later a she-bear, whose cubs had been taken by hunters, came by; her teats were swollen and heavy with milk. Then, by some divine providence, she took pleasure in the infant and suckled it. Thus the beast was relieved of her pain, and she provided nourishment to the babe. And so, the bear repeatedly returned to provide her milk, and since she was no longer a mother to her own offspring, she became the nurse of a child who was not her own. Hunters watched for this bear, as they had been plotting against the beast to take her young from the beginning. Having scouted out every part of the thickets, they waited until the bear had gone out as usual to hunt and forage, and then they stole away Atalanta, who had not yet—

# BOOK THIRTEEN.

...her, for they themselves gave her the name <note>Atalanta</note>. She was raised by them on the food of the mountains, and as she grew, her body reached the prime of her youth. She came to love virginity, fled the company of men, and longed for solitude, occupying the highest peaks of the Arcadian mountains. There, there was a well-watered valley, full of great oaks, a refreshing breeze, and a deep forest. Why should it trouble us to hear of Atalanta's cave, just as we hear of Calypso's in Homer? <note>Aelian refers here to the famous description of the goddess Calypso’s lush grotto in Book 5 of the *Odyssey*.</note> In a hollow ravine there was a very deep cavern, fortified at the front by a steep cliff. Ivy flowed around it, and the ivy entwined itself with tender trees, climbing up through them. Crocuses grew softly around the place in the thick woods, and with them blossomed hyacinths and many other colors of flowers, which were not only able to provide a feast for the eyes, but their scents also filled the air all around. In addition to the celebratory feel of the woods and the essential banquet it provided, there were many laurels—a plant that flourishes in perfection—with lovely tresses of leaves. Vines also flourished exceedingly, heavy with clusters of grapes even before they ripened, which testified to Atalanta’s industrious care <note>original: *philorgon*, suggesting she tended to the wild growth or that nature responded to her presence</note>. Waters, constant and ever-flowing, clear to the sight and cold to the taste, poured forth abundantly. These same waters also flowed continuously to provide irrigation for the aforementioned trees, thickening as they meandered through them. The place was thus full of grace and at the same time most solemn, appearing as a modest sanctuary for a virgin.

<margin>A small cross-like ink mark in the left margin next to this paragraph.</margin>
For Atalanta, her bedding consisted of the skins of the beasts she had hunted, her food was their meat, and her drink was water. Her clothing was fashioned from what she had captured, in a style that did not fall short of the example of Artemis. For she said she strove to emulate the goddess in this, and she did not wish to lead a domestic life until the end. She was naturally very swift of foot, and neither a wild beast nor any man plotting against her could escape her when she wished to flee; but no one could have caught her in the first place. Many fell in love with her—not only those who had seen her, but those who had already fallen in love with her through her reputation. Come then, let us also describe her physical appearance, even if it is a difficult task—though it is no trouble at all, since in these matters...

### BOOK 13.
<meta>catchword: revelers,</meta>

...skill and experience in speech might be added to her. For even while she was still a child, her height exceeded that of grown women, and in beauty, she was unlike any other maiden in the Peloponnese <note>The Peloponnese is the large peninsula forming the southern part of mainland Greece.</note> at that time. She had a masculine and fierce gaze, partly due to being raised by a wild beast <note>original: *thēriou trophēs*, referring to the she-bear that suckled her</note>, for she was high-spirited; and already, from her exercises and hunting in the mountains, she possessed nothing delicate. She did not emerge from a lady's bedchamber, nor was she one of those girls carried about by mothers and nurses. She did not even have an oversized or bulky body, and quite reasonably so, since she had thoroughly worked out her entire frame through hunting and her various exercises. Her hair was blonde, not from any womanly vanity or the use of dyes and tinctures, but its color was a work of nature. Her face was reddened by the sun, and it might have seemed like a harsh flush; yet what could be more beautiful than that? For what is as lovely to look upon as fire, or the face of a maiden educated in modesty?

She possessed two striking qualities: an invincible beauty, and with it, the power to inspire fear. No lazy man, upon seeing her, would have fallen in love with her, nor would he have even dared to look the girl in the eye—such was the brilliance that shone upon those who saw her, along with her youthful bloom. She was remarkable to encounter for other reasons too, including her rarity; for one would not see her in social circles, but she would appear suddenly and unexpectedly while pursuing a beast, or defending herself against one. Like a shooting star, she would flash out like a bolt of lightning, and then hide herself as she ran through a thicket, a grove, or some other mountain forest.

And once, those who lived in the neighboring regions—bold lovers and most troublesome revelers <note>original: *kōmastai*, usually referring to drunken youths who go door-to-door at night; here used ironically for the monstrous Centaurs</note>—approached her at midnight: these were two of the Centaurs, Hylaeus and Rhoecus. Their "revelry" did not involve flute-girls, nor was it the kind of thing practiced by youths in the city; rather, they carried pine torches, and having lit and set them ablaze, the first sight of that fire would have terrified even a whole populace, let alone a lone maiden. They broke off freshly cut branches of pine and, weaving these around themselves, they made wreaths. Constantly and frequently clashing their weapons together through the mountains and setting trees on fire as they went, these wicked suitors hurried toward the girl with insolence, offering lawless gifts in exchange for marriage. But their plot did not escape her; for she saw the fire from her cave and recognized who they were...

### BOOK THIRTEENTH
<meta>catchword: revelers,</meta>
<meta>signature: p</meta>

...revelers <note>original: *kōmastai*. This refers to the centaurs Rhoecus and Hylaeus, who approached her as if joining a drunken procession (*kōmos*) rather than a battle.</note>, not at all disturbed or intimidated by their appearance, she bent her bow and let fly an arrow. She struck the first one quite effectively, and he fell. But the rest no longer came like revelers, but in a warlike fashion, wishing to avenge him and to satisfy their own impulse against her.
