->How Macareus was punished by the gods<-
->for his own destruction.<-

A man of Mitylene <note>The capital of the island of Lesbos.</note> named Macareus, a priest of Dionysus, appeared decent in everything one could see him do; yet he was actually the most unholy of men. When a guest came to him and gave him a large amount of gold for safekeeping in the inner sanctum <term>sanctum: *anaktōron*, the most sacred part of a temple where only priests could go</term> of the temple, Macareus dug up the earth and buried the gold there. After some time, the stranger returned and asked for his gold. But the priest, swearing a false oath <note>original: *epiorkian*. Swearing falsely by the gods was considered a supreme crime in the ancient world.</note> that he owed nothing, murdered the man and dug up the gold. He buried the stranger in the same spot, thinking that just as he could hide from men, he could likewise escape the notice of the god. 

However, things did not turn out that way—how could they? A short time later, the festivals of the god arrived. Macareus was sacrificing magnificently and was occupied with the Bacchic rites. His two sons, who had been left behind in the house, began mimicking their father’s priestly ritual. They approached the ancestral altar while the sacrificial fires were burning. The younger boy offered his neck, and the elder, having found a sacrificial knife left lying about, killed his brother like a sacrificial animal. 

Those in the house saw this and cried out. The mother, hearing the shout, rushed out; seeing the corpse and the other boy still holding the bloodied knife, she seized a half-burnt brand from the altar and killed the child with it. Word reached Macareus; leaving the rite as he was, he rushed into the house in a fit of rage and killed his own wife with the thyrsus <term>thyrsus: a ritual staff topped with a pinecone and wrapped in ivy, carried by followers of Dionysus</term> that he was holding. These daring crimes became known to everyone. Macareus was seized and, while being tortured, confessed to everything he had done in the inner sanctum. In the midst of these punishments, his soul departed. The man who had been lawlessly slaughtered was brought out publicly because of you <note>Possibly an address to the readers or the citizens.</note> and was buried by the god's command...

### BOOK THIRTEENTH

...ficing. But the wicked man paid a penalty that was not to be complained of <note>That is, a punishment so fitting that no one could argue against its justice.</note>, paying the price with his own head, and that of his wife, and one by one, with those of his children as well. :~

->Concerning the tomb of Belus, and the evil omen that appeared there for Xerxes :~<-

Xerxes, the son of Darius, after digging up the tomb of the ancient Belus <note>Belus was a legendary king of Babylon, often identified with the god Marduk.</note>, found a glass sarcophagus <term>sarcophagus: *pyelos*, originally a tub or vat, here used for a burial vessel</term> where the corpse lay in oil. However, the sarcophagus was not full; it lacked about a palm's width <note>original: *palaistēn*. An ancient Greek unit of measurement roughly equal to four fingers or 3 inches.</note> from the rim. Beside the sarcophagus stood a short pillar, upon which was written: "To him who opens the tomb and does not fill the sarcophagus, it shall not go well." When Xerxes read this, he became afraid and ordered oil to be poured in as quickly as possible. Yet it did not fill up. He ordered it poured in again, but it received no increase, until at last he gave up, having wasted the oil poured in to no purpose. After closing the tomb again, he departed not without distress <note>original: *mē adēmonōn*. In the context of the omen, this suggests he was deeply troubled by his failure to satisfy the tomb's requirement.</note>. Nor did the pillar prove false in what it foretold. For after gathering seven hundred thousand men against the Greeks, he fared miserably; then, having returned home, he died the most shameful of deaths, being slaughtered at night in his bed by his own son. :~

->Concerning Euripides being drunk at a banquet :~<-

King Archelaus prepared a costly banquet for his companions. When the drinking had progressed and Euripides was drinking quite strong wine <note>original: *zōroteron*. The Greeks usually diluted their wine with water; drinking it "zōron" or "unmixed" led to rapid intoxication.</note>, he was gradually overtaken by drunkenness. Then, embracing Agathon—the tragic poet who was reclining next to him and was perhaps forty years of age—he began to kiss him. When Archelaus asked him if Agathon still seemed desirable to him, Euripides replied: "By Zeus, yes; for not only is the spring of beautiful people most lovely, but so also is their autumn."

->Who was the first to fall in love with noble boys :~<-

They say that Laius was the first to fall in love with noble boys, when he kidnapped Chrysippus, the son of Pelops; and from this, the custom of loving those in their prime <note>original: *hōraiōn*, meaning those in the "bloom" or "flower" of youth.</note> came to be considered an honor among the Thebans. :~

->Concerning the properties of Arcadian, Thasian, and Achaean wines :~<-

In Ceraia <note>A town in Arcadia.</note> of Arcadia, they say there are vines from which a wine is produced that drives men out of their minds and makes the Arcadians lose their wits, yet it makes the women fertile. :~

### THIRTEENTH BOOK

They say that in Thasos two types of wine are produced: one, when drunk, brings on a sleep that is not very deep, and for this reason it is considered pleasant; the other is of an opposite nature to life <note>original: *antipalon tō biō*. This likely suggests it disturbs the natural rhythm of waking life.</note>, for it causes wakefulness and causes the bowels to run <note>original: *trechein*. This is a euphemism for a laxative effect.</note>. They also say that in Achaea, near Keryneia, a wine is produced which helps women who wish to miscarry.

---

->Concerning Thebes being captured by Alexander,<-
->and concerning Pindar.<-

When Alexander captured the city of Thebes, he sold all the free people into slavery, except for the priests. He also exempted from the sale those who had been guest-friends <term>guest-friends: *xenous*, a formal relationship of hospitality and protection</term> to his father, for Philip had been a hostage among them while he was still a child. He also released their relatives. Furthermore, he honored the descendants of Pindar <note>Pindar was the most famous lyric poet of ancient Greece, known for his victory odes.</note> and allowed only Pindar’s house to remain standing. About nine thousand of the Thebans were killed, and thirty thousand were taken captive.

---

->Concerning Lysander.<-

They say that while Lysander the Spartan was spending time in Ionia, he cast aside the customs of Lycurgus <note>Lycurgus was the legendary lawgiver of Sparta, credited with establishing their austere and militaristic way of life.</note>, which were very strict <note>original: *epitona*, meaning "tightly strung," like the string of a bow or a musical instrument.</note>, and became corrupted by a life of luxury.

---

->Concerning Lamia.<-

Lamia, the Athenian courtesan, once said: "The lions from Greece become foxes when they arrive in Ephesus." <note>This is a jab at how Greek soldiers and leaders often lost their courage or integrity when they encountered the wealth and "softness" of the Ionian cities in Asia Minor.</note>

---

->Concerning Dionysius marrying two women in one day.<-

In a single day, Dionysius married two women: Doris of Locris, and Aristomache, the daughter of Hipparinus and sister of Dion. He spent time with each of them in turn; one would follow him when he went on military campaigns, while the other would receive him upon his return.

---

->Concerning the enslavement of the Persians, and Isocrates.<-

A certain report reached me saying that Isocrates the orator was the cause of the Persians being enslaved by the Macedonians. The fame of the *Panegyricus* <note>One of Isocrates' most famous speeches, which urged the Greeks to stop fighting each other and unite in a war against Persia.</note>, which Isocrates delivered to the Greeks, reached Macedonia and incited Philip against Asia. When Philip died, it prepared Alexander, his son and paternal heir, to take up the momentum of Philip's drive.

# BOOK THREE

### How Meton escaped the military expedition, and concerning the madness of Odysseus.

Meton the Astronomer <note>Meton of Athens was a famous 5th-century BC mathematician and astronomer, best known for the Metonic cycle used to reconcile lunar and solar calendars.</note>, when the Athenians were already preparing their armies to sail against Sicily <note>The Sicilian Expedition (415–413 BC) was a massive military operation during the Peloponnesian War that ended in a total disaster for Athens.</note>, was himself included on the official muster roll. Knowing clearly the misfortunes that were destined to happen on the voyage, he was on his guard and afraid, seeking to rescue himself from the departure. When he achieved no success by ordinary means, he feigned madness. He did many things to confirm the appearance of his disease, but finally, he set his own apartment building on fire. This building happened to be a neighbor to the Painted Porch <term>Painted Porch: The *Stoa Poikile*, a famous public hall in Athens decorated with murals of great battles.</term>. Because of this, the officials released him from service. It seems to me that Meton acted out his madness better than Odysseus of Ithaca. For Palamedes exposed the trickery of Odysseus <note>Legend says Odysseus tried to avoid the Trojan War by pretending to be mad and plowing his fields with salt; Palamedes stopped him by placing Odysseus' infant son in the path of the plow.</note>, but not one of the Athenians caught Meton.

### Concerning the generosity of Ptolemy.

They say that Ptolemy, the son of Lagus <note>Ptolemy I Soter, the founder of the Greek kingdom in Egypt after the death of Alexander the Great.</note>, took great joy in making his friends wealthy. He used to say that it was better to enrich others than to be rich oneself.

### Concerning the epic verses and poetry of Homer.

In the old days, the ancients used to sing the verses of Homer in separate, divided sections. For example, they would speak of "The Battle at the Ships," the "Story of Dolon," the "Excellence of Agamemnon," the "Catalogue of Ships," the "Deeds of Patroclus," the "Ransom," the "Games for Patroclus," and the "Violation of the Oaths." These were all parts of the *Iliad*. From the other epic <note>The *Odyssey*.</note>, there were the "Events in Pylos," the "Events in Lacedaemon," "Calypso’s Cave," the "Story of the Raft," the "Tales to Alcinous," the "Cyclops Story," the "Voyage to the Dead," the "Tales of Circe," the "Foot-washing," the "Slaughter of the Suitors," the "Events in the Countryside," and the "Story of Laertes."

Lycurgus the Spartan <note>The legendary lawgiver who established the strict social and military systems of Sparta.</note> was the first to bring the poetry of Homer in a single collection into Greece. He brought it as a cargo from Ionia when he was traveling abroad. Later, Peisistratus <note>The 6th-century BC ruler of Athens who is often credited with commissioning the first "standard" written edition of Homer's works.</note> collected the pieces together and presented the *Iliad* and *Odyssey* as we know them.

### Concerning some who were exceedingly foolish.

The comic poets say that certain men were very "thick" in their thinking <note>original: *pachytaton... tēn dianoian*. A Greek idiom meaning slow-witted or dull.</note>. These included a certain Polyidorus, nicknamed "the one with the ankle-bone skin," and a man clearly named Koikylion, who in his extreme madness used to count the waves. The story goes that Sannyrion was also like this—he was the one who searched for a ladder inside an oil-flask <note>A lekithos, or oil-flask, was a small vessel; searching for a ladder inside one was a proverbial way to describe an impossible or nonsensical act.</note>—as were Koroibos and Melitides <note>Koroibos and Melitides were stereotypical "fools" in Greek folklore, similar to characters in modern "numbskull" jokes.</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the astronomer Meton's clever ruse to avoid military service, the generosity of King Ptolemy I, the early oral transmission and eventual codification of Homeric epics, and a list of figures famous for their stupidity in Greek comedy.</summary>
<keywords>Meton the Astronomer, Sicilian Expedition, Odysseus, Ptolemy I, Homer, Iliad, Odyssey, Lycurgus, Peisistratus, Greek Comedy, Sannyrion, Koroibos</keywords>

# THIRTEENTH

...and they say to those foolish ones:

### Concerning the people of Apollonia, their land, and concerning Epidamnus

The people of Apollonia inhabit a city neighboring Epidamnus <note>original: "Ἐπιδάμνου"; Epidamnus, later known as Dyrrachium, is modern-day Durrës in Albania.</note> on the Ionian Gulf. In the lands nearby, asphalt <note>original: "ἀσφάλτου"; a natural form of petroleum or bitumen used in antiquity for mortar and fuel.</note> is dug up, and as it sends up a vapor from the earth, it fills a stream that flows just as the water does around oars. Not far away, a "perpetual fire" is shown. This is a small burning hill that does not cover a large area; its circumference is not great, and it smells of sulfur and alum <term>Alum: A naturally occurring mineral salt used in ancient times for dyeing, medicine, and leather tanning.</term>. Around this hill, the trees are flourishing and many are green; the fire, though burning intensely nearby, does not harm the growth of the plants or the flat ground. This fire burns both night and day, and the people of Apollonia say it never ceased until the war they fought against the Illyrians <note>The Illyrians were a group of Indo-European tribes who inhabited the western Balkans.</note>.

The people of Apollonia held their public assemblies according to the Lacedaemonian <note>original: "λακεδαιμόνιον"; referring to the laws of Sparta.</note> law. The people of Epidamnus, however, allowed anyone who wished to live there as a resident alien <note>original: "μετοικεῖν"; to live as a 'metic,' a foreign resident with limited rights.</note> to do so freely.

### A proverb, and concerning Phrynichus

"Phrynichus strikes a number of wasps, just like a rooster." This is a proverb used for those who suffer some misfortune. For when Phrynichus the tragic poet <note>A famous early Athenian playwright (c. 511 BC), a pioneer of Greek tragedy.</note> staged a play about the capture of Miletus <note>Miletus was a powerful Greek city in Ionia destroyed by the Persians in 494 BC.</note>, the Athenians were moved to tears, banned the play, and fined him. <note>The Athenians fined Phrynichus 1,000 drachmas for "reminding them of their own calamities," as Miletus was a close ally.</note>

### Concerning Dionysius

Dionysius of Sicily <note>Dionysius I, the "Tyrant of Syracuse," who ruled from 405–367 BC.</note> embraced and praised tragedy; indeed, he even labored to write tragic dramas himself. However, he was hostile toward comedy because he was not a man fond of laughter.

### Concerning the words spoken by Cleomenes regarding Homer and Hesiod

Cleomenes <note>Cleomenes I, a King of Sparta (r. 520–490 BC).</note> used to say in the Spartan way, according to their local custom, that Homer was the poet of the Lacedaemonians because he spoke of how one must wage war. Hesiod, however, he said was the poet of the Helots <term>Helots: An unfree, state-owned serf population that worked the land for the Spartan citizens.</term>, because he spoke of how one must perform agriculture.

### Concerning one who dies happily, so that he might see those who have died

A man from Megalopolis in Arcadia, named Cercidas <note>Cercidas (c. 290–220 BC) was a Cynic philosopher, poet, and statesman.</note>, said to his relatives while he was dying that he was departing this life happily and with a good heart so that he might see some of the dead—specifically, he hoped to meet the thinkers and poets who had gone before him.

<summary>This page contains a collection of geography-based curiosities and historical anecdotes, including the natural fires of Apollonia, the censorship of the poet Phrynichus, and King Cleomenes' Spartan perspective on epic poetry.</summary>
<keywords>Apollonia, bitumen, perpetual fire, Phrynichus, Miletus, Dionysius of Syracuse, Cleomenes, Homer, Hesiod, Helots, Cercidas of Megalopolis</keywords>

# BOOK THIRTEENTH

...to live. For he held the hope of meeting with the wise Pythagoras <note>A famous Greek philosopher and mathematician.</note>, the godlike Hecataeus <note>Likely Hecataeus of Miletus, an early historian and geographer.</note>, the musical Olympus <note>A legendary figure credited with inventing various musical modes.</note>, and the poet Homer; and upon these thoughts, as the story goes, his soul departed him.

### Concerning the Phrygian Harmony

In Celaenae <note>A city in Phrygia, modern-day Turkey.</note>, they say that if someone plays the flute in the Phrygian harmony <term>Phrygian Harmony: An ancient musical mode often described as exciting, emotional, and religious in nature.</term> near the skin of the Phrygian <note>original: "δορᾷ τοῦ φρυγὸς"; This refers to the satyr Marsyas, who was flayed by the god Apollo after losing a musical contest. His skin was said to have been hung in a cave at Celaenae.</note>, the skin moves. However, if one plays a hymn to Apollo, it remains still and seems deaf.

### Concerning the Temple and Statue of Homer

Ptolemy Philopator <note>Ptolemy IV (reigned 221–204 BC), a king of Egypt known for his patronage of the arts.</note> built a temple for Homer and placed a fine seated statue of him; around the statue, he positioned representations of the cities that claimed Homer as their own. Galaton the painter, however, depicted Homer himself vomiting, and the other poets scooping up his vomit <note>A satirical artistic commentary suggesting that later poets merely "consumed" and recycled the genius of Homer rather than creating original work.</note>.

### Concerning Lycurgus the Lacedaemonian

Lycurgus the Lacedaemonian <note>original: "Λακεδαιμόνιος"; the legendary lawgiver of Sparta.</note>, the son of Eunomus, wishing to make the Spartans righteous, was poorly rewarded by them. For it happened that one of his eyes was struck out by a man named Alcander. Some say he was hit by a stone while in the council, while others say, according to the account, he suffered this fate by the blow of a staff. This saying is directed at those who indeed wish for certain things but receive others. Ephorus <note>An ancient Greek historian whose works are mostly lost.</note> says that Lycurgus died in exile by starving himself to death.

### Concerning Some Who Were Harmed by Their Own Laws

Lycurgus the orator <note>An Athenian statesman and orator (c. 390–324 BC), distinct from the Spartan lawgiver mentioned above.</note> enacted a law that women should not drive in carriages during the Mysteries <note>The Eleusinian Mysteries, the most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece.</note>, or else pay a fine that he deemed sufficient. His own wife was the first to disobey the decree, and she was convicted and paid the fine. Pericles <note>The famous Athenian statesman during the Golden Age of Athens.</note> also enacted a law that no one should be an Athenian citizen unless born of two citizen parents; later, after losing his legitimate sons, he was left only with his illegitimate son, Pericles. It is clear that Pericles intended one thing but experienced another.

Cleisthenes the Athenian, having first introduced the practice of ostracism <term>Ostracism: A democratic procedure where a citizen could be expelled from the city for ten years to prevent tyranny.</term>, was himself the first to suffer that sentence. Zaleucus, the lawgiver of the Locrians, decreed that anyone caught in adultery should have his... <note>Historical accounts usually complete this by saying "have his eyes put out." The text ends abruptly here at the bottom of the page.</note>

<summary>This page recounts various historical and cultural anecdotes, including the skin of Marsyas, Ptolemy's temple to Homer, and the irony of famous lawgivers like Pericles and Cleisthenes falling victim to their own legal decrees.</summary>

# BOOK THIRTEENTH

...eyes. Therefore, things he did not even expect, these the divinity <note>original: "ὁ δαίμων" (ho daimōn); often referring to a divine power or fate.</note> brought upon him contrary to his reputation and hope. For indeed, the boy, having been caught in adultery, was about to suffer the penalties according to his father's law. But so that the law, once ratified, might not be destroyed, the very man who had introduced the law suggested—on behalf of one of his son's eyes—to give up one of his own, so that the young man would not be completely blinded <note>This refers to Zaleucus of Locri, who decreed that adulterers should lose both eyes. When his own son was the first caught, Zaleucus blinded one of his own eyes and one of his son's to satisfy the law while showing mercy.</note>.

### ->Concerning Pindar being defeated in a contest by Corinna<-

Pindar the poet, while competing in Thebes, encountered unlearned listeners and was defeated by Corinna five times. Rebuking their lack of musical taste, Pindar called Corinna a "sow" <note>original: "σῦν" (syn). Pindar was famously frustrated by the Boeotians—residents of the region around Thebes—who had a reputation among other Greeks for being uncultured "sows."</note>.

### ->How Diogenes, falling into a lack of all things,<-
### ->comforted himself<-

Diogenes of Sinope <note>The most famous of the Cynic philosophers, known for living in a ceramic jar and rejecting social conventions.</note> was in a state of isolation and was cast aside alone. He neither took up any particular lifestyle, nor did anyone entertain the man. Driven by the fear of being proven wrong, he was not displeased by his hardships and lacks. Nevertheless, Diogenes was disheartened, and his spirit began to sink <note>original: "φύλλον ἄκρα καθίει"; literally "let down the tips of the leaves," a poetic way of describing losing heart.</note>. But a mouse began to frequent him, making use of the crumbs of bread that fell away. Diogenes carefully observed this companion and, smiling, became more cheerful and gracious toward himself, saying: "This mouse requires nothing of the Athenian luxury. But you, O Diogenes," he said, "are you distressed because you do not dine with the Athenians?" And thus he cured himself of his untimely dejection.

### ->Concerning the body of Socrates<-

It was believed that the body of Socrates was so orderly and possessed of self-control that when the Athenians fell ill as a whole people <note>A reference to the Great Plague of Athens (c. 430 BC) during the Peloponnesian War.</note>, and some were dying while others were near death, Socrates alone did not fall ill at the start. Since he lived with such a body, what kind of soul do we think he possessed?

### ->Concerning the servant of Diogenes <gloss>torn apart by dogs</gloss><-
### ->who ran away<-

When Diogenes left his homeland, one of his servants followed him, named Manes. He, unable to bear the life with him, ran away. When some urged Diogenes to seek him out, he said: "Is it not shameful if Manes does not need Diogenes, but Diogenes needs Manes?"

# BOOK THIRTEENTH

...and this servant, having arrived at Delphi, was torn apart by dogs, paying the penalty in the name of his master for the things on account of which he had been slaughtered.

### ### On hope.

Plato used to say that hopes are the dreams of those who are awake.

### ### Concerning Olympias on the death of Alexander, 
### ### and her distress at his being unburied.

Olympias, the mother of Alexander, learning that her son had remained unburied for a long time, groaned heavily and lamented quite shrilly. "O child," she said, "you indeed desired to have a share of heaven, and were hastening toward this; but now you do not even partake of that which is common to all—and which it is permitted for all men to share—the earth and a burial." Thus she pitied her own fortunes and exposed the vanity <note>original: "τετυφωμένον" (tetuphōmenon); meaning "puffed up" or "conceited," referring here to Alexander's divine ambitions.</note> of her son.

### ### That Xenocrates was full of pity.

Xenocrates the Carthaginian <note>Though the text says Carthaginian, this refers to Xenocrates of Chalcedon, a student of Plato and head of the Academy.</note>, the companion of Plato, was in other respects full of pity; and not only was he a friend to humanity, but he also pitied many of the irrational animals. Once, while he was sitting in the open air, a sparrow, being violently pursued by a hawk, flew into his lap. He gladly received the bird and protected it, hiding it until the pursuer had gone away. When he had freed it from its fear, he opened his lap and released the bird, remarking that he had not betrayed his suppliant.

### ### How Socrates exposed the arrogance of a certain courtesan.

Xenophon says that Socrates entered into conversation with Theodote the courtesan <note>A famous Athenian beauty mentioned in Xenophon's *Memorabilia*.</note>, who was a very beautiful woman. Even with the most beautiful, he entered into debate. She said, "O son of Sophroniscus, I am better than you. For you cannot pull away any of my followers, but I, if I wish, can pull away all of yours." He replied, quite reasonably, "Indeed, for you lead them all down a slope; but I compel them toward the better strength, and the ascent is steep <note>original: "ὀρθία" (orthia); a steep or straight-upward path, contrasting with the "downhill" path of pleasure.</note>, and unfamiliar to the many."

### ### Concerning the fortune of the courtesan Rhodopis.

They say that Rhodopis was a courtesan of the Egyptians, exceedingly beautiful. Once while she was bathing, Fortune—who loves to work the paradoxical and the unexpected—arranged for her a destiny, not because of her mind, but because of her beauty...

This page concludes her story—a legendary precursor to the Cinderella tale—and moves into several short accounts concerning the tyrant Dionysius II, the medicinal habits of deer, the ruthless actions of Alexander the Great's mother Olympias, and the populist tactics of the Syracusan ruler Gelon.</meta>

# BOOK THIRTEENTH

...worthy of her beauty. At any rate, while she was bathing and her handmaidens were guarding her clothes, an eagle flew down, snatched one of her sandals, and flew away. It carried it to Memphis while Psammetichus <note>Psammetichus I, a Pharaoh of the 26th Dynasty (7th century BCE).</note> was sitting in judgment, and dropped the sandal into his lap. Psammetichus, marvelling at the shape of the sandal, the elegance of its workmanship, and the strange action of the bird, sent messengers throughout all Egypt to seek out the woman to whom the sandal belonged. Having found her, he took her as his wife.

### ### Concerning Dionysius.

That Dionysius <note>Likely Dionysius II, the tyrant of Syracuse, notorious for his volatile and suspicious temperament.</note>, after giving the order for the execution of Leon, three times commanded his bodyguards to lead him away. Three times he changed his mind, and at each recall he kissed him while weeping, cursing himself because he had not taken up the sword himself. In the end, he was overcome by fear and ordered him to be slaughtered, saying: "O Leon, I cannot live if you are alive."

### ### Concerning the natural medicine of a deer in sickness.

Naturalists say that if a deer falls ill and requires a purge, it eats celery; and when it is afflicted by the bites of venomous spiders <note>original: "φαλαγγίων" (phalangiōn); referring to the "phalangium" spider, whose bite was considered highly toxic in antiquity.</note>, it eats crabs.

### ### Concerning the death of Eurydice, daughter of Philip.

Olympias <note>The mother of Alexander the Great, known for her ruthless elimination of rivals to her son's throne.</note> sent hemlock, a noose, and a sword to Eurydice, the daughter of Philip <note>Philip II of Macedon, Alexander's father.</note> born to him by an Illyrian woman. Eurydice chose the noose.

### ### Concerning Gelon and those plotting against him.

Gelon, the tyrant of the Syracusans, established his rule with great mildness. When some people were plotting a conspiracy against him, Gelon learned of it and called the Syracusans to an assembly. Gelon entered fully armed and, after recounting all the good deeds he had performed for them, he revealed the conspiracy. He then stripped off his full suit of armor and said to everyone: "Behold, I stand before you in a simple tunic <note>original: "χιτωνίσκῳ" (chitōnisko); a short, light undergarment worn without armor.</note>, stripped of my weapons. I give you leave to do with me whatever you wish." The Syracusans marveled at his spirit. They handed the conspirators over to him for punishment and confirmed his rule. He, however, allowed the people themselves to punish them. The Syracusans set up a statue of him wearing an ungirded tunic, and this served as a memorial of his popular leadership and was intended for those who came after...

->BOOK<-
7

...a lesson for those who will rule in the future. ~

### Concerning Alcibiades. ~

Alcibiades <note>An influential and controversial Athenian general and politician during the Peloponnesian War (5th century BCE).</note> greatly admired Homer. Once, when he visited a school for children, he asked for a volume of the *Iliad*. When the teacher replied that he had nothing written by Homer, Alcibiades struck him with his fist—a blow that was not particularly light—and walked away. He did this to show that the man was uneducated and was making the children the same. When he was called from Sicily by the Athenians to face a trial for which the penalty was death, he did not obey, saying it was foolish for a man under indictment to seek to be acquitted when he could simply flee. When someone asked him, "Do you not trust your own country’s judgment regarding yourself?" he replied, "I do not even trust my mother; for I fear that in her ignorance she might make a mistake and cast the black pebble <note>In Athenian courts, a black pebble indicated a vote for 'guilty,' while a white pebble indicated 'innocent.'</note> instead of the white one." Upon learning that he had been condemned to death by the citizens in his absence, he said, "I will show them that I am alive." Setting off to the Spartans, he incited the Decelean War <note>original: "δεκαλεικὸν" (dekaleikon); a misspelling of the Decelean War (413–404 BCE), the final phase of the Peloponnesian War.</note> against the Athenians. ~

<margin>A</margin>
He used to say that the Spartans did nothing extraordinary by dying without fear in war, for by leaving behind the hardship imposed by their laws, they were eagerly exchanging their labors for death. ~

They say he was also accustomed to say of his own affairs that he lived the life of the Dioscuri <note>The "Dioscuri" refers to the twins Castor and Pollux, who, according to myth, shared immortality by alternating daily between the world of the living and the underworld.</note>, being dead one day and restored to life the next. For when he was in favor with the common people, he was considered equal to the gods, but when he fared poorly, he differed not a bit from the dead. ~

### Concerning Ephialtes. ~

When a certain general reproached Ephialtes <note>An Athenian politician (5th century BCE) known for democratic reforms and his reputation for integrity.</note> for his poverty, he replied: "And why do you not mention the other half of the matter? That I am also just." ~

### Concerning Themistocles. ~

When Themistocles <note>The brilliant Athenian naval strategist who defeated the Persians at Salamis in 480 BCE.</note> came across a twisted Persian ornament <term>streptos <gloss>a torque or collar made of twisted gold worn by Persian nobles</gloss></term> lying on the ground, he said to his servant, "Pick up this find, boy; for you are not Themistocles." <note>Themistocles implies that it is beneath his dignity to scavenge for loot, or perhaps that his reputation is worth more than gold.</note> ~

On one occasion, when the Athenians had caused him distress and then later called him back to leadership, he said: "I do not praise such men, who use the same vessel as a chamber-pot and a wine-jug." <note>original: "ἀμίδα" (amida - chamber-pot) and "οἰνοχόην" (oinochoēn - wine-jug). A metaphor for the citizens using him for "dirty" work and then expecting him to serve as their finest leader.</note> ~

To Eurybiades the Spartan, he was saying something in opposition, and he...

->Concerning Phocion.~<-

**A**s those with Phocion were lamenting their impending execution, Phocion said, "Are you not content, Thudippus, to be dying alongside Phocion?" <note>Phocion (c. 402–318 BCE) was an Athenian statesman and general known for his extreme frugality and honesty. He was eventually forced to drink hemlock by the Athenian assembly.</note> ~

->Concerning Epaminondas.~<-

**E**paminondas faced a capital trial <note>original: "δίκην θανάτου" (dikēn thanatou); a trial where the penalty is death.</note> after returning from Laconia <note>The region surrounding Sparta.</note>, because he had held the office of Boeotarch <note>A chief magistrate of the Boeotian League.</note> for four months longer than the law allowed. Since his colleagues had no resources of their own, he ordered them to place all the blame on him, claiming he had forced them against their will. When he himself came before the court, he stated that his deeds spoke better than any words. But if they did not agree, he asked that they kill him, but to engrave this on his monument: "Against the will of the Thebans, Epaminondas forced them to lay waste to Laconia—which had been unravaged for five hundred years—and to resettle Messene after three hundred years, and to organize and unite the Arcadians, and to restore autonomy to the Greeks." Out of respect for him, the judges acquitted him. When he returned home from the court, a little Melitean dog <note>A breed of ancient lapdog, similar to a Maltese.</note> wagged its tail at him, and he said to those present, "This creature returns gratitude for my kindness, but the Thebans—despite how much good they have received from me—tried me for my life." ~

->Concerning Timotheus.~<-

**T**imotheus, the Athenian general, was believed to be "the Lucky," and he used to say that Fortune was responsible for his successes, and Timotheus for nothing. Because of this, painters used to depict him sleeping while Fortune stood over his head, drawing cities into his fishing net. <note>This was a satirical jab at Timotheus, suggesting his victories were accidental rather than earned through skill.</note> When someone asked Themistocles what was the greatest moment of his life, he replied, "When I saw the whole theater at the Olympic Games turn toward me as I entered the stadium." ~

->Concerning the Rivalry of Themistocles and Aristides.~<-

**T**hemistocles and Aristides, the son of Diomachus <note>The text reads "Diomachus," though historically Aristides was the son of Lysimachus; this may be a transcription error in the original manuscript.</note>, had the same guardians, and because of this, they were raised and educated together by a common teacher. Nevertheless, they were in a state of rivalry even while they were still children, and this competitive spirit <note>original: "φιλονεικία" (philoneikia); a love of strife or intense rivalry.</note> remained with them from their youth until the very end of their old age. ~

BOOK 13.

## Concerning the downfall of Dionysius. ~

Dionysius <note>Dionysius I (c. 432–367 BCE), the tyrant of Syracuse, known for his cruelty and paranoia.</note> murdered his own mother using poison. Furthermore, although he had the power to save his brother Leptines <note>A Syracusan general who died at the Battle of Cronium in 375 BCE.</note> during a naval battle, he stood by and watched him perish. ~

## Concerning a serpent that remembered a kindness. ~

There is a city in Achaia called Patrae. A boy there once bought a small <term>serpent</term> <note>original: "δράκοντα" (drakonta). In antiquity, this referred to a large snake or python rather than a winged, fire-breathing dragon.</note> and raised it with great care. As it grew, he would speak to it as if it could understand him, play with it, and even sleep beside it. When the serpent reached an enormous size, the citizens had it released into the wilderness. 

Later, when the boy had become a young man, he was returning from a public spectacle when he and his companions were attacked by bandits. When a cry was raised, the serpent suddenly appeared! It scattered some of the attackers and killed others, ultimately saving the man's life. ~

# AELIAN’S HISTORICAL MISCELLANY:
# BOOK FOURTEEN. ~

## How Aristotle felt regarding ambition. ~

Aristotle, the son of Nicomachus—a man who was truly wise and was recognized as such—had certain honors that had been voted to him at Delphi taken away. Writing to Antipater <note>A powerful Macedonian general and regent of the empire under Alexander the Great.</note> about these matters, he said: "Regarding the honors voted for me at Delphi, which have now been rescinded, my feelings are such that I neither care for them intensely, nor do I not care at all." 

I would not call this an example of <term>ambition</term> <note>original: "φιλοδοξία" (philodoxia), literally "love of glory" or vanity.</note>, nor would I ever accuse Aristotle of such a thing. Rather, being a man of good sense, he believed that it was not the same thing to never receive an honor as it was to be deprived of one after having received it. For while failing to obtain something is of no great consequence, it is painful to be stripped of what you once possessed. ~

## Concerning Agesilaus and the barbarians who broke their oaths. ~

Agesilaus <note>Agesilaus II (c. 444–360 BCE), the famous warrior-king of Sparta.</note> actually praised those barbarians who broke their oaths. He reasoned that by committing perjury, they had made the gods their own enemies, while for him...

## Concerning Aristophon ~

Timotheus <note>Timotheus (d. 354 BCE) was a prominent Athenian general and statesman.</note>, addressing Aristophon <note>Aristophon of Azenia was a powerful Athenian politician known for his longevity in public life and his many legal battles.</note> when the latter was in a wretched state, rebuked him most bitterly, saying that for a man to whom nothing is enough, nothing is shameful. ~

## Concerning Aristides, who was bitten by a weasel and died ~

Aristides the Locrian <note>A philosopher from Locri and a character in Plato's dialogues.</note>, having been bitten by a Tartessian <term>weasel</term> <note>original: "γαλῆς" (galēs). In ancient Greece, the weasel or polecat was often kept to control pests before the domestic cat became common. Tartessos, in modern-day Spain, was famous for its exotic exports.</note>, said as he was dying that it would have been much more pleasant for him to die from the bite of a lion or a leopard, if indeed he had to die by some beast. It seems to me that he bore the disgrace of such a bite much more heavily than he did death itself. ~

## Which men the Athenians employed for their magistracies ~

The Athenians did not only employ citizens whom they deemed suitable for magistracies and military commands, but they also chose foreigners and entrusted public affairs to them, provided they judged them to be good men and fit for such duties. ~

They frequently chose Apollodorus of Cyzicus as a general, even though he was a foreigner, as well as Heraclides of Clazomenae; for once these men proved they were of notable worth, they were not deemed unworthy to lead the Athenians. In this respect, the city should be praised for not sacrificing the truth for the sake of its citizens, but rather granting honors even to foreigners who did not belong to the city by birth, but were worthy of honor because of their virtue. ~

## Aristippus’s opinion on cheerfulness ~

Aristippus <note>Aristippus of Cyrene (c. 435–356 BCE), a pupil of Socrates and founder of the Cyrenaic school of philosophy, which emphasized pleasure and the present moment.</note> seemed to speak with great vigor when he advised people neither to trouble themselves over things past nor to worry in advance about things to come. Such a state is a sign of <term>euthymia</term> <note>original: "εὐθυμίας" (euthymias). Often translated as "cheerfulness" or "contentment," it refers to a steady, tranquil state of mind.</note> and a proof of a gracious intellect. He commanded that one should keep one’s mind focused on the present day, and furthermore, on that specific part of the day in which one is either acting or thinking. For he used to say that only the present belongs to us, and neither what has passed nor what is expected; for the one has perished, and the other—it is unclear whether it will ever even be. ~

This is a Spartan law, and the law states that no Spartan should appear to have a complexion or a bodily bulk that is too shameless...

### BOOK FOURTEEN

...to keep the body [fit] through gymnastic exercises. For it seemed that while the one state confessed to laziness, the other refused to admit to anything shameful. It was also written in the law that every ten days the **ephebes** <note>Young men, typically aged 18–20, undergoing military and civic training.</note> must present themselves naked in public before the **ephors** <note>original: "ἐφόροις" (ephorois). The five senior Spartan magistrates who oversaw the conduct of citizens and kings.</note>. If they appeared well-built, strong, and as if "sculpted" by their exercises and properly proportioned, they were praised. But if any part of their limbs was flabby, or if they were somewhat bloated and puffed up with fat due to idleness, they were beaten and punished on the spot. The ephors also took great care to busy themselves with investigating their attire, ensuring that every part of it did not fall short of the required modesty. The **Lacedaemonian** <note>Another name for the Spartans.</note> cooks were required to prepare meat only. Anyone who understood more than this <note>i.e., someone who attempted to cook gourmet or complex meals.</note> was driven out of Sparta, like the refuse of the sick.

<margin>O</margin> These same officials accused Naucleides, the son of Polybiades, who was fleshy in his body and had become somewhat soft through luxury. In the assembly of the Lacedaemonians, they brought charges against him and threatened him with a penalty unless he changed his way of life—which he was then living in a blameworthy manner, more **Ionic** than **Laconic** <term>Laconic</term> <note>original: "Λακωνικὸν" (Lakonikon). Referring to the Spartan ideal of austerity, brevity, and discipline, as opposed to "Ionic" luxury.</note>—for the future. They told him that his very appearance and the condition of his body were a disgrace both to Lacedaemon and to its laws.

<margin>A</margin> ->How Polykleitus and Hippomachus exposed the<-
->ignorance of the masses.<-

<margin>P</margin> **Polykleitus** <note>One of the most famous Greek sculptors of the 5th century BCE, known for his "Canon" on human proportions.</note> crafted two statues at the same time: one to please the crowds, and the other according to the law of art. He sought to please the masses in this way: according to the advice of each person who entered his workshop, he changed and reshaped something, following the suggestions of everyone. He then put both statues on display. The one was admired by all, while the other was laughed at. Polykleitus then spoke up and said, "But this one, which you criticize, you made yourselves; but this other one, which you admire, I made."

**Hippomachus** the flute-player, when a student of his made a mistake while playing the flute but was nevertheless praised by the onlookers, struck the student with a rod and said, "You played poorly, for otherwise these people would not have praised you."

### How Phocion replied with silence to Demades.

When they proposed to **Phocion** <note>An Athenian statesman and general known for his integrity and biting wit.</note> that they wanted **Demades** <note>An Athenian orator and rival of Phocion, often associated with corruption and pro-Macedonian views.</note> to serve as general, Demades, who had already sent for him and was feeling very proud, approached Phocion and said: "Lend me the dirty cloak you usually wear during your generalship." To which Phocion replied: "You will never lack for anything dirty, so long as you are the man you are." ~

### How a ruler should behave toward his subjects.

**Philiscus** <note>A Cynic philosopher.</note> once said to **Alexander** <note>Alexander the Great.</note>: "Take care for your reputation, but do not be a plague, nor a great disease, but rather peace and health." He said this because to rule by force and bitterness, to seize cities, and to destroy populations is the mark of a plague; whereas to provide for the health and safety of subjects is to bring the blessings of peace to all. ~

### What the Persian King did while traveling.

The King of the Persians, while traveling, so as not to be idle, kept a small piece of scrap wood and a knife to carve the wood, and this is what the King's hands worked at. He certainly had no book, nor any deep thought, so that he might read something serious or solemn, or deliberate on something noble and worthy of speech.

### Regarding the tragedies of Agathon.

**Agathon** <note>A famous Athenian tragic poet, known for his beautiful appearance and highly stylized, ornamental language.</note> frequently used **antitheses** <note>A rhetorical device involving the use of contrasting concepts or words in a balanced way.</note> in his many works. When someone attempted to correct him and wanted to remove these from each of his plays, Agathon said: "My good man, you have unknowingly erased Agathon from Agathon!" So much did he pride himself on these things, and he believed his tragedy consisted of this very style. ~

### Regarding Stratonicus the citharode.

Someone received **Stratonicus the citharode** <note>original: "κιθαρωδὸν" (kitharodon). A professional performer who sang while playing the kithara (lyre). Stratonicus was famous for his wit.</note> with great hospitality. Stratonicus was delighted by the invitation, for he happened to have no place to stay upon arriving in a foreign land. However, he soon became annoyed with the man because of the lack of privacy in the shared living space. When it became clear that the house was open to everyone who chose to stay there—making it loud and, in a sense, unrestrained—Stratonicus said to his servant: "Let us get out of here, boy, for we seem to have a 'vocal' house instead of an inn, carrying ourselves from the house into public lodgings." <note>Stratonicus is making a pun on the noise and lack of privacy, suggesting the "private" home was louder and more crowded than a commercial hotel.</note> ~

BOOK
80

### Regarding the words of Socrates. ~

A certain story went around saying that the speech of **Socrates** <note>The famous Athenian philosopher (c. 470–399 BC) who never wrote anything himself, but whose "Socratic method" of questioning often seemed indirect or confusing to his listeners.</note> was like the paintings of **Pauson** <note>A Greek painter often criticized for his realism or focus on the unseemly side of life.</note>. For they say that Pauson the painter, having heard from someone a request to paint a horse rolling on its back, instead painted it running. When the man who commissioned the tablet expressed his anger, as he had painted it contrary to their agreement, the painter replied: "Just turn the tablet over, and the running horse will be the rolling one for you." And so Socrates did not converse clearly; however, if one "turns" <note>reinterprets or looks at it from another perspective</note> what he says, it is perfectly correct. It seems he did not wish to speak at length <note>original: "ἀποτείνεσθαι" — to stretch out a speech or speak pedantically</note> among those with whom he conversed, and because of this, his actions seemed enigmatic and indirect. ~

### Regarding the ambition of Hipponicus. ~

**Hipponicus**, the son of Callias <note>A member of one of the wealthiest and most influential families in Athens, known for their diplomatic roles.</note>, wanted to set up a statue as a dedication to his fatherland. When someone advised him to have the statue crafted by **Polyclitus** <note>One of the most celebrated sculptors of classical antiquity, famous for his idealized proportions of the human body.</note>, he said he would not pay attention to such a dedication, where the glory would belong not to the person who dedicated it, but to the person who made it. For it was clear that those seeing the artistry were going to admire Polyclitus, but they would not marvel at him (Hipponicus). ~

### Regarding Archelaus and the painting of Zeuxis. ~

Socrates used to say that **Archelaus** <note>King of Macedon (r. 413–399 BC), who sought to modernize his kingdom by inviting famous Greek artists and poets to his court.</note> spent forty minae <note>A significant sum of money; one mina was roughly 100 days' wages for a skilled laborer.</note> on his house, hiring **Zeuxis of Heraclea** <note>One of the most famous painters of the 5th century BC, known for his lifelike style.</note> to decorate it with paintings, but spent nothing on his own character. Consequently, those who wished to see the house were seen arriving with great haste, but no one set out for Macedonia for the sake of Archelaus himself, unless he persuaded them with money or lured them with bribes—by which means a man would not be chosen to be among the virtuous. ~

### How a certain man, being angry, wished to punish his servant. ~

A worthy man, being angry with his servant, said: "I will not throw you into the mill <note>Sending a servant to the mill to grind grain was a common and grueling punishment in antiquity.</note>, but I will take you to **Olympia**." For he thought that punishment was much more bitter, as it seems, to be "roasted" by the sun's rays while watching the games at Olympia, than to be handed over to grind at the mill. <note>The heat and dust of the mid-summer Olympic Games were notoriously uncomfortable for spectators.</note> ~

### Stories regarding the self-control of Archytas. ~

**Archytas** <note>A Pythagorean philosopher, mathematician, and statesman of Tarentum (428–347 BC).</note> was clearly a man of self-control <note>original: "σώφρων" — referring to the virtue of *sophrosyne*, or moderation and soundness of mind.</note> at that time, and he especially guarded himself against unseemly words. When he was once pressured to say something inappropriate, he was not overcome; rather, he remained silent himself, but wrote the word upon the wall and pointed to it. What he was pressured to speak, he did not say, even under pressure. ~

FOURTEENTH [BOOK]

### Regarding a foolish story ~

A **Sybarite** <note>A resident of Sybaris, a Greek city in Italy famous for its extreme luxury and the self-indulgent lifestyle of its citizens—the origin of the word "sybaritic."</note> man, who was a **pedagogue** <note>original: παιδαγωγὸς — a household slave whose duty was to supervise a child and escort them to school, rather than a teacher of academic subjects.</note>—for indeed, like the other Sybarites, even the pedagogues lived luxuriously—was leading a boy along the road. When the boy happened upon a dried fig and picked it up, the tutor rebuked him most severely. However, in a most ridiculous turn, he then snatched the find from the boy and ate it himself. When I encountered this in the Sybarite histories, I laughed; I have committed it to memory and recorded it here, out of a spirit of kindness, so that someone else might also share in the laugh without envy. ~

### Regarding the poet Syagrus ~

**Syagrus** <note>A legendary or semi-historical poet often cited as a bridge between the mythical era of Orpheus and the historical era of Homer.</note> was a poet who came after **Orpheus** and **Musaeus**. It is said that he was the first to sing of the **Trojan War**, having taken hold of this greatest of themes and having dared to attempt it. ~

### Regarding a tyrant who prevented his subjects from conversing with one another ~

A certain tyrant named **Hiero** <note>Likely Hiero I of Syracuse (r. 478–467 BC), who was known for employing a network of spies and informers called "ear-droppers."</note>, wishing to root out conspiracies and plots against him, ordered the local people that no one should speak to anyone else, neither in public nor in private. This was an impossible and difficult situation. They obeyed the tyrant’s command, yet they would nod to one another and use hand-gestures to communicate. Sometimes they would show their feelings through a look of fear while remaining silent, or again by looking cheerful, or by knitting their brows in a dark and incurable gloom. In this way, each person clearly demonstrated the emotion of their soul to their neighbor through their facial expression. This also troubled the tyrant; he believed that some evil would surely be born from this, fearing their silence because of the variety of their gestures. Consequently, he tried to put a stop to this as well.

One man, however, distressed by this impossible situation and suffering under the weight of it—and thirsting to destroy the monarchy—came into the marketplace where he wept openly before the crowds with thick, streaming tears. The multitude gathered around him and joined him, and they too were seized by wailing. Word reached the tyrant that no one was obeying his law anymore; speech was gone, but weeping had become the local custom. Hurrying to stop this as well—condemning to slavery not only the tongue and thoughts, but now even closing off the natural freedom of the eyes—he arrived as fast as his feet could carry him with his bodyguards to suppress the weeping. But they had no sooner seen him...

<meta>catchword: σ (likely continuing the scene of the uprising or the tyrant's confrontation)</meta>

### Regarding Cleinias, and how Achilles philosophically restrained his anger with music :~

**Cleinias** <note>A Pythagorean philosopher from Tarentum (4th century BC). The Pythagoreans believed music had mathematical and medicinal power over the human soul.</note> was a man of serious character, and a **Pythagorean** in his philosophy. Whenever he fell into a state of anger and felt his temper rising—before the rage could fully overcome him and it became obvious to others—he would take up his lyre and play. When asked for the reason, he would respond harmoniously, "I am calming down." It seems to me that even **Achilles** in the *Iliad* <note>Referring to the scene in Book 9 where the embassy finds Achilles singing of the "glories of men" (klea andron) to soothe his resentment against Agamemnon.</note>, singing to his lyre and bringing the famous deeds of those who came before him to mind through melody, was soothing his wrath; for being musical, the lyre was the first thing he chose from the spoils of war. :~

### Regarding those who despised wealth for the sake of their fellow citizens, and those who killed their creditors :~

They despised wealth and displayed great magnanimity when they saw their fellow citizens in poverty while they themselves were rich: **Theocles** and **Thrasynides** in **Corinth**, and **Praxis** in **Mitylene**. Indeed, they advised others to provide for the needs of the poor, but since they could not persuade them, they at least forgave the debts owed to themselves. They profited not in silver, but in their own souls. For those who were not forgiven, being hard-pressed by their creditors and arming themselves with anger—using the most reasonable of motives, an unbeatable need born of their circumstances—killed their creditors. :~

### How someone persuaded a city to establish harmony :~

The **Chians** <note>Residents of the island of Chios.</note> once fell into a most strange civil strife <note>original: ἐστασίασαν — from "stasis," the Greek term for internal political conflict or revolution, often described by ancient writers as a biological disease of the state.</note>, suffering from this very heavy disease. A man among them, a politician by nature, said to his companions who were eager to drive out all their opponents: "Not at all! Since we have prevailed, let us leave some behind, so that as time goes by, if we have no rivals, we do not start warring with ourselves." He spoke and persuaded them, for he seemed to speak well, since he spoke thus. :~

### Regarding Antagoras reviling Arcesilaus :~

**Antagoras** <note>Antagoras of Rhodes, a poet known for his wit at the court of Antigonus Gonatas.</note> the poet used to revile **Arcesilaus** <note>The founder of the Middle Academy, known for his skepticism.</note> of the **Academy**...

...bringing these very things <note>The "things" mentioned here are eels. According to the full anecdote, the poet Antagoras of Rhodes was mocked for cooking eels himself; he retaliated by bringing them into the public square to show he wasn't ashamed.</note> into the marketplace. He walked with great **magnanimity** toward where he saw the largest crowds gathered; there he went about discussing his business so that the person insulting him would appear more disgraceful in front of a larger audience. Those who heard the exchange turned away, however, and accused **Antagoras** of madness. :~

### Regarding Agesilaus :~
I give the highest praise to those who always destroy the first seeds of evil, getting rid of them before they can seize any real power. **Agesilaus** <note>Agesilaus II (c. 444–360 BC), the famous warrior-king of Sparta.</note> therefore advised that those who were caught gathering secretly at night during the **Theban invasion** should be executed without a trial. :~

### Regarding the orator Pytheas :~
Someone once reproached the orator **Pytheas** <note>An Athenian orator known for his opposition to Demosthenes.</note>, saying that he was a wicked man. He did not deny it, for his conscience would not allow him to; however, he replied that he had been wicked for the shortest amount of time compared to anyone else in Athenian politics. He took great pride in the fact that he was not like that throughout his whole career, and that by remaining quiet, he avoided doing wrong since he did not surround himself with the most villainous men. This saying of Pytheas was beneficial; for in my judgment, not only is the one who commits a crime wicked, but also the one who even intends to do wrong. :~

### That Lysander brought money into Sparta :~
When **Lysander** <note>The Spartan admiral who defeated Athens in the Peloponnesian War.</note> brought wealth into **Lacedaemon** [Sparta], he caused the Spartans to hesitate regarding the law of the god <note>The Oracle of Delphi (the Pythian), who reportedly warned that "love of money will destroy Sparta."</note>, which commanded that **Sparta** should be inaccessible to gold and silver. Some of the wiser men, still possessing the old **Spartan** spirit and being worthy of **Lycurgus** <note>The legendary lawgiver of Sparta who established their austere, money-free constitution.</note> and the **Pythian** oracle, tried to prevent this. But those who were persuaded by the money were corrupted, and their original virtue gradually failed. :~

### How Hanno wished to be seen as a god :~
**Hanno the Carthaginian** <note>Likely Hanno the Great. This story illustrates the theme of "hubris" or excessive pride.</note>, driven by a natural vanity, did not think it enough to remain within the limits of humanity. Instead, he devised a plan to have reports spread about himself that were greater than the nature allotted to any man. He took a vast number of **songbirds** and raised them in the dark, teaching them to say a single phrase: "**Hanno is a god.**" When the birds had mastered this one sound from hearing it constantly, he released them in different directions, believing that through these birds, a hymn to his own divinity would be spread. But once the birds had spread their wings, gained their freedom, and returned to their natural habitats, they sang their own native songs, and...

...the birds <note>original: "τὰ τῶν ὀρνίθων"</note> insulted me, bidding a long farewell to **Hanno** <note>Hanno the Navigator (5th century BC), a Carthaginian explorer who, according to legend, caught birds and taught them to say "Hanno is a god," but once released, they sang their own songs again.</note> and the lessons they had learned during their slavery. :~

### Regarding Ptolemy, nicknamed "The Luxurious" :~

**Ptolemy**, nicknamed **Tryphon** <note>original: "τρύφων" – meaning "The Luxurious" or "The Reveler." This refers to Ptolemy VIII Physcon, known for his opulence and cruelty.</note>—for so they called him because of his lifestyle—was approached by a beautiful woman who wished to win his favor. He said to her, "My sister <note>Likely referring to Cleopatra II, his wife and sister, who held significant power.</note> has forbidden me from entertaining words from any beautiful woman." She replied, quite fearlessly and with great wit: "You could say you heard these words from a handsome man." Upon hearing this, he praised her. :~

### Regarding Timandridas, who did not praise his son for becoming wealthy.

A Spartan man named **Timandridas**, having gone abroad, left his son in charge of the household. When he returned some time later and found that his son had increased their estate far beyond what had been left to him, he said that the youth had greatly wronged the gods, his own kin, and their guests. He believed that the majority of such wealth should have been spent by a free man on those very duties. For a man to appear as a pauper while living, only to be found wealthy after death, is the most shameful thing among men. :~

### Regarding Plato and Diogenes :~

**Plato** was once lecturing on certain topics when **Diogenes** <note>Diogenes of Sinope (c. 412–323 BC), the famous Cynic philosopher known for his public defiance and simple living.</note>, who was present, paid very little attention to him. **Aeschines** <note>Aeschines of Sphettus, a follower of Socrates.</note> was indignant at this and said, "Listen to the words, you dog!" <note>A play on Diogenes' nickname, "The Cynic," which translates to "The Dog-like."</note> Diogenes, not at all disturbed, replied, "But I did not return from that place where I suffered just to behave like a dog," <note>original: "ἐκείθεν ὅθεν ἐπάθω" — Diogenes is making a biting pun.</note> alluding to Plato’s failed voyages to **Sicily**. It is said that Plato used to say of Diogenes: "That man is a **Socrates** gone mad." :~

### From whom the Egyptians learned their laws, and regarding their judges :~

The **Egyptians** claim that their laws were inspired by **Hermes** <note>Often identified with the Egyptian god Thoth, the patron of writing and law.</note>. In this way, all peoples are naturally inclined to treat their own traditions with great solemnity. In ancient times, the judges among the Egyptians were priests; the eldest among them was the leader and judged all cases. It was required that he be the most just and impartial of men. He wore an image around his neck made of sapphire stone, and the image was called **Truth** <note>original: "ἀλήθεια"</note>. However, I believe it is best that a judge does not wear a truth made of stone or a mere likeness of it, but should hold truth within his very soul. :~

### Regarding Lais :~

That **Lais** <note>A famous and high-priced hetaira (courtesan) of Corinth.</note> was also called "The Ax" <note>original: "ἀξίνη" (axine)</note>. This nickname proved her character...

-># BOOK FOURTEENTH<-

...the harshness of the sun, and that it had been greatly scorched, and his mind became even more gentle, as they would soon be released.

### That those who are proud because of their fathers are ridiculous.

It is right to laugh at those who take great pride in their fathers and yet have no noble lineage of their own. We do not know the father of **Marius** <note>Gaius Marius (c. 157–86 BC), a Roman general who was a "novus homo" (new man), meaning he was the first in his family to serve in the Roman Senate.</note>, yet we admire the man himself because of his great deeds; the same is true of **Cato**, **Servilius**, **Osilius**, and even **Romulus** <note>The legendary founder of Rome, whose parentage was often debated or attributed to the god Mars, emphasizing merit over established human nobility.</note>.

### Concerning statues and portraits.

He says that we should not look simply at the statues shown to us by the art of sculpture, nor at portraits. For there is a certain habit in craftsmanship, even in these things. While it is possible to find fault with many other things for having such a habit, no painter or sculptor was ever able to lie so as to make the works of the daughters of **Zeus** <note>original: "Διὸς θυγατέρων" — The Muses, who presided over the arts and sciences.</note> counterfeit or foreign to them. Nor was any creator so youthfully mad as to craft the Muses for us as armed figures. He perhaps agrees that a life lived among the Muses should be both peaceful and gentle, and worthy of them.

### Concerning Epaminondas and Pelopidas.

I know many other fine things about **Epaminondas** of **Thebes** <note>A celebrated Theban general (c. 418–362 BC) known for his tactical genius and integrity.</note>, but this too was well-said: he used to tell **Pelopidas** <note>Epaminondas's closest friend and fellow general.</note> never to leave the marketplace for the day until he had made a new friend to add to his old ones.

### How Antalcidas exposed a gift dipped in perfume that was sent to him.

The King of the Persians, wanting to say something pleasant to you, dipped a crown of woven roses into perfume and sent it to **Antalcidas** <note>A Spartan diplomat who negotiated the "King's Peace" in 387 BC.</note> while he was acting as an ambassador for peace. Antalcidas accepted the gift and said, "I praise your kindness, but you have destroyed the scent of the roses and the natural fragrance through the counterfeit nature <note>original: "κιβδηλίαν" — referring to something adulterated or fake, like a forged coin.</note> of art."

### Concerning the cruelty of Alexander, the tyrant of Pherae.

**Alexander**, the tyrant of **Pherae** <note>A ruler of Thessaly (reigned 369–358 BC) famous for his extreme bloodthirstiness.</note>, was considered to be among the most exceptionally cruel of men. However, when **Theodorus**, the tragic actor, performed a role before him with great passion, the tyrant burst into tears, as he saw...

-># BOOK FOURTEENTH<-

...the setting of the theater; as he was leaving, he told **Theodorus** <note>Theodorus was one of the most famous tragic actors of the 4th century BC, known for his ability to evoke deep emotion.</note> that he had not departed out of contempt or a desire to dishonor him, but because he was ashamed: he felt it was wrong that he was able to pity the sufferings of an actor on stage, yet could not show the same pity for the citizens of his own government.

### Concerning the madness of Apollodorus caused by wine

It is said that **Apollodorus** <note>Apollodorus of Cassandreia (3rd century BC) was a notoriously cruel tyrant of the city of Cassandreia in Macedonia.</note>, who drank more wine than almost any other man, did not try to hide the vice of his drinking, nor did he attempt to distance himself from intoxication and the evils that follow it. Instead, he would become inflamed and kindled by the wine, growing more murderous in his disposition, as his body was naturally prone to its effects.

### The opinion of Xenocrates

**Xenocrates** <note>A student of Plato and the third leader of the Academy in Athens (c. 396–314 BC), known for his stern moral character.</note>, the companion of **Plato**, used to say that there was no difference between putting one’s feet or one's eyes into another person’s house. He believed that a person committed the same sin whether they looked upon things they should not, or entered places where they were not permitted.

### Concerning Ptolemy and Berenice

They say that **Ptolemy** <note>Likely Ptolemy I Soter, the founder of the Ptolemaic Kingdom in Egypt, or his son Ptolemy II Philadelphus.</note> was sitting at the dice and spent his time playing. While he was playing, a clerk stood by him and read out the names of those who had been condemned to death and their sentences, so that Ptolemy might signify who was to be executed. But **Berenice** <note>The queen and wife of Ptolemy, known for her influence and wisdom.</note>, his wife, took the book from the servant and would not allow the rest to be read. She told him that he ought to apply his mind with the utmost seriousness when debating the life of a human being, and not treat it like a game; for the "fall" of dice and the "fall" of human lives are not at all the same thing. Ptolemy was pleased by this advice, and thereafter, whenever he played dice, he never again listened to such judicial matters.

### A Spartan law concerning the love of money

A **Lacedaemonian** <note>Spartan.</note> youth once bought a plot of land for less than its value. He was then brought before the magistrates and fined. The reason for his condemnation was this: that even though he was young, he had already begun to focus too sharply on making a profit. For the Spartans considered it most shameful not to be battle-ready against money in the same way they were battle-ready against their enemies.

### Concerning certain women worthy of praise

Among the women of the Greeks, we praise **Penelope** <note>The faithful wife of Odysseus.</note>, **Alcestis** <note>Who offered her own life to save her husband, Admetus.</note>, and the wife of **Protesilaus** <note>Laodamia, who was so devoted she died of grief for her husband.</note>. Among the Romans, we praise **Cornelia** <note>Mother of the Gracchi, famed for her virtue and education.</note>, **Portia** <note>Wife of Brutus, known for her stoicism.</note>, and **Castlia** <note>Likely referring to Caecilia Metella, though the transcription "Kastlia" is slightly unusual in Greek for this name.</note>. I could mention many others, but I do not wish to name only a few Greeks and then flood the page with Roman names...

<meta>This page concludes the fourteenth and final book of Aelian’s "Various History." The opening sentence finishes the previous anecdote regarding Alexander of Pherae’s reaction to a tragic performance, where he felt self-conscious about his own display of emotion compared to his harsh rule.</meta>

...this very praise might seem to be a favor I grant myself on account of my homeland. <note>original: "διὰ τὴν πατρίδα." Aelian likely refers to his Roman roots or his Greek education, suggesting he is biased toward the virtues he is describing.</note>

->### Concerning the battle formation of the Magnesians against the Ephesians<-

The **Magnesians** <note>Inhabitants of Magnesia on the Maeander, an ancient Greek city in Ionia (modern-day Turkey).</note> who lived along the **Maeander River**, when at war with the **Ephesians**, each had a companion in battle: a hunting dog and a servant who was a javelin-thrower. When it was time to engage the enemy, the dogs would spring forward first and throw the enemy ranks into confusion; they were terrifying, wild, and relentless in their pursuit. Following them, the servants would spring forward ahead of their masters and hurl their javelins. In this way, the disorder caused by the dogs and the sudden charge of the servants left the enemy vulnerable, and then the masters themselves would strike to finish the battle.

->### Concerning Zeuxis' painting of Helen, and the painter Nicostratus<-

**Zeuxis of Heraclea** <note>One of the most famous painters of the 4th century BC, renowned for his realism.</note> painted a portrait of **Helen**. The painter **Nicostratus** was so struck with amazement upon seeing the image that he appeared to be in a state of passion, visibly overwhelmed. Someone approached him and asked, "What has happened to you that you admire this piece of art so much?" **Nicostratus** replied, "You would not have asked me that if you possessed my eyes." I would say the same thing regarding the power of words: who has ears so well-educated as to match the technical eyes of these artists?

->### Whom Alexander suspected<-

**Alexander** <note>Alexander the Great.</note> looked upon **Ptolemy** with suspicion because of his influence; he feared the disorderly nature of **Arribas**, and was wary of the revolutionary spirit of **Python**.

->### Why Philip kept the sons of the most distinguished men<-
->### in his personal service<-

**Philip** <note>Philip II of Macedon, father of Alexander the Great.</note> took the sons of the most distinguished men in **Macedonia** and kept them in his personal service. It is said he did this not to insult them or to treat them as low-status, but to refine them through their daily habits. He intended to make them hardy and accustomed to toil, rendering them ready for all necessary duties. However, toward those who lived luxuriously or who were lazy regarding their assigned tasks, he was said to be hostile. For instance, he whipped **Aphthonetus** because he left his position in the marching line to go to an inn because he was thirsty. He also executed **Archelaus** because, although the king had ordered him to remain in his armor, the young man had taken it off. Archelaus had hoped to win over the king through flattery and subservience, being a man who valued profit above all else.

<meta>This page transitions from the scholarly definitions and literary criticism of the previous page to a historical and political summary of the Athenian state. It begins a series of excerpts from Heraclides Lembus’s epitome (a condensed version) of Aristotle’s "Constitution of the Athenians."</meta>

73
<header>FROM THE WORKS OF HERACLIDES ON CONSTITUTIONS.</header>
<sig>T</sig>

### ~ The Athenians ~

From the beginning, the Athenians were governed by a king. When Ion established their settlement, they were then for the first time called "Ionians." Pandion, who reigned after Erechtheus, divided the rule among his sons, and they continued in a state of civil strife. Theseus, however, made a proclamation and brought them together on equal and like honor <note>This refers to the "synoecism," the political unification of Attica under Athens</note>. He died upon going to Scyros, having been pushed down the cliffs by Lycomedes, who feared that Theseus would seize the island for himself. Long after the Persian Wars, the Athenians brought back his bones. After the descendants of Codrus <note>The Codrids were the last semi-mythical royal family of Athens</note>, they no longer chose kings because they seemed to live in luxury and had become soft. Hippomenes, one of the Codrids, wishing to dispel this slander, caught an adulterer with his daughter Leimone; he killed the man by yoking him to a chariot alongside his daughter, and he shut her in with a horse until she perished. ~

Those who followed Cylon in his attempt at tyranny and fled to the altar of the goddess <note>The goddess Athena</note> were killed by Megacles and his followers; they drove out those who had committed the deed as being under a curse <note>original: "ἐναγεῖς" - referring to the "Cylonian Curse" that haunted the Alcmaeonid family for generations</note>. ~

Solon, the lawmaker of the Athenians, performed a cancellation of debts, which was called the "Shaking-off of Burdens" <term>Seisachtheia</term>. When certain people began to trouble him regarding the laws, he went abroad to Egypt. ~

Pisistratus, having ruled as a tyrant for thirty-three years, died in his old age. ~

Hipparchus, the son of Pisistratus, was fond of play, amorous, and a lover of the arts. ~

Thessalus was younger and bold. When the people were unable to kill him while he was tyrant, they killed his brother Hipparchus. ~

Hippias ruled more harshly as a tyrant; he introduced the law concerning ostracism <term>Ostracismus</term> <note>While the text attributes this to the tyrant Hippias, most historical sources credit the reformer Cleisthenes with the law of ostracism</note>, which was established because of those seeking to become tyrants. Among others, Xanthippus and Aristides were ostracized. ~

Ephialtes, in his own field, did whatever those who wished required of him as long as he lived <note>This likely refers to Ephialtes’ reputation for extreme personal poverty and integrity while he reduced the power of the aristocratic council of the Areopagus</note>.

<vocab>Heraclides, Athenian Republic, Ion, Theseus, Codridae, Solon, Seisachtheia (Relief of debts), Pisistratus, Hipparchus, Hippias, Ostracism, Ephialtes</vocab>

<summary>This page provides a condensed history of early Athens, tracing its evolution from a monarchy to a tyranny and the beginnings of democratic reforms under Solon and the law of ostracism.</summary>
<keywords>Athenian history, Theseus, Solon, Pisistratids, Ostracism, Ephialtes, constitutional history</keywords>
