<meta>This page continues the excerpts from Heraclides Lembus’s epitome of Aristotle’s "Constitutions." It transitions from the reforms of Solon and the era of the tyrants into the late 5th-century political shifts in Athens, before moving to a new section on the Lacedaemonian (Spartan) constitution.</meta>

...from which he used to feast many people. ~

**C**leon took over and corrupted the government, and even more so those who came after him; they filled everything with lawlessness and killed no fewer than fifteen hundred citizens. <note>This likely refers to the "Thirty Tyrants," a pro-Spartan oligarchy that ruled Athens after the Peloponnesian War.</note> ~

**W**hen these men were overthrown, Thrasybulus and Rhinon came forward to lead; Rhinon was a man of noble character and high standing. <note>original: "καλὸς κἀγαθός" (kalos kagathos) — a term describing the ideal gentleman-citizen who possesses both physical beauty and moral virtue.</note> ~

**T**hemistocles, Aristides, and the Council of the <term>Areopagus</term> <note>The "Hill of Ares," the oldest and most prestigious council in Athens, composed of former high officials.</note> held great power. They took care of the roads, ensuring that no one built upon them or cut into the railings. Likewise, they also appointed "The Eleven," who were to take charge of those in the prison. There are also nine officials known as <term>Thesmothetai</term> <note>Law-givers or junior archons.</note>; after they are scrutinized for office, they swear an oath to rule justly and not to accept bribes—or else they must dedicate a golden statue. The King <note>The "Archon Basileus," a magistrate who held the religious titles of the old kings.</note> manages matters concerning the sacrifices and, according to this text, matters of war. ~

->Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans</note>. ~<-

**S**ome attribute the entire <term>Politeia</term> <note>The constitution or way of life of a city-state.</note> of the Lacedaemonians to Lycurgus. As for Alcman, he was a servant of Agesidas, but because he was naturally gifted, he was freed and became a poet. ~

**L**ycurgus died in Samos. Having obtained the poetry of Homer from the descendants of Creophylus, he was the first to bring it to the Peloponnese. Finding great lawlessness in his homeland, he removed Charillus from his tyrannical rule and established the "sacred truce" as a common good. It is also said that he introduced the <term>Krypteia</term> <note>A "secret police" system where young Spartans were sent into the countryside.</note>, according to which even now they go out and hide during the day, but at night they go about with weapons and kill as many of the <term>Helots</term> <note>The state-owned serfs of Sparta.</note> as is deemed necessary. ~

**T**hey also appoint <term>Ephors</term> <note>Five "overseers" who held vast power to check the kings.</note>, and these men have the greatest authority; they rise for no one except the King and their fellow Ephors. When a King dies, nothing is sold for three days, and the marketplace is strewn with chaff. ~

**T**he Lacedaemonians honored the singer from Lesbos, for the god commanded them through an oracle to listen to him. It is considered shameful for Lacedaemonians to sell land; it is not even permitted to sell any of the "ancient portion." ~

**A**ll ornamentation has been taken away from the Lacedaemonian women; they are not allowed to wear their hair long, nor to wear gold. They raise their children so as never to be full, so that they may become accustomed to being able to go hungry; they also habituate them to stealing...

<summary>This page outlines the transition of Athenian leadership from Cleon to the restoration of democracy under Thrasybulus, followed by an overview of Spartan (Lacedaemonian) laws, the legend of Lycurgus, and the harsh social customs of the Spartan state.</summary>
<keywords>Cleon, Thrasybulus, Areopagus, Lycurgus, Sparta, Krypteia, Helots, Ephors, Spartan women, Athenian law, Homeric poetry</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Lacedaemonian (Spartan) customs before transitioning to the constitution and social habits of the Cretans. These excerpts are likely derived from an epitome of Aristotle’s lost "Constitutions," possibly by the 2nd-century BCE scholar Heraclides Lembus.</meta>

...and if they are caught, they punish them with lashes, so that through this they may be able to endure labor and go without sleep during wars. From childhood they practice speaking briefly <note>This is the origin of the term "laconic," named after the Lacedaemonian region of Laconia.</note>, then harmoniously, as well as how to mock and be mocked. Burials are simple and equal for all. No one eats wheat bread; indeed, they do not even bring in flour, but instead eat barley-groats.

Note that those Lacedaemonians who were once captured were enslaved, just as the Thessalians enslaved the **penestai** <note>original: "πενέστας" — a class of unfree laborers in Thessaly, similar to the Spartan Helots.</note>.

Note that when the Lacedaemonians were at war with the Messenians, the women—while the men were absent—bore certain children whom the fathers held in dishonor, as they were not their own; they called them **Partheniae** <note>original: "Παρθενίους" — literally "maiden-born," these were children born of illegitimate unions during the decades-long Messenian wars who later founded the colony of Taras (Taranto).</note>, and these sons were deeply resentful.

### Of the Cretans :-

It is said that Minos was the first to establish the Cretan <term>politeia</term> <note>The political and social constitution of the state.</note>, having become both a powerful ruler and a serious legislator; he used to carry out the revision of the laws every nine years. That the Cretan constitution is the most ancient is shown by Homer, who says their cities are "well-inhabited," and by Archilochus, who, when mocking someone, says: "A Cretan law is being taught."

The boys in Crete live together; they are well-clothed in both summer and winter. They are gathered into "herds" <note>original: "ἀγέλαι" (agelai) — the Cretan system of communal upbringing.</note>, and over each herd a leader is appointed whom they call a **herd-leader** <note>original: "ἀγελάτην" (agelaten)</note>. He gathers them wherever he wishes and leads them out to the hunt. They often sleep together and engage in ritual battles with fists and wooden clubs. When flute-players and lyre-players join them, they are trained in courage and endurance. They are educated only in letters, and even then, only moderately. They seem to have been the first to practice erotic associations between males, and among them this is not considered shameful. When they win someone over, they lead him away to the mountains or to their own estates, and there they feast for sixty days (for no more is permitted). The **lover** <note>original: "φιλήτωρ" (philetor)</note> gives the boy a robe and other gifts, including an ox. All Cretans dine while sitting on chairs. They begin serving the food to the guests first; after the guests, they give the leader four portions: one which is also given to the others, a second as the leader's share, a third for the house, and a fourth for the equipment. Generally, there is great hospitality toward guests in Crete, and they are invited to the seats of honor.

<summary>This page describes Spartan social discipline and the origins of the "Partheniae" before detailing Cretan customs, including their communal "herd" education system and dining protocols.</summary>
<keywords>Sparta, Crete, Minos, agelai, Partheniae, penestai, education, laconic speech, hospitality</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of summaries of ancient Greek constitutions, likely compiled by Heraclides Lembus from Aristotle’s lost works. It follows the section on the Cretans, moving now to the histories and customs of Cyrene, Corinth, and Elis.</meta>

### Of the Cyrenians :-

Cyrene was founded by Battos, who was previously called Aristotle. Because the Pythia <note>The priestess of the Oracle at Delphi.</note> addressed him as "Battos," he took that name. He had gone to Delphi to inquire about his voice, for he had a speech impediment <note>original: "ἰσχνόφωνος" — Battos suffered from a stutter or a weak voice; notably, "Battos" meant "king" in the Libyan language, but in Greek, it sounded like the word for "stammerer."</note>. When the god commanded him to settle Libya, he was initially reluctant and dissatisfied with the order.

Pheretima, the mother of Arcesilaus, ruled harshly and waged war against the people of Barca. Having captured Barca, she impaled the men and cut off the breasts of the women; not long afterward, she rotted away while still alive <note>Ancient historians often recorded the "living rot" or infestation by worms as a divine punishment for cruel tyrants.</note>.

Battos, known as "the Fair," reigned as the seventh king from the first founder.

During the reign of Arcesilaus, a white crow appeared, concerning which there was an oracle of hardship <note>A "white crow" was a Greek proverb for a miraculous but often ominous impossibility.</note>. When a democracy was later established, Battos fled to Hesperides where he died; they took his head and threw it into the sea. There was a law among them that litigious people and busybodies were to be led around by the <term>ephors</term> <note>Overseers or high-ranking magistrates.</note>, fined, and stripped of their civil rights.

### Of the Corinthians :-

Corinth was formerly called Ephyra, until the time of Corinthus, from whom it took its name. Bacchiades the Third also reigned; he was lame and of mean appearance, but he was a fine ruler and statesman. He had three daughters and seven sons, who increased the family's power to such an extent that their descendants were called the Bacchiadae of the <term>Heraclidae</term> <note>The "Descendants of Heracles," a prestigious lineage claimed by many Dorian Greek ruling houses.</note>.

Periander was the first to change the government into a tyranny <note>In this context, a "tyranny" refers to a ruler who took power through non-traditional means, rather than necessarily a "cruel" leader.</note> by maintaining bodyguards. He did not allow people to live in the city, and he further restricted the ownership of slaves and completely removed luxury. However, he was moderate in other respects: he collected no taxes, sufficing himself with the income from the market and the ports, and he was neither unjust nor abusive. Hating wickedness, he drowned all the procuresses. He also established a council of elders who did not allow people to spend more than their income.

### Of the Eleans :-

Pantoleon reigned among them, a man both abusive and harsh. When ambassadors came to him, he castrated them and forced them to eat their own testicles.

<summary>This page summarizes the legendary and historical foundations of Cyrene and Corinth, detailing the transition from kingship to tyranny and democracy, alongside a brief, graphic anecdote regarding the cruelty of the Elean king Pantoleon.</summary>
<keywords>Battos, Cyrene, Pheretima, Periander, Corinth, Bacchiadae, Pantoleon, Elis, colonization, tyranny, ancient constitutions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the collection of "Constitutions" or local histories attributed to Aristotle and excerpted by later writers. It moves from the history of Cyrene and Corinth to the unique cultural traditions and founding myths of several Greek islands: Tenedos, Paros, and Keos.</meta>

## Of the Tenedians :-

Tenedos, the island, was originally called Leukophrys <note>original: "Λεύκοφρυς" — meaning "White-Brow," likely referring to its white cliffs.</note>. Some time later, after the Trojan War, Tennes settled there after a falling out with his father. It is said that after his stepmother falsely accused him and a certain flute-player gave false testimony that Tennes had forced himself upon her, his father Kyknos placed him in a chest and threw him into the sea. He was saved and brought to the island. Because of the flute-player's role in the accusation, it is not lawful for a flute-player to enter the temple.

It is also said that Amyros, who was lame in his feet, ruled over it.

They say that King Tennes established a law that if anyone caught an adulterer, both were to be killed with an axe. When his own son was caught, and the man who caught him asked the king what should be done, he replied that the law must be followed. Because of this, their coinage is struck with a double-headed axe; on the other side is the face of a man and a woman <note>Scholars believe the double face represented Tennes and his sister, Hemithea, or perhaps the adulterous couple from the legend.</note>. From this, it is said of those who face a swift and harsh judgment that they have been "cut off by the Tenedian axe."

## Of the Parians :-

The island of Paros was settled by Paros, who led a people out of Arcadia.

The poet Archilochus <note>A famous 7th-century BCE lyric poet known for his biting wit and iambic verse.</note> was killed by a man named "Crow" <note>original: "Κόραξ" — Korax.</note>. They say the Pythia <note>The priestess of the Oracle at Delphi.</note> said to the killer: "Leave the temple!" But he replied, "I am pure, O Lord, for I killed him in the law of combat" <note>He argued the death occurred in fair battle rather than by murder.</note>.

## Of the People of Keos :-

The island used to be called Hydrooussa <note>original: "ὑδρόουσα" — "The Watery Island," due to its many springs.</note>. It is said that nymphs first inhabited it, but because a lion terrified them, they crossed over to Karystos; for this reason, a certain promontory of Keos is called "The Lion." Keos, having crossed over from Naupaktos, settled the land and named it after himself.

They say that Aristaios <note>A minor god and culture hero in Greek myth.</note> learned from the nymphs how to care for sheep and cattle, and from the Boeotians how to tend bees. When the plants and animals were being destroyed by the heat, he devised a remedy against the winds <note>Likely referring to the Etesian winds or the "dog days" of the star Sirius.</note>.

A woman named Ariste took care of the orderly conduct and modesty of the women.

In ancient times, the boys and girls drank only water until they reached the age of marriage.

On this island, there is no mourning for those who have died, neither among the men regarding their clothing nor the cutting of their hair—not even if a youth dies. Because the island is so healthy and the people live to a great age, especially the women, they do not die as grey-haired elderly people, but rather, before they grow weak, p... <unclear>they drink hemlock</unclear> <note>The text cuts off here, but it traditionally refers to the famous Kean custom where the elderly, upon reaching a certain age, would drink poison at a banquet to avoid the infirmity of old age and preserve the island's resources.</note>

<meta>This page continues the "Constitutions" or local histories, concluding the description of the island of Keos before moving on to the history and legends of Samos and Cyme.</meta>

...to reach a certain age, some by poppy and others by hemlock, they led themselves out <note>original: "ἐξαγαγοῖσι" — This refers to the famous Kean custom of "rational suicide," where the elderly who reached the age of sixty would drink poison to ensure there were enough resources for the young.</note>.

->## Of the Samians<-

Samos was said to have been deserted in the beginning, occupied by a multitude of wild beasts that let out a great cry; these beasts were called *Neides* <note>original: "νηΐδες" — mythical creatures often associated with water, though here described as loud beasts.</note>. The island was first called Parthenia <note>meaning "Maiden-land"</note>, and later Orgousa <note>meaning "Lush" or "Passionate"</note>. Ankaios reigned over it. During his time, the proverb was first spoken: "There is many a slip 'twixt the cup and the lip" <note>original: "πολλὰ μεταξὺ πέλει κύλικος, καὶ χείλεος ἄκρου" — Legend says a seer told King Ankaios he would never drink from his new vineyard; just as he raised the first cup to his lips, he was told a boar was ravaging the fields, and he died in the hunt before tasting the wine.</note>.

It is also said that a white swallow appeared among the Samians; and that there are "no partridges among the deer" <note>A proverb suggesting that things of a different nature do not mix, or referring to the lack of certain wildlife on the island.</note>.

Pherecydes of Syros <note>A 6th-century BCE philosopher and teacher of Pythagoras.</note> died in Samos, having been eaten away by lice. When Pythagoras <note>The famous mathematician and philosopher.</note> came to visit him, Pherecydes showed him his skin through a hole in the door to show how much he had suffered.

Aesop, the fable-maker, was flourishing at that time. He was Thracian by birth and was freed from slavery by Iadmon the Deaf. He was first a slave to Xanthos. The government of the Samians was later arranged for Syloson, from whom comes the proverb: "Thanks to Syloson, there is plenty of room" <note>original: "ἕκητι Συλοσῶντος εὐρυχωρίη" — Syloson ruled Samos so harshly (with Persian help) that the island became depopulated, hence "plenty of room."</note>.

There was a certain Theagenes among the Samians, a man of natural talent but otherwise profligate and wicked. Having fled his fatherland, he stayed in Athens with the playwright Euripides and corrupted his wife. When he was caught red-handed, he persuaded the Athenians to send three thousand men to Samos; when they arrived, they drove everyone out.

->## Of the Cymaeans<-

Telephanes was the first king, and it was he who first planted the territory of the Cymaeans <note>Cyme (or Kyme) was an important Greek city in Aeolis, Asia Minor.</note>. The Lydians, who were being harshly ruled by masters, heard that there was good government <term>eunomia<gloss>a state of well-ordered laws</gloss></term> in Cyme. They sent for a man to be their king, and he happened to be serving as a slave to a wagon-builder. The Lydians gave a ransom and took him. However, a man in Cyme named Hys <note>original: "ὗς" — literally "the Pig," likely a nickname for his employer.</note>, who had rented out wagons to him, held him back. Even when many followers arrived, Hys would not stop, but rather mocked him, saying he would rather have the profit from the wagon-work than have the man be King of the Lydians.

They say that Hermodike, the wife of the Phrygian King Midas, was not only exceptionally beautiful but also wise and skilled in the arts; she was the first to strike coinage for the Cymaeans.

It was a custom among them for neighbors to contribute toward the value of stolen goods <note>A form of collective insurance or communal responsibility for crime.</note>. Because of this, very little was lost, for everyone watched over things equally. It is because of this that the poet Hesiod is thought to have said: "Not even an ox would be lost, if the neighbor were not bad."

<meta>This page continues the collection of summaries regarding the "Constitutions" or local customs of various Greek city-states and tribes, likely following the section on Cyme where the transition to monarchy under Cyrus is mentioned.</meta>

...[if a man] were bad; but Cyrus, having dissolved the constitution, made them be ruled by a monarchy <note>original: "μοναρχεῖσθαι" — This likely refers to the Persian conquest of the Greek cities of Asia Minor.</note>.

Phaidon, a notable man, shared the citizenship with a greater number of people after establishing a law that made it compulsory for each citizen to maintain a horse <note>In ancient Greece, the "horse-breeding" class (hippeis) usually formed the aristocracy, as keeping a horse was expensive. This law effectively defined the citizen body through military and financial capability.</note>. A certain Prometheus, a vigorous man and a capable speaker, shared the citizenship with one thousand men.

->## Of the Eretrians<-

To Dicaearchus, when he was ferrying to Sparta and while he was growing wealthy in Corinth, the Eretrians <note>Inhabitants of Eretria on the island of Euboea.</note> set up a statue.

->## Of the Peparethians<-

This island is rich in wine, well-wooded, and produces grain.

->## Of the Lepreans<-

Whenever the Lepreans <note>A people from Triphylia in the Peloponnese.</note> catch an adulterer, they lead him bound around the city for three days and sentence him to a lifetime of <term>atimia</term> <note>original: "ἀτιμοῦσι" — Atimia was the loss of civil rights, effectively making one a social outcast.</note>. As for the woman, they stand her in the marketplace for twenty days, unbelted and wearing only a transparent tunic, and she also suffers the loss of her civil rights.

->## Of the Lycians<-

The Lycians used to live as pirates; they do not follow written laws but rather ancestral customs. Since ancient times, they have been ruled by women <note>original: "γυναικοκρατοῦνται" — Lycia was famous among Greek writers for its matrilineal customs, where children took their mother's name rather than their father's.</note>. They sell those who give false testimony into slavery and hand over their property to the public treasury.

->## Of the Tyrrhenians<-

These people <note>The Etruscans of Italy.</note> possess many arts and crafts. They all lie down and mingle with women under the same cloak; they were among the first to do so, and they are very fond of providing hospitality to strangers. When someone owes a debt and does not pay it back, children follow them around carrying an empty purse as a way to bring them into public ill repute.

->## Of the Molossians<-

The Molossians, having plundered the temple of Artemis and stripped a golden crown from her <term>xoanon</term> <note>original: "ξόανον" — an ancient, highly revered wooden cult image, often believed to have fallen from the sky.</note>, offered a sacrifice to the goddess herself. Regarding the other head-ornaments that had been added, the goddess cast these off, and they were found lying on the ground. The Cephallenians were named after the hero Cephalus.

->## Of the Phasians<-

They say that in the beginning, the Heniochi <note>original: "ἡνίοχοι" — meaning "Charioteers." A tribe located on the northeast coast of the Black Sea.</note> dwelt there, a tribe of man-eaters who would flay their victims; later, the Milesians settled there. They are so hospitable that they provide supplies to shipwrecked sailors and send them sailing off with three <term>minas</term> <note>original: "μνᾶς" — a unit of weight and currency. Three minas was a significant sum, enough for several months of basic living.</note>.

->## Of the Amorgians<-

Amorgos produces much wine, olive oil, and autumn fruit.

<meta>This page continues the ethnographic and historical survey of various Greek city-states and tribes, following the discussion of the Lycians and their ancestral customs. The text provides a mixture of foundational myths, etymological origins, and brief descriptions of social or legal structures.</meta>

### Of the Lucanians

The Lucanians <note>An Italic people of southern Italy (modern Basilicata). Their name was often associated by Greeks with the word *lykos*, meaning "wolf."</note> are hospitable and just. Lamiskos ruled over them; he had a wolf’s claw on his third finger, counting from the thumb.

### Samothrace

Samothrace <note>An island in the northern Aegean Sea, famous for its "Great Gods" mystery cult.</note> was originally called Leucania because it was "white" <note>original: "λευκὴν" (*leuken*) — The name Leucania derives from the Greek word for white.</note>. Later, after Thracians occupied it, it was called Thracia. Then, after seven hundred years had passed and they had left, Samians who had been expelled from their own home settled there and called it Samothrace <note>A compound name combining Samos (the settlers' origin) and Thrace (the location).</note>.

### Of the Magnesians

These people were much afflicted by an excess of misfortunes, which is why the poet Archilochus says: "I weep for the woes of the sea, not for those of the Magnesians." They are horse-breeders, as are the Photians and the Colophonians, because they possess a plain land. Phalmis was their ruler; his son died during a <term>hydrosylia</term> <note>original: "ἱδροσυλείᾳ" — This term is rare and likely a variant or error for *hierosylia* (temple-robbery/sacrilege). The context implies a sacrilegious theft involving sacred vessels.</note> while sacrificing, which they say also happened around Egypt. Those people were destroyed in the act of sacrilege when a golden bowl was discovered hidden in their bedding.

### Of the Athamanians

In the land of the Athamanians <note>An ancient Greek tribe living in the Pindus mountains of Epirus.</note>, the women practice agriculture, while the men tend the flocks.

### Of the Cytherians

The Cytherians use cheese and figs as their side-dishes. For the island <note>Cythera, located off the southern tip of the Peloponnese.</note> produces many things, as well as honey and wine. They are fond of money and fond of hard work.

### Of the Rhegians

Rhegium <note>Modern Reggio Calabria in Italy.</note> was settled by Chalcidians from the Euripus <note>The strait separating Euboea from mainland Greece.</note> who emigrated because of a famine. They were joined by those Messenians from the Peloponnese who happened to be in the greatest distress because of the outrage regarding the "first-fruits" of the maidens <note>A reference to a legend where Spartan or Messenian girls were reportedly assaulted at a festival, a traditional cause for the Messenian Wars.</note>. They first settled together around the tomb of Gocatus, one of the sons of Diolos, who they say died after being bitten by a serpent. They received an oracle stating they should settle where "the female embraces the male"; seeing a grapevine entwined around a holm-oak, they recognized this was the place. The location where they founded the city was called Rhegium after a local hero. They established an aristocratic constitution, for one thousand elected men chosen according to their property value <note>original: "ἀπὸ τιμημάτων" (*apo timematon*) — a timocracy where political power is proportional to wealth.</note> administer everything. They used the laws of Charondas of Catana <note>Charondas was a celebrated 7th-century BCE lawgiver whose codes were adopted by many Chalcidicus colonies in Sicily and Italy.</note>. Anaxilas the Messenian became their tyrant; having won the Olympic games with a mule-cart, he hosted a banquet for the Greeks, and someone mocked him...

<summary>This page catalogs the origins and customs of several Greek and Italic groups, focusing on foundation myths, local diets, and the transition of Rhegium from a colony to an aristocratic and then tyrannical state.</summary>
<keywords>Lucanians, Samothrace, Magnesians, Archilochus, Athamanians, Cythera, Rhegium, Chalcidians, Charondas, Anaxilas, colonization, aristocratic government</keywords>

--- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page continues an ethnographic survey of Greek city-states and tribes. The previous page concluded with the Athamanians and likely transitioned into a discussion of Rhegium (in modern Calabria, Italy), as evidenced by the mention of "the Rhegians" in the opening lines of this page.</meta>

...having said, this man did so by winning with his own horses. The poet Simonides <note>Simonides of Ceos (c. 556–468 BC), a famous lyric poet known for his victory odes.</note> also wrote a victory song for him: "Hail, daughters of storm-footed steeds!" <note>original: "χαῖρετ' ἀελλοπόδων θυγατέρεσ' ἵππων" — This is a famous opening line where the poet cleverly addresses the mules or horses of a victor.</note> There were also other judges among the Rhegians.

->Of the Corcyraeans<-

The Corcyraeans <note>Inhabitants of the island of Corcyra, modern-day Corfu.</note> called upon Diomedes <note>A hero of the Trojan War who, according to legend, wandered through the Adriatic and Italy after the war.</note> for help. There was a "dragon" <note>original: "δράκων" (*drakon*) — In Greek, this refers to a large serpent or a mythical guardian creature.</note> among them performing the same deeds, with whom Diomedes also fought as an ally. He arrived in Iapygia <note>The "heel" of Italy.</note> with a large fleet to assist them in their war against the Brundisians, and for this, he received great honors.

->Of the Thracians<-

Each man marries three or four wives; there are some who even have thirty. They use these women as if they were servants. These marriages are a result of great wealth. The husband spends time with them in rotation, and he bathes and serves them. Most of the women sleep on the ground after their duties are finished. If the women are treated harshly, the parents may take their daughter back, provided they return the payment they received for her. Since the men provide a price to take them, they treat the women as property; and when a man dies, his wives are inherited just like the rest of his estate.

->Of the Minoans<-

The city of Minoa in Sicily <note>Known as Heraclea Minoa.</note> was formerly called Makara <note>original: "μακάραν" (*makaran*), meaning "the Blessed."</note>. Later, Minos <note>The legendary King of Crete.</note>, hearing that Daedalus was there, arrived with a fleet. He traveled up the Lycus River to the city and took control of it. After defeating the barbarians, he renamed the city after himself and established Cretan laws there.

->Of the Locrians<-

A poet blind from birth was chosen to judge Euthymus the Locrian <note>Euthymus was a famous Olympic boxer from Locri Epizephyrii in Southern Italy.</note>, as was Erasippus. Among these people, it is not permitted to mourn for the deceased; instead, after they have carried out the burial, they hold a feast. Furthermore, there is no middle-man trading among them; rather, the farmer sells his own produce directly.

If anyone is caught stealing, his eyes are put out. The son of Zaleucus <note>Zaleucus was the legendary first lawgiver of the Locrians, famous for his strict "eye for an eye" justice.</note> was caught in such an act. Even though the Locrians were willing to let him go free, Zaleucus would not allow it. Instead, he put out one of his own eyes and one of his son's eyes <note>By doing this, Zaleucus satisfied the requirement of the law (two eyes for the crime) while showing mercy to his son.</note>.

Polemarchus, having escaped under oath, fled from a Corinthian fleet. The legends say that while he was sleeping at night, weasels <note>original: "γαλᾶς" (*galas*) — These could be weasels, polecats, or martens, often seen as omens or pests in antiquity.</note> bit him, and finally, being in a state of despair, he took his own life.

->Of the Chalcidians<-

The Chalcidians also settled Cleonae <note>A town on the Athos peninsula.</note>. These were people who had been driven out of Elymnium on Mt. Athos. As the stories go, they were driven out by mice, which among other things...

<sig>υ</sig> <note>A signature or quire mark indicating the sequence of the manuscript pages.</note>

<meta>This page continues the ethnographic and historical survey of various Greek city-states and regions. The text transitions from the customs of the Thracians (briefly mentioned at the end of the previous page) to the laws of the Chalcidians and then provides anecdotal histories of Cephalonia, Rhodes, Ephesus, Phocaea, Croton, and Akragas.</meta>

...their breast-ornaments <note>original: "τήθιον" (*tethion*) — possibly referring to a specific type of pectoral jewelry or garment worn by the Thracian women mentioned on the previous page</note>, and the iron.

There was a law among the Chalcidians <note>Inhabitants of Chalcis, the chief city of the island of Euboea.</note> that no one younger than fifty years of age should hold office, nor should they serve as ambassadors.

### Cephalonia

**I**n Cephalonia <note>The largest of the Ionian Islands in western Greece.</note>, the son of Promnesos seized power. He was a harsh ruler and did not permit more than two festivals to be held; furthermore, he did not allow anyone to reside in the city for more than ten days a month. He also insisted on "knowing" <note>A common euphemism for sexual violation or the "right of the first night."</note> the young maidens himself before they were given in marriage. However, a man named Antenor, taking a small dagger and dressing himself in women’s clothing, killed the tyrant in his bed. The people honored Antenor for this deed and established him as their leader. The maiden through whose aid he gained entry became famous in song. Homer also testifies that he traveled from Tyrrhenia <note>Ancient Etruria, in modern-day Italy.</note> to Cephalonia and Ithaca during the time it is said his eyes were ruined by disease.

### Rhodes

**T**hey say that in ancient times the island of Rhodes was hidden beneath the sea, but it appeared later after the waters dried up. It used to be called *Ophiusa* <note>original: "ὀφιοῦσα" — meaning "Snake-island," from the Greek *ophis* (snake).</note> because of the great multitude of snakes that were produced there.

### Ephesus

**T**hey say that Ephesus was named after one of the Amazons <note>In Greek mythology, a tribe of female warriors said to have founded many cities in Asia Minor.</note>. Others, however, claim the name comes from what Hercules said to the Amazons—"to permit" <note>original: "ἐφᾶναι" (*ephanai*), a pun on the name Ephesus</note>—regarding the land from Mycale to Pitane.

### Phocaea

**S**ome named Phocaea after a leader named Phocus; others say it was because they saw a seal <note>original: "φώκην" (*phoken*) — "phoke" is the Greek word for seal, which became the city's emblem.</note> coming out onto the dry land.

### Croton

**C**roton was settled from the beginning by a man named Croton.

### Akragas

**P**halaris <note>A notorious tyrant of Akragas (modern Agrigento, Sicily), ruling c. 570–554 BC.</note> ruled as a tyrant over the people of Akragas, surpassing all others in lawlessness. For he not only murdered many people but also employed illegal forms of punishment. Some he threw into boiling cauldrons, and others he cast into craters of fire; he even cast some into a bronze bull <note>The "Brazen Bull" was a hollow brass statue; a fire was lit beneath it, roasting the victim inside while their screams were transformed by pipes to sound like the bellowing of a bull.</note> and burned them alive. The people eventually took their revenge on him; he even burned his own mother and friends. After him, Alcamenes took charge of affairs, and after him, Alcandros stood as leader—a man of equity. Under his rule, the people flourished to such an extent that they wore garments of rich purple.

<summary>This page records various foundation myths and historical anecdotes of Greek cities, highlighting the transition from cruel tyrants like Phalaris and the son of Promnesos to more equitable leaders.</summary>
<keywords>Cephalonia, Rhodes, Phalaris of Akragas, Brazen Bull, Antenor, Ephesus, Amazon founders, seal etymology, Chalcidian law</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the ethnographic survey of Greek regions and cities, likely from a collection of "constitutions" or local histories attributed to the school of Aristotle. It follows the discussion of Rhodes and moves through Cephalonia, Ithaca, Aphytis, Iasos, Icarus, Argilos, and Thespiae.</meta>

To Cephalus <note>A hero of Attic mythology and ancestor of the kings of Ithaca.</note>, when he consulted the oracle about having children, the god told him to have intercourse with the first creature he encountered. He met a bear, and by approaching it, he fathered a child who became a woman. From her, it is said that Arkeisios <note>The father of Laertes and grandfather of Odysseus.</note> was named according to the word <note>original: "φερωνύμως" (*pheronumos*) — "bearing the name," a pun on *arktos*, the Greek word for bear.</note>.

### Of the Ithacans:

**A** ferryman named Parios transported some pirates, along with an elderly captive and a jar. He bought these from the pirates. Upon the old man’s request—for there was gold hidden in the jar—he was freed. Having become wealthy, it is said the ferryman sacrificed an ox to the old man. Because of this, a proverb arose: "No benefactor ever sacrificed an ox, except for Parias." <note>A joke on the rarity of such gratitude; Parias is a variant of the name Parios.</note>

### Of the Aphytaeans:

**T**hey live justly and temperately, and they do not touch the property of others even when doors are left open. They say that once a stranger who was tasting wine did not take it with him because his voyage was hurried. He left it in the marketplace without handing it over to anyone for safekeeping. Later, when he returned for trade by way of Delos, he found it untouched.

### Of the Iasians:

**A**mong these people, it was not permitted for more than ten guests to be present at weddings, with an equal number of women. Nor could they celebrate a wedding for more than two days. They also supervised orphans to ensure they were being properly educated, and they returned their estates to them once they reached twenty years of age.

### Of Icarus:

**T**he island was called *Ichthyoessa* <note>original: "Ἰχθυόεσσα" — meaning "Abounding in Fish."</note> because of the beauty of the fish found there. Icarus landed there, and from him the island takes its name. The myth says he came from Crete using wings, but others say he was merely looking away from his father <note>original: "ἀφορῶντα μὴ τοῦ πατρὸς" — perhaps implying he was not following his father's nautical or aeronautical instructions.</note>. There is a likeness <note>A statue or monument.</note> of him because he showed Theseus the way out of the labyrinth.

### Argilos:

**T**he Thracians call a mouse an *argilos*. When one was spotted, a city was founded there according to an oracle, and they named it Argilos.

### Of the Thespians:

**A**mong the Thespians, it was considered shameful to learn a craft or to spend one's time in agriculture. Because of this, the majority of them were poor, and they owed many debts to the Thebans, who were a frugal people.

->~ THE END ~<-

<meta>This page marks a transition from the ethnographic "Constitutions" of the previous pages to a new work: the "Physiognomonica" of Polemon of Laodicea, a 2nd-century AD sophist. This text focuses on the pseudo-science of reading a person’s character through their physical features.</meta>

-># POLEMON’S PHYSIOGNOMONICS<-

**I**f anything is clear, it is that the study of physiognomy <note>original: "φυσιογνωμονεῖν" (*physiognomonein*) — the practice of judging a person's character or disposition from their outward appearance, especially the face.</note> provides many great benefits to those who learn it. For no one would entrust a financial deposit, nor would they have a wife they could trust, nor a slave, nor even a family treasure <note>original: "κειμήλιον" (*keimelion*) — an heirloom or precious object stored away.</note>, nor would they enter into any friendship with someone, if that person bore a sign <term>semeion: a physical mark or characteristic used as evidence of a personality trait</term> of licentiousness, untrustworthiness, or any other kind of wickedness. 

It is, so to speak, a god-sent and most keen form of divination <note>Polemon argues that physiognomy is as accurate as prophecy because the soul's nature is "written" on the body.</note> for all people, through which their appearance and the purpose of their lives are recognized. It shows these things so that the practitioner may choose to associate with good men and guard themselves against the vices of the wicked. For in the most important matters, the examination of these signs benefits those who know them; it is more appropriate to apply every effort to this than to know things by mere chance. 

This science has been described as "judging the mind" <note>original: "γιγνωμονεῖν" (*gignomonein*), a play on words with "physiognomy."</note>. It must be applied according to the ages and the races <note>original: "γένεσι" (*genesi*) — types, kinds, or ethnic groups.</note> of men. For there are many traits that are common to certain regions; what is a common local trait is not a "great sign" in itself. However, if a trait appears that is contrary to a person's age or is foreign to their race <note>For example, if a young man has the physical markers of an old man, it is considered a significant "sign" of his inner nature.</note>, this contributes more as a significant sign. 

The differences given to each person according to their nature will be discussed, along with most of the signs. There is also a physiognomy based on the races of men, for many signs and appearances are common to each group. One can easily identify many signs for each, not just for the recognition of individual men; these too shall be discussed. For whatever a specific sign means in an individual, it means the same when found in the majority of a population. Among nations, we look for the signs that are not common to all, from which we can identify the race through physiognomy—not only for the **Egyptians**, but also for the **Scythians** <note>Ancient Greeks often used Egyptians and Scythians as the two extremes of human "types"—the hot/southern type and the cold/northern type.</note>, and other peoples according to their kinds, as will be explained later. Individually, for every man, the differences between signs are of great importance. Indeed, one must learn to distinguish the individual character of a man more than the general nature of nations, as the most accurate and certain signs are found in the individual. You must choose the most prominent and proven signs to speak with clarity...

<summary>This page introduces Polemon's treatise on physiognomy, arguing that the ability to read character from physical signs is a vital social tool for choosing friends and avoiding the wicked. It explains the methodology of distinguishing between common ethnic traits and significant individual "signs."</summary>
<keywords>physiognomy, Polemon, character signs, divination, ethics, Egyptians, Scythians, ethnic traits, social trust</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory theory of physiognomy by Polemon of Laodicea, moving from the general utility of the science to the specific methodology of "mixing" signs and analyzing the proportions of the head.</meta>

...is available. The most significant factor in this art is the mixture of signs <term>semeia: physical features used as evidence of character</term>, which produces the necessary distinctions. For the ancient dispositions <note>original: "ἤθη" (*ethe*) — the habitual character or moral nature of a person.</note> and the secret designs of men are understood through this blending of signs. 

It happens that a person’s appearance <note>original: "εἴδους" (*eidous*) — the outward form or look.</note> sometimes undergoes a change: sometimes when they are in a settled state, and other times when they are preoccupied with worries, or are grieving, or angry, or afraid, or under the effects of fasting, or from overindulgence, or some desire, or while they are contemplating, seeing, or hearing something. In all such circumstances, the form is not entirely carried away, but remains fixed within its signs. However, to the extent that the appearance does change, it will not be imitated in the same way by everyone, but will remain close to each person’s own particular nature. 

One must realize that whatever signs appear upon a person when they are in an emotional state <note>original: "ἐμπαθῶς" (*empathos*) — passionately or under the influence of strong emotion.</note> are the very same signs that, if found in someone's normal state, indicate that the passion associated with those signs is part of their inherent character. For example, if someone possesses signs like those of a man deep in deliberation, you may call him a "thinker" and "prudent." If someone has the signs of one who is brewing treachery or some deceit—even if you do not see him currently engaged in a new plot against someone—he is nevertheless a man of pure deceitfulness <note>original: "ἀκραιφνοῦ δολιερόν" (*akraiphnou dolieron*) — unmixed or absolute cunning.</note>. In the same way, if someone possesses the signs of a person in a bad mood, even if he is not currently joined in a fit of rage, you should consider him an irascible man. This applies to all other traits as well.

One must likewise observe what kind of feminine appearance <note>original: "γυναικεῖον εἶδος" (*gynaikeion eidos*)</note> might be prominent in a man’s form, whether in the eyes or other features. Those traits tending toward luxury and the pleasures of Aphrodite <note>original: "ἀφροδίτην" (*aphroditen*) — a reference to sexual desire or goddess-given charms.</note> are feminine; such men are bold, shameless, and cunning, and they delight in untrustworthiness and deceit. These are feminine in that regard. On the other hand, those who are young but have the appearance of an old man are cowardly, difficult, suspicious, stingy, cautious, and lacking in counsel; they may desire to act, but it is clear they do not move because they are paralyzed by fear.

->## POLEMON’S PHYSIOGNOMONICS: ON THE HEAD<-

**A** very small head indicates a man who is insensitive and unintelligent. 

A head larger than what is symmetrical indicates a man who is insensitive, yet also brave and illiberal <note>original: "ἀνελεύθερον" (*aneleutheron*) — lacking the qualities of a free-born gentleman; stingy or base.</note>. 

A very large head reveals a man who is dull-witted and extremely ignorant. 

A slanting or crooked head indicates a man’s shamelessness. 

Those who have very high, peaked heads are stubborn and arrogant people. 

Those who have the areas behind the hollows of the head set low are people lacking in spirit. 

Whoever has hollows on either side of the head are deceitful and spiteful. 

A head of middle size, slightly low, possessing good proportion and uprightness, is the best of all types.

<summary>This page explains how temporary emotional expressions reveal permanent character traits and begins a catalog of physical features, specifically focusing on how the size and shape of the head correlate to intelligence and morality.</summary>
<keywords>physiognomy, Polemon, character signs, emotional states, facial expressions, gender traits, head shape, intelligence, deceit</keywords>

...head, for sensitivity and high-mindedness.

## On Complexions and Hair

It is not sufficient on its own to practice physiognomy <term>physiognomonesai: the art of judging character from physical appearance</term> based solely on the signs of complexions and hair. Therefore, one cannot make declarations based simply on nationality, but rather through the carefully observed relationship of features to one another and the influence of those nations. For instance, Syrians are found in Italy, and Libyans in Thrace; distinct peoples are scattered everywhere for the most part. 

Those living under the North <note>original: "ἄρκτον" (*arkton*) — literally 'the Bear', referring to the constellation Ursa Major used to denote the northern regions.</note> are white-skinned, snub-nosed, thick-legged, and numerous; they have lean flesh, protruding bellies, and are simple-minded, hot-tempered, and lacking in judgment. They possess a "hot" temperament but are slow to learn, holding many shifting opinions. They are light-headed, liars, of a withered appearance, and have stealthy thoughts; they are also somewhat softer in nature. People possess these various traits according to how close they are to one another, or whether they are separated by a greater distance from the South <note>original: "μεσημβρίας" (*mesembrias*) — literally 'midday', used to denote the southern regions.</note> or the North. 

The middle regions of the inhabited world possess a balanced appearance and a mixture <term>krasis: the 'blending' of physical elements or humors</term> of both signs and dispositions. Those in the East, West, and the further South differ among themselves. For example, there is a difference between the Libyans at the edges of Libya and those inhabiting Spain and Iberia, as they are settled toward the Outer Sea <note>The Atlantic Ocean.</note>, even though both are southern peoples. Some Libyans resemble Ethiopians, while others are like Cretans, as I shall briefly explain. 

The South is generally tempered by dryness and heat, while the North is characterized by moisture and cold. Specific regions take on the character of whatever qualities they are near, sharing in that mixture; this affects their natural growth, their appearance, and their character <note>original: "ἤθη" (*ēthē*) — the moral character or habitual temperament.</note>. The reason why many people appear so diverse is explained by the fact that many have moved from their own nations to settle in others, which is evident from the signs mentioned previously. 

A black complexion indicates cowardice and craftiness <note>original: "πολυμηχανίαν" (*polymēchanian*) — resourcefulness or 'many-shifting' schemes.</note>. A fair and white skin speaks of strength and spirit. However, a complexion that is unmixed and purely white signifies a lack of manliness. A ruddy or reddish body suggests a destructive nature and characterizes a man of many shifts. A complexion that has turned sallow <note>original: "ὑπόχλωρος" (*hypochloros*) — a greenish-yellow or pale tone.</note> indicates a nature full of cowardice and malicious scheming, unless the color is caused by disease. The color called "deeply sallow" signifies a man who is a coward, a glutton, prone to anger, and talkative. A fiery or glowing complexion signifies that the man is an accuser. A mild redness characterizes a man who is naturally gifted, quick-moving, and a fast learner.

<summary>This page discusses how geographic location and climate (North vs. South) shape physical traits and temperament, while also detailing the character traits associated with various skin colors.</summary>
<keywords>physiognomy, complexion, geography, climate theory, North and South, skin color, temperament, ethnic migration</keywords>

...skin. These things apply to the entire body.

# On Foreheads

A narrow forehead is a significant sign of a lack of learning <note>original: "ἀμαθίας" (*amathias*) — refers to being uneducated or having a natural difficulty in acquiring knowledge.</note>. A large forehead, for the most part, indicates a sluggish man. A long forehead similarly testifies to a lack of perception and a difficulty in learning. A low forehead is a sign not to be praised. A forehead that is bulging, high, and rounded reveals a man who is both unperceptive and shameless. A rough-textured forehead reveals a man who is not good. A forehead that has both ridges and hollows within it <note>The text likely refers to deep furrows or an uneven bone structure.</note> denounces a man as a rogue and untrustworthy. Such a person is also foolish and prone to madness, especially if he possesses other signs of these traits. A square forehead is a good sign according to the reasoning of the first type; for it signifies the best character: one who is wise, high-minded, and courageous. A downward-sloping forehead reveals a stubborn man. A forehead that is completely turned back signifies a carefree man. A wrinkled forehead reveals a thinker <note>original: "φροντιστὴν" (*phrontisten*) — can mean a deep thinker, but often implies someone burdened by cares or worries.</note>.

# On Hair

A man with shaggy hair <term>ylotheis: literally "woody" or "shaggy," likely referring to thick, curly hair</term> is completely cowardly and loathsome. One with straight hair is more useless and more foolish. The best sign is to have hair that is midway between these two. Stiff hair reveals a beast-like man. Indeed, among wild beasts, some say this indicates malice and craftiness. If the hair is of a medium texture, it is a praiseworthy sign; but to have very soft hair reveals an effeminate character. Nevertheless, very stiff hair is also not good, for it denounces a person for their savagery. If it is in the middle, it is the best sign for them. Black hair indicates a coward and one who is greedy for gain. Blonde or whitish hair—of the sort seen in pale or ashen people—reveals awkwardness, or rather, wickedness and savagery. But hair that is gently yellowish-blonde tends toward ease of learning, a mild nature, and good fortune. Pure redness in the bloom of the color is not a good sign; for the most part, their characters are beast-like. Legs covered in very thick and large hairs signify a person who is slow to learn and rather savage. Men who have many hairs on their loins and thighs, apart from their other limbs, are lustful. If the belly and chest are very hairy, they reveal a man who is light-minded, fickle, and weak. But breasts that are themselves covered in thick hair belong to those who are hot-headed <note>original: "θερμοβούλου" (*thermoboulou*) — literally "hot-counselled," referring to someone who makes impulsive or rash decisions.</note>,

<summary>This page explores the physiognomy of the forehead and hair, linking physical characteristics like square foreheads or medium hair texture to virtues like wisdom and courage, while associating extremes with negative traits like cowardice or savagery.</summary>
<keywords>physiognomy, forehead, hair, character analysis, ancient Greek medicine, temperament, behavioral signs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of physical traits as markers of character, transitioning from the qualities of hair and shoulders to a detailed study of the eyebrows and eyes. It links specific physical "clouds" or patterns in the eye to both animalistic tendencies and the tragic fates of mythological figures like Oedipus.</meta>

...[good] counselors <note>original: "μεβούλους" (meboulous) — likely the end of *symboulous* (counselors) or *euboulous* (well-counseling).</note>, and high-spirited men. If you see shoulders and the space between the shoulder blades <note>original: "μετάφρενα" (*metaphrena*) — the back of the torso or the area behind the diaphragm.</note> looking as if they were winged, compare these men to birds in their souls; for their thoughts are high, but their wills are shown to be unstable. A whole body covered with thick hair, forming a firm and dense wall, indicates a slow-moving man. Regarding the forehead, if a straight line descending into the middle is hairy, but the areas on either side are not, it is a sign of magnanimity <term>megalopsychia: greatness of soul, a virtue associated with high-mindedness and leadership</term> and full power.

Hair that stands straight up signifies the cowardice and malice of a man. For those whose eyebrows slope down toward the nose but extend outward toward the temples, compare them to pigs. For such they are in their mind and soul. Those whose eyebrows are very much joined together <note>The "unibrow" or *synophrys* was often viewed negatively in ancient physiognomy as a sign of gloom or trickery.</note> are troublesome; for grief is what suits them.

### On Eyes

Moist eyes that shine like water signify a good and kind character. However, if they have broad pupils, they signify malicious men. If they have long pupils, they indicate men who are evil-contrivers; for snakes, and ichneumons <note>The Egyptian mongoose, often cited in ancient texts for its cleverness in fighting snakes.</note>, monkeys, and foxes, and whatever beasts have a narrow gaze, are naturally malicious. Therefore, those possessing such eyes are likened to the malice of these animals.

Those who have an uneven circle in the iris are foolish men. If, in addition to this sign, "clouds" settle upon their forehead—whether they be dark blue, pale green, or of another color—a demon will harm such people with all kinds of injuries; let this not escape your notice. If these clouds sit upon the eyebrows, and the circles of the iris always follow the pupils in the same fixed way, such men are cowardly and will perform lawless deeds—either murders of kin <note>original: "φόνους συγγνώμη" (*phonous syngnome*) — likely a corruption of *syngeneis* (kindred) or a reference to crimes requiring "pardon."</note>, or lawless sexual unions, or the eating of forbidden things and food sacrificed to idols. These are the kinds of sacrifices that occurred in the city of Mycenae, or the deeds of Oedipus son of Laius in Thebes, which are said to have happened according to the word of the Thracian <note>Possibly a reference to Orpheus or a Thracian oracle.</note>.

And if the circles of the eyes always follow in that same way, such people are led astray. Those who have the circle revolving around the pupil with restricted movement are destined for lawless deeds; their soul, being driven to madness, will act with evil counsels, as if being dragged along by a demon that is not good. On account of these things, they think of different...

<meta>This page continues the physiognomic analysis of the face, moving from the eyebrows into a detailed catalog of the eyes. It interprets various ocular movements, colors, and shapes as direct indicators of a person's hidden character and moral failings.</meta>

...[conspiring] <note>original: "νοῦντες" (nountes) — likely the suffix of a word like *epinoountes* (devising/plotting).</note>, they accomplish nothing, but rather overturn everything. The eyes of these men are, as it were, accusers of their secrets. For the signs found in the eyes are reflections of the passions residing in the heart.

Those who have sunken and deep-set eyes signify eccentric men. The moister types of such eyes signify cowardly men. The drier ones signify those full of inner turmoil. The paler ones indicate men prone to madness. Those who raise their eyebrows and draw up their breath are ill-advised people, cruel-minded, malicious, and deceitful <note>original: "παλίσκοποι" (paliskopoi) — literally "looking backward," implying one who watches for a chance to betray or strike.</note>. Shaded, reddish, and large eyes testify to a man who is both gluttonous and lustful. If such eyes are seen looking downward, it is a sign that impudence dwells in the man, and that he is unjust and utterly shiftless. Small, fixed eyes signify a money-loving man and one who devises every possible profit. If the whole body also matches these signs, it indicates a quick-tempered and violent man. If he also draws his forehead together with his eyebrows toward the center, it signifies a buffoonish man <term>kordaleutan: one who performs the kordax, a lewd and provocative dance of ancient Greek comedy</term>.

Regarding one who has gleaming <note>original: "Γλαυκοὺς" (Glaukous) — a color ranging from light blue to grey or sea-green, often associated by ancient Greeks with owls or predatory intensity.</note> eyes that are sunken and lack luster: never form a friendship with him, nor have him as a neighbor, for he is treacherous, lying awake to plot terrible things and to gloat over the misfortunes of others. Flee from such a person; do not even travel upon the road with him. Shifting eyes that appear to be in turmoil indicate a calculating and corrupt man. Those with large eyes who blink their eyelids excessively signify a man who is weak in spiritual strength. 

Those who move their eyes quickly, but do not move their eyelids in the same manner, are courageous and daring people; however, their daring is used for terrible deeds. Slow-moving eyes indicate sluggish and slow people, both idle and difficult to stir to action. Eyes that view everything through a mist testify to a vain and undisciplined man. Large eyes signify a man's greed and vanity, as well as gluttony, wine-drinking, and lust—especially if the eyes are inflamed. Small, slightly trembling, gleaming eyes signify shameless, untrustworthy, and unjust men, who dwell on the evils of others and live off of misfortune. Small, bright <note>original: "χαροποί" (charopoi) — often used to describe amber or light-colored, "joyful" eyes, though here interpreted negatively.</note>, and black eyes signify the same things.

<summary>This page provides a systematic guide to reading a person's moral character through their eyes, associating specific traits like eye color, movement, and depth with vices such as greed, treachery, and cowardice.</summary>

->φ ii<-

...by which amber <note>original: "χαροποὶ" (charopoi) — often used to describe bright, piercing, or lion-like eyes.</note> eyes signify a more impulsive man, while black eyes signify one who is more quick-tempered and shameless, based on the same sign. Eyes that surge and roll like waves <note>original: "κλυζόμενοι κυμαίνοντες" (klyzomenoi kymainontes) — implying a watery, shimmering, or unstable movement.</note> reveal a man who is charming and passionate, yet neither unjust, nor a criminal, nor unrefined, nor—I say—is such a sign inherently bad. Small eyes having small pupils signify a mean-spirited, money-loving, and villainous man. Drier, gleaming eyes <note>original: "γλαυκοὶ" (glaukoi) — a light grey, blue, or sea-green color.</note> testify to a man's lawless character, for such a person is destined to be corrupted. Deep blue eyes, when they are moister and more striking than other common eyes, testify to a deceitful man. However, those whose deep blue eyes are moist, steady, and large, show the best of characters, though these men are also hot-tempered. 

Those who have weak circles around their eyes and are somewhat pale are incapable men and exist as cowards. Weakness in the eyes occurs more often in those with amber eyes than in those with gleaming eyes. For now, the discussion concerns those with gleaming, weak eyes. Specks <note>original: "ψεῖδες" (pseides) — likely "psiches," referring to small spots or grains.</note> in the eyes, whether dark blue or around the pupils, similar in size to a millet seed, or others being yellow—when the rows of these spots are parallel to the iris's whorls, like a necklace following the pupil—such eyes testify to the deceitful and thieving character of the man. 

When the eyes are weak and small, they signify a man who is more buffoonish <term>kordaleutoteron: resembling a performer of the kordax, a lewd and provocative dance of ancient comedy</term> and slave-like, intent on money and gain, saying one thing while doing another in secret. Such a man rejoices in these things and nothing makes a difference to him; quite simply, whatever is evil, he yearns to make it profitable, whether in secret or in public. However, he is often hindered by his own cowardice, for such a man is as weak as a hare—especially since the very form of his eyes resembles one. 

An upright, steady gaze reveals the best type of man. Eyes cast upward are a sign of vanity and uselessness, or of the so-called "sacred disease" <note>original: "νόσον τῆς ἱρᾶς" (noson tēs hieras) — the ancient term for epilepsy.</note>, as well as gluttony, lust, and drunkenness. If trembling is also present with these signs, he is not far from an epileptic fit. If the eyes are pale or yellowish, they signify a cruel and inhuman disposition, even a murderer or one stained with blood. But those that are reddish and large signify a man who loves drinking and revelry, a lover of women, a lover of strife, a flaming... <note>The text cuts off mid-word: "φλεγγό-" (phleggo-), likely meaning flaming or burning with passion/anger.</note>

<summary>This page provides a detailed physiognomic guide to the eyes, linking colors (amber, black, blue, grey), moisture levels, and patterns (like millet-sized spots) to specific personality traits ranging from charm and passion to deceit, greed, and epilepsy.</summary>
<keywords>physiognomy, eye color, character analysis, epilepsy, kordax, greed, cowardice, Greek medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the detailed classification of eye types within the study of physiognomy, specifically focusing on the positioning of the eyes, the presence of specific color patterns and "millet-seed" spots (kenchroi), and how these correlate to specific character defects or virtues like nobility and aggression.</meta>

# 18

...firm, strong, and vain. Eyes turned downward, or appearing as if they are pleading, reveal the same traits as those turned upward, but in a more varied way; specifically, they signify a wild spirit and an anger that cannot be softened. If one eye moves upward while the other moves downward, and if there is also a trembling in them, and the eyebrows are drawn together with the space between them appearing quite rough and crowded, this man has driven himself to the very limit of wickedness. Compressed eyes, if they move toward the right, signify a fool; if they move toward the left, they signify a lecher. 

If the eyes are observant and look inward toward the nose, they signify a man who is malicious <note>original: "ἐπιχαρῆ" (epicharē) — one who takes pleasure in the misfortunes of others.</note>, lustful, friendly, and amorous. If they are quite dry and wide open without being well-formed, it signifies diseases of trembling; such a man is not favored by Aphrodite, nor does he possess graces or loves, for their natural course has been overtaken by fate and justice. If there should be trembling along with tears in the eyes, they signify a man who is reckless and all-daring.

Amber eyes <note>original: "χαροποὶ" (charopoi) — a bright, piercing eye color often associated with lions.</note> are distinguished from black ones, for there are many varieties of them. Black eyes reveal a shameless character, cowardice, and faithlessness. Amber eyes are of the following sort: while their base color appears black, they contain variety. If there are millet-specks <note>original: "κέγχροι" (kenchroi) — small, grain-like spots or granules in the iris.</note> within the black that are reddish, or white, or somewhat whitish, and others that are golden-colored, or some of the sallow ones mixed with the red or the black. There are also blood-colored millet-specks, even in the black eyes. These are evident even when they do not form a perfect "millet-seed" shape, but occur within the various types of eyes. 

Those who have a sallow color on the outer black ring and a reddish color upon that; and others who have the reddish parts appearing somewhat whitish within the black—though not entirely red, because from a distance the black seems to run over them—these signify the characters of noble men: wise, reserved <note>original: "ἀλάλων" (alalōn) — literally "speechless," suggesting a person who is not boastful or who thinks before speaking.</note>, and great in soul. 

For those in whom these reddish parts are not mere specks but have thickened and shine from beneath like fire, and alongside these, sallow specks are mixed with the reddish ones, while others are gleaming <note>original: "γλαυκαὶ" (glaukai) — light blue or grey.</note>, and blood-red or dark-blue colors surround the pupils: these, along with large eyes that shine while moving and glare just as angry men look with wide-open eyelids—all these belong to a useless man. Such traits belong to wolves and wild boars, especially if fire shines from within and, in addition to these, sallow millet-specks are mixed with the reddish ones, and others...

<meta>This page continues the specific character analysis based on eye color and physical depth. It transitions from the discussion of "millet-specks" (kenchroi) to the behavioral traits associated with blue-grey, bloodshot, and sunken eyes, maintaining the systematic categorization typical of ancient physiognomic manuals.</meta>

Blue-grey eyes <note>original: "γλαυκαὶ" (glaukai) — a light, gleaming color often associated with owls or the goddess Athena, but here linked to predatory animals.</note>, simply as mentioned above, just like wolves and wild creatures that possess these signs, belong to men who are crueler, wilder, and more predatory. Those with amber eyes <note>original: "χαροποῖς" (charopois) — a bright, piercing eye color.</note>, resembling millet-specks—whether these specks are larger or smaller—show a gentler character. Pale eyes signify more deceitful men. Bloodshot eyes signify those who are more thoughtless and who impulsively carry out whatever they might decide. 

Those eyes that do not have amber millet-specks, but are instead variegated in size or color, such that they appear as black eyes or even fiery ones within moist eyes—provided no other strange sign is obvious—reveal a man who is high-minded, just, lively, and gifted; however, regarding sexual matters, he is a lover of boys. If there is a greenish-yellow tint beneath the black of such eyes, it signifies a deceitful, unjust man, a thief of money, and one who associates with women in a shameful and dissolute manner. 

If the eyes of some are entirely the color of a pomegranate and are quite dry, they accuse the man of vanity; but if they are moist, they attest to the man's high-mindedness, courage, prudence, and sound judgment. They also reveal a man who is quick to anger and easily excited by the works of Aphrodite <note>original: "ἀφροδίσια ἔργα" (aphrodisia erga) — sexual activities or pursuits of passion.</note>. 

Deeply sunken eyes are not praiseworthy. Those that are sunken like water in a vessel, provided they do not shift and are large, do not signify wicked men, unless some other clear sign follows. In this case, their size mitigates the boldness of the sunkenness. Small, sunken eyes signify a deceitful character, a man plotting against others and consumed by jealousy and envy. If they are dry, in addition to the aforementioned evils, they indicate untrustworthiness and accuse the man of sacrilege <note>original: "ἱεροσυλίαν" (hierosylian) — literally "temple-robbing."</note>. 

Steady eyes display a more upright character, while wavering eyes signify more deceitful traits. If they waver with a certain delicacy or softness, they signify more foolishness. Bulging eyes belong to men who are not praiseworthy. Those whose gaze circles around, while the area around them is like a deep trench followed by a protruding ridge, signify a deceitful man. Eyes that are entirely upturned reveal a lion-like nature, vanity, and madness in men. 

A compression of bloodshot eyes is a sign of drunkards and gluttons. If they are a dull blue-grey, they signify that the man is unjust and senseless. If their eyelids are also heavy, they signify a greater degree of folly. Those that are swollen and dry signify knavishness... <note>The text cuts off mid-word: "πανο-" likely completing as "πανούργος" (panourgos), meaning a rogue or trickster.</note>

It also links certain eye shapes to specific vices like gluttony, sacrilege, and madness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of deeply sunken eyes from the bottom of page 27, listing the severe moral failings associated with them before moving into a comparative study of various eye shapes and movements, including a rare positive reference to the philosopher Socrates.</meta>

...murders, and blood-stained child-corrupters <note>original: "παιδοφθόροι" (paidophthoroi) — literally "corrupters of children," often used in a moral or sexual sense in ancient texts.</note>, sorcerers, and things similar to these. 

But if the eyes are set high, large, bright, and cautious, with a moist gaze, they indicate men who are just, lively, fond of learning, and full of love—such as the philosopher **Socrates** was <note>While many ancient sources describe Socrates as having protruding, "bull-like" eyes, this text uses him as an archetype for the "high and bright" eye, suggesting a noble and inquisitive character.</note>. 

Eyes that protrude far from the face, being very small and reddish, do not indicate a prudent man, but rather one who is flighty in both his speech and his body. Dark eyes signify men who are not very thoughtful. When the eyes are dry, they indicate men of a superior <note>original: "ἀνώτεροι" (anōteroi) — can mean superior in rank or perhaps haughty/supercilious in this context.</note> nature. If they are small and deceitful, they signify men who are treacherous, crafty, and wicked. 

Dark, moist eyes of sufficient size indicate men who are sensitive, thoughtful, observant, quick to learn, and cautious; yet they also reveal men who are manifest <note>plain to see or obvious in their nature</note> and corruptible. Those eyes that are parched signify deceitful, untrustworthy, and unrestrained men. 

Bright, shining eyes are the best, unless some other sign contradicts this. If the eyes flicker <note>original: "μαρμαρύσωσιν" (marmarysōsin) — to flash, sparkle, or quiver</note> and there is a light within them—specifically in blue-grey or bloodshot eyes—they indicate a man who is hot-headed in every matter and reckless, even to the point of madness. 

If this flickering occurs in amber eyes <note>original: "χαροποῖς" (charopois) — a bright, piercing honey or amber color.</note>, it signifies cowardice. Such men fear everything; they are wretched and suspect everyone. Black, flickering eyes indicate men who are like ivy <note>original: "κισοὶ" (kisoi) — perhaps implying they are "clinging" or "twisting" in their nature</note>, being deceitful and extremely malicious. Those who possess such eyes and also smirk or sneer are destined to reach the heights of all wickedness. 

Fierce eyes with a sharp gaze belong to men who are angry, strong, manic, harsh-spoken, quick-acting, and unthinking; though they may be timid in speech, these men are nevertheless very wicked. Dry eyes accuse men of being most base and lawless. If the eyes are smaller and sunken, they are even worse, yet they still possess a certain prideful spirit. Such men are treacherous and secretive, hiding a single purpose in their minds; they desire all things, or are lustful while being lawless. 

If, alongside such eyes, there is a habit of suddenness, combined with thick eyebrows and eyelids that stand upright, these signs signify anger, power, thoughtlessness, wickedness, and a lack of daring. As many as have their eyelids and eyebrows in a jagged, saw-like <note>original: "ἀπριόνιον" (aprionion)</note> fashion, and if their gaze is naturally harsh and bitter, these traits are even worse than what has been said...

...signs of these. For those whose eyes seem full of pleasure and laughter, they are not highly praised; for the nature of such men is deceitful, hiding their true thoughts <note>original: "βλέποντες" (blepontes) — looking/observing; the OCR "βλε-νοῦ" likely represents a split form of "looking with their mind."</note>, and regarding their goals, they are malicious and evildoers.

But those who look askance <note>original: "ὑποβλέπουσιν" (hypoblepousin) — to look from under the brows, often suggesting a suspicious or stealthy glance.</note> at the same time as they laugh, and whose eyes are also dry, these show the sign of the worst of men. Sunken eyes that hold laughter testify that the man is plotting some evil. If there are some who have eyes that are wide open and grinning <note>original: "σεσηρότες" (sesērotes) — literally "grinning like a dog," often implying a menacing or involuntary sneer.</note>, their actions are directed toward lawless deeds. Those men who have eyes that are completely wide open and laughing are testified to be treacherous. 

If the eyes are dry, they indicate a harmless character and an indifference to evils, but such eyes are also vain, heartless <note>original: "ἄστοργοι" (astorgoi) — lacking "storge," or natural family affection.</note>, and unrestrained, especially when smiling along with moisture. Relaxed eyelids and a long forehead—both together indicate relaxed characters. Such a man is testified to be just, gentle, pious <note>original: "εὐσεβεῖς" (eusebeis) — showing proper respect for the gods and social order.</note>, hospitable, lively, well-counseled, quick to learn, and full of love. 

Eyes that are gloomy at the same time as being moist testify to a man who is either wicked or a "craftsman of schemes" <term>philotechnon: literally "one who loves art or craft," but in physiognomy, it refers to someone who uses their intellect for craftiness or trickery</term>. If it should also happen that the eyebrows are always relaxed and the forehead is loose along with that gloominess, these are the signs of a faithful man, who is also pious, well-counseled, and temperate <term>sophron: a key Greek virtue meaning self-controlled, sane, and balanced</term>. 

Dry eyes that look mournful are full of friendliness. But if the forehead is rough and the gaze intent, with the eyelids held straight, they signify a man of savage will, hastening to perform every deed and sparing no evil, but driven as if by necessity—thus he seems to hasten to fulfill everything that occurs to him. 

Eyes that are constantly closing and opening again signify a treacherous man who is "worn down" <note>original: "ἐπίτριπτον" (epitripton) — often used to describe a "practiced rogue" or someone rubbed smooth by villainy.</note> by his own cunning. And if they are moist, they signify a thinker and a schemer. But if there is a trembling in them along with a sallow complexion, it indicates that the man suffers from madness or epilepsy <note>original: "ἐπιληψίαν" (epilēpsian) — the "seizing" disease, which ancients often associated with divine influence or internal imbalance.</note>. 

For those whose eyes have a constant tension, if they flow downward toward closing and move upward, they testify to an unrestrained, vain, and mad man. But if the eyes are moist and straight, and they close with moderate openings, and the gaze is gentle and the forehead smooth, they signify a harmless man, well-counseled, fond of learning, sweet-minded, and full of love. 

If they are dry, they signify a man who is bold, malicious, and unjust. But he who has a rough forehead and eyebrows that are live...
