<meta>This page continues the detailed physiognomic analysis of the eyes, specifically focusing on the movement of the eyelids, the frequency of blinking, and the significance of eye color. It follows the previous discussion regarding how moisture and expression reveal inner character. Catchword: "δεινόν τε" (and a terrible thing).</meta>

...revealed, hard eyelids, fixed in place, indicate a more savage spirit, attempting to be daring; such a man is puffed up by praises and honors, but is easily persuaded by gifts. Those who do not have upright eyelids, nor unyielding eyebrows, but rather a trembling <note>original: "τρόμος" (tromos) — a shaking or quivering motion.</note> is within them, and both the eye and the gaze are constantly in motion—these men, being naturally inclined to shame, are forced into that state again. Eyes that open very wide and frequently, and stay that way at the same time, as if they are thinking, reveal their underlying cause and their goodwill. Such eyes also reveal dryness, moisture, brightness, softness, brilliance, darkness, greatness, smallness, hollowness, height, intensity, and all the other qualities often mentioned in these studies. Those whose eyes are wide open, yet dark and moist, reveal men of reflection <note>original: "φροντιστάς" (phrontistas) — deep thinkers or those burdened with intellectual care.</note>. If, in addition to these traits, they look gently, they signify men of good character.

Eyes that are spread wide and are dry, not darting about but clear and shining, reveal shameless and all-daring men.
Blinking eyes <note>original: "σκαρδάμυκτοι" (skardamyktoi) — eyes that twitch or blink rapidly.</note> are accusers of cowardice; such men are also treacherous, hiding deceit, or perhaps they are preparing to hide some evil once again. These same men, if their eyes are distorted or downcast, reveal foolishness. Unblinking eyes, looking terribly, accuse the man of plotting some evil; but if they are steady and gentle, with moisture within, it signifies a reflective man, a lover of learning, a lover <note>original: "ἐρωτικόν" (erōtikon) — likely referring to a passionate or amorous nature.</note>, and one possessing gentle habits.

Those who are unblinking and appear pale or murderous, combined with dryness, accuse themselves of malice, a love of fighting, and anger, or even a demonic wickedness, or a foolishness regarding shame. Such men are disciplined within themselves, held by a powerful madness. The skin of the eye <note>original: "κύτις" (kytis) — here referring to the flesh or "bags" under the eye.</note> sagging from below signifies drunkards and sleepy men. If it sags from above as well and not only from below, it signifies the same. Regarding eyes that are somewhat reddish <note>original: "ὑποφοινιάζουσιν" (hypophoiniazousin) — literally "becoming somewhat purple/crimson."</note>: if they are also dry, they accuse the man of having a temper; but if they are moister, they signify a drunkard.

A grey-blue color <note>original: "γλαυκότης" (glaukotēs) — a light, gleaming color often associated with owls or lions.</note> of the eyes reveals a more savage man. For most irrational animals have light-colored eyes. You will find that the gentle animals are darker-eyed. Among those with light eyes, the shade that is too white reveals a coward, while the greenish shade reveals a rustic or boorish man. That which is similar in color to an olive reveals a just man. As for the black eye, the shade that is very black...

<meta>This page continues the physiognomic analysis of the eyes and transitions into the study of the ears and the nose. It maintains the theme of relating human facial features to animal characteristics (lions, dogs, bulls, foxes) to deduce moral and intellectual qualities. Catchword from previous page: "δεινόν τε" (and a terrible thing).</meta>

...a thing both terrible and deceitful. But eyes that are somewhat yellowish <note>original: "ὑπόξανθον" (hypoxanthon) — a pale yellow or tawny hue, often associated with a lion-like temperament.</note> signify a man who is strong and magnanimous. Blinking, flickering eyes <note>original: "μεμιρμύαζοντα" (memirmyazonta) — eyes that twitch or sparkle rapidly</note> that are deep in color signify a man who delights in thievery. Sharp, agitated eyes signify plunderers <note>original: "ἅρπαγας" (harpagas) — those who are predatory or prone to seizing things by force.</note>. Those who make their eyelids thin and cast them down signify a shameless disposition. Those who cast down one eyelid while the other remains, or whose eyes turn back with a certain dimness—looking mildly and softly—signify a man who is ostentatious, a dandy, and an adulterer. Others close and draw down the middle of their eyelids while pulling up the corners as if they are afraid; these men are clearly also adulterers.

->On the Ears:<-

Large ears signify an insensitive <note>original: "ἀναίσθητον" (anaisthēton) — lacking in perception or dull-witted.</note> man. Small ears reveal a malicious and villainous man. Small, upright ears, appearing like those of dogs, accuse a man of being foolish. Square ears <note>original: "τετράγωνα" (tetragona)</note> of moderate size bear witness to a brave and noble man. Flared or distended ears reveal a man with a brutish nature. Ears that are deeply hollowed and round—especially if excessively so—reveal ignorance. Narrow and long ears accuse a man of being envious and malicious.

->On Noses:<-

If the tip of the nose is thin, it signifies that the man is prone to fits of sudden anger. If it is thick and low-hanging, it reveals a small-minded man. A nose with a tip that is thick, blunt, round, and firm is a sign of a brave and magnanimous man; for this is the mark of lions and noble dogs. A nose that is long and thin is bird-like, and such is the character of the man. A nose that is well-defined <note>original: "διώρισται" (dioristai)</note> where it meets the forehead signifies a good, brave, and intelligent man. The opposite of this signifies an effeminate and rather ignorant man. A hollowed or sunken nose speaks of the lack of self-control found in foxes. A larger nose reveals a better man. But very small noses, you see, are an indictment of a fickle <note>original: "παλιμβούλου" (palimboulou) — one who constantly changes his mind or plans</note> man. Those with hooked or aquiline noses are high-minded men. Those with snub noses <note>original: "σιμοὶ" (simoi)</note> are lewd and adulterous men. Those who have wide-open nostrils are violent, hot-tempered, and powerful, just as is seen in bulls and lions. Those whose nostrils are narrow or closed reveal foolish men. Extremely crooked noses signify...

<meta>This page continues the physiognomic observations regarding facial traits. The preceding page concluded with a discussion of the nose; the opening fragment here likely finishes that thought or transitions from the nose to the overall mental state revealed by the features. The text then shifts to "Breath" (respiration as a sign of character) and "Cheeks and the Face."</meta>

...and the thoughts and reflections of a man, as they for the most part reveal.

->On Breath:<-

Breath <note>original: "πνεῦμα" (pneuma) — here referring to the manner of breathing as a physical sign rather than "spirit."</note> that is always drawn up as if breathless belongs to a man of deep thought <note>original: "φροντιστοῦ" (phrontistou) — a thinker or one who is weighed down by cares.</note>; such a preoccupation is most difficult to conceal near the eye. If the breathing is calm, yet he speaks all at once for a long time—like a flood or a high tide—he is gripped by some grief. If he should sigh while moving his head, he seems to be repenting for doing or seeing something evil; if he does this with his eyes fixed, he is planning ahead, and even more so if he is in a state of repentance. A breath that goes forth with bitterness <note>original: "χολῇ" (cholē) — literally "bile," implying a sharp or bitter temperament.</note> and without sound reveals a man with good inner strength. But when the breath rumbles beneath and he breathes deeply, it reveals wildness and a tendency toward drunkenness <term>oinophlygia</term> <note>original: "οἰνοφλυγία" (oinophlygia) — habitual wine-drinking or intoxication.</note>. For those whose breathing is always driven inward, such men are lacking in counsel, ill-tempered, full of desire, and talkative. Because they breathe shallowly <note>original: "ὑψηλὸν" (hypsēlon) — literally "high," referring to high-chest breathing.</note>, and the breath is frequent and light in their inhalations, there is cowardice and despondency in them; they become shameful when other signs concur with these.

->On Cheeks and the Face:<-

Fleshy cheeks accuse a man of laziness and drunkenness. Cheeks that are entirely thin accuse a man of malice and villainy. Thick jaws, if they are set apart from the eyes, reveal a malicious <note>original: "βάσκανον" (baskanon) — someone who is envious or possesses the "evil eye."</note> man. Round cheeks reveal a man possessing a deceitful character. Cheeks that are long in every way reveal a chatterer and a speaker of vanities. A face that is entirely fleshy belongs to a shameless and unmanly man. A face without flesh is the sign of a thinker and a plotter. A face that is too large accuses a man of folly and ignorance. For in the face especially are manifested both magnificence and a lack of freedom <note>original: "ἀνελευθερία" (aneleutheria) — a state of being base or illiberal.</note>, as well as simplicity and malice. Indeed, there is a face that is painful, one full of seriousness, or education, or good counsel, or mindlessness, or ignorance, or lamentation, or wakefulness; and every other trait, whether of a good or base nature, appears clearly there. What kind of passion resembles each type and how to practice physiognomy <term>physiognomonein</term> <note>original: "φυσιογνωμονεῖν" — the art of judging character from physical features.</note> regarding them has been discussed above, but shall be stated again. Spasms of the cheeks and faces, if they occur frequently...

<meta>This page continues a systematic treatise on physiognomy—the ancient "science" of judging a person's character by their physical appearance. This page transitions from the final observations on the face (specifically color and the nose) into detailed sections on the mouth and the voice as indicators of moral fiber.</meta>

...appearances [of the eyes] they have, they indicate adultery. But if they are pale yet bright, they indicate adultery and the wicked <unclear>character</unclear> of the man. Red faces in and of themselves indicate shameful madness; however, if only the noses are such a color, they indicate drunkards <note>original: "οἰνόφλυγας" (oinophlygas) — those prone to wine-drinking or intoxication.</note>.

### On the Mouth ~

A mouth that is neither protruding nor too low is a sign of talkativeness and a changeability of mind. A small mouth reveals a feminine character <note>original: "ἦθος" (ēthos)</note> in a man. A larger mouth is a sign of manliness <note>original: "ἀνδρείου" (andreiou)</note>. But a very wide opening of the mouth indicates an altogether foolish, cruel-minded, and unholy man. For such are the mouths of dogs. For those whose lips hide their canine teeth <term>kynodontas</term> <note>original: "κυνόδοντας" — literally "dog teeth."</note>, they are ill-tempered men, insolent, loud-mouthed, and treacherous. For such are the mouths of dogs. For those whose mouth is fixed, and whose lips are very thick, round, and pulled outward, their characters and deeds are like those of pigs, being more prone to foolishness by their nature. If the lower lip protrudes, the character is innocent, but for the most part rather vain and vulgar. A small mouth protruding outward signifies a malicious and treacherous man. A crooked <note>original: "μυλλὸν" (myllon) — distorted, slanted, or twisted to one side.</note> and sunken mouth signifies a babbling, malicious, and undisciplined man ~

### On the Voice ~

To speak with a very deep and broken voice indicates an unintelligent, insolent, and gluttonous man. A sound that is harsher and like a bleating indicates a senseless man. Those who begin with a deep tone and end with a sharp one are both harsh and ill-tempered. A sharp and dry voice is a sign of craftiness <note>original: "ποικιλίας" (poikilias) — complexity, or a "dappled" cunning nature.</note>. But if one speaks with a sharp, soft, and flexible voice, it is a sign of an effeminate man <term>androgynos</term> <note>original: "ἀνδρογύνου" — a person possessing both male and female characteristics, used here pejoratively regarding temperament.</note>. Those who shout with a hollow sound and resonate without bending have noble characters; high-mindedness and justice adorn them. Those who speak in a soft yet deep manner have good thoughts as well. But those who cry out in a wandering way with something sharp and bird-like—these are vain, and their thoughts are frivolous and haughty. A weak voice that is at the same time somewhat weeping signifies a lover of gain, a sorrowful person, and one suspected by all. Those who speak through the nose are false, malicious, deceptive <note>original: "φαῦδροι" (phaudroi) — likely "phaidroi" meaning cheerful/bright, used here to mean "slick" or "smooth-talking" in a negative sense.</note>, and clever at harming others. And to speak with a somewhat cracked voice indicates an angry, violent, lawless, and misanthropic man. A sharp and weak voice is a sign of cowardice and envy <note>original: "βασκανίας" (baskanias) — malice or the "evil eye."</note>. Speaking quickly indicates one who is thoughtless and unholy. A slow—

<meta>This page continues the systematic analysis of physical traits, transitioning from the features of the mouth to the qualities of the voice, beards, lips, and the neck. The author employs "zoological physiognomy," a method common in antiquity where human character is judged by comparing physical features to those of animals believed to possess certain temperaments.</meta>

...to speak [thickly] <note>original: "πα]χὺ" (pachy) — likely completing the word for a thick or heavy voice.</note> is a sign of laziness and cowardice. Those who speak with difficulty are, for the most part, wicked and foolish. If there is anything else concerning the voice, one must compare each person to the animal they sound like and categorize them according to that animal's similar character <note>original: "ἦθος" (ēthos)</note>. For indeed, some people—and even herds of sheep and other animals—emit voices similar to those of pigs, monkeys, donkeys, and horses. Furthermore, one must know what is appropriate: just as in movement, voice, complexion, size, and the harmony of the limbs, the middle path between extremes is generally useful, while the opposite is considered burdensome.

### Concerning Beards ~

**T**hose who have long beards and who talk a great deal about their obligations are <term>shifty-tongued</term> <note>original: "ὑπογλωσσότροποί" (hypoglossotropoi) — literally "turning under the tongue," implying deceitful or glib speech.</note>. Those with small beards are, in all respects, especially wicked men and schemers. For even snakes are of such a kind, being beardless. If a rounded shape grows on those with thin hair, the sign indicates an unmanly and feminine character. A jaw that is split at the tip <note>original: "ἐχισμένη" (echismenē) — divided or cleft, like a viper's tongue.</note>, so that it appears double-pronged, signifies a deceitful man if the shape is very pronounced. However, if the shape of the beard is moderate, the sign relates to sexual desire <note>original: "ἀφροδίτην" (aphroditēn)</note> and the Graces <note>The Charites, goddesses of charm, beauty, and social grace.</note>.

### Concerning Lips ~

**L**oose lips, such that the upper ones hang over the lower, reveal high-mindedness and courage, for the lips of lions are like this. As for thin lips where the mouth itself is stretched wide—or if the lips are thin in small mouths—they signify a cowardly, unmanly, and deceitful person.

### Concerning the Neck ~

**A** neck that is very thin, if it is also long, signifies a cowardly and malicious man. But a thick neck that is also quite long signifies a spirited man, high-minded and stubborn. A neck that is of moderate length and moderate thickness, provided it is well-built and strong, signifies a man who is powerful, quick to learn, and naturally gifted toward excellence <term>arete</term> <note>original: "ἀρετὴν" — moral virtue or functional excellence.</note>. However, such a neck sometimes also signifies a weak, villainous, and devious man. For those whose neck is strained by the tendons, such men are wicked; if other signs are present, they behave foolishly and are seized by bouts of folly. Those who have large, braided veins around the neck are voiceless <note>original: "ἄφωνοι" (aphōnoi) — literally "without voice," perhaps referring to a weak voice or a lack of authority.</note>. Those who have rough and well-formed flesh on the neck are cowardly men, who...

<meta>This page continues the systematic analysis of physical traits used to judge character. Having previously discussed the beard and jaw, the text now moves to the neck (including the nape and vertebrae), the throat, and the collarbones. The focus remains on "physiognomy"—the ancient belief that outward physical features reveal inward moral and intellectual qualities.</meta>

Those whose vertebrae at the nape of the neck are rough are much harsher men than others. Stiff necks signify ignorant men. <note>The text suggests that physical rigidity mirrors a "stiff" or unteachable mind.</note> Soft necks also signify ignorant men. A thick neck at the ridge <note>original: "λοφίας" (lophias) — literally the "crest," referring to the upper part of the neck and shoulders.</note> testifies to ignorance combined with insolence. A neck that is drooping and yet unbending testifies to an evil, stubborn, and ignorant man. Necks become weak because of folly in those who possess the other relevant signs; others, however, have unbending necks but affect a certain <term>coyness</term> <note>original: "ἀκκίζουσιν" (akkizousin) — to behave with an affected daintiness or to play hard-to-get, often used to describe effeminate behavior in this context.</note> and strain their tendons, even though they are quite limp under great exertion. 

These men, being <term>effeminate</term> <note>original: "ἀνδρόγυνοι" (androgynoi) — literally "men-women." In physiognomy, this refers to men who display traits considered "soft" or feminine, which was viewed as a character flaw.</note>, try to straighten the neck. They imagine they are hiding a certain shamelessness and lack of restraint, but they are betrayed by the raising of their hands, the rolling of their eyes, the swaying of their feet, the movement of their hips, the limpness of their hands, and the screaming of their voices. Their necks appear to be fixed in a hollow way, yet they tremble because their tension is forced. Limpness of the hands also indicates an effeminate nature, especially if the other signs align.

Moderate proportion is the sign of the best kind of modesty. A neck thrust forward <note>original: "ἀπεσταλμένος" (apestalmenos) — sent out or jutting forward.</note> is the sign of an insolent and mindless person; indeed, every shameless movement is a sign of this, unless it is caused by physical weakness. He whose neck is stooped is accused of having little modesty. This trait sometimes also indicates a deep thinker, but it can also reveal a miser, one who is malicious, or one who lacks simplicity, cheerfulness, and brilliance. A neck looking toward the right belongs to an orderly, temperate, and thoughtful man. If it looks toward the left, it belongs to a fool. If the neck looks toward any other direction, the sign is not good, for the man is damaged in his mind.

### Concerning the Throat ~

A short throat signifies a shallow-minded, talkative, babbling, and glib man. If a vertebra <note>original: "σπόνδυλον" (spondylon) — while usually meaning a spinal vertebra, here it likely refers to the prominent cartilage of the Adam's apple or the rings of the windpipe.</note> protrudes at the <term>larynx</term> <note>original: "βρόγχου" (bronchou)</note>, it speaks of a temperate man. To shrink inward signifies one who is not bold. Internal vertebrae accompanied by moisture in the throat reveal men who are difficult to work with, annoying, and bad drinking companions. 

Collarbones that are cramped together signify that a man does not have good perception, nor is such a man good in character. Those collarbones that stand out prominently signify shamelessness in such a person. Those that have a moderate symmetry of movement signify an intelligent and brave man.

<meta>This page continues the systematic analysis of physical traits used to judge character, moving from the neck down to the shoulders, arms, chest, and upper back. The focus remains on "physiognomy"—the ancient practice of reading a person’s moral nature through their physical appearance. This transition follows the previous discussion of the neck's stiffness and movement.</meta>

# On Shoulders:

Thick shoulders do not speak well of a man. <note>original: "οὐκ ἐπαινοῦσι τὸν ἄνδρα" — literally "do not praise the man." Thick shoulders were often associated with a coarse, brutish nature.</note> Slack or sunken shoulders, however, signify unmanliness and cowardice. Those that are thin and sharp at the tips reveal a man of a malicious nature.

# On Hands and Arms:

If the forearms are long, such that the hands hang down as far as the knees, this is a sign of a sharp-witted and strong man. But if the arms are <term>stunted</term> <note>original: "κολοβαὶ" (kolobai) — meaning docked, shortened, or underdeveloped in proportion to the body.</note>, so that they do not reach the knees and the man is forced to bring his head down to meet his hands, such men are ill-willed and most wicked; they rejoice in evil, plot against others, and are full of envy. 

Arms, forearms, and jointed elbows that are "sharp" or well-defined are the sign of a <term>steadfast</term> <note>original: "ἀείστου" (aeistou) — a difficult term here, likely meaning constant, excellent, or perhaps "never-failing."</note> man. Those that appear thin or plucked are a sign of an unmanly nature. Very fleshy arms, however, signify an ignorant and insensitive man. 

Soft and tender hands signify a man of natural talent and good wit. Hands that are extremely short are a sign of madness. If the hands are thick but not heavy—being hollow and thin—they reveal a most untrustworthy man. Thick hands with short, stubby fingers reveal an insatiable man. Finally, very small hands reveal a rogue and a thief.

# On the Chest:

In those where the distance from the long chest to the upper back is found to be greater than the distance to the base of the neck—that is, the "yoke"—the text identifies them as fools and gluttons. One should praise large, well-proportioned chests, for this is the sign of a brave man. Conversely, weak chests signify a small-souled and feeble man. Very fleshy chests signify an ignorant and cowardly man. In those whose breasts hang weakly and the entire chest is large, flat-fleshed, and slack, such men are adulterers and <term>drunkards</term> <note>original: "οἰνόφλυγες" (oinophlyges) — literally "wine-bubbles," describing those who are habitually overflowing with wine.</note>.

# On the Upper Back:

A strong <term>upper back</term> <note>original: "μετάφρενον" (metaphrenon) — the region between the shoulder blades.</note> is the mark of a steadfast man. A weak and small one is the sign of a cowardly and small-souled person. Flesh that has "blossomed" or swelled upon the upper back, making it appear enlarged, signifies a senseless man. If the flesh has completely overgrown the upper back, it signifies a fool. A broad upper back reveals a great-minded man. One that is gently rounded is the mark of a firm, charming, and experienced person. A back that is hunched <unclear>...</unclear>

<meta>This page continues the systematic analysis of physical traits, moving from the shoulders down to the chest, torso, and lower limbs. The author maintains the traditional physiognomic approach of comparing human features to those of animals or gendered stereotypes to deduce character.</meta>

...and shoulders sloped toward the chest indicate a malicious and **slanderous** man. If the body also appears bent or hunched in addition to the traits already mentioned, and if judged from the upper parts, it identifies a man who is envious, greedy, and stingy. A heaving chest announces a boiling impulse of shame, just as those who have visible, swollen veins in the neck and around the temples, giving the complexion a blood-red color.

## On Hunching:

A hunched man is not good, unless there is a <term>suppleness of limb</term> <note>original: "ὑγρομελία" (hygromelia) — referring to fluid, flexible, or well-proportioned limbs despite a spinal curve.</note> in him, or the other appropriate signs. In a dry or withered man, consider him a <term>hunter</term> <note>original: "φιλόθηρα" (philothera) — literally a "lover of the hunt," but in this context implying a predatory or aggressive nature.</note>. For so the sign bears witness to such a thing.

## On Ribs, Stomach, Loins, Knees, Hips, and Shins:

Thin ribs signify a weak, unmanly, and cowardly possessor. Those that are very hard and fleshy signify an ignorant man. Those that are prominent and full, like those of **oxen**, bear witness to a malicious and talkative man. 

A flabby stomach signifies sickness of the soul and levity. Those that are very lean and empty accuse the man of being deceitful, malicious, and <term>shifty</term> <note>original: "χαμαιλέλιον" (chamailelion) — likely a variation of "chameleon," suggesting one who changes character for deceptive purposes.</note>. Regarding the stomach, if it is flabby and fleshy, and if the parts are soft and hang outward, they reveal a foolish, drunken, and licentious man. If, however, the stomach is firm with hard flesh, it reveals a malicious and gluttonous man. 

Bony and strong loins are a sign of a brave man. Fleshy and soft loins accuse the man of having feminine habits. Loins that taper to a sharp point accuse the man of being womanish, licentious, and cowardly. A broad back signifies a firm and noble man. If the opposite is true, then the opposite qualities apply. 

Those whose knees <term>turn inward</term> <note>original: "ἐξυπνόοντες" (exypnoontes) — likely referring to a knock-kneed or inward-rotated posture.</note> are feminine and womanish. Thick hips signify a womanish man. Bony hips bear witness to bravery in each of them. Thin hips with little flesh, appearing as if wasted away, are the sign of a rogue—for such are the hips of **monkeys**. 

Shins of good size and well-articulated joints reveal a man of firm stock and high character. But soft and "jointless" shins accuse one of being a coward and unmanly. Very thin shins signify a cowardly and malicious man. Sinewy shins share in much licentiousness. Those shins that are thick in the middle <note>Referring to the calves.</note> indicate people who are loathsome, shameless, and profligate, like the...

<meta>This page continues the downward anatomical progression of physiognomic analysis, transitioning from the lower limbs (shins and ankles) to the feet, toes, and fingernails. The text maintains the standard format of linking physical proportions to specific moral or intellectual qualities, often using the mythological figure of Hercules as an ideal archetype.</meta>

...reveals many things. Thickness of the shins and heels indicates a slavish and ignorant man. Apply the same reasoning to the thighs as was said of the shins. Well-articulated and solid ankles reveal a noble man. However, excessively soft ankles signify shamelessness. Extremely thin ankles are a sign of a deceitful and undisciplined man. Those who develop thick and stunted ankles, thick toes and heels, fleshy feet, and thick shins, for the most part, waste away, meet an early end, or are <term>possessed by demons</term> <note>original: "δαιμονίζονται" (daimonizontai) — in this context, it could refer to demonic possession, epilepsy, or severe mental fits.</note>. 

Sinewy, well-jointed feet signify a man of noble character and courage, such as **Hercules** was. On the other hand, soft and fleshy feet produce a similar character—that is, soft men—and are a sign of those not <term>well-formed for movement</term> <note>original: "οὐ τύποις εὐορχουμένοις" — literally "not of well-dancing forms," implying a lack of grace or athletic coordination.</note>. Stunted and thick feet are signs of a beast-like soul. Those with very long feet are meddlesome and malicious men. Small and thin feet signify a rogue. Curved feet that are hollow underneath signify a bad man. Similarly, those who have soft feet and walk flatly up to the ankles are rogues.

### On Fingers and Toes

Those who have <term>joined fingers</term> <note>original: "συμφυεῖς" (symphyeis) — likely referring to webbed digits.</note> are wretched, in the manner of **swine**; if they have crooked fingers, they are rogues and malicious people. Very long and thin fingers signify cowardly men. Thick, stunted fingers reveal bold, harsh, and beast-like men. Those that are excessively long and thin signify more foolish, <term>vacuous</term> <note>original: "ἀποχαυνοτέρους" (apochaynoterous) — meaning empty-headed or prone to gaping in distraction.</note>, and talkative men. Those with well-sized and well-formed fingers are the best of men. 

Broad, white, or slightly yellowish nails reveal a man of good nature. Narrow, elongated, and curved nails signify a dull-witted and beast-like man. Extremely crooked nails reveal a predatory man. Very small, black nails signify a rogue. Round nails signify men who are great **adulterers**. It has been stated before that many other things are noteworthy to me, if one only considers each part individually, but especially the nails:

<meta>This page transitions from the specific anatomical catalog of the previous page—which focused on feet and toes—to broader "Definitions of Human Types" (or "Forms"). It moves from isolated physical parts to complete character archetypes (the brave man, the coward, the gifted, and the dullard) as described by Polemon of Laodicea, a 2nd-century scholar of physiognomy.</meta>

->DEFINITIONS OF HUMAN TYPES, FROM THE SAME POLEMON’S <term>PHYSIOGNOMY</term><note>original: "φυσιογνωμονικῶν" (physiognomonikōn) — the study of determining a person's character or moral nature from their outer appearance.</note><-
+

### On the Form of the Brave Man

<margin>E</margin>
The form of the brave man is as follows: the entire posture is upright; the ribs and the joints of the body are all strong and large. The hair is coarse and the belly is flat and hollow. The shoulders are sturdy, and the shoulder blades are wide and set apart. The chest and the upper back are powerful; the hips are firm, and the legs are fleshy. There is strength around the ankles, and the feet are well-jointed. His complexion is <term>fiery</term> <note>original: "ὀξύπυρον" (oxypyron) — literally "sharp-fire," suggesting a reddish, glowing, or ruddy skin tone associated with a hot temperament.</note>. His gaze is "moist" <note>In ancient medicine, a "moist" eye often meant bright, lively, and full of vital spirit, rather than watery.</note>, and the eyes are neither large nor wide open, nor again are they tightly squinted. The eyebrows are not strained, and the forehead is perfectly proportioned—certainly not small. The voice is somewhat harsh, powerful, and large, and his breathing is steady. Such is the man of high spirit and endurance. These are his signs.

### On the Form of the Coward

The form of the coward is thus: his hair is soft and his entire posture is relaxed and slumped. The neck is long and the complexion is either dark or sallow. The eyes are <term>agitated</term> <note>original: "ταραχώδη" (tarachōdē) — suggesting a restless, fearful, or blinking movement.</note>, and the eyelids move quickly. The breath is weak and faint. The legs are thin, the eyebrows are long, and the chest is weak. The hands are long and the voice is shrill and soft. These are the signs of a coward.

### On the Form of the Gifted Man

Understand the <term>gifted man</term> <note>original: "εὐφυοῦς" (euphyous) — literally "well-natured," referring to a person naturally endowed with intelligence, quick learning, and good character.</note> to be like this: he is <term>well-proportioned in size</term> <note>original: "εὐμεγένειον" — likely a scribal variation for "well-sized" or "of good stature."</note>, with a complexion that is white mixed with red. His hair is not overly curly, nor again is it straight. The entire body is taut and upright, with the joints distinct from one another. The flesh is moderate and soft. The legs and thighs are well-nourished, the ankles are firm, and the joints of the feet and hands are noble. The fingers are smooth, quite long, and set apart from one another. The face is lifted and fleshy, but not thin. The eyes are moist, bright, and sparkling, and the character within them is calm.

### Signs of the Insensitive Man

These are the signs of the <term>insensitive man</term> <note>original: "ἀναισθήτου" (anaisthētou) — referring to someone who is dull-witted, slow of perception, or lacking in emotional depth.</note>. He is either very white or very dark; he is fleshy, pot-bellied, and thick-legged. His joints are small and <term>bound together</term>... <note>The text likely implies the joints are not well-defined or appear fused by fat.</note>

<summary>This page defines four primary human archetypes—the brave, the cowardly, the gifted, and the dull-witted—correlating their physical traits (such as skin tone, joint definition, and voice) with their moral and intellectual nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the list of character types based on physical traits, following the "Gilded Man" or "Dullard" category which began on the previous page. It outlines the specific physical "signs" (semeia) for various moral and emotional temperaments, ranging from shamelessness to mildness.</meta>

...and having fused collarbones, a short, thick neck, fully-formed extremities, fleshy temples, a round forehead, and a gaze that is fixed and gaping.

## Signs of the Shameless Man

The signs of the shameless man are these: eyes that are wide open and bright; eyelids that are thickened, turned inward, and broad; a heavy-set face; a fixed stare <note>original: "ἄϋλον ὁρῶν" (aylon horon) — literally "looking immaterially," but in this context, it suggests a staring gaze that does not look away or show proper social deference.</note>; he carries himself with a stretching, upward tension; his complexion is ruddy; and his voice is high-pitched.

## Signs of the Modest Man

These are the signs of the modest <note>original: "κοσμίου" (kosmiou) — refers to a person who is orderly, well-behaved, and possesses self-control.</note> man: he speaks with a deep voice, and briefly; he moves his eyelids moderately—neither constantly blinking nor keeping them still for long intervals. His eyes are bright-eyed <note>original: "χαροπὸς" (charopos) — often used to describe light-colored or sparkling eyes, like those of a lion.</note>, with a "moist" gaze that is not overly brilliant. He is prone to blushing. Aim for such a man to be your model of modesty.

## Signs of the Cheerful Man

His forehead is low, smooth, and fleshy, and his entire face is quite fleshy. His appearance is somewhat sleepy <note>original: "ὑπνωλέν" (hypnolen) — suggesting a relaxed, un-stressed disposition.</note>. His eyes are "moist" and not piercing, though they appear observant. His movement is leisurely, and his voice is mild.

## Signs of the Distressed Man

His face is gaunt and his forehead is wrinkled. His eyebrows are spread out and his eyelids droop. He moves as if he is weighed down by heavy thoughts <note>original: "ὑπέρνοι" (hypernoi) — suggests being over-anxious or lost in brooding.</note>.

## Signs of the Androgynous Man

The androgynous man <note>In ancient physiognomy, "androgynous" or effeminate traits were analyzed to identify men perceived as lacking traditional masculine "hardness" or "heat."</note> has a moist and sharp gaze; his eyes quiver and dart about. His forehead and temples appear parched, and his eyebrows are puffy in their place. His neck is tilted to the side, and his loins are unsteady. He moves all his limbs with a sort of bouncing skip. He displays a knocking of the knees and fluttering of the hands. Like a bull, he looks around at himself and looks down. His voice is slender, but he cries out shrilly; his path is very crooked, and he is prone to trembling.

## Signs of the Bitter Man

His face is fixed in a sneer <note>original: "σέσηρε" (sesere) — describes a face with teeth bared or a cynical, grinning expression.</note>; he is pale and gaunt. He has a thick, dry gaze and a face that appears puckered <note>original: "ἐντερώδες" (enterodes) — literally "intestine-like," referring to a face full of deep, convoluted wrinkles or folds.</note>. He speaks vehemently and stubbornly. He turns dark when angry. In moments of weakness, he claps or rubs his hands together, and he frequently stamps his feet.

## Signs of the Mild Man

He is strong in appearance; his flesh is moist, abundant, of good size, and well-proportioned in shape. The flesh of his limbs is soft. His gaze is somewhat dull <note>original: "δυσαυθὴς" (dysauthes) — lacking a bright or sharp gleam.</note>; his movement is slight and indirect; his voice is deep...

<summary>This page catalogs the physical "signs" associated with seven different human temperaments, including the shameless, modest, cheerful, and bitter types. It reflects the ancient belief that moral character is directly visible in the body's proportions, movements, and gaze.</summary>

->The Ironical Man and the Schemer<-

He has both eyes blinking <note>original: "δι’ ἀκαρώς" (di' akaros) — an adverbial phrase meaning "in an instant" or "frequently," suggesting a rapid, twitchy movement.</note> at the same time, a dull gaze, and a lowered voice. He walks unsteadily, yet moves all his parts in a rhythmic manner.

->Signs of the Greedy Man<-

Understand the greedy <term>philochrematos: lover of wealth</term> man to be such: small-limbed, small-eyed, and small-faced. He walks quickly, stooped over; he is fast-talking, high-pitched in voice, and his complexion is somewhat reddish. He is fond of his hair and a heavy drinker; he will be a man hairy with hair, though not densely so, and dark-complexioned, having a thick beard and hairy temples. His eye is oily, shining, and seems as if perfumed; he is a lover of revelry and a lover of wine.

->On the Stupidly-Wicked<-

There also exist those who are "stupidly-wicked" <note>original: "μωροπόνηροι" (moroponerone) — a compound of "moro" (foolish/dull) and "poneros" (wicked/painful). This refers to a specific type of malicious person who lacks intelligence.</note>. Just as with beasts, those of the same genus have some that are tame and some that are wild. One must inspect each of these specifically; the tame ones are more easily moved, more restless, and cowardly. One should observe this especially in wild goats and sheep, and also in horses, donkeys, and other such species. Generally, the tame ones are gentler, smoother, and milder. The wild ones are drier, or thicker-set, or more violent. Note that their character follows these traits.

It is necessary to divide men in this way as well. For the dual nature of appearance is visible in men; according to each of the signs, they become just. It is clear that some possess wilder characters, while others are gentler, temperate, and brave likewise. The distinction of the signs is found in roughness, hardness, softness, shamelessness, and mildness. And a division must be made thus in "moisture" and "dryness." Likewise for injustice and licentiousness. Nature possesses both the tame and the wild in these signs. Call the wilder state "injustice" and the milder state "justice." Among men, the majority are wild, while the others are persuasive <note>original: "πιθανοί" (pithanoi) — meaning plausible, credible, or easily won over; the civilized man.</note>. Even when all the signs are not present, other signs alone may be used to differentiate, as you might say. The stupidly-wicked man has fluttering hair, a well-rounded <note>original: "τερεάν" (terean) — often used to describe something smooth, firm, or well-turned.</note> head, a sideways glance, and somewhat large ears that are bent back. His neck and throat are round,

...extremities of the ankles, a hard and thick forehead, and eyes that are dark, 
small, dry, and hollow, looking downward intently, with narrow eyelashes

as if split open—and very long. He carries his face as if it were torn; he is 
humpbacked, pot-bellied, and thick-legged. The joints of his feet and hands 
are very long, thick, and hard. He is somewhat sallow <note>original: "ὑπόχλωρος" (hypochloros) — a greenish-yellow or pale complexion often associated with ill health or bile.</note> in complexion, 
appearing as if full of sleep or a hangover. His voice is bleating, 
feeble, small, yet bold.

->These are the signs of the passionate man:<-

**U**pright in his posture, well-ribbed, well-proportioned, and fiery <note>original: "ἐπίπυρος" (epipyros) — meaning "on fire" or "glowing," referring to a ruddy complexion or a hot-tempered nature.</note> in temperament. His shoulder 
blades are set apart, large, and powerful; the areas around his chest and 
groin are smooth. He is thick-bearded and broad-backed, often looking 
down toward the lower tendon <note>possibly the Achilles tendon or the nape of the neck</note>. This type also includes those whose skin 
around the head is loose, those with round faces, and those with 
curved <note>original: "σεφανῶν" (stephanon) — literally "crown-like" or circular.</note> eyebrows and a general air of mobility.

->Signs of the slanderer:<-

**T**he upper lip is raised and the lower one hangs forward; they are reddish, 
fiery, and the lip turns up under the canine teeth. One might see 
this same expression in aging horses.

->Signs of the lustful man:<-

**P**ale-complexioned, hairy on the upper lip, having straight and thick hair, 
and an eye that is oily and frantic <note>original: "μαργον" (margon) — meaning "mad," "gluttonous," or "lustful."</note>. His legs are thin and sinewy, 
and his whole frame is bird-like. He has hairy legs, but a thinness 
of the stomach and eyes. He tends to pull his chin up toward his nose 
and has a hollow curve in the space between the nose and the chin—
just as the "beautiful" Socrates <note>A common ironic reference to the philosopher Socrates, who was famously described as having satyr-like features despite his inner beauty.</note> had. Those with moist eyes 
are also lustful, as are those with a prominent vein in the arm and those 
with "empty" <note>thin or lacking muscle</note> calves.

->Signs of the talkative man:<-

**T**hose having the upper parts of the body larger, refined in their 
outward appearance, and hairy around the belly.

->Signs of the lover of sleep:<-

**T**hose having the upper parts of the body larger, who appear 
sleepy and "hot," and who possess well-nourished <note>original: "εὔφητος" (euphetos) — likely a variant of "euphetos" or "euphyes," suggesting healthy or soft flesh.</note> flesh.

->Signs of the envious man:<-

**T**hose having the left side of the body larger, and those whose 
eyebrows hang down beneath the eyelids.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of physical signs and their corresponding character traits. It follows the previous entry on the "Slanderer," transitioning into signs for the well-natured, the coward, the slothful, and finally the lover, concluding with a brief philosophical justification for the science of physiognomy.</meta>

### Signs of the Well-Natured: <note>original: "Εὐφυεῖς" (euphyeis) — though this usually means "naturally gifted" or "well-formed," the text paradoxically lists traits like insolence and stupidity here.</note>
For those whose vertebra <note>original: "σπόνδυλος" (spondylos) — specifically the prominent bone at the base of the neck.</note> protrudes toward the outside of the neck, they are **insolent**; this trait is compared to **swine**. Those who have the lower lip projecting over the upper are **stupid**, **flighty**, **weak**, and **wretched**; this is compared to the **donkey**.

### Signs of the Coward:
Those who have an excessively wide nose are **cowardly**; this is compared to **swine**.

### Signs of the Slothful:
Those who have an excessively short nose are **slothful**; this is compared to **cattle**. Those who naturally possess a nose like that of flat-billed, straight-beaked birds are compared to the **female** and to **hens**. Those who have a short and snub nose are **thieves** and **lustful**; this is compared to **deer** and **wild swine**. Those whose nostrils are flared and snub are **quick to anger**; this refers to the passion that arises in the temperament. One might also see this in **noble horses**. Those who have a fleshy forehead are **slothful**; this is compared to the **female** and to **cattle**. Those who have an excessively bony forehead are **laborious**, **cowardly**, and **cold**; this is compared to the **donkey**, **monkeys**, and **deer**. Those who have an excessively small face are **rogues**; this is compared to the **donkey**. Those who have a mean-looking face are **small-minded** and **illiberal** <note>original: "ἀνελεύθεροι" (aneleutheroi) — lacking the qualities of a free person; stingy or base.</note>. Those who have large lips are **wanton**. For those whose upper lip is larger than the lower, they are **spiritless**. For those where the opposite is true—the lower larger than the upper—they are, conversely, **stupid**. Regarding the canine teeth: those whose lower canines project are **malicious** <note>original: "ἰώδεις" (iodeis) — literally "rust-like" or "venomous."</note>, and as for their hatred, they are **lewd** <note>original: "κιναίδοι" (kinaidoi) — refers to men who were considered effeminate or sexually passive, often used as a term of moral reproach.</note>.

### The following signs belong to the Lover:
Hollow eyes that appear to be weeping, yet seem as if they were molded for pleasure; their eyelids move frequently. While other parts of the body may fail or collapse, these parts do not collapse for lovers alone. The lechery of a man or a woman is recognized in the lifting of the eyes and in the eyelids. All these things that have been described are just like in drawings; whether it be a color or a line, they are merely modeled likenesses of men. At that time, many and various types of men are found from the many individual signs in their combinations and their powers. By being persuaded by one sign at one time and another at another, one finds this science to be the most secure evidence.

<meta>This page concludes the physiognomic treatise, moving from the previous discussion of facial features and general dispositions to specific categories of people (masculine women and eunuchs) and the anatomy of the feet. It ends with a formal closing marker.</meta>

### Women of the Companion-Type <note>original: "Γυναῖκες ἑταιρίστριαι" (Gynaikes hetairistriai) — a term used in antiquity to describe women who took a masculine role or engaged in same-sex relationships, often translated as "tribades."</note> :~

They are effeminate, yet possess a masculine spirit <note>original: "φιλανδρεῖοι" (philandreioi) — literally "man-loving," but in physiognomic contexts, it often implies women who adopt male behaviors or appearances.</note>, and are unclean. Their lower legs are thick toward the ankle, and their toes are slightly spread out, just like **web-footed birds**. They also have hair that is stiff and sword-like, which is a residue <note>original: "περίττωμα" (perittoma) — a technical term in Aristotelian biology referring to a metabolic byproduct or "excretion" of the body, such as hair, nails, or sweat.</note> of the head :~

### Concerning Eunuchs :~

Signs of innate wickedness exist for eunuchs just as they do for other men. For the most part, they are **small-minded**, **deceitful**, and **villainous**; some are even masters of craftiness beyond others. As for the "honorable" eunuch, if he changes his nature through daring, he exerts a force even greater than his natural disposition, as he lacks the strength to remain in that same state <note>This suggests that if a eunuch overcomes his "natural" tendency toward vice, it requires an extraordinary and perhaps unsustainable effort of will.</note> :~

### Concerning the Soles of the Feet :~

Those with narrow feet are **foolish** and **cowardly**. Those who do not have a hollow arch in the foot <note>original: "ἀντίκοιλον" (antikoilon) — the instep or arch of the foot.</note>, but walk upon the whole surface, are **rogues**, just as the proverb says of the **fox**. Those with short and thick fingers and toes are **foolish**; however, one must not assume this is always the case, but rather when the rest of the body is neither short nor thick. Furthermore, those who have joined toes <note>original: "συμπεφυκότας" (sympephykotas) — toes that are webbed or grown together.</note> are **cowardly**, just as **dogs** are :~

<meta>The previous page appears to have concluded a different section or an earlier version of the text, ending with specific physical traits like the feet and the character of eunuchs. This page, however, marks a clear beginning (Book 1) and contains the author’s preface and dedication to Constantius.</meta>

# THE PHYSIOGNOMICS OF ADAMANTIUS THE SOPHIST. BOOK 1.

<margin>T</margin>he method of **physiognomy** <term>Physiognomy: the ancient art of judging a person's character or moral nature from their physical appearance, especially the face.</term> is something I have gathered from **Aristotle**, collecting even more of it from other sources, and training myself in the art by observing the actual lives of men. I decided it was necessary to enshrine this knowledge in writing, just as one might place a sacred statue in a holy temple, as it will be of great benefit to those who come after us. For even when people are silent, nature provides an answer; she speaks through signs, as it were, proclaiming each person’s character without making a sound. Therefore, I have condensed the writings of **Polemon** <note>Polemon of Laodicea (c. 88–144 AD) was a famous orator whose work on physiognomy was the primary source for later authors like Adamantius.</note> into a more common style of language, seeking to make them a shared benefit for readers, and I have added my own observations to the teaching. 

I have written these things over time, leaving their publication to others after my life is over, thus storing up beautiful treasures of education for scholars <note>original: "φιλολόγοις" (philologois) — literally "lovers of learning" or "lovers of words."</note>. I was not eager to bring any of my other observations into the public eye during my lifetime, as I suspected the plots born of envy that are so often accustomed to follow those who are diligent. But since you, my dearest **Constantius**, have called upon me to produce this work at your request, I have been persuaded by my affection for you and have given you this writing without delay—acting "willingly, yet with an unwilling heart," as **Homer** says <note>original: "ἑκὼν ἀέκοντί γε θυμῷ" (hekon aekonti ge thymo) — a famous phrase from the Iliad (4.43), expressing a person doing something out of necessity or friendship despite personal reluctance.</note>. So great is the power of friendly grace to change the judgment of one's will. 

The practice of physiognomy is a discovery of divine men, if anything is, and it can benefit those who learn it in many great ways. One would not entrust a financial deposit, nor trust a wife or children to someone’s company, nor enter into any simple friendship with those who carry the shining signs of untrustworthiness, licentiousness, or some villainy upon their physical form. For the physiognomist understands the character and life-purpose of nearly all men, as if through some unerring prophecy sent by the gods. Thus, one can choose to befriend only the virtuous and guard against the vices of the wicked before ever having to experience them. Therefore, it is fitting for the prudent to [pursue] this art with all diligence <unclear>epi-</unclear> <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "epimelesthai" meaning to take care of or study.</note>

<summary>In this preface to Book 1, Adamantius explains his sources—primarily Aristotle and Polemon—and his motivation for writing the treatise as a guide for judging character. He dedicates the work to his friend Constantius, emphasizing that physiognomy acts as a "god-sent prophecy" to protect the virtuous from the wicked.</summary>

<keywords>Adamantius the Sophist, Physiognomy, Aristotle, Polemon of Laodicea, Constantius, Homer, moral character, signs of nature, friendship, envy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Adamantius's instructional guide on the methodology of physiognomy. The previous page introduced the work as a compilation of Aristotle and Polemon’s teachings; here, the author explains how to distinguish between individual traits and those shared by ethnic groups or caused by temporary emotions.</meta>

# ADAMANTIUS

...to make the observation, and to practice **physiognomy** <term>physiognomy: the practice of judging a person's character or personality from their outer appearance.</term>, especially by looking for what is unusual or changed, both in terms of a person’s age and their natural appearance. For many people possess signs that are inconsistent with their age or their ethnicity <note>original: "ἔθνους" (ethnous) — meaning race, nation, or tribe.</note>, and these signs have a manifest entry and perception. Even more manifest, however, are the signs belonging to specific cities and nations. For it is easy to recognize the character of an entire race according to the tribes of men. Among these nations, the common signs are very few, and it is in individuals that the signs differ most of all. 

For all **Egyptians** have common signs from which the whole race is judged, as do **Ethiopians**, and also **Scythians**, and other men according to their race, as will be discussed later. But individually, for each person, the differences in signs are great. Indeed, one must learn to recognize traits more on an individual basis—how people differ specifically—than by nation. To practice physiognomy on an individual in this way is the most effective method. 

From the signs available, choose the greatest and most reliable, and trust in clear signs rather than a few uncertain ones. For clear signs prevail in power over a few. Most importantly, the mixtures <note>original: "μίξεις" (mixeis) — the blending or combination of different physical features.</note> of signs produce variety in the art. For the many characters and intentions of men are understood from the mixture of these physical signs. 

It happens that certain departures from the usual appearance occur, such as from joy, or grief in mourning, or in those who have been or are currently angry, or from fasting, or from some fullness <note>satiety or overindulgence</note>, or desire, or in those who are reflecting, seeing, or hearing. In such circumstances, the appearance does not change entirely; rather, there are some signs that remain unmoved. Whatever signs do shift do not change equally for everyone, but remain close to each person's own nature. 

One must understand that if someone possesses certain signs permanently—the kind of signs that usually only appear during a temporary emotion <note>original: "πάθει" (pathei) — an emotion, passion, or state of being.</note>—then that person is most prone to the state indicated by those signs. For example, if a man has signs like those of one who is deliberating, you may call him thoughtful and prudent. If he has signs of deceit, or of someone brewing some trickery, even if you do not see him plotting a deceit at that very moment, you should declare him to be deceitful. In the same way, if someone has the signs of an angry man, even if he is not gripped by rage at that moment, you shall call him quick-tempered. You should apply similar precautions for other passions. 

As for those who have a feminine appearance <note>original: "γυναικεῖον εἶδος" (gynaikeion eidos)</note> spread over their eyes and other features: those signs relating to pleasure and **Aphrodite** <note>original: "ἀφροδίτην" (aphroditēn) — referring to sexual love or the influence of the goddess of love.</note> indicate they are feminine; but they are also bold, and shameless, and 

<meta>catchword: crafty/villainous</meta>

<summary>This page explains the methodology of physiognomy, emphasizing the importance of distinguishing between ethnic traits, individual characteristics, and temporary physical changes caused by emotions like anger or grief. Adamantius argues that permanent physical markers reflecting temporary emotional states indicate a person's inherent character.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the physiognomist, transitioning from a discussion on ethnic and individual "mixtures" of character to how age and animal resemblances provide clues to a person's inner nature.</meta>

## PHYSIOGNOMY. BOOK 1.

...delighting in villainy, and deceits, and distrust, and along with these, womanish traits. Those who, while being young, have an elderly appearance, these are cowardly, difficult, suspicious, stingy, cautious, and pious, and they possess whatever other good or bad qualities belong to old age. On the other hand, those who, even into old age, preserve a childish appearance, these are fond of playing, fond of dancing, ignorant scoundrels, simple, and to put it briefly, they correspond to the form they possess. 

There occur among men both bird-like and beast-like forms—not entirely, but resembling them in certain ways, having more or less of the beast-like nature. To whatever beast a person’s form inclines him, according to that one must judge him through **physiognomy** <term>physiognomy: the ancient practice of determining a person's character or fate from their physical features, particularly the face.</term>. Even if traits from many beasts happen to appear in a single man, one must judge them based on all of these. 

For as many forms as humans have, they also possess as many characters. Signs exist in the whole of a man’s body. Both voice and breath, and complexion, contribute to the practice of physiognomy. But most of the signs, and the most comprehensive ones, are found in the eyes. As if through these **gates** <note>original: "πυλῶν" (pylōn) — gates or portals.</note>, the soul shines through. One must see accurately and precisely, and measure out the signs existing in them; for though they are small, they hold great differences. One should not announce beforehand to the person you are going to examine that you are practicing physiognomy on them, so that they do not prepare themselves by changing their expression and disturb the signs. ~

### ON THE EYES : ~

Moist eyes, shining like springs <note>original: "λιβάδες" (libades) — pools, springs, or moisture-drops.</note>, reveal a good character. Children’s eyes are like this. Great width of the pupils indicates foolishness. Smallness of the pupils, on the other hand, denounces **malicious craftiness** <term>malicious craftiness: "κακομηχανία" (kakomēchania), a tendency toward plotting, trickery, or harmful deceit.</term>. Since among other animals, snakes and mongooses <note>original: "ἰχνεύμονες" (ichneumones) — the Egyptian mongoose, legendary for its cleverness and for hunting snakes.</note>, monkeys, and foxes, and all such as are craftily malicious, have a sharp and piercing look. Sheep and oxen, and all such as are simple-minded, have a broad or flat look. Those that have a "shuddering" look <note>original: "φρικώνουν" (phrikōnoun) — a term suggesting a flickering, trembling, or wide-eyed stare.</note> have pupils that are proportional to their eyes. 

Those who have pupils that move contrary to the circles of the eyes are lawless; those who have them balanced are friends of justice. Those who have circles around the pupils that appear to be driven or whirled are lawless. But if this sign should sit upon the forehead itself—such as green or dark-blue clouds, or clouds of different colors, or misty ones appearing above the eyebrows—a **spirit** <note>original: "δαίμων" (daimōn) — a supernatural power or guiding spirit that could influence a person's fate.</note> having descended upon their household drives them into all kinds of ruin. But if the clouds do not sit upon the eyebrows, but the...

<meta>This page continues the specific anatomical analysis of the eyes, which Adamantios considers the "gates of the soul." The text moves from general circular movements in the pupils—associated with extreme moral failings—to a detailed catalog of how deep-set and moving eyes reveal a person's temperament and moral fiber. It picks up from a discussion on how subjects might try to hide their true nature if they know they are being observed.</meta>

### ADAMANTIOS THE SOPHIST

...if circles should battle the pupils, one must observe if they always revolve in the same way, which is a sign that they perform unlawful acts, such as the murder of kin, or forbidden sexual unions, or the eating of forbidden food—even a **Thyestean feast** <note>original: "θυεσίου" (thuesiou) — a reference to the mythical King Thyestes who was unknowingly fed his own children; in ancient literature, this represents the ultimate taboo of cannibalism.</note>. But if in their movement the circles turn back and take up some stillness or movement in between, for these people no crime is actually committed <note>original: "σφάται" (sphatai) — literally 'slaughtered,' here referring to the execution of a violent act.</note>, but unlawful deeds are still desirable to them; at one time they yearn to do them, but at another they are hindered by fear or hesitation.

->About Deep-set Eyes<-

Deep-set eyes are all strange. Those that are more moist signify cowards; those that are drier signify the gluttonous; and those that are paler signify the **thunderstruck** <note>original: "ἐμβροντήτους" (embrontētous) — meaning stunned, senseless, or mentally unstable, as if hit by a bolt of lightning.</note>. Those who also raise their eyebrows and draw up their breath are ill-advised, cruel-minded, evil-counseling, empty-headed, and spiteful. Deep-set eyes that are reddish and large belong to gluttons and are witnesses of lust; if, in such people, the areas beneath the eyes are also pale, this accuses them of even more: shamelessness, injustice, greed, and helplessness. Small, fixed eyes will reveal a lover of money, one who procures profit from every side. If the forehead together with the eyebrows clearly meets in the middle, the man is even more profit-seeking. If the whole body, along with these signs, is withered, it indicates that he is impoverished, prone to anger, and worthless. If someone has **gleaming blue-gray eyes** <note>original: "γλαυκοὺς ὀφθαλμοὺς" (glaukous ophthalmous) — the term 'glaukos' describes a light, piercing color, often associated with owls or predators.</note> that are fixed and without luster, never join in friendship with him, nor have him as a neighbor, nor receive him as a fellow citizen. For such a man is treacherous, lying awake through the night for terrible deeds at the misfortunes of others. Small, moist, fixed eyes, a parched forehead, and moving eyelids seem shameful, yet they indicate a thinker, a **lover of words** <term>lover of words: 'philologos,' referring to someone devoted to literature, learning, and reasoned argument.</term>, and a lover of learning—this is the only "best" form of fixed eyes.

->About Moving Eyes<-

Eyes that move quickly indicate a troubled, calculating, untrustworthy person, one who is more of a worrier than a man of action. Those who move their eyelids along with their eyes are for the most part also weak in soul. Those who move their eyes quickly, but do not move their eyelids in the same way, are courageous, for they are daring in the face of dangers. Those who move slowly are sluggish, lazy, hard to perceive, slow to start, and difficult to stop. The middle ground of movement signifies the middle of all the qualities I have mentioned. Eyes that run about everywhere and are misty charge one with folly...

<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of the eyes as a mirror of character. The previous page ended while describing the physical traits of those prone to evil, specifically how pale areas under the eyes "accuse them of evil." # PHYSIOGNOMONICS, BOOK 1

...evil ones. Large, bloodshot eyes signify inactivity, foolishness, gluttony, drunkenness, and laziness. Small, quick-moving, blue-grey eyes are shameless, untrustworthy, and unjust; such people meddle in the misfortunes of others and live off the calamities of their neighbors. Small, quick-moving eyes that are either **bright-eyed** <note>original: "charopoi" — a term often used to describe the fierce, glinting, or amber-colored eyes of a lion; here it suggests a piercing or restless quality.</note> or black signify the same traits. Between them, however, the bright-eyed are more impulsive and ill-advised, while the black-eyed are more irreverent and shameless. Eyes that surge and billow within themselves like the waves <note>This "moistness" or "surging" was anciently associated with the influence of Aphrodite and a passionate temperament.</note> are desirable for their charm and indicate a capacity for deep passion. Yet, these people are neither unjust, nor criminal, nor of a foul nature, nor unrefined. ~

### About Blue-Grey Eyes ~

Those who have blue-grey <term>glaucous</term> <note>original: "glaukoi" — this color term describes a range from light blue to a pale, silvery-grey or sea-green, famously used to describe the eyes of the goddess Athena.</note> eyes with very small pupils are found to be more illiberal, villainous, and money-loving than others. Drier blue-grey eyes indicate wild and lawless habits; these are, as it were, "dipped in bile" rather than in dark blue. Dark blue eyes, however, are generally moister and are much better than the others. 

Those whose eyes are moist, steady, lacklustre, and large—or where the white parts show equally—are weak-spirited. Those with weak circles around the eyes are more prone to grief, powerless, and the most cowardly of all people we know. As for the obvious signs, use them just as you would apply other markers to these cases. 

**Mottled** <term>aioloi</term> <note>original: "aioloi" — meaning shimmering, shifting, or variegated; eyes that seem to change color or have distinct flecks.</note> eyes occur more often among the bright-eyed than the blue-grey. I shall explain how the blue-grey eye becomes mottled: there are dark blue specks around the pupils the size of a millet seed, and others that are yellow, equal in size to the others; when these sit parallel to one another, they encircle the pupil like a necklace. Such eyes possess a deceitful and especially thievish character; they have a sufficient share of shrewdness and understanding, but they are timid. 

Small mottled eyes are even more cunning, slavish, and flatterers of the powerful; they are money-loving, saying one thing while doing another. They take a secret joy in deception; even if there were no difference between speaking or acting openly versus in secret, they would still choose to do everything underhandedly, as they are driven to "theft" by their cowardice. For such people are powerless and in no way good. 

Those whose upper parts of the eyes are similar and who possess a straight gaze are the best. Eyes that are distended or bulging are a sign of vanity and inactivity, or perhaps a "watery disease" <note>likely referring to edema or a buildup of fluid, which ancient doctors linked to lethargy</note>, as well as lust, gluttony, and drunkenness.

# ADAMANTIUS

If, in addition to these traits, a tremor is present, such people are not far from epilepsy <note>original: "epilepsias" — in antiquity, this term referred to the "falling sickness," often interpreted as a sign of deep internal instability or divine affliction.</note>. If they are pale, they are cruel-minded, inhuman, cowardly, envious, and murderous. But if they are rather reddish and large, they become heavy drinkers, contentious, womanizers, and dog-lovers <note>To ancient physiognomists, "dog-lover" (philokynos) could imply a lack of human dignity or a shameless, "cynical" nature.</note>; they speak indecently, lack self-control, and are especially gluttonous. Eyes turned downward, or those that seem to sink, reveal the same as those turned upward, but more persuasively: they indicate a "sunken" nature and a wild, merciless wrath. If one eye moves upward while the other moves downward and they remain motionless—and if a tremor should be in them, the eyebrows knit together, and the breath is drawn short and fast—these people have reached the final stage of epilepsy. ~

->About Eyes Turned Aside:<-

Eyes that are turned aside, if they move toward the right, accuse the person of folly; if toward the left, of malice. But if they squint, inclining inward toward the nose, they are friends to the Graces, to Aphrodite, and to Love. If they are drier and wide open without any tremor, they are not inherently bad or wicked, yet they are not favored by the Graces or by love; rather, a sense of shame and justice has taken hold of them first. But if a tremor is present, they are villains and capable of any daring act. ~

->About Mottled Eyes:<-

Bright eyes <note>original: "charopoi" — see the previous page for the lion-like, amber quality of this term.</note> are distinguished from black eyes by their mottled <note>original: "aioloi" — meaning shimmering, variegated, or shifting in color.</note> quality, for their forms are many. Black eyes indicate a sharp perception or certain habits, yet also a love of gain and untrustworthiness. "Bright" eyes appear as follows: if a black color is prominent so that they look simple—not quite human in appearance—there are very red "millet seeds" <term>kenchroi</term> <note>These are small specks or pigment spots in the iris, likened to millet seeds by the author.</note> within them. Some are white or even whiter, others sallow, and others a sallow color mixed with red, or even black in addition to these. There are also blood-red specks within the black. It is clear that these eyes do not always bear these "millet-like" forms, but rather the whole circle of the eye has a black rim on the outside, with a reddish color beneath it. Others have these reddish parts appearing somewhat whiter, while in black eyes, the parts are not very red. For those in whom only the black is prominent from a distance, it signifies a noble character—lively, just, naturally gifted, and great. But for those whose red parts are turned very red and are not "millet-like," and a light shines within them like fire, and in addition sallow specks are mixed with the reddish ones, and others are blue-grey, or even blood-red or dark blue of many kinds...

# PHYSIOGNOMONICS. BOOK 1

...the pupils, these are the worst of all. Large eyes that are sparkling, moving, and staring—just as men stare when they are enraged—with their eyelids wide open, are the most wicked of all. For their character resembles that of wolves and wild boars; indeed, such people are even more cruel, more savage, and more predatory than these beasts. Those with glaring <note>original: "charopois" — a term often describing bright, piercing, or blue-gray eyes, frequently associated with lions or a spirited nature.</note> eyes, if the millet-seed spots <note>original: "kenchroi" — literally "millet seeds," referring to small, seed-like textures or granules visible in the iris.</note> are equal in size, produce characters that are more savage, more prone to anger, more bitter in their insolence, and more inclined toward adultery. If these spots are larger or smaller, they signal a milder nature. Blood-red spots among the black parts of the eye reveal people prone to poisoning <note>original: "pharmakōdeis" — this can refer to someone who uses drugs, poisons, or sorcery.</note>, just as pale yellow spots reveal those who are skilled in sorcery and the use of magic charms. Those with pale yellow spots are more cowardly, while those with blood-red spots are more thoughtless and hot-tempered. For those who do not have millet-seed spots in their glaring eyes, but rather a variegated rim <note>original: "itys" — literally the "rim" or "felloe" of a wheel; here referring to the outer edge of the iris or the iris itself.</note>, they are more deceitful. If it is blood-red, consider the size and the color of the rim: a narrow black rim with another reddish one on top of it, in moist eyes—provided no other strange sign appears—reveals a man who is high-minded, intelligent, just, naturally gifted, and at times a lover of youths <note>original: "paiderastēn" — in the context of ancient physiognomy, this was often categorized as a specific behavioral trait alongside other social or moral qualities.</note>. However, a green rim on top of a black one indicates a deceitful, unjust man, a thief of money, and one who consorts shamefully with women. Shimmering rims, similar in color to rainbows, reveal the small-minded if the eyes are dry; but if the eyes are moist, they indicate high-mindedness, eloquence, courage, and uprightness <note>original: "orthodoxian" — here meaning right-thinking or sound judgment rather than religious "orthodoxy."</note>. Such people are also quicker to anger and are easily excited by the works of Aphrodite <note>A poetic way of referring to sexual desire or romantic pursuits.</note>.

### On Sunken Eyes

Sunken eyes are not at all to be praised. Those which are sunken but move like water in a vessel and are large are not wicked, unless some other sign should accuse them; for the moisture and the size compensate for the sunkenness. For a sunken and small eye indicates a deceitful character, plotting against men, and wasted away by zeal and envy. If they are drier in addition to these aforementioned evils, they signal faithlessness and treachery. Those eyes that seem to "flow down" <note>meaning downcast or drooping eyes.</note> are more deceitful; however, if they should flow down along with moisture, they indicate a more foolish nature.

### On Bulging Eyes

Regarding bulging eyes, which are not to be praised: some are surrounded by a mark like a circle, while others have a deep and narrow periphery like a trench; these indicate turbulence and jealousy. Those eyes that are raised up accuse the person of being small-minded...

# PHYSIOGNOMONICS. BOOK 1

...bloodshot eyes belong to those who are fond of wine and are gluttonous. If these eyes are bluish-gray <note>original: "glaukoi" — a term for light-colored eyes, ranging from blue to gray or sea-green, often considered "staring" or "fierce" in ancient physiognomy.</note>, the individuals are unjust, yet they tend to escape punishment. If their eyelids weigh heavily upon them, it indicates a greater degree of folly. If the eyes are swollen and small, such people are killers of fathers, mothers, and children, as well as poisoners <note>original: "pharmakeis" — can refer to those who deal in poisons, drugs, or harmful sorcery.</note>. If, however, the eyes are set high, being large, bright, and clear, with a moist or shining gaze, they belong to the just, those who are like the gracious gods, lovers of learning, and full of love—just as the philosopher Socrates was. Eyes that protrude far from the face, being very small and reddish, indicate that the body and the tongue are not sound of mind, but rather discordant and prone to error.

### On quivering eyes

**S**mall quivering eyes belong to the malicious and deceitful; but large ones belong to the senseless and those convicted of adultery. Those whose eyes quiver as if leaping up are wicked, unless the eyes have a good size and a moist, bright gaze. For eyes of that specific type are naturally observant of lofty things and bold in great deeds, reaching the peak of daring and high-mindedness; yet they are also uncontrollable in anger and drunkenness, boastful, light-minded, and not far from epilepsy. Such people desire to seize more than is humanly possible, just as Alexander the Macedonian did. When these quivering eyes are frequent in their movement, the people are unloving, cruel-minded, gluttonous, lewd, devoted to the pleasures of Aphrodite <note>original: "kythērioi" — literally "Cytherean," referring to the island of Cythera, a cult center for the goddess of love.</note>, and easily overcome by the music of the flute and song. You must judge other traits by comparing them with other physical signs. Livid <note>original: "plidnoi" — dark, lead-colored, or discolored.</note> eyes, if the other signs agree, indicate the untrustworthy, the unjust, and those who will dare anything.

### On squinting eyes

**U**ntrustworthiness dwells within squinting eyes; if they move with a dry appearance, they are even more untrustworthy. If they are also small, they belong to the deceitful, the treacherous, the mischievous, and the fickle. Squinting eyes that are moist and of sufficient size reveal a sensitive person, a thinker, an observer, one who learns easily, and one who is cautious, bordering on stinginess. Those eyes that are misty or cloudy are deceitful, untrustworthy, and unrestrained.

### On dimly lit eyes

**T**he opposite of these are eyes that are somewhat bright <note>original: "upheggeis" — literally "under-shining" or having a moderate light.</note>; these are the best for those prone to eye-disease, unless some other sign contradicts this. For one must examine every detail with accuracy.

### On sparkling eyes

**I**f the eyes sparkle and have a light within them, they are not a good sign. For a sparkle, if it occurs in bluish-gray or bloodshot eyes, indicates a hot-headed nature and a total recklessness that comes close to madness. If...
