# PHYSIOGNOMONICS. BOOK 1

...if the eyes are bright-gray <note>original: "charopois" — a shimmering blue-gray or amber color often associated with lions; in ancient physiognomy, it was frequently linked to a fierce or predatory nature.</note>, it indicates cowardice, for such people fear every thing; they will collapse in terror and are suspicious of everyone. Dark eyes that are not steady are the worst of all: they are cowardly and extremely mischievous. If such eyes also appear to be laughing, the individuals have reached the very height of malice. Eyes with a fierce gaze are dangerous. 

Those with a "moist" or shimmering gaze are high-spirited, brave, constantly restless, hard to persuade, quick-moving, reckless, and unyielding—yet they are without malice. If, however, the gaze is "dry," they are the worst sort of people and lawless. If the eyes are also smaller and hollow, they are malicious; they are not foolish, but rather plotting, secretive, and meddling in everyone's business. 

If beneath such eyes there is a heavy brow <note>original: "episkēnion" — the fleshy part of the forehead or upper eyelid that hangs over the eye, often likened to a "canopy."</note> along with thick eyebrows and straight eyelids, it signifies bravery combined with good judgment. But for those whose brow, eyebrows, and eyelids are not shaped this way, but whose gaze is quick and bitter, they possess the most wicked appearance of all.

->**On Laughing Eyes**<-

Eyes in which pleasure and laughter seem to dwell are not entirely blameless. For among such people are the deceitful, the secretive, the mischievous, and the criminal. When they look askance <note>original: "hypobleponsi" — to look from under the brows or out of the corner of the eye, often implying suspicion or craftiness.</note> while laughing and their eyes are "dry," they have the most wicked eyes of all. Hollow eyes that hold a laughter like a destructive fire are crafting some evil. 

If the areas around the eyes move—such as the forehead, the cheeks, the eyebrows, or the lips—this is an "evil laughter." For it signifies a wicked plan or an unjust intention; in these cases, the person squints while grinning and stares intently, plotting unjust deeds. Those who have their eyes wide open or grinning are not entirely lawless in their actions; but if they laugh loudly, you might predict a plotting nature and a greater tendency toward crime, especially if the eyes are dry. 

If the eyes are moist, the character is harmless, though they appear vain, unaffected by the suffering of others, senseless, unloving, and undisciplined. Smiling eyes accompanied by moisture, relaxed eyelids, and a soft forehead in the temple region reveal a character that is magnificent, just, gentle, pious, hospitable, intelligent, wise in counsel, and full of love.

->**On Gloomy Eyes**<-

Eyes that appear gloomy or sullen <note>original: "skythrōpazontes" — a word describing a downcast, frowning, or somber expression.</note> while also being moist belong to those who are thoughtful and skilled in the arts. If the eyebrows meet as if relaxed, and the forehead is also relaxed despite the gloominess, it indicates a character that may be prone to mistakes but is ultimately stable, good, wise, and prudent. But dry eyes with a sorrowful look are full of ruin <note>original: "atēs" — referring to "Até," the Greek personification of ruin, delusion, and reckless folly.</note>. If the forehead is also thick and the gaze...

...intense, and the eyelids are held straight; these individuals possess wild intentions. They would perform any deed, and no evil is far from them.

## On Eyes that Close

Those eyes that close frequently and then open halfway again belong to the treacherous and the thievish. If, however, they appear moist, they indicate deep thinkers and those fond of the arts. If there is a trembling in them, together with a pale complexion, they signify foolishness or epilepsy. For those whose gaze is always constant, if the eyes should close in a certain way and seem to flow down <note>original: "aporrheoien" — likely referring to a "melting" or drooping look often associated with desire or lack of focus.</note> and move toward one another, they accuse the person of lewdness and wickedness. 

But if they should close while being held straight, and if they are not moist but large and lacking luster, and the forehead is smooth, such people are cautious, well-advised, studious, sweet-tempered, and romantic. If they are dry, they are bold, mischievous, and unjust. The person who has a thick forehead, distorted eyebrows, and stiff, fixed eyelids is more savage in spirit; "hope" sits upon his forehead <note>original: "elpis epi metōpou" — an idiomatic expression in Greek physiognomy suggesting a visible air of expectation, forwardness, or perhaps arrogance in the brow.</note>, yet he is softened by praise and honors, and can be persuaded by gifts. Those who are unable to keep their eyelids straight or their eyebrows steady, but instead have a trembling and a gaze that shifts slightly, are effeminate <note>original: "androgynoi" — literally "man-woman"; in this context, it refers to men who lack the "firmness" of character expected in ancient gender roles</note> and are forced into a state of shame.

## On Eyes that are Wide Open

Eyes that open wide frequently and remain fixed belong either to those who are easily startled or those with great minds. You must dictate how one should interpret their meaning—whether they are dry, moist, dark, bright, small, large, hollow, high, soft, intense, or other such qualities as have been mentioned many times. By remembering these, you may pursue the study of physiognomy even regarding things not yet discussed, thinking it through for yourself. 

Those eyes that are always open, remaining dark and moist, belong to thinkers. If they look like a horse's eyes <note>original: "ippon blepoien" — the ancient Greeks frequently compared human features to animals (the lion, the ox, the horse) to categorize personality types.</note>, they tend toward being good. Eyes that are spread wide, dry, and unblinking, looking with a cheerful and bright gaze, signify shamelessness and total recklessness.

## On Blinking and Unblinking Eyes

Blinking eyes are accusers of cowardice. If they are also dry, they are plotting against someone, or hiding a trick, or secretly preparing some evil. These same eyes, if distorted or pale, bear witness to foolishness. Unblinking eyes that gaze terribly are likely to be devising some evil. But if they are gentle and steady, or perhaps feeling a touch of moisture...

<meta>This page continues Adamantius's detailed physical catalog of the eyes, concluding the first book and beginning the second. The text transitions from discussing the blinking and shifting of the eyelids to a general methodology of physiognomy, emphasizing that no single sign should be used for judgment without considering the eyes first.</meta>

...intellectuals, those fond of learning, those possessing gentle characters, and the romantic. If, however, while remaining unblinking, the eyes appear pale or reddish along with a certain dryness, they reveal a quickness of temper <note>original: "attō" — to dart or rush; here meaning a volatile or impulsive nature</note>, a sluggish memory, a tendency toward wrath, and a projection of envy, or they reveal some uncanny <note>original: "daimonian" — can mean divine, but in this context refers to an extreme or supernatural level of malice</note> wickedness. If, being of this sort, the eyes should roll within themselves, they are gripped by a powerful madness.

->On the bags under the eyes<-

A bag <note>term: "kystis" — literally a bladder or pouch; refers to swelling or "bags" under the eyes</note> protruding from below the eyes indicates drunkards; if above, it indicates those prone to sleep; if both are present, they signify both traits.

->On sharp eyes<-

Sharp eyes indicate the turbulent and the rapacious. Those who squint <note>original: "illōptontes" — to look askance or peer with half-closed eyes</note> are modest. Those who lower one of the eyelids and pull it up while the eye is moist, looking mildly and softly, are refined, dandies, and adulterous. But if they should draw down the middle of the eyelids and pull up the corners while squinting, you would not be wrong to call such a person an adulterer. Likewise, it is the same if they pull up the middle of the eyelids while closing the corners.

# ADAMANTIUS THE SOPHIST'S
## ON PHYSIOGNOMY. BOOK II.

One must practice physiognomy <note>term: "physiognomy" — the ancient art of judging a person's character or future from their physical features, particularly the face</note> by examining individual parts and members, and by each complexion, movements, breaths, voices, and whatever else accompanies these. One must know, however, that it is not right to think one can give a firm judgment from a single sign on its own, nor even from two; rather, one must judge from more and greater signs, when all are in agreement with one another. 

The other signs should be compared against those found in the eyes, for these are the dominant features. For if the external signs agree with the eyes, the physiognomic reading will be unerring. Among the other signs, the most powerful are those near the eyes, such as the forehead, nose, mouth, cheeks, and head. Second are those around the neck and chest. Third are the...

<summary>This page concludes the study of eye movements and bags, linking them to traits like alcoholism and adultery, before transitioning to Book II, which establishes the eyes as the primary evidence in physiognomic judgment.</summary>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues from the opening of Book II of Adamantius’s treatise. Having finished the specific examination of the eyes, the author now moves toward a synthetic approach, looking at the body as a whole and introducing the core methodology of physiognomy: the comparison of human features to those of animals and the distinction between male and female "types."</meta>

->ADAMANTIUS<-

...around the shoulders, the hands, the legs, and the feet. Finally, one must consider the parts around the belly. The greatest factor for a final judgment <note>original: "epikrisin" — a judicial decision or final assessment</note> seems to me to be the overall appearance <note>term: "epiprepeia" — the general "fittingness" or outward impression of the whole person</note> of the man; one must look upon this as the seal that confirms all the other signs. On its own, a single part does not possess its own logic; rather, the individual signs—both those in the eyes and the others—together compose the entire form of the person. For when all these traits are gathered together, then the reliability of the diagnosis becomes clear.

The greatest points of distinction and the most obvious signs concern that which resembles the male, that which resembles the female, and those traits that resemble other animals. For the characters <note>original: "ēthē" — the habitual moral character or disposition</note> of each of the wild beasts are made manifest in their forms. Insofar as a man’s character resembles those beasts, his form will also be similar. For example, the character of a **lion** is high-spirited and valiant, and its form is likewise. The **leopard** is delicate <note>original: "habron" — luxurious, dainty, or refined; often used disparagingly of men</note>, irascible, prone to ambushing and treachery, yet clearly also bold; its shape corresponds to these traits. The **bear** is savage-minded, crafty, and clumsy. In other animals, their own particular nature is equally apparent. In the **wild boar**, there is a senseless rage. In the **ox**, a sense of dignity and guilelessness. In the **horse**, boastfulness and ambition. In the **fox**, craftiness and a plotting nature. In the **ape**, buffoonery <note>original: "bōmolochikon" — literally one who waits by the altar to steal meat; a vulgar jester</note> and mockery. In the **sheep**, a simple-mindedness. In the **starling**, a frantic greed. In the **swine**, filth and gluttony. Likewise, the habits of other reptiles and birds resemble their inward characters.

Therefore, if a man has some part or limb that resembles a beast or a bird, he must be judged by the standards of physiognomy belonging to that creature. If he has gleaming eyes <note>original: "charopō" — often used of lions; a bright, fierce, or amber-colored eye</note> that are moderately hollow, remember the **lion**. If the eyes are very hollow and constantly shifting, consider the **ape**. If they are flat and level, call such eyes **ox-like**. If the eyes are wide and dull <note>original: "palas" — likely referring to a broad, flat, or lackluster stare</note>, they indicate a senseless and insolent person, just like a **donkey**.

In most respects, the male types are superior to the female. The **male** is noble, fearless, just, high-spirited, ambitious, and guileless. The **female** is ignoble, bitter, crafty, light-minded <note>original: "kouphonoön" — literally "light-minded" or flighty</note>, unjust, contentious, and "bold-cowardly" <note>note: "thrasydeilon" refers to someone who is rash or aggressive when safe but turns cowardly when faced with real danger</note>. As a general rule, the female has a smaller head than the male, a smaller body, softer hair, narrower ribs, and eyes that are sparkling or glittering. She has a thinner neck, a weaker chest, and lacks prominent ribs; her hips and thighs are fleshier, her calves are thinner, and the extremities of her hands and feet are more delicate. Her entire physical appearance is softer, more slack, more dainty, characterized by moist flesh, less distinct joints, and wider loins...

<sig>A</sig>

<summary>Adamantius explains that a final character judgment must rely on the "seal" of a person's overall appearance rather than isolated signs. He introduces the practice of comparing human features to animal traits (lion, leopard, ape, etc.) and establishes a physical and moral dichotomy between male and female types.</summary>
<keywords>physiognomy, animal symbolism, male vs female traits, diagnostic methodology, Adamantius the Sophist, character analysis</keywords>

--- Page 216 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "male" and "female" types in nature, which the author uses as a foundational binary for interpreting human character. The previous page established that certain animals embody specific temperaments (like the lion’s courage or the leopard’s treachery); here, the author extends that logic to birds, reptiles, and specific physical features like nails and feet.</meta>

->PHYSIOGNOMY. BOOK II.<-

...is stronger, the stride is smaller and more frequent, the limbs bend more easily, and the movement is more fluid <note>original: "hygrotera" — literally "moister," but in a physiological sense referring to suppleness or grace associated with the "feminine" temperament</note>. The male, however, carries the opposite of these traits. Therefore, among all the wild beasts, the **lion** is the one that partakes most in the male form. The **leopard** <note>original: "pardalis" — likely referring to a panther or leopard</note> is the most female-formed. Among birds, **eagles** are masculine in appearance, while the **partridge** is female-formed. Among the various kinds of reptiles, the **serpent** <note>original: "drakōn" — a large snake or dragon-like creature</note> is the most masculine, while the **viper** is female-formed.

### Concerning those who are eunuchs from birth

<margin>E</margin>For those who are eunuchs by nature, the signs are worse than for other men. For the most part, they are cruel-minded, deceitful, and prone to mischief, though some are more so than others. Among those eunuchs who have been castrated <note>original: "tmēthentōn" — literally "the cut ones"</note>, some of their character changes at the moment of the operation; however, in most cases, the signs of their innate <note>original: "syngenous" — inborn or kindred</note> nature remain.

### Concerning nails

<margin>N</margin>Nails that are broad, white, or slightly yellowish-red <note>original: "hypoxanthoi" — a ruddy or golden-white hue</note> indicate a man of natural talent and good character. Those that are narrow, long, and curved suggest a person who is insensitive <note>original: "anaisthētou" — lacking in perception or feeling</note> and beast-like. Nails that are extremely hooked or crooked indicate a shameless person and a predator. Those that grow closely into the flesh are, in most cases, a sign of an insensitive and beastly nature. Very small nails are a sign of unscrupulous cunning <note>term: "panourgia" — a "do-anything" cleverness used for villainy</note>. Likewise, nails that are pale, black, or rough carry negative meanings. Those that are very round are a sign of the lustful. As I have told you before, most of these individual signs are not worth much consideration if one only looks at them in isolation—and this is especially true of the nails.

### Concerning toes

<margin>T</margin>Feet that have toes joined together <note>original: "sumphueis" — webbed or growing close together</note> are swine-like and small. If the toes are crowded or compressed together, it indicates people who are villainous, malicious, and lovers of money. When they are very small and thin, they reveal a rather foolish disposition. Toes that are stumpy <note>original: "koloboi" — shortened or mutilated in appearance</note> and thick belong to the daring, the disobedient, and the beast-like. Those that are very long and thin also suggest a lack of intelligence. Those who have large and proportionate toes are the best.

### Concerning feet

<margin>F</margin>Feet that are sinewy and well-jointed indicate noble and brave characters. When they are soft and more fleshy, they produce softer characters. Stumpy and thick feet are a sign of a beastly nature. Feet that are very long belong to busybodies and malicious schemers. Small feet indicate a villainous person. Arched feet, which are hollow underneath, are a bad sign. Similarly, those who have feet that are flat <note>original: "homala" — smooth or level</note> on the bottom and who walk as if on their ankles are unscrupulous.

<sig>A ii</sig>

The text emphasizes that while individual parts like nails offer clues, they must be interpreted as part of a whole.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the physiognomic analysis of the human body, moving from the extremities (nails and feet mentioned on the previous page) upward to the ankles, shins, knees, back, and torso. Adamantius continues to categorize physical traits as either "masculine" (associated with courage and nobility) or "feminine" (associated with cowardice or weakness).</meta>

->ADAMANTIUS<-

### Concerning ankles

Well-defined ankles indicate a noble man, whereas those that are soft and smooth suggest a less manly character. Very thin ankles belong to the cowardly and the unrestrained <note>original: "akolastou" — one who lacks self-control, often in a physical or moral sense</note>. Those who possess thick ankles, rough heels, fleshy feet, short, stumpy toes, and thick shins are, for the most part, prone to madness or folly.

### Concerning shins and thighs

Shins that are of a good size, well-jointed, and firm indicate a noble and naturally gifted man. Shins that are soft and lack visible joints belong to the more cowardly and less manly. Very thin shins indicate a cowardly and malicious person; if they are also sinewy, they suggest one who partakes in much unrestrained behavior. Those whose shins are full in the middle, appearing swollen <note>original: "hōs kyousai" — literally "as if pregnant," describing a bulging or swollen calf muscle</note>, reveal a disgusting, licentious, and shameless nature. Generally, thickness in the shins and heels suggests a slavish and ignorant disposition. You may say the same regarding the thighs as you do for the shins.

<margin>O</margin>
### Concerning knees and hips

Those whose knees turn inward, as if knocking together, are feminine and effeminate. Thick, fleshy hips are feminine, while bony hips are manly. Thin, meager, and shrunken hips—appearing as if they have wasted away—belong to a villainous man. Indeed, these are the traits of monkeys and apes.

<margin>K</margin>
### Concerning the loins

A bony and strong lower back <note>original: "osphys" — the loins or lumbar region</note> indicates manliness. A fleshy and soft one is feminine. A lower back that comes to a sharp point accuses the person of both unrestrained behavior and cowardice.

### Concerning the back

A broad and firm back makes for a noble and high-spirited man. The opposite traits, however, suggest the opposite character.

<margin>E</margin>
### Concerning hunching

A hunched man is not of good character, unless he possesses supple limbs <note>original: "hygromeleia" — a state of being "moist" or flexible in the joints, often seen as a mitigating positive trait</note> and other fine signs are present. There is, however, one animal—the crane <note>original: "ainoei" is likely a corruption of "geranos" (crane), though the text literally says "praises the truth-loving one"</note>—which is praised for its love of truth despite its form.

### Concerning the ribs and the belly

Thin and weak ribs signify unmanliness and cowardice. Very hard and fleshy ribs signify ignorance. Ribs that are rounded and full, appearing swollen, signify babbling and malice. Ribs that are very lean and "empty" signify cowardice, malice, and gluttony. On the other hand, lean bellies signify strength of soul and high-mindedness...

<summary>This page catalogs the physiognomic meanings of the lower body and torso, equating firm, bony features with nobility and soft, fleshy, or "swollen" features with cowardice or vice.</summary>
<keywords>physiognomy, ankles, shins, knees, hips, loins, back, ribs, belly, masculinity, femininity, character analysis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the physiognomic analysis of the human body, moving from the lower torso to the chest, shoulders, and arms. The text maintains the systematic approach of Adamantius, attributing specific personality traits—ranging from nobility and quick-wittedness to malice and gluttony—to physical proportions and textures.</meta>

# PHYSIOGNOMICS. BOOK II.

...indicate. Large, fleshy bellies, if they are soft and hanging, show insensitivity, drunkenness, and a lack of restraint <note>original: "akolasian" — often referring to a lack of moral or physical discipline</note>; but if they are hard and packed with firm flesh, they reveal gluttony and cunning.

->Concerning the chest, the breasts, and the midriff.<-

When the area measured from the navel to the top of the chest is found to be larger than the area from there to the base of the neck, such individuals become voracious and gluttonous. Large, masculine chests are praiseworthy, while thin and weak chests indicate small-mindedness and shamelessness. Very fleshy chests suggest a lack of sympathy and a certain clumsiness <note>original: "skaiotēta" — can mean awkwardness, rudeness, or a left-handed/clumsy nature</note>. Those whose breasts are hanging and whose entire chest is covered in loose flesh are lustful and prone to drunkenness. A strong upper back <note>original: "metaphrenon" — the region between the shoulder blades</note> is best. A weak and thin upper back suggests cowardice and small-mindedness. When the flesh on the upper back is excessive, it accuses one of insensitivity, and when it is thin, of folly. A broad upper back is a sign of magnanimity <note>original: "megalonoias" — literally "greatness of mind"</note>, while a gently rounded one is a sign of quick-wittedness and grace. He who carries a hunched upper back, with the shoulders bent forward toward the chest, is malicious and spiteful <note>original: "baskanos" — often used to describe one who is envious or possesses the 'evil eye'</note>. If the entire body is bent forward, the person is stingy and money-loving.

->Concerning the collarbones and shoulders.<-

Collarbones that are closed in toward each other indicate a person who is neither sensitive nor capable in action. Those that are set wide apart indicate the shameless. A well-proportioned opening between them indicates a vital and brave nature. Thick shoulders are not praiseworthy. Strong shoulders belong to those of brave character. Slack shoulders belong to the unmanly and the cowardly. Those with thin shoulders that are sharp at the peaks are malicious; if they are entirely fleshless, they indicate fools.

->Concerning the upper arms, forearms, and hands.<-

If the upper arms are so long that the hands reach down to the knees when hanging, it is a sign of inaction <note>original: "apraxias" — though in some contexts long arms were seen as a sign of kingship, here it may imply a lack of manual labor or a specific temperament</note> and strength. If they are short <note>original: "kolobai" — stunted or docked</note>, so that they do not reach the knees and the person is forced to bring their head down to their hands to eat, it reveals those who are ill-willed, spiteful, and envious. It is best for the upper arms and forearms to be strong and the elbows to be well-jointed. Sharper elbows indicate the shameless, while very fleshy ones indicate the ignorant and insensitive. Soft and gentle hands belong to a naturally gifted man. Hard hands, when combined with great size, [indicate...]

<meta>This page continues Adamantius’s systematic physiognomic study, concluding the analysis of the hands and fingers before moving into a detailed examination of the neck (trachēlos). The text links physical proportions—such as the length of fingers or the stiffness of the neck—to specific moral failings or intellectual capacities, following the Aristotelian tradition of reading the soul through the body.</meta>

# ADAMANTIUS

...indicate a shameful person and one who is slow to learn. Very short <note>original: "kolobai" — stunted or docked</note> hands are a sign of folly. If they are thick, they also indicate small-mindedness. Narrow and thin hands are extremely predatory, while thick hands with short fingers are most prone to theft. Very small hands are a sign of villainy and thievery. From thin and crooked hands, infer nonsense and gluttony.

---

->Concerning the neck.<-

A neck that is very long and thin belongs to cowards and the malicious. One that is both thick and long belongs to the hot-tempered, the boastful, and the arrogant. One of moderate length and thickness, well-built and strong, belongs to the brave, the quick-to-learn, and those naturally gifted toward virtue. A sunken and weak neck belongs to villains and the over-refined. Those whose neck is strained with prominent tendons are clumsy <note>original: "skaioi" — can mean awkward, socially rude, or unlucky</note>. If other signs are present, they are subject to monthly fits <note>original: "mēnaioisi" — likely referring to the lunar cycle or epilepsy</note> and madness. Those with large, visible, intertwined veins on the neck are also clumsy. Those who are naturally thick-necked are slow to act, clumsy, ignorant, and sluggish. Those with stunted necks are "bold-cowards" <note>original: "thrasydeiloi" — people who act tough or reckless to hide their fear</note>. When the area at the base of the neck where it meets the shoulder blades protrudes significantly, these people are insolent.

The stiff-necked are ignorant. A rough-textured neck, like a crest <note>original: "lephia" — a ridge or mane</note>, indicates ignorance combined with insolence. A rigid, unbending neck suggests clumsiness, arrogance, and ignorance. Such unbending necks also occur because of folly; such people, being shameful and effeminate <note>original: "aischrogynoi"</note>, then try to stand straight and think they are hiding their lewdness <note>original: "machlosynēn" — sexual profligacy or lust</note>. But they are betrayed by the twitching of their lips, the rolling of their eyes, the stumbling of their feet, the movement of their hips, the flailing of their hands, and the shouting of their voices. Their necks cannot stay firmly fixed but tremble because the posture is forced. A bent or drooping neck is usually the sign of a shameful man, especially if other signs agree. An upward-turned neck belongs to the insolent, the mindless, and the empty-headed, unless this is done out of mere physical weakness. A neck bent forward can indicate folly, but sometimes also deep thought—

<summary>This page catalogs how the proportions and movements of the hands and neck reveal character flaws like gluttony, cowardice, and "shameful effeminacy." It particularly emphasizes how a rigid neck may be a failed attempt to hide a lustful nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Adamantius’s analysis of the neck (trachēlos) and moves into the features of the lower face, specifically the jaw (genys) and the mouth (stoma). The author continues to use animal analogies—the lion, the dog, the snake, and the pig—to correlate physical traits with internal character.</meta>

# PHYSIOGNOMICS. BOOK II.

...stiff nature, as well as a bold, bright, and malicious one. On the other hand, a neck that inclines toward the outside <note>original: "exō ōtotētos" — likely referring to a neck that stands out or is held with a certain air</note> signifies cheerfulness and a sense of refinement. A neck that looks toward the right belongs to a person who is orderly, thoughtful, and prudent. One that leans toward the left belongs to the lewd and the foolish. If it is slightly inclined to either side, it is not a good sign, for it shows the mind <term>phrenas</term> <note>the "phrenes" were considered the seat of the intellect and emotions</note> has been damaged. A rough throat belongs to a light-minded man, a chatterer, and a loudmouth. If the laryngeal prominence <note>original: "bronchou spondylō" — the "vertebra of the throat," or the Adam's apple</note> protrudes, he is light-minded, though not bold; such men conceive lofty thoughts in their speech, but when they have been drinking, they are known to be mournful, suspicious, quick-tempered, and annoying drinking companions.

### Concerning jaws and lips.

Those with long jaws are not entirely bad, but they are more talkative than necessary and somewhat prone to empty boasting. Those with small jaws are bad in every way, especially cruel and treacherous; for snakes are like this. If the lower jaw is described by a very round shape, it is unmanly and feminine. A somewhat square jaw is masculine. As for the lower jaw with a split tip, so as to be "double-peaked" <note>a dimpled or cleft chin</note>, if the split is very large, it signifies a treacherous person. But if the division is moderate, the sign relates to love and the Graces <note>the Charites, goddesses of charm and beauty</note>. Thin lips, where the middle is larger and the area around the lips is such that the lower parts seem to recede, show magnanimity and courage, for such are the lips of lions. However, very thin lips on small mouths are a sign of the cowardly and the deceitful.

The author praises a mouth that is neither very flat nor protruding. For the protruding mouth signifies folly, idle talk, and recklessness. A flat mouth is a sign of cowardice and fickleness. A small mouth resembles feminine character and appearance, while a larger one is masculine. A very wide opening of the mouth <note>original: "anatomē stomatos" — the gape or cut of the mouth</note> indicates one who is gluttonous, cruel-minded, foolish, and unholy; for such are the mouths of dogs. Those whose lips peak at the canine teeth are ill-tempered, insolent, loud shouters, and abusive; for such are the mouths of dogs. Those whose entire mouth is very wide, and whose lips are thick, round, and turned outward, are swine-like <note>original: "hyeis" (emended) — relating to pigs/swine</note> in their character and deeds. An upper lip that overlaps the lower indicates a more sensible person. If the lower lip protrudes, the character is ignorant, mostly vain, and rather foolish. A small mouth protruding outward belongs to the slanderous and the treacherous. A hollow mouth, as if pitted or sunken, belongs to the envious and malicious, and especially the licentious.

<summary>This page details the physiognomic significance of the neck's posture, the jaw's shape, and the mouth's structure, often comparing human features to those of lions, dogs, and snakes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the physiognomic treatise of Adamantius, moving from the structure of the jaw and mouth into a detailed analysis of the nose, forehead, cheeks, and the overall face. The author continues to link physical proportions to specific character traits, often using the "physiognomic principle" of comparing human features to those of animals like lions, dogs, and birds.</meta>

# ADAMANTIUS

->Concerning the nose<-

**A** thin tip of the nose indicates a tendency toward anger, but one that is very thick and low down <note>original: "chamalon" — meaning flat or set low on the face</note> suggests a certain softness or a slavish nature. If you should see a nose tip that is large, blunt, round, and strong, it bears witness to bravery and great pride in those who possess it; look for this in the lion and in noble dogs. If it is long and very thin, it is bird-like; expect the character to be such as well. If the nose is well-proportioned in relation to the face, it signifies a noble, brave, and wise person. The opposite of this is feminine and more ignorant. Straightness of the nose suggests an inability to control the tongue. Where the nose is larger, it is better, but one that is very small suggests a tendency toward theft and indecision. Hooked noses <note>aquiline or "eagle-like"</note> befit magnanimity <term>megalanoia</term> <note>literally "greatness of mind" or high-mindedness</note>, but snub noses <note>original: "simois" — upturned or flat noses</note> signify lust. Consider wide-open nostrils to be a witness of spirit and strength, but those that are narrow, round, and closed off are signs of stupidity. Where the nostrils are very crooked, the thoughts are also very crooked, for thus is a person's nature cross-examined.

->Concerning the forehead<-

**A** narrow forehead is no small sign of ignorance, and a hairy one indicates sluggishness as a general rule. A long forehead shows sensitivity and a readiness to learn. Do not praise a forehead that is very low, for such belong to the unmanly; nor one that is convex, high, and rounded, for it signifies insensitivity and shamelessness. Do not rejoice at a rough forehead, nor one that has ridges and hollows, for all such things are indicators of villainy and distrust, and sometimes of stupidity and madness, alongside other fearful signs. A square forehead, well-sized and in proportion to the whole appearance, is judged best for bravery, wisdom, and magnanimity. Those with overhanging foreheads are stubborn; those whose foreheads are turned back are carefree; those with wrinkled foreheads <note>original: "rhysta" — flowing or lined</note> are worriers.

->Concerning the cheeks and the face<-

**S**ay that fleshy cheeks belong to laziness and drunkenness. But in those whose cheeks are excessively thin, there is malice and villainy; those whose thick jaws are set far apart from the eyes are envious. Round cheeks belong to the deceitful, while those that are excessively long belong to babblers and those who speak vanities. A person's whole face, if it is fleshy, belongs to one who is pleasure-loving and possesses a youthful modesty; if it is thin, it belongs to a worrier and a schemer. Small faces indicate small characters, while those that are excessively large suggest stupidity, and

<summary>This page details how the shapes of the nose, forehead, and cheeks reveal a person's inner character, from the "lion-like" bravery of a strong nose to the "bird-like" nature of a thin one. It emphasizes the importance of proportion and uses animal archetypes to categorize human behavior.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the physiognomic treatise attributed to Adamantius, picking up from the discussion of facial features begun on the previous page. The text continues to categorize human character through physical traits, concluding the general facial analysis before moving to the ears, the head, and the influence of climate and ethnicity on appearance.</meta>

# PHYSIOGNOMONICS. BOOK II.

...and it charges them with ignorance. Magnificence, lack of generosity <term>aneleutheria</term> <note>original: "aneleutheria" — meaning a stingy or servile spirit</note>, and ill-will are especially evident in the face. For there is also the "painful" face, the senseless and full face, the educated face, and the face that is quick to learn or ignorant, wailing, sleepy, or wakeful, and all other such things that nature possesses where they are most visible. By whatever emotion a person's face resembles, according to that one must judge their character. Spasms of the face in those who are otherwise gentle, if they occur under frequent gazes, reveal madness or folly; but if they occur during cheerful expressions, they reveal wickedness.

### Concerning the Ears:

Those with large ears are insensitive <note>original: "anaisthētoi" — lacking in perception or dull-witted</note>. Those with small ears are ill-natured and cunning. Those whose ears are excessively small, appearing as if they have been cropped, are fools. Those who have ears that are plugged <note>perhaps meaning narrow or lacking definition</note> but of sufficient size are likewise insensitive. Those who have ears that are well-carved <note>well-defined or shapely</note> are quick to learn and intelligent, while those with ears that lack definition are ignorant.

### Concerning the Head:

A very small head signifies an insensitive and senseless person. One that is larger than the symmetrical ideal signifies an insensitive, common <note>original: "agoraion" — meaning vulgar or of the marketplace</note>, or sometimes a magnificent person. One that is asymmetrical is insensitive, unrefined, and entirely lacking in generosity. A very large head indicates one who is slow to perceive and ignorant. Those who have slanted heads are shameful and shameless. Those with very high heads are stubborn. Those who have the back of the head flat <note>original: "lachamala" — low or level</note> are spiritless <note>original: "apsychoi" — cowardly or lacking "soul"</note>. Those who have the area in front of the crown of the head hollow are deceitful and spiteful. A head that is moderately flat and of medium size and uprightness is the best of all for both sensitivity and greatness of mind <term>megalonoia</term> <note>high-mindedness or noble intelligence</note>.

### Concerning Complexion and Hair:

The signs derived from hair or complexion are not self-sufficient for the practice of physiognomy. Therefore, it is not easy to declare a person's character based solely on their nation, or even which nation they are from. This is especially true because of the mixing of nations with one another; Syrians are found in Italy, Libyans in Thrace, and others are scattered elsewhere. As a general rule, those who live under the Bear <note>original: "hypo tē arktō" — referring to the North Star, meaning Northern peoples</note> are tall, blonde, white-skinned in their hair <note>very fair-haired</note>, soft-haired, grey-eyed, snub-nosed, thick-legged, numerous, with loose flesh, matted hair, simple, spirited, short-sighted in counsel, hot-headed, and slow to learn. Those who live under the midday sun <note>original: "hypo tē mesēmbria" — the South</note> are black-haired, curly-haired, black-eyed, thin-legged, ignorant, of many opinions, and light-minded...

<sig>B</sig>

<vocab>Physiognomonica, Aristotle, ears, head, complexion, hair, folly, ignorance</vocab>

<summary>This page details how the size and shape of the ears and head reveal intelligence or malice, and introduces the concept of environmental physiognomy, contrasting Northern and Southern peoples.</summary>
<keywords>ears, head shape, intelligence, ethnicity, Northern peoples, Southern peoples, Adamantius, temperament</keywords>

--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 100 regarding how geography and climate influence human character and physical appearance. The previous page ended with a discussion on facial symmetry and the shape of the head; this page transitions into the "national" characteristics of different peoples, focusing specifically on the ideal Greek type and the significance of skin color.</meta>

...rustics, and sluggish, having thieving thoughts. The degree to which each of them possesses these traits depends on how close they are to one another, or if they are separated by a greater distance toward the south or toward the north. Those living in the middle regions of the inhabited world possess a moderate appearance, with mixed physical signs and dispositions. The easterners—those toward the rising stars—and the southerners differ from one another, just as the northerners do. For example, there is a difference between those inhabiting the western ends of Libya <note>The ancient Greek name for the continent of Africa</note> and those Iberians <note>People of the Spanish peninsula</note> whose land reaches down to the outer sea <note>The Atlantic Ocean</note>; though both are westerners, the Libyans resemble Ethiopians, while the Iberians resemble Celts <note>Ancient peoples of Western and Central Europe</note>. To put it briefly: the south is mostly characterized by a mixture of dryness and heat, while the north is characterized by moisture and cold. Other regions vary according to which of these they are closer to, with their physical forms and characters following that climate. However, a complicating factor for many is that many people from various nations have migrated and settled in other lands.

### Concerning the Greek Race:

**I**f the Greek and Ionian <note>The Ionians were one of the major tribes of Greece, often associated with the culture of Athens and the coast of Asia Minor</note> race has been preserved in its purity, its members are sufficiently tall, broader, upright, and well-built. They are fairer of skin, blond, and have a firm yet moderate amount of flesh. Their legs are straight and their extremities well-formed. The head is medium-sized and rounded. The throat is strong. The hair is somewhat blond, soft, and gently curling. The face is square, the lips thin, and the nose straight. The eyes are moist, bright <term>charopous</term> <note>original: "charopous" — a term for blue, gray, or amber eyes that suggest a piercing, lion-like gaze</note>, fierce, and full of light. For the Greek race is the most "well-eyed" of all nations.

### Concerning Skin Color:

**I**t is clear from what has been said before that a black complexion indicates cowardice and a devious mind. A white and slightly blond complexion indicates strength and spirit. An extremely white, unmixed complexion leads to manliness <note>This is a surprising claim in ancient physiognomy, as extreme whiteness was often linked to "cold" or "feminine" temperaments; the author here suggests a pure, bright white is a sign of high character</note>. A ruddy color over the whole body is a sign of a deceitful and shifty sense of shame. A complexion tinged with a sickly green <note>original: "chlorou" — can mean pale green or yellowish-pallor</note> is a sign of both cowardice and malice, unless it is caused by disease. The color known as "honey-pale" <term>melichloron</term> <note>A yellowish-pale or "honey-toned" complexion</note> suggests a person who is cowardly, gluttonous, talkative, hot-tempered, and biting in their speech. A flame-colored complexion indicates madness. A gentle red is the sign of a gifted person—one who is quick to learn and agile. These signs apply to the whole body, but regarding specific parts: a reddish chest indicates a "boiling" of anger.

<summary>This page discusses how geographic location and climate shape the physical and moral traits of different nations, specifically defining the "pure" Greek physical ideal and the personality traits associated with various skin complexions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the physiognomic analysis of physical traits, moving from general geographic influences to specific facial features. It concludes the discussion on facial flushing and begins a detailed catalog of eye and hair characteristics as indicators of internal character.</meta>

# PHYSIOGNOMONICS. BOOK II.

...reports. Just as if someone has the veins about the neck and the veins in the temples swollen, so as to be visible, and has a blood-red complexion, these faces by themselves reveal those who are bashful <note>Original: αἰσχυντηλούς (aischyntēlous) — those prone to shame or modesty</note>. But if only the "sources" <note>likely referring to the tear ducts or the origins of the veins in the temples</note> are such, call them wine-bibbers <note>Original: οἰνόφλυγας (oinophlygas) — literally "wine-bubbles," a term for habitual drunkards</note>. Regarding the colors in the eyes, it has already been said, and here too, as much as need requires, shall be spoken:

The eyes that are somewhat reddish: if combined with dryness, you would not be wrong in calling such people prone to anger. If combined with moisture, they are wine-bibbers. A glaucous <note>Original: γλαυκότης (glaukotēs) — a shimmering blue-gray or pale green color, often associated by Greeks with the eyes of owls or lions</note> quality in the eyes indicates a wilder nature; blackness indicates a milder one. For most wild animals have glaucous eyes. How will you find those that are well-portioned <note>Original: ἔμμορα (emmora) — referring to those with a balanced or fortunate share of nature</note> having black eyes? Among the glaucous, those that are excessively glaucous are cowardly. Those of a greenish-yellow <note>Original: χλωρῶδες (chlōrōdes) — the color of young grass or pale honey</note> hue are wild. That which is similar to the color of olives is brave. The greenish-dark <note>Original: κεχλωμέλαν (kechlōmelan)</note>, which is not quite black, is both cowardly and deceitful. If the eyes are yellowish-tawny, the person is firm and high-minded. For "membranous" <note>Original: μεμβράνοντα (membranonta) — likely referring to a wide-eyed, staring, or bulging appearance where the whites or lids are prominent</note> eyes accuse one of a lewd madness <note>Original: μαργοσύνην (margosynēn) — a term for uncontrollable lust or gluttony</note>, for the goat looks in such a way. Reddish eyes indicate a sense of shame; you may judge this from the eyes of a dog. Pale eyes, or those having shifted colors, or those that are quickly fluttering, signify a suspicious person. Sparkling eyes are lustful <note>Original: ἀφροδίσιοι (aphrodisioi) — literally "belonging to Aphrodite"</note>, such as birds possess.

## Concerning Hair.

Dry and curly hair <note>Original: Αὔαρχα οὐλότριχα (auarkha oulotrikha)</note>: call such a person cowardly and shifty <note>Original: κορδαλέον (kordaleon) — related to the 'kordax,' a vulgar and suggestive dance of the ancient comedy</note>. But the straight-haired person is wilder and more restless. The best hair is that which holds the middle between these. For an extreme density of hair is beast-like. And sparseness <note>Original: ψευδότης (pseudotēs) — though literally "falseness," in this context of hair it likely refers to thinness or a "bald-faced" lack of texture</note> is a sign of malice and trickery. The middle ground between these is praiseworthy. To be covered in very soft hair is womanish. Yet, very hard hairs are not good either, for they are a sign of wildness. The middle, in this too, is best. Black hair reports cowardice and excessive chatter <note>Original: πολυκορδίαν (polykordian) — possibly a corruption of a term for talkativeness or complexity of heart</note>. But hair that is excessively blonde or whitish-pale, such as that of the Scythians <note>Nomadic people from the Eurasian steppes</note> and Celts <note>Ancient peoples of Western Europe</note>, signifies ignorance, clumsiness, and wildness. But a gentle tawny-yellow is sought after for readiness to learn, mildness, and skill. Unmixed redness and a fiery skin color are not good. For for the most part, their characters are beast-like, shameless, and greedy. Legs that are very hairy with dense and large hairs signify a difficulty in learning and wildness.

# ADAMANTIUS

Those whose waist and thighs (excluding the genitals) are very hairy, you should suppose to be lustful. Call those with long hair on the belly and chest light-minded, fickle, and licentious. Hairy shoulders and backs indicate a resemblance to the seagull <note>Original: αἴθυιαν (aithyian) — a water-bird, likely a gull or cormorant, associated in ancient lore with restlessness and a lack of solid ground</note>. For such people are shown to have high-flown thoughts that are not solid. As for those whose chests are covered in thick hair by themselves, call them hot-headed <note>Original: θερμοβούλους (thermoboulous) — literally "warm-counseled," meaning impulsive or passionate</note> and double-minded. When the entire body is covered in stiff, thick hair, it is ox-like <note>Original: βοῶδες (boōdes) — suggesting the physical strength but potential dullness of a bull</note>. To have a hairy neck near the head, consider a sign of strength and courage. And if the hair of the forehead descends into the middle but retreats on either side, such a person is full of magnanimity and strength. Hair that stands straight up is a witness to cowardice and malice. Those whose eyebrows slant downward toward the nose but move upward toward the temples, compare them to pigs. Those with very bushy eyebrows are troublesome; a charge of guilt is fitting for them.

->Concerning Movement.<-

Regarding movement, know the following. Since the natural cause is spontaneous, one must distinguish it from that which is intentional. There are those who fashion themselves and move with a studied, ambitious, and vigorous effort. The worse of these is the "maker of friendship" <note>likely referring to one who affects friendliness for gain</note>. For some, acting out a kind of solemnity, court offices and powers without being true friends, and seek marriages without affection; they are able to reshape themselves into cheapness, or stinginess, or industriousness, or frequent activity, by whatever way of life they think will serve those they are courting. Others among them act delicately. Some wish to appear as boyish favorites, while others are luxurious in their own right, like women, inviting attention. There is a third type: those who are modest by nature but wander into an affectation of manliness, which is why they are easily suspected. For though they strive to imitate the gait, voice, and look of a man, if they should suddenly be frightened or troubled, they quickly run back to their own nature. And even without this, one could not easily fail to learn what is affected in them. Those who take long strides are effective in every deed and are high-minded. Those who take small strides are ineffective, bitter, and by nature stingy and thievish, desiring secret things. Speed in movement, when combined with an upright posture and a bright expression, produces a hot-headed, enterprising, and active character. But if one walking fast looks down as if in suspicion, and seems about to fall, and his whole appearance is enchanting <note>possibly meaning deceptive or overly charming</note>, and—

## PHYSIOGNOMY. BOOK II.

...shaping himself in this way provides faithful evidence of stinginess, cowardice, malice, and a lack of generosity. Along with this, rapid movement, agitation in the eyes, an unsteady head, and humming <note>Original: ἄσματα (asmata) — literally "songs," here referring to nervous humming or singing to oneself</note> are signs of a man who is prone to evil-doing, boastfulness, and violence. He who walks quickly but with small steps is greedy and light-minded, clearly reaching an extreme in these traits. A slow-moving gait <note>Original: χολαία βάδισις (cholaia badisis) — literally a "lame" or "halting" walk, though here describing a natural, leisurely pace</note>, if it is natural, is somewhat sluggish, but indicates a deep-thinking and gentle person, unless more significant signs conflict with it. But if a man should slow down intentionally, stopping in the middle of the road and looking around with a high-held neck, know that he is insolent, proud, and prone to adultery. It is good to pay attention to the hands and feet, for they carry the signs of the whole body’s movement.

A man who moves slightly in his shoulders while being gently hunched over does not lack high-mindedness and courage, for this is how a lion walks. But one who moves in the shoulders while standing upright with a high-held neck is stubborn and insolent, for this is how a horse walks. You can infer the rest of the character traits from these examples. Those who move with their entire body—in the shoulders and in every single limb—are unmanly, for such a style is outright feminine. Also effeminate <note>Original: ἀνδρόγυνοι (androgynoi) — literally "men-women," used here to describe men with feminine mannerisms</note> are those who lean to the right as they move. Those who lean to the left are more foolish. Those who cringe and bow themselves down are flatterers, acting just like a dog when it wags its tail and fawns.

---

->Concerning Breath.<-

Smooth breath, so quiet as to seem as if one is not breathing, belongs to a man of thought; learn the nature of his thought from his eyes. If the breath should be released in a sudden burst, as if from a receding tide, he is gripped by some grief. If he gasps while shaking his head, he seems to be mad and speaks or acts without sense. If he does these things with fixed eyes, he is planning or scheming even more. Smooth breath, proceeding without bile or effort, shows a healthy mind. When the breath is a low growl and the person inhales deeply, it indicates savagery and drunkenness. Those who are short-breathed, whose breath is drawn in like a draft feeding a fire, are ill-advised, evil-spirited, and prone to every whim and word. Those who breathe high in the chest, and whose breath is frequent and light, are marked by cowardice and despondency. These signs can also indicate an effeminate nature when other signs bear witness to it.

It details how intentional or natural movements can signal traits ranging from courage and deep thought to pride, greed, and cowardice.</summary>

->ADAMANTIUS<-

## Concerning the Utterance, or the Voice ~

To speak with a very deep and **shattered** <note>original: σαθρὸν (sathron) — literally "rotten" or "unsound," describing a voice that is cracked, hoarse, or lacks resonance</note> voice befits the foolish, the insolent, and the gluttonous. A voice that is somewhat muffled or **bleating** <note>original: βληχωδεστέρα (blēchōdestera) — literally sheep-like</note> belongs to the senseless. Those who begin with a deep tone but end on a high pitch are prone to anger and despondency. To speak with a high and **harsh** <note>original: αὐχμηρὸν (auchmēron) — literally "parched" or "dry"</note> voice is characteristic of a shifty man. He who speaks with a high, soft, and flexible voice indicates an **effeminate nature** <note>original: ἀνδρόγυνον (androgynon) — literally "man-woman," referring to a man with feminine traits</note>. Those who sound hollow, deep, and inflexible possess noble characters; they are graced by high-mindedness and justice. Those who speak with a certain softness and depth have gentle thoughts. 

Those who speak with a clamor, in a high and nasal tone, are gluttonous, vain, and flighty in their thinking. A **weak** and simultaneously **cold** <note>The "cold" voice in ancient medicine suggested a lack of vital heat and spirit</note> voice signifies greed, a miserable nature, and a suspicion toward all people. Those who speak through their noses are liars, malicious, and envious, rejoicing in the misfortunes of others. To speak with a forceful and slightly shattered voice signifies anger, violence, lawlessness, and a hatred of mankind. To speak in a high and weak voice indicates laziness and cowardice. Speaking quickly is a sign of rashness and folly. Those who speak with difficulty are, for the most part, bad and foolish. 

If someone’s voice resembles that of a specific animal, one must categorize their character according to that animal. For some let out a voice like dogs, others like monkeys, others like donkeys, others like horses, others like bears, and others like sheep or other creatures. One should know as a general rule that a **moderate state** <note>original: τὸ μέσον (to meson) — the "middle" or "mean," a core Greek virtue of balance</note> in movement, voice, complexion, size, and the harmony of limbs is usually good. The opposite, however, is bad. ~

## CONCERNING THE TYPES OF MEN ~

It is necessary to illustrate the types of men—not every kind, but those who are noteworthy. From these examples, it will not be difficult to recognize the rest. ~

### The Courageous Type

For the truly courageous type, the entire body is upright; the ribs, the joints, and all the extremities of the body are strong. The bones are large but not hard. The belly is broad, the shoulders are **hollow** <note>original: κοῖλοι ὦμοι (koiloi ōmoi) — likely referring to well-defined musculature that creates "hollows" rather than being flat or fatty</note>, the shoulder blades are powerful, and the ribs are well-spaced. The chest and upper back are robust, the hip is firm, the legs are fleshy and strong around the ankles, and the feet are well-articulated.

<meta>This page continues Adamantius’s analysis of physical types, moving from the qualities of the voice to a detailed catalog of "types" (eide) of men—the brave, the cowardly, the gifted, the dull, the shameless, the orderly, and the cheerful—correlating specific facial and bodily features with internal character.</meta>

### ON PHYSIOGNOMY. BOOK II.

...an intense complexion. A gaze that is moist and **bright** <note>original: χαροπόν (charopon) — often used to describe blue-gray or gleaming eyes, associated with a lion-like spirit</note>; eyes that are large, yet not entirely wide open, nor squinting, but rather half-closing. The eyebrows are **extended** <note>original: πεταμέναι (petamenai) — meaning spread out or wingspan-like, rather than bunched together in a frown</note>. The forehead is neither too smooth nor too short. The voice is simple, strong, and great. The breath is not rapid. Such is the man who is brave and powerful.

->Appearance of the Coward<-

The coward is as follows: his hair is soft, and his entire posture is **unbalanced** <note>original: ἀσαμούνες (asymmetron?) — the OCR suggests a corrupted form of "disproportionate" or "lacking firmness"</note>. The neck is long, the complexion is dark, and the eye is troubled. The eyelids move quickly, and his **breathing** <note>original: ἆσθμα (asthma) — here meaning labored or panting breath caused by fear</note> is agitated. His legs are thin, his waist is long, and his chest is weak. He is long-handed, and his voice is shrill and soft.

->Appearance of the Gifted<-

The man of natural talent should be of a good size, with skin that is white and reddish, and hair that is fair—not entirely curly, yet not perfectly straight. The whole body is held upright, the limbs are large, and the joints are **well-fitted** to one another. The flesh is moderately soft, and the legs and thighs are well-nourished. He stands on strong ankles, and the hollows of his hands are well-formed. The fingers are soft, long, and set apart from one another. The extremities are not overly fleshy, nor yet too thin. The eyes are moist, clear, and bright, and within them dwells a joyful character.

->Appearance of the Dull-Witted<-

The man who is dull and insensitive is likely to be very white, fleshy, **pot-bellied** <note>original: προγάτορα (progastora) — meaning having a prominent belly, which was often associated with sluggishness or gluttony in antiquity</note>, and thick-legged. The joints are small and buried in flesh. The collarbones are fused together. The fingers are stunted and thick. The extremities are underdeveloped, the cheeks are fleshy, the forehead is round, and the gaze is dull and gaping.

->Appearance of the Shameless<-

A shameless man should be born with these traits: eyes that are upcast and gleaming; eyelids that are wide open and thick. He is **quick-tempered** <note>original: ταχύπυρο (tachypyro) — literally "quick to fire," referring to a volatile or glowing appearance</note>, thick-nosed, and always looking for an opening. He carries himself with tension, has a ruddy complexion, and a sharp voice.

->Appearance of the Orderly<-

The orderly or modest man speaks little and walks with short steps. He moves his eyelids at a moderate pace—neither constantly nor too rarely. He has a bright gaze, not overly gleaming, yet moist to the look, and he is often full of **blushing** <note>original: ἐρυθήματος (erythēmatos) — ancient physiognomists viewed the ability to blush as a sign of a healthy sense of shame and morality</note>.

->Appearance of the Cheerful<-

Signs of the cheerful man: a forehead that is fleshy, smooth, and low, and the entire face is rather fleshy. The appearance is **subdued** <note>original: ὑπηκλόν (hypēklon?) — likely meaning gentle or mild</note>. The eyes are moist, not overly bright, but not staring. His movement is slow, and his voice is gentle.

<meta>This page continues Adamantius’s catalog of physical and moral types. Having previously described the brave, the cowardly, and the gifted, the author now defines the specific anatomical signs of the distressed, the effeminate, the bitter, the gentle, the foolish, the greedy, and the gambler.</meta>

<header>ADAMANTIUS</header>

### The Type of the Distressed Man ~

The face of the distressed man <note>original: ἀνιαροῦ (aniarou) — meaning one who is in sorrow, vexed, or burdened by grief</note> is thin, the forehead is wrinkled, the eyebrows are turned away, and the eyelids are stretched tight. He moves like those who are heavy with sleep.

### The Type of the Androgynous Man ~

The androgynous man <note>original: ἀνδρόγυνος (androgynos) — used here to describe an effeminate man or one whose character lacks masculine firmness</note> has a "moist" or shimmering gaze; his eyes move restlessly from one thing to another, and he has a [particular] wandering step. The forehead and cheeks are thin. <margin>o</margin> The eyebrows are raised and unsteady; the neck is tilted, and the waist is never still. All his limbs are in motion, and he tends to hop about. He is prone to leaping, his knees knock together, his hand gestures are made with palms turned upward, and he is constantly looking around at himself. His voice is thin, shrill <note>original: ἐπικλάζουσα (epiklazousa) — literally "breaking" or "clanging," like a bird's cry</note>, clear, and very high-pitched.

### The Type of the Bitter Man ~

One should recognize the bitter man by his tendency to snarl <note>original: σεσηρέναι (sesērenai) — to show the teeth, like a grinning dog or a person in a sneer</note>; he is somewhat sallow, thin, and has a dry look in his eyes. His forehead is wrinkled, his speech is intense, and he pants for breath. He claps his hands or often throws them upward, and he drags or tosses his feet as he walks.

### The Type of the Gentle Man ~

The gentle man is by nature robust in every aspect of his form and is well-fleshed. His flesh is moist and soft. He is well-proportioned in his limbs. His gaze is steady and slow-moving. His voice is resonant <note>original: χολαία (cholaia) — likely referring to a "bilious" or clear, sharp tone</note> and soft. The frame of his chest <note>original: τῶν τειχῶν ὁ περιδεσμος (tōn teichōn ho peridesmos) — literally "the binding of the walls," a metaphorical way to describe the ribcage or torso</note> appears to rise upward.

### The Type of the Foolish Man ~

The foolish man is fickle in his intentions. Let him have the skin around his eyes loose, a drooping gaze, and a lowered voice. Let him walk with a heavy, difficult gait, and let his movements be entirely without rhythm.

### The Type of the Miser ~

Say that the man who loves money <note>original: φιλάργυρον (philargyron) — literally a "lover of silver"</note> is of this sort: small-limbed, small-eyed, and small-faced. He is a fast walker, hunched over in posture, and a fast talker with a shrill cry. His complexion is somewhat reddish.

### The Type of the Gambler ~

<margin>o</margin> A man hairy with straight, black hair. Having a thick beard, thick hair at the temples, and eyes that are oily, gleaming, and twinkling—such a man is a lover of dice, a lover of dancing, and a lover of wine <note>original: φιλόποινος (philopoinos) — while literally "lover of punishment," in this context of vices it likely refers to a lover of drinking bouts</note>.

### On the Tame and the Wild ~

There also exist the "foolishly wicked" <note>original: μωροπόνηροι (mōroponēroi) — a compound describing those who are both stupid and malicious</note>, who possess a mixture of signs of both folly and malice. Animals also exhibit this "foolish-wickedness," just as among the wild beasts, where some belonging to the same genus are tame while others are wild. For of these...

<meta>catchword: For of these</meta>

<summary>This page catalogs the physical traits associated with various character types, including the effeminate, the bitter, the gentle, and the greedy. Adamantius concludes by noting that even animals can display a mixture of "foolish-wickedness" based on their wild or tame nature.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Adamantius’s specific catalog of human types. It transitions from the previous discussion of gentle and harsh temperaments into a final character portrait—the "foolishly-wicked" man—and concludes with an epilogue explaining that these descriptions are merely sketches to be combined by the observant student of physiognomy.</meta>

<header>PHYSIOGNOMONICS, BOOK 2</header>

For if you examine individual cases, you will see that the forms of both seem to resemble each other. Nevertheless, the forms of the tame <note>original: ἡμέρων (hēmerōn) — referring to domesticated animals or civilized, gentle humans</note> are simpler and more good-natured, while those of the wild <note>original: ἀγρίων (agriōn) — meaning savage, wild, or unrefined</note> are usually coarser, more clumsy, and more erratic. You might observe this especially among wild animals, and also sheep, horses, donkeys, and the like. While they may share similar traits, some are tame and milder, while others are wild, rougher, more awkward, more violent, and "drier" in appearance. Their characters follow their physical forms. Therefore, one must distinguish among men in the same way. For there is a twofold nature to the appearance that grows in men according to each of the signs. Just men are formed from the same signs [as others], but some possess these signs in a wilder way and others in a tamer way; the same applies to the self-controlled man and to the brave. The difference in the signs must be distinguished by thickness and thinness, and by "wildness" and "dryness." This also applies to injustice and licentiousness. For even when these signs are the same, the natural presence of the tame or the wild indicates either a more savage form of injustice or a milder one. Even among the brave, some are wild while others are persuasive <note>original: πιτανοί (pitanoi) — likely a scribal error for πιθανοί (pithanoi), meaning plausible, persuasive, or winning</note>. These signs do not distinguish them by just a little, but by these factors alone, as I have said, one distinguishes...

### The Type of the Foolishly-Wicked Man ~

As for the foolishly-wicked man <note>original: μωροπονήρου (mōroponērou) — a compound of "mōros" (foolish) and "ponēros" (wicked or burdened); a character who is both malicious and dim-witted</note>, his hair is stiff and straight, and his head is narrow and slanted. His ears are large and bent back; his throat and neck are round. His eyes are dry, dark, small, sunken, watery, and staring fixedly. His chest is narrow and elongated. His knee is long. His mouth is dry, quite long, and unyielding, so that his face appears as if it has been split apart. He is hunchbacked, pot-bellied, and thick-legged; the extremities of his feet and hands are thick and hard, and his complexion is sallow. He looks as though he has just risen from sleep or is suffering from the bloat of a hangover <note>original: κραιπάλης (kraipalēs) — the distress or sickness following a bout of drinking</note>. His voice is bleating <note>original: βληχώδης (blēchōdēs) — like the sound of a sheep or goat</note>, small, and impudent.

### Epilogue ~

All these things that have been said are like uncolored paintings; they are sketches of men depicted by line alone. However, the many and varied types of men arise from many signs existing in each individual part; you must judge them by their mixtures and their relative strengths, comparing them to one another, both trusting and doubting the evidence as you go.

->~ THE END ~<-

It ends with an epilogue advising the reader that these descriptions are foundational sketches to be synthesized when observing real people.</summary>

<meta>This page marks a transition from the physiognomic character studies of Adamantius to a different form of ancient divination: palmomancy, the art of interpreting muscle twitches. It begins with a dedication to a King Ptolemy by Melampus, a figure styled as a "Sacred Scribe."</meta>

# MELAMPUS THE SACRED SCRIBE,
## On the Divination of Twitches, addressed to King Ptolemy.

Whatever I have written for you in the previous books, O most excellent King Ptolemy <note>likely Ptolemy II Philadelphus, though these texts are often later works attributed to the Ptolemaic era</note>, I noted down the outcomes based on the moon’s cycles and recorded the omens. Since you have now sent word to me to write for you concerning the twitches <term>palmoi (παλμοί) — involuntary muscle spasms or "jumping" sensations used in divination</term> that occur in the limbs of men, I consider this command a great and most pleasant one; and having already received other gifts from you, I have collected these findings for you as well.

If the **crown of the head** <note>original: κορυφὴ (koruphē)</note> should twitch, it signifies grief and unpleasantness, or a journey on a long road. For a slave, it means an insult. For a virgin, a public gathering <note>original: ἀγοράν (agoran) — referring to the marketplace or a public assembly</note>. For a widow, grief. For those in public life <note>original: ἐν δῆλοις (en dēlois) — referring to people in prominent positions or matters out in the open</note>, it signifies an escape from evils, as clearly as they shall fall. For a virgin, marriage—just as Zeus released Athena from the crown of his head. For a widow, likewise marriage. For a rich man, financial loss and a plot. For a soldier, advancement. For a sailor, a storm; however, those caught in the storm will be saved by that same twitch of the crown.

If the **head** should twitch, or if the hair stands on end, or if it persists for a longer time in those with long hair, it signifies a plot, or a loss caused by some friend or relative. For a slave, it means disease. For a virgin, fear. For a widow, an insult. For the poor and those who happen to be in want, it indicates good things. For the rich, dejection and an upheaval of their way of life. For the sick, it indicates life.

Alternatively, if the **head** twitches for a longer time, it signifies something evil. For a slave, it means a sudden death. For a widow, harm. For those in public life, it indicates submission and then, in turn, freedom.

If the **whole head** twitches, it indicates death. For those in public life, it signifies many good things. If the **back part of the head** <note>the occiput</note> as far as its circumference should twitch, it indicates an enemy in every respect, a plot, and the death of a relative. For everyone else, it means a disaster. For a slave, something good. For a virgin, success yet again. For a widow, disease. For those in public life, for a rich man, danger. For a farmer, toil. If the **right side of the head** should twitch, it indicates something good. For a slave, joy and freedom. For a virgin, blame. For a widow, something beautiful and the persistence of her needs. If the **left side of the head** should twitch, it indicates harm. For a rich man, disturbances. For a slave, a change of house. For a virgin, blame. For a widow, an insult.

ι <note>This "iota" serves as a signature or quire mark, indicating this is the tenth section or sheet of the manuscript.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Melampus's treatise on palmomancy (divination by twitches), dedicated to King Ptolemy, and catalogs the prophetic meanings of twitches in various parts of the head. The interpretations vary significantly based on the social status of the person, such as whether they are a slave, a virgin, a widow, or a soldier.</summary>
<keywords>Melampus, palmomancy, divination, muscle twitches, King Ptolemy, head omens, ancient superstition, social status in divination</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the divinatory manual of Melampus, moving from the crown of the head to specific areas of the face and eyes. It cites several authorities on the subject, including Phemonoe and Antiphon.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>
<sig>Γ ii</sig>

If the **brain** <note>original: Ἐγκέφαλος (enkephalos); in this context, it refers to a sensation of throbbing or twitching felt deep within the head</note> should throb, it indicates disease for the healthy. But for the sick, it indicates health, growth of the body, and life. For the childless, a child. And joy. For those at home, travel; and for those abroad, a return to their own fatherland. For a soldier and a sailor, it indicates danger to the head.

If the **forehead** should twitch, it indicates falling into reckless and shameless deeds. For a slave, the death of a master. For a virgin, a plot. For a widow, harm. If the **right part of the forehead** should twitch, such a person is great and self-controlled. For a slave, an insult. For a virgin, counsel. If the **left part of the forehead** should twitch, it indicates bad things in every respect, and weeping. For a slave, a benefit. For a virgin, marriage. For a widow, blame. If the **middle of the forehead** should twitch, it indicates mourning. For a slave, evil. For a virgin, a benefit. For a widow, an insult. Others, however, say it indicates good in everything.

If the **right temple** should twitch, it is good in every respect. For a slave, instability. For a virgin, a temporary feast. For a widow, a journey and benefit. If the **left temple** should twitch, it is good in every respect. It indicates an unexpected benefit. For a slave, instability. For a virgin, the breaking off of a marriage. For a widow, good.

If the **right eyebrow** should twitch, it indicates disease for a short time, and after a little while, prosperity. For a poor man, wealth. For a slave, good. For a virgin, blame. For a widow, an insult. If the **left eyebrow** should twitch, it signifies much unexpected good. Furthermore, the person will be fortunate and will be trusted. If the **middle of the eyebrows** should twitch, according to Phemonoe <note>a legendary Greek prophetess or Sibyl said to have invented hexameter verse</note>, it is bad for everyone. For a slave, good. For a virgin, counsel. For a widow, benefit.

If the **right eye** should twitch, according to Phemonoe, and the Egyptian <note>likely referring to Hermes Trismegistus or a generic "wise Egyptian" source</note>, and Antiphon <note>an Athenian writer famous for his work on the interpretation of dreams and omens</note>, the person will have an enemy under his power. It also brings back those who are abroad.

If the **upper eyelid of the right eye** should twitch, it certainly indicates an acquisition. According to Antiphon, it indicates success and health. For a slave, a plot. For a widow, a journey. If the **lower eyelid of the right eye** should twitch, it indicates tears. For a slave, good. For a virgin, an insult. For a widow, submission. If the **corner of the right eye** <note>original: κανθὸς (kanthos)</note> should twitch, or the **dark part** <note>original: τὸ μέλας (to melas); referring to the iris or pupil</note>, it indicates unpleasantness for a poor man. For a slave, slander. For a virgin, danger. For a widow, blame. It indicates the arrival of an enemy from abroad. If the **left eye** should twitch, someone whom the person especially loves will come to them from a foreign land. Or the person themselves will go on a journey. And if anyone has fled far away from them, they will find them abroad. For female persons, it indicates hope. For a poor man...

<summary>This page lists the divinatory meanings of twitches in the brain, forehead, temples, eyebrows, and eyes, categorized by the person's social status (slave, virgin, widow, etc.). It cites traditional authorities like Phemonoe and Antiphon to validate its omens of health, travel, and social conflict.</summary>
